Psychiatric Disorders, Chapter 8

Final Textbook 3, Psychiatric Disorders, full-length edition: chapter 8 of 11. The book goes up chapter by chapter; the A4 colour and black and white PDFs come once all 11 chapters are in.

Chapter 8 Feeding, Eating, Elimination and Sleep-Wake Disorders

In this chapter
  1. 8.1 Eating, Elimination and Sleep: Three Families of Disorder and Their History: explain what the feeding and eating, elimination and sleep-wake disorders have in common, trace how the eating disorders were named from Morton and Gull to binge-eating disorder, and place every disorder of the chapter in DSM-5-TR, ICD-10 and ICD-11.
  2. 8.2 Pica, Rumination Disorder and Avoidant/Restrictive Food Intake Disorder: diagnose the three feeding disorders that usually begin in childhood, describe who develops them and the dangers they carry, and tell ARFID apart from picky eating and from anorexia nervosa.
  3. 8.3 Anorexia Nervosa: Criteria, the Starving Body and the Course: apply the DSM-5-TR criteria, subtypes and BMI severity ratings for anorexia nervosa, compare them with DSM-IV, ICD-10 and ICD-11, recognise its medical findings and explain why it is the deadliest eating disorder.
  4. 8.4 Bulimia Nervosa, Binge-Eating Disorder and the Other Specified Eating Disorders: define a binge, diagnose bulimia nervosa and binge-eating disorder by their frequency and features, recognise the marks of purging, and tell the three main eating disorders and their atypical forms apart.
  5. 8.5 Why Eating Disorders Develop and How They Are Treated: evaluate the sociocultural, family, personality, cognitive and biological explanations of the eating disorders, and describe their medical management, the drugs that do and do not help, and the psychological treatments with the best evidence.
  6. 8.6 Enuresis and Encopresis: The Elimination Disorders: diagnose enuresis and encopresis with their subtypes and age rules, explain why children wet and soil, and describe the medical, drug and behavioural treatments for each.
  7. 8.7 Normal Sleep, the Sleep-Wake Disorders and Insomnia Disorder: use the vocabulary of the sleep laboratory, name the ten DSM-5-TR sleep-wake disorder groups and their ICD homes, and diagnose, explain and treat insomnia disorder, including the hypnotic drugs.
  8. 8.8 Hypersomnolence Disorder, Narcolepsy and Kleine-Levin Syndrome: distinguish the disorders of excessive sleepiness, explain narcolepsy's tetrad as REM sleep breaking into waking after the loss of hypocretin, and tell cataplexy apart from catalepsy and its other look-alikes.
  9. 8.9 Breathing-Related and Circadian Rhythm Sleep-Wake Disorders: diagnose obstructive sleep apnoea hypopnoea, central sleep apnoea and sleep-related hypoventilation, describe the five circadian rhythm sleep-wake disorders, and explain the treatments for each.
  10. 8.10 Parasomnias, Restless Legs Syndrome and Substance-Induced Sleep Disorders: tell sleepwalking, sleep terrors, nightmares and REM sleep behaviour disorder apart by sleep stage, time of night and recall, and diagnose restless legs syndrome and the substance/medication-induced sleep disorders.

Weightage. 20 of the 411 questions mapped in this book (4.9%) test this chapter, 9 of them from real past papers. 2 of its 10 sections are High yield.

On 19 November 1944, in Minneapolis, thirty-six young men moved into the Laboratory of Physiological Hygiene of the University of Minnesota, whose rooms lay beneath the stands of the university's football stadium. They would eat and sleep there for most of the next year. They were conscientious objectors, men whose consciences would not let them fight, and they were doing Civilian Public Service instead of military service; fifteen of them belonged to the historic peace churches, the Mennonites, the Church of the Brethren and the Quakers. Several hundred objectors had volunteered, and these thirty-six were chosen because they were healthy in body and in mind. What they had volunteered for was to starve. The physiologist in charge, Ancel Keys (1904 to 2004), who had already helped the army to develop the K-ration that soldiers carried into combat, wanted to know what prolonged hunger does to the body and to the mind, and how a starved person is best fed back to health. The war was going to leave millions of people starving across Europe and Asia, and nobody knew how to bring them back safely.

For the first twelve weeks the men ate normally, about 3,200 kilocalories a day, while the scientists measured everything about them. Then, for twenty-four weeks, their food was cut to about 1,570 kilocalories a day, roughly half of what they had been eating, served in two meals made of the foods of a European famine: potatoes, turnips, swedes (rutabagas), bread and macaroni. Each man was also expected to walk about 22 miles (35 kilometres) a week. On average they lost about a quarter of their body weight. Then their minds began to change. Food took over their thoughts. They talked about it, read about it and daydreamed about it; many collected recipes and cookbooks and hoarded food-related things; they made their small meals last as long as they possibly could. One of them put it like this: "I wait for mealtime. When it comes I eat slowly and make the food last as long as possible. The menu never gets monotonous even if it is the same each day or is of poor quality. It is food and all food tastes good. Even dirty crusts of bread in the street look appetizing." The men became low in mood, irritable and withdrawn, they lost interest in sex, and their scores rose on the depression, hysteria and hypochondriasis scales of the Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory (MMPI). Their bodies slowed down to save energy: heart rate, body temperature and breathing all fell, and some of the men developed swelling (oedema) of the limbs. Two men could not keep to the diet and were dismissed from the study.

Giving the food back did not end it. Twelve weeks of controlled rehabilitation followed, in which the men were divided into groups that received different amounts of food, and then eight weeks in which they could eat as much as they liked. The hunger did not switch off when the food returned. Some men ate enormous meals, a few of them until they were sick, and many of them said afterwards that rehabilitation, not starvation, had been the hardest part of the whole experiment. In the third week of rehabilitation one of them, Sam Legg, cut off three fingers of his left hand with an axe; half a century later he still could not say whether he had meant to do it. Some of the men said that it took them a year or more to recover their strength and return to a normal weight. The results filled two volumes and 1,385 pages, The Biology of Human Starvation, published by the University of Minnesota Press in 1950 under the names of Keys and his colleagues Josef Brožek, Austin Henschel, Olaf Mickelsen and Henry Longstreet Taylor.

Look at what these healthy, motivated young men developed simply because they were starved: a mind taken over by food, rituals around eating, hoarding, low mood, irritability, withdrawal from other people, and afterwards an appetite they could neither satisfy nor control. Every one of those features turns up in people with anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa, and DSM-5-TR itself warns that many of the depressive and obsessional features seen in anorexia nervosa may be secondary to the physical effects of semistarvation. So how much of an eating disorder is hunger, and how much is the person? And why did the men of Minnesota long for food, while a girl with anorexia nervosa refuses the food on the plate in front of her, and a young woman with bulimia nervosa eats far more than she wants and then makes herself sick? This chapter answers those questions, and then it turns to two other things that every human body must learn to regulate: emptying the bladder and the bowel, and sleeping. Remember the men of Minnesota; you will return to them at the end of the chapter.

8.1 Eating, Elimination and Sleep: Three Families of Disorder and Their HistoryLower yield · not asked yet

What do a girl who will not eat, a boy who wets his bed and a man who stops breathing in his sleep have in common? At first sight, almost nothing. One problem looks like a matter of willpower and vanity, one looks like a matter of growing up, and one looks like a matter for the ear, nose and throat surgeon. Yet DSM-5-TR places their disorders side by side, in three neighbouring chapters, and a clinical psychologist meets all three in ordinary practice. This first section explains why the three families belong together, tells the long and surprisingly human story of how the eating disorders came to be named, and gives you the map of codes and chapters that the rest of the chapter fills in.

Three things the body must learn to regulate

It is a Monday morning in the psychiatry outpatient department of a large government general hospital, and the waiting benches are full. Three of the people waiting will become the patients of this chapter. Shreya is seventeen and in her Class 12 year. Her parents have brought her because, in her mother's words, "she eats nothing". Over eight months she has cut out rice, then sweets, then oil, and now she eats a few pieces of cucumber and a bowl of thin dal, weighs her portions on a kitchen scale and goes for long walks after every meal. Her school uniform hangs off her shoulders, but when the doctor asks her how she feels about her body she says, quite calmly, that her thighs are still too big. On the next bench sits Varun, who is seven. His mother has brought him because he wets his bed on most nights. He was never fully dry at night, but it did not seem to matter until a school trip came up and he refused to go; now he cries when his cousins tease him. Beside them sits Sudha, who has brought her husband, a forty-five-year-old bank manager. He has come under protest. Sudha tells the doctor what he cannot: that his snoring shakes the bedroom, that every few minutes he goes silent, stops breathing, and then gasps and snorts as if he is drowning, and that last week he dozed off at a red light with their daughter in the car. He thinks he has simply been working too hard.

Each of these three people has a problem with something the human body is not born knowing how to do. A newborn baby has to learn to feed, first at the breast and then, over the following years, at the family table, eating what the family eats in the amounts and at the times the family eats it. In the preschool years a child learns to control the bladder and the bowel, first by day and later by night, so that urine and faeces are passed only in the right place. And a newborn sleeps in short bouts scattered across the day and the night; only gradually is that sleep gathered into one long night and a waking day (Book 1, Section 5.7.5, describes how sleep changes across the lifespan; toilet training and normal sleep development belong to the Developmental book). Eating, voiding and sleeping are bodily functions, so their disorders always have a biological side. But each is also learned, practised and shaped by family, culture and feeling, so each can go wrong in ways that are partly psychological. A frightened child withholds stool; a young woman who believes her worth depends on her weight starves; a worried man lies awake. That mixture of body and mind is exactly why these disorders are in a psychiatric manual at all.

DSM-5-TR gives each function its own chapter: feeding and eating disorders, elimination disorders and sleep-wake disorders. They come one after another, as chapters 10, 11 and 12 of the 22 chapters of the manual's Section II (twenty of which hold mental disorders), straight after the somatic symptom and related disorders (Section 1.8.2 lists the chapters in their order). Figure 8.1 is the map of this chapter. The feeding and eating family has six named disorders and its "other specified" and "unspecified" categories; the elimination family has two; and the sleep-wake family is the largest, with ten disorders or groups of disorders. The bottom row of the figure shows where the same disorders live in the World Health Organization's ICD-11, and it already hints at one of the stories of this section: ICD-11 keeps the eating and elimination disorders among the mental disorders, but gives the sleep-wake disorders a chapter of their own.

Why should a clinical psychologist care about all three families? There are four good reasons. First, these disorders are common: DSM-5-TR calls insomnia disorder the most prevalent of all the sleep disorders, and the eating disorders begin mostly in adolescence and young adulthood, the very years in which young people sit in classrooms and counselling rooms. Second, they rarely travel alone. People with eating disorders often have depression, anxiety disorders or substance use; children who wet or soil often have behavioural or emotional problems as well; and DSM-5-TR reminds clinicians that sleep disorders are often accompanied by depression, anxiety and changes in thinking that have to be addressed in the same treatment plan. Third, some of them kill. Anorexia nervosa has one of the highest death rates of any mental disorder, from the medical effects of starvation and from suicide (Section 8.3.7), and untreated sleep apnoea puts a sleepy driver like Sudha's husband on the road. Fourth, sleep is a window onto the rest of mental health. DSM-5-TR states that persistent sleep disturbance, both insomnia and excessive sleepiness, is an established risk factor for the later development of mental illnesses, including substance use disorders, and of other medical conditions, and that it may be the first, prodromal sign of an episode of mental illness, which gives the clinician a chance to intervene before the full episode arrives.

Clinical link

Ask about eating, sleeping and toileting in every assessment, whatever the referral question. A student referred for "exam stress" who has stopped eating dinner, a man referred for irritability who sleeps four hours a night, and a child referred for "naughtiness" who soils at school are all common. Patients rarely volunteer these problems: anorexia nervosa is often denied, bulimic episodes are kept secret out of shame, bedwetting embarrasses the whole family, and a person with sleep apnoea usually has no idea that he stops breathing. Often, as with Sudha, the most useful informant is the person who shares the bed or the dinner table.

What makes eating disordered

Everybody's eating goes wrong sometimes. You skip lunch before an interview, overeat at a wedding, fast on a religious day, or live on biscuits and tea during board examinations. None of that is a disorder. So where is the line? DSM-5-TR draws it in one sentence. The feeding and eating disorders are characterised by a persistent disturbance of eating or eating-related behaviour that results in an altered consumption or absorption of food and that significantly impairs physical health or psychosocial functioning. Read that definition slowly, because each part of it rules something out. "Persistent" rules out the passing episode: the week of skipped meals before an examination, or a few days of poor appetite during an illness. "Eating-related behaviour" widens the net beyond what goes into the mouth, to the vomiting, the laxatives, the rituals and the hours of exercise that surround eating. "Altered consumption or absorption" tells you what the behaviour does: the person eats too little, too much or the wrong things, or eats and then gets rid of the food before the body can absorb it. And "significantly impairs physical health or psychosocial functioning" is the familiar clinical-significance rule that runs through the whole manual (Book 2, Section 1.6.5, the four Ds): a fussy eater who grows normally, enjoys school and eats happily with friends has no disorder, however strange her preferences.

Within that definition DSM-5-TR gives full criteria for six disorders. Three of them, pica, rumination disorder and avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder (ARFID), are disturbances of what or how a person eats, and none of them involves any concern about body weight or shape; they usually begin in childhood and are taught in Section 8.2. The other three, anorexia nervosa, bulimia nervosa and binge-eating disorder (BED), are the eating disorders in the everyday sense, organised around restriction, binges and, in the first two, an overvalued concern with weight and shape; they are taught in Sections 8.3 and 8.4. Presentations that cause real distress or impairment but do not fit any of the six are recorded as other specified or unspecified feeding or eating disorder (Section 8.4.7).

One rule about these three main eating disorders is worth learning now, because it shapes every diagnosis in the rest of the chapter. The criteria for anorexia nervosa, bulimia nervosa and binge-eating disorder form a mutually exclusive classification: during a single episode, only one of the three can be diagnosed. Why not simply diagnose two when a person has the features of both? DSM-5-TR gives the reason directly: despite many shared psychological and behavioural features, the three disorders differ substantially in their clinical course, their outcome and their treatment needs, so the clinician must decide which one is present. In practice the criteria themselves do the deciding. A woman who binges and makes herself vomit while she is at a significantly low weight has anorexia nervosa of the binge-eating/purging type, not anorexia plus bulimia, because low weight is asked about first; a man who binges but never compensates has binge-eating disorder, not bulimia (Section 8.4.6 sets out the full decision tree). Notice the words "during a single episode". Over a lifetime, a person can and often does move from one diagnosis to another, a movement called diagnostic crossover; DSM-5-TR describes, for example, a minority of people whose bulimia nervosa later turns into anorexia nervosa (Section 8.4.4).

DSM-5-TR adds a thoughtful paragraph about a resemblance that the newspapers often exaggerate. Some people with the disorders in this chapter describe their eating in the language of addiction: craving, loss of control, and compulsive eating that continues despite harm. The manual suggests that the resemblance may reflect the involvement of the same brain systems, those concerned with reward and with regulatory self-control, in both the eating disorders and the substance use disorders. But it is careful to add that the relative contributions of shared and distinct factors in the development and maintenance of eating and substance use disorders remain insufficiently understood. That caution matters, because food addiction is a popular idea and the subject of much research, but it is not a diagnosis in DSM-5-TR or in ICD-11. A clinician who hears "I am addicted to sweets" writes down the binge-eating pattern, if there is one, and diagnoses binge-eating disorder or bulimia nervosa by their own criteria.

The last paragraph of DSM-5-TR's introduction answers a question that students often ask: if eating too little can be a mental disorder, why is eating too much not one? Why is obesity missing from the manual? Obesity is excess body fat, and it results from a long-term excess of energy taken in over energy used. DSM-5-TR leaves it out because a wide range of genetic, physiological, behavioural and environmental factors, varying from one person to the next, contributes to its development: it is a condition with many different causes, not a single pattern of disturbed behaviour and experience. (The usual adult definition, a body mass index of 30 or more, and Stanley Schachter's externality account of overeating are taught in Book 1, Section 10.9.4.) But leaving obesity out of the manual does not mean that psychology has nothing to say about it. DSM-5-TR lists robust associations between obesity and several mental disorders, among them binge-eating disorder, the depressive and bipolar disorders and schizophrenia; it notes that the side effects of some psychotropic drugs contribute importantly to the development of obesity; and it adds that obesity may itself be a risk factor for developing some mental disorders, such as depressive disorders. The relation between binge-eating disorder and obesity, which overlap but are not the same thing, is taken up in Section 8.4.5.

Clinical link

The weight gain caused by psychotropic drugs is not a cosmetic side effect. Some antipsychotics, olanzapine and clozapine above all, cause marked weight gain and rises in blood sugar and lipids (Section 4.10), and patients who gain weight on a drug often stop taking it. When a patient with schizophrenia or bipolar disorder starts such a drug, weight, waist and eating habits belong on the treatment plan from the first week, and an eating disorder history should be asked about before, not after, the weight starts to climb.

Exam trap

Obesity is not a mental disorder in DSM-5-TR, because it has many different genetic, physiological, behavioural and environmental causes; it is associated with binge-eating disorder, depressive and bipolar disorders and schizophrenia, but it is not itself a diagnosis. Only one of anorexia nervosa, bulimia nervosa and binge-eating disorder can be diagnosed in a single episode, because they differ in course, outcome and treatment needs. And "food addiction" is not a diagnosis in either manual, however often the phrase is used.

Self-starvation before anorexia nervosa

Refusing food is very old. What has changed over the centuries is what people thought the refusal meant. In medieval Europe some deeply religious women ate almost nothing for years, and their contemporaries read the fasting as holiness. The best known is Catherine of Siena (1347 to 1380), the Italian mystic, later a saint, who lived for long periods on almost no food and who, even when churchmen pressed her to eat and she prayed to be able to eat like other people, still could not give up fasting. In 1985 the American historian Rudolph M. Bell gathered the lives of such women in a book called Holy Anorexia, and his title gave the phenomenon its name: holy anorexia, self-starvation in the service of religious devotion. Whether these women had anorexia nervosa in the modern sense is disputed, and with good reason. Some of them were tirelessly active and resisted every attempt to make them eat, which is strikingly familiar; but they starved, as far as anyone can tell, for God, for purity and for control over a body they regarded as sinful, not to be thin.

In the nineteenth century the religious fasters were followed by the fasting girls, young women and girls who were said to live without any food at all and who drew crowds, journalists and doctors to their bedsides. Their stories often ended badly. Sarah Jacob (1857 to 1869), the "Welsh fasting girl", was a farmer's daughter from near Pencader in Carmarthenshire who was said to have eaten nothing for about two years. In December 1869 a team of four nurses was brought in to watch her day and night, to settle the matter. She died a little over a week after the watch began, and her parents were convicted of manslaughter. The fasting girls show how public and how powerful food refusal can be, but they are a story of claims and of spectacle; none of them was described as afraid of becoming fat.

The first medical account of self-starvation as an illness, rather than a miracle, is usually credited to the London physician Richard Morton (1637 to 1698). His book on consumption, Phthisiologia, appeared in Latin in 1689 and in English in 1694. Most of it is about the wasting disease of the lungs that we now call tuberculosis, but Morton set aside a separate kind of wasting that he called nervous consumption, or "nervous atrophy", a wasting that he traced to the nerves rather than to the lungs. He described two patients. One was a girl, Mr Duke's daughter, who fell ill in 1684 in her eighteenth year and wasted until, in his words, she was "like a skeleton only clad with skin", with no fever and no cough; she died about three months after coming under his care. The other was a boy, the son of a minister, who at about sixteen fell gradually into "a total want of appetite", which Morton blamed on too much study; Morton advised him to give up his studies, ride in the country air and drink milk, and the boy recovered his health "in a great measure". A girl and a boy, then, at the very start of the medical story.

Here is the point to carry forward. The modern diagnosis of anorexia nervosa is built around an intense fear of gaining weight and a disturbed experience of body shape (Section 8.3.2). That fear of fatness is not described in the saints, in the fasting girls or in Morton's patients, and, as you will see in the next subsection, neither Gull nor Lasègue described it either. It entered the clinical picture later, and it entered the DSM's diagnostic criteria only with DSM-III in 1980 (Table 8.1). Even today it is not universal. DSM-5-TR notes that the absence of an expressed intense fear of weight gain, sometimes called "fat phobia", appears to be relatively more common among patients in Asia, who more often explain their refusal to eat by a culturally sanctioned complaint such as discomfort in the stomach (Section 8.5.1 discusses culture in full, and Section 1.10.2 the debate over anorexia nervosa as a "Western culture-bound syndrome").

Exam trap

The first medical description of anorexia-like wasting is usually credited to Richard Morton, in Phthisiologia (Latin 1689, English 1694), who called it nervous consumption; the name anorexia nervosa came nearly two centuries later, from Gull. Holy anorexia is Rudolph Bell's term (1985) for the religious fasting of medieval women such as Catherine of Siena, and whether they had anorexia nervosa is disputed, because their motive was religious, not a fear of fatness.

Gull, Lasègue and the naming of anorexia nervosa

The disorder got its modern name in 1873, from two physicians working in two capitals at almost exactly the same moment. The first was William Withey Gull (1816 to 1890), a physician who had joined Guy's Hospital in London in 1837 and risen to become one of its leading physicians and teachers. By the 1870s he was among the most eminent doctors in Britain: in 1871 he was credited with saving the life of the Prince of Wales during an attack of typhoid, and he was made a baronet and one of Queen Victoria's physicians. In August 1868, in a long address on medicine to the British Medical Association meeting at Oxford, Gull had mentioned in passing a peculiar condition, occurring mostly in young women and marked by extreme emaciation, which he called apepsia hysterica, "hysterical indigestion". Apepsia means a failure of digestion, and the name shows that he still half suspected the stomach.

Five years later he had changed his mind. On 24 October 1873 Gull read a paper to the Clinical Society of London, and it was printed in the Society's Transactions in 1874 under the title "Anorexia nervosa (apepsia hysterica, anorexia hysterica)". He gave the details of two patients, Miss A. and Miss B., "as fair examples of the whole", and added a third case sent to him by another doctor. Miss A. was seventeen when she was brought to him, and she had lost 33 pounds, more than a quarter of her weight. His published patients were all young women, emaciated and weak; he recorded amenorrhoea, and he noticed two things in the body that you will meet again in Section 8.3.5: the pulse was slow and the breathing was slow (in his last case, of 1888, he also recorded a low body temperature and cold, blue hands and feet). He noticed something else that surprised him. Starving as they were, his patients were restless and full of energy, constantly on the move. And he could find nothing wrong with their stomachs. "The want of appetite is, I believe, due to a morbid mental state," he wrote. He explained his choice of name with equal care: "We might call the state hysterical without committing ourselves to the etymological value of the word ... I prefer, however, the more general term 'nervosa', since the disease occurs in males as well as females, and is probably rather central than peripheral." The word hysteria comes from the Greek for the womb, and Gull, who had seen the illness in young men too, wanted a name that pointed to the nervous system instead.

His advice on treatment has worn remarkably well. The patient's inclination, he insisted, "must be in no way consulted". He warned against the common advice to anxious parents, "let her do as she likes. Don't force food", saying that he had once thought it proper but that larger experience had shown him the danger of allowing the starvation to go on. Warmth and steady supplies of food would gradually restore the strength, and "the patients should be fed at regular intervals, and surrounded by persons who would have moral control over them; relations and friends being generally the worst attendants." Feeding as the first treatment, firm and caring control by others, and a wary eye on the family: all three are still debated in the treatment of anorexia nervosa today (Section 8.5.6, and Book 2, Sections 7.4.6 and 7.7.7, for the very different modern views of the family). In March 1888, less than two years before his death, Gull published in The Lancet one more case, Miss K. R., a girl of fourteen brought to him by a country doctor, illustrated with wood engravings made from photographs of the starved girl and of her recovery.

The second physician was Ernest-Charles Lasègue (1816 to 1883) in Paris, whom you met in Chapter 2 as one of the two authors of folie à deux. In April 1873, six months before Gull's London paper, Lasègue published "De l'anorexie hystérique" in the Archives générales de médecine, the journal he edited, drawing on eight cases; an English translation appeared in a British medical journal the same year, although, as later scholars discovered, it left out some important passages. His name for the illness, anorexie hystérique (hysterical anorexia), kept the link with hysteria, and he described it only in women. What makes his paper remarkable is its psychology. Lasègue traced the illness through three stages, beginning with a gradual cutting down of food and a growing strictness about what could be eaten, and ending in severe emaciation. He described patients who insisted that they were well while those around them grew frantic, and he gave the family a central place in the story, writing about family interactions and parental attitudes as part of the illness itself. Put simply, Gull described the body and Lasègue described the family.

Who came first? Gull is usually given a slight priority because of his brief mention of 1868. Lasègue, for his part, printed an immediate editorial reply in his journal after reading the report of Gull's London paper. A century later the Belgian psychiatrist Walter Vandereycken and his colleague Ron van Deth looked again at the documents and concluded, in 1989, that the two men's work was a remarkable but independent coincidence and that no clear priority can be established for either. Most textbooks therefore give the two joint credit. One more detail deserves notice. Neither Gull nor Lasègue described a patient who was afraid of becoming fat. Their patients refused food and kept on the move, but the fear of fatness that DSM-5-TR now places at the centre of the diagnosis is absent from both papers, a reminder that the picture of a disorder can change even while its name stays the same.

Key study: Gull's paper to the Clinical Society of London (read 1873, printed 1874)

The question. Why were some young women wasting away when no disease of the stomach, the lungs or the womb could be found? The method. Careful case histories followed over years: Miss A. and Miss B. in detail, with a third case in an addendum. The findings. Severe emaciation, amenorrhoea, slow pulse, slow breathing, restless overactivity and no organic disease; recovery with regular feeding under firm control. The conclusion. The loss of appetite comes from "a morbid mental state"; the illness is "nervosa" because it is central and occurs in males as well as females. The limits. A handful of patients, probably from wealthy families seen in private practice, no fear of fatness described, and a name, "anorexia", that misdescribes the illness, as the trap below explains.

Exam trap

Anorexia means "loss of appetite", but most people with anorexia nervosa are hungry; they restrict because they fear weight gain, not because they do not want food. The name is a historical accident. Gull (London) named it anorexia nervosa, in a paper read in October 1873 and printed in 1874; Lasègue (Paris) called it anorexie hystérique in April 1873. Hilde Bruch did not name it: she wrote about it a century later. And the first description of anorexia-like wasting is Morton's (1689), not Gull's.

Hilde Bruch and the twentieth century

For the first half of the twentieth century anorexia nervosa was often treated as a disease of the glands. In 1914 the German pathologist Morris Simmonds described fatal wasting caused by destruction of the pituitary gland, and in the 1920s and 1930s many patients with anorexia nervosa were misdiagnosed as having this pituitary disease and treated, without success, with pituitary extracts. For a generation the psychological view of Gull and Lasègue went out of fashion. The person who did most to bring it back, and to turn attention from the stomach and the glands to the person, was Hilde Bruch (1904 to 1984). Bruch was born in the small German town of Dülken, trained in medicine in Germany, and emigrated to the United States in 1934, after the Nazis had come to power. She worked first in New York, at Columbia University, where she studied obese children, and from 1964 she was professor of psychiatry at Baylor College of Medicine in Houston. In her work with young people who starved themselves she was struck by how little their illness had to do with appetite.

In a paper of 1962 in Psychosomatic Medicine, "Perceptual and conceptual disturbances in anorexia nervosa", Bruch described what she saw. Her patients had a disturbance of body image "of delusional proportions": an emaciated girl would look in the mirror and see herself as too fat, and defend that perception fiercely (Section 2.10.3 examines body image in anorexia, and Section 2.5.6 why fatness in anorexia is classed as an overvalued idea). They also had trouble recognising the signals from their own bodies: they did not seem to read hunger, fullness or fatigue accurately, as if these sensations had never been properly learned. And underneath both, Bruch argued, lay a paralysing sense of ineffectiveness, a feeling of having no control over one's own life, against which control of the body became the one achievement that was truly one's own. Bruch's explanation of where those disturbances come from, in the early relationship between child and parent, is a theory of cause and is taught in Sections 8.5.2 and 8.5.3. What matters here is the historical shift: after Bruch, the core of the disorder was understood to lie in the person's experience of her body and herself, not in her stomach.

She wrote two books that every student of the subject knows. Eating Disorders: Obesity, Anorexia Nervosa, and the Person Within (1973) was her full clinical account, and its subtitle, "the person within", announced her approach. The Golden Cage: The Enigma of Anorexia Nervosa, published by Harvard University Press in 1978, distilled the same experience for parents, patients and general readers, through vivid stories from her own case material, and it reached a very wide lay readership.

Public awareness grew alongside the professional literature. One death in particular made the disorder known around the world. The American singer Karen Carpenter died on 4 February 1983, aged thirty-two, of heart failure attributed to the effects of anorexia nervosa, and the extensive news coverage taught millions of people that the illness can kill. By then researchers had also begun to notice how closely the eating disorders follow the values of a culture. Barlow and Durand go so far as to call anorexia nervosa, and particularly bulimia nervosa, "the most culturally specific psychological disorders yet identified", a claim weighed against the evidence in Section 8.5.1.

Exam trap

Hilde Bruch (1904 to 1984) wrote Eating Disorders: Obesity, Anorexia Nervosa, and the Person Within (1973) and The Golden Cage (1978), and in 1962 described the body-image disturbance "of delusional proportions". She did not name the disorder (Gull did, in 1873 to 1874) and she did not describe bulimia nervosa (Russell did, in 1979). "The Golden Cage" is Bruch; the "psychosomatic family" is Salvador Minuchin (Section 8.5.2).

Bulimia and binge eating enter the manuals

Eating far too much at one sitting is as old as feasting, and the word for it is old too: bulimia comes from the Greek for "ox hunger", a hunger big enough for an ox. Barlow and Durand note that clear cases of bulimic behaviour have been described for thousands of years, and, more surprisingly, that for hundreds of years most of the recorded cases were men. What is new is the idea that bingeing, together with the attempts to undo it, forms a distinct psychological disorder; Barlow and Durand date the recognition of bulimia nervosa as a disorder of its own to the 1970s. The road there had two stages. First the binge was noticed among people who were overweight, in the 1950s; then, in the 1970s, the binge followed by vomiting was noticed among young women who feared fatness, many of whom had once had anorexia nervosa.

The first step was taken by the American psychiatrist Albert J. Stunkard (1922 to 2014), who spent most of his career at the University of Pennsylvania studying obesity. In 1955, with William Grace and Harold Wolff, he published in the American Journal of Medicine a description of the night eating syndrome, "a pattern of food intake among certain obese patients" who ate much of their day's food late in the evening and at night (its modern definition, as an example of other specified feeding or eating disorder, is in Section 8.4.7, and the different sleep-related eating disorder in Section 8.10.2). In 1959, in a paper called "Eating patterns and obesity" in the Psychiatric Quarterly, Stunkard described binge eating as a distinct pattern among some of his obese patients: eating very large amounts of food in a short time, followed by distress. Those patients were the forerunners of the binge-eating disorder that DSM-5 recognised more than half a century later.

The second step came in the 1970s. In 1976 the American writer Marlene Boskind-Lodahl, writing from a feminist perspective, described young women caught in cycles of gorging and purging, and three years later, in London, the British psychiatrist Gerald Russell (1928 to 2018), then professor of psychiatry at the Royal Free Hospital and from 1979 at the Institute of Psychiatry and the Maudsley Hospital, gave the pattern the name that stuck. His paper in Psychological Medicine in 1979 was called "Bulimia nervosa: an ominous variant of anorexia nervosa", and it is the founding document of bulimia nervosa. The Key-study box tells it. Russell's sign, the marks on the knuckles of patients who make themselves vomit, is named after him and explained in Section 8.4.3, and his later work on family therapy for adolescent anorexia at the Maudsley is in Book 2, Section 7.7.7.

Key study: Russell's thirty patients (1979)

The question. What was wrong with the young women who kept their weight down not by starving but by overeating and then vomiting? The method. Russell selected thirty patients for a prospective study using two criteria: an irresistible urge to overeat, followed by self-induced vomiting or purging, and a morbid fear of becoming fat. The findings. Most had a previous history of anorexia nervosa, either obvious or "cryptic". Like patients with anorexia nervosa, they were determined to keep their weight below a threshold they set themselves, lower than their healthy weight before the illness. Unlike them, they tended to be heavier, more sexually active, more likely to menstruate regularly and to remain fertile. The vomiting and purging were dangerous, habit-forming and caused potassium loss; depression was often severe, with a high risk of suicide. The conclusion. A self-perpetuating vicious circle of overeating and vomiting, which treatment must interrupt while persuading the patient to accept a higher weight. Russell judged the outlook to be less favourable than in uncomplicated anorexia nervosa, hence "ominous". The legacy. DSM-III (1980) already listed a looser diagnosis called simply "bulimia"; DSM-III-R (1987) adopted Russell's name, bulimia nervosa, and with it the requirements of weight-control behaviour and of concern with shape and weight.

The manuals then took over the story, and Table 8.1 follows it edition by edition; Figure 8.2 puts the whole history, from Morton to DSM-5-TR, on a single timeline. Read the table down each column and three trends stand out. The first is a move from description to rules. DSM-I (1952) and DSM-II (1968) gave only brief descriptions of disorders and no diagnostic criteria at all (Section 1.7). DSM-III (1980) gave anorexia nervosa five explicit criteria, among them an intense fear of becoming obese and a weight loss of at least 25 percent of original body weight, and it brought in a new diagnosis called simply "bulimia". The second trend is that the numbers were first added and then taken away. The 25 percent weight-loss rule became a weight at least 15 percent below what was expected (that is, below 85 percent) in DSM-III-R (1987) and DSM-IV (1994), and then DSM-5 (2013) dropped the percentage altogether in favour of a "significantly low" weight judged in context. Amenorrhoea, the absence of menstrual periods, became a criterion in DSM-III-R and was dropped in DSM-5, because it does not occur in every case and cannot apply at all to men, to girls before puberty or to women taking hormonal contraceptives (Section 8.3.3 tells that story in full). Bulimia had no frequency rule in DSM-III, required at least two binges a week for 3 months in DSM-III-R and DSM-IV, and needs only one a week for 3 months in DSM-5. The third trend is a gathering together. DSM-III-R kept the eating disorders in the section for disorders first evident in infancy, childhood or adolescence; DSM-IV gave anorexia and bulimia nervosa a chapter of their own but left pica, rumination disorder and feeding disorder of infancy or early childhood behind in the childhood chapter; and DSM-5 put all of them into one lifespan chapter, Feeding and Eating Disorders, replaced feeding disorder of infancy or early childhood with the broader avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder, and promoted binge-eating disorder from the appendix to a full diagnosis.

The other change in the last row deserves a sentence. Under DSM-IV, the leftover label eating disorder not otherwise specified (EDNOS) became the commonest eating disorder diagnosis in routine clinical practice, more common than anorexia nervosa or bulimia nervosa, as Christopher Fairburn and Kristin Bohn pointed out in 2005 (Section 1.8.4). A residual category that outnumbers the main ones is a sign that the main categories are drawn too narrowly. DSM-5's lower thresholds, its new diagnoses and its other specified feeding or eating disorder (OSFED), which names the reason the presentation does not fit, were designed to shrink the leftover group (Section 8.4.7). DSM-5-TR (2022) kept the same set of disorders and revised the text, adding to anorexia nervosa, bulimia nervosa and binge-eating disorder a section on their association with suicidal thoughts and behaviour.

Table 8.1 The eating disorders in the DSM, edition by edition.
EditionAnorexia nervosaBulimia and binge eatingPlacement and other categories
DSM-I (1952) and DSM-II (1968)Brief descriptions only; no criteria for any disorderNot recognised as a separate disorderNo diagnostic criteria anywhere in the manual
DSM-III (1980)Intense fear of becoming obese; body-image disturbance; loss of at least 25 percent of original weight; refusal to keep a minimal normal weight; no physical illness to explain it (no amenorrhoea criterion)"Bulimia": recurrent binges plus three of five features (such as secret eating, ending a binge by vomiting, repeated dieting); no frequency rule; compensation not requiredAtypical eating disorder for other cases
DSM-III-R (1987)Weight at least 15 percent below expected; amenorrhoea becomes a criterionRenamed bulimia nervosa: binges with a feeling of lack of control, at least two a week for 3 months; regular vomiting, laxatives, diuretics, strict dieting or fasting, or vigorous exercise; persistent overconcern with shape and weightEating disorders in the section for disorders first evident in infancy, childhood or adolescence; EDNOS
DSM-IV (1994)Below 85 percent of expected weight; amenorrhoea (three missed cycles in a row); restricting and binge-eating/purging typesBulimia nervosa twice a week for 3 months, purging and nonpurging types; binge-eating disorder only in Appendix B (2 days a week for 6 months)Own Eating Disorders chapter for anorexia, bulimia and EDNOS; pica, rumination and feeding disorder of infancy or early childhood stay in the childhood chapter
DSM-5 (2013)"Significantly low" weight judged in context; amenorrhoea dropped; BMI used for severityBulimia nervosa once a week for 3 months, subtypes dropped; binge-eating disorder a full diagnosis, once a week for 3 monthsOne lifespan chapter; ARFID replaces feeding disorder of infancy or early childhood; OSFED and unspecified replace EDNOS
DSM-5-TR (2022)Same disorder; text updatedSame disorders; text updatedNew text sections on suicidal thoughts and behaviour for anorexia nervosa, bulimia nervosa and binge-eating disorder
Exam trap

Learn the frequency rules by edition. DSM-III-R and DSM-IV: bulimia nervosa needed binges (and, in DSM-IV, compensation) twice a week for 3 months. DSM-5 and DSM-5-TR: once a week for 3 months, for bulimia nervosa and for binge-eating disorder alike. No edition ever asked for bulimia "for 6 months"; the 6 months comes from DSM-IV's research criteria for binge-eating disorder in Appendix B, which is how options such as "twice per week for 6 months" or "once weekly for 6 months" are built. Two more favourites: amenorrhoea entered the criteria in DSM-III-R and left them in DSM-5, and binge-eating disorder became a full diagnosis only in DSM-5 (2013).

Where the disorders live in the manuals

How the World Health Organization's two classifications are built, ICD-10 with its Chapter V of F codes and ICD-11 with its Chapter 6 of codes from 6A00 to 6E8Z, adopted in 2019 and in force from 1 January 2022, is explained in Sections 1.9.3 to 1.9.6. What this subsection adds is the address of every disorder in this chapter, and the two stories that the addresses tell. Table 8.2 is the master list. Its second column needs a word of explanation. DSM-5-TR prints the codes of ICD-10-CM, the clinical modification of ICD-10 that the United States uses for its health records, and the clinical modification often adds digits that the WHO's own ICD-10 does not have. That is why anorexia nervosa is F50.01 or F50.02 in DSM-5-TR but simply F50.0 in the WHO's ICD-10, and why pica carries two DSM-5-TR codes, F98.3 in children and F50.89 in adults: the American codes inherit the WHO's habit of filing childhood pica in a different block from adult eating problems.

Table 8.2 The disorders of this chapter in DSM-5-TR, ICD-10 and ICD-11.

The first story concerns feeding and childhood. The WHO's ICD-10 split the feeding and eating problems between two blocks of Chapter V. Anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa, with their "atypical" forms (F50.1 and F50.3) for incomplete pictures, sat in F50, within the block of behavioural syndromes associated with physiological disturbances and physical factors, alongside two codes for overeating and for vomiting associated with other psychological disturbances (F50.4 and F50.5) and a code for other eating disorders (F50.8) that held pica in adults and psychogenic loss of appetite. The feeding problems of young children, meanwhile, sat at the far end of the chapter, in the block of behavioural and emotional disorders with onset usually occurring in childhood and adolescence: feeding disorder of infancy and childhood (F98.2, which included rumination disorder of infancy) and pica of infancy and childhood (F98.3), next to nonorganic enuresis (F98.0) and nonorganic encopresis (F98.1). DSM-IV made the same split, as Table 8.1 showed. DSM-5 and ICD-11 abandoned it. Both now gather every feeding and eating disorder, whatever the age of the patient, into one family; in ICD-11 that family is the grouping of feeding or eating disorders, 6B80 to 6B85, which also brings in two diagnoses that ICD-10 never had, binge eating disorder (6B82) and avoidant-restrictive food intake disorder (6B83), and renames rumination as rumination-regurgitation disorder (6B85). The elimination disorders, enuresis (6C00) and encopresis (6C01), get a small grouping of their own straight afterwards, instead of being two lines in a block of assorted childhood problems.

The second story concerns sleep, and it is the more important one. ICD-10 divided the sleep disorders by their presumed cause. Its Chapter V held the nonorganic sleep disorders (F51), and the WHO's definition is explicit: the category includes only those sleep disorders in which emotional causes are considered to be a primary factor, and which are not due to identifiable physical disorders classified elsewhere. So F51 held nonorganic insomnia (F51.0), nonorganic hypersomnia (F51.1), nonorganic disorder of the sleep-wake schedule (F51.2), sleepwalking (F51.3), sleep terrors (F51.4) and nightmares (F51.5). Their "organic" counterparts lived in a different chapter altogether, Chapter VI, Diseases of the nervous system: disorders of initiating and maintaining sleep (G47.0), disorders of excessive somnolence (G47.1), disorders of the sleep-wake schedule (G47.2), sleep apnoea (G47.3), narcolepsy and cataplexy (G47.4) and other sleep disorders, including Kleine-Levin syndrome (G47.8); restless legs syndrome was filed among the extrapyramidal and movement disorders (G25.8). The trouble with this division is easy to see. The same complaint, a man who cannot sleep, had to be coded F51.0 or G47.0 according to whether the clinician judged its cause to be emotional or physical, and for most sleep disorders the honest answer is "both": worry keeps a person awake, and so do pain, apnoea and caffeine, often in the same person on the same night. It is the same objection that led DSM-IV to drop the old division between organic and functional mental disorders (Section 1.6.3).

ICD-11 abolished the division by giving the sleep-wake disorders a chapter of their own, Chapter 7, outside the chapter of mental, behavioural or neurodevelopmental disorders and separate from the chapter on diseases of the nervous system. Its codes begin with 7A and 7B, and it has six groupings: insomnia disorders (chronic insomnia 7A00 and short-term insomnia 7A01), hypersomnolence disorders (narcolepsy 7A20, idiopathic hypersomnia 7A21, Kleine-Levin syndrome 7A22 and others), sleep-related breathing disorders (central sleep apnoeas 7A40, obstructive sleep apnoea 7A41, sleep-related hypoventilation or hypoxaemia disorders 7A42), circadian rhythm sleep-wake disorders (7A60 to 7A65, including a jet lag type that DSM-5-TR does not have), sleep-related movement disorders (restless legs syndrome 7A80, periodic limb movement disorder 7A81 and others) and parasomnia disorders (disorders of arousal from non-REM sleep 7B00, parasomnias related to REM sleep 7B01 and other parasomnias 7B02). Figure 8.3 shows both reorganisations at a glance. DSM-5-TR, for its part, keeps the sleep-wake disorders inside the manual, because it is written for mental health and general medical clinicians who meet these complaints every day; but look at its codes in Table 8.2 and you can still see the ghost of the old split: insomnia, hypersomnolence, the NREM arousal disorders and nightmare disorder carry F codes, while narcolepsy, the breathing disorders, the circadian disorders, REM sleep behaviour disorder and restless legs syndrome carry G codes. How the sleep classifications developed, from DSM-III-R's dyssomnias and parasomnias to the sleep specialists' ICSD-3, is the subject of Section 8.7.3.

Exam trap

In ICD-11 the sleep-wake disorders are Chapter 7, not Chapter 6 (Chapter 6 is mental, behavioural or neurodevelopmental disorders, where the feeding, eating and elimination disorders stay, at 6B80 to 6B85 and 6C00 to 6C01). In ICD-10, narcolepsy (G47.4) and sleep apnoea (G47.3) are G codes in the nervous system chapter, not F codes, while sleepwalking (F51.3), sleep terrors (F51.4) and nightmares (F51.5) are F codes. DSM-5-TR prints ICD-10-CM codes, which is why pica has two codes, F98.3 for children and F50.89 for adults. And binge-eating disorder and ARFID have no category at all in ICD-10.

A first look at who is affected

Before the detailed sections, it helps to have a rough picture of who develops each disorder, because the pattern itself teaches something. Table 8.3 gathers, for every disorder of the chapter, the usual age at which it begins and how it is shared between the sexes; each disorder's own section gives the full prevalence figures and the reasons behind them. Three patterns stand out. The eating disorders, apart from the childhood feeding disorders, begin mostly in adolescence and young adulthood and are far commoner in girls and women: in two community studies in the United States, the lifetime prevalence of anorexia nervosa was about 0.9 to 1.42 percent among women against 0.12 to 0.3 percent among men. Binge-eating disorder is the least lopsided of the three, two to three times commoner in women, and ARFID is about equally common in boys and girls. The elimination disorders, by contrast, belong to childhood by definition and lean towards boys. And the sleep-wake disorders spread right across the lifespan, from the sleepwalking child to the sleep apnoea of middle age and the REM sleep behaviour disorder of older men, with a sex ratio that tilts one way for some disorders and the other way for others. Sudha's husband, a man in his forties, is exactly who obstructive sleep apnoea picks out; Shreya, a girl of seventeen, is exactly who anorexia nervosa picks out; and Varun, a boy of seven, is a very ordinary bedwetter. The reasons for those patterns, which are partly biological and partly social, are the business of Sections 8.3 to 8.10.

Table 8.3 Who is affected: typical onset and sex ratio (DSM-5-TR; details in each disorder's own section).

Remember

A quick way to hold the sex ratios: mostly women for anorexia nervosa, bulimia nervosa, insomnia disorder and restless legs syndrome; mostly men or boys for enuresis, encopresis in older children, obstructive sleep apnoea and REM sleep behaviour disorder; about equal for ARFID, hypersomnolence disorder and narcolepsy. Binge-eating disorder is the eating disorder with the smallest female excess, which is one reason it is so often missed in men.

Scan Your Brain

8.2 Pica, Rumination Disorder and Avoidant/Restrictive Food Intake DisorderLower yield · not asked yet

Ask most people what an eating disorder is and they will describe a thin teenage girl who is afraid of getting fat. That picture fits anorexia nervosa well, and Section 8.3 is devoted to it. But some of the oldest and, in their own way, most dangerous disturbances of eating have nothing to do with weight or shape at all. Think of a toddler who picks plaster off the wall and swallows it, an infant who brings up every feed and chews it again with apparent contentment, a boy who has eaten only three foods since the day he choked on a piece of apple. None of them wants to be thin. None of them is looking in a mirror. Yet each is eating in a way that can stunt growth, poison the body or, rarely, kill. This section is about these three disorders, which are usually grouped together as the feeding disorders: pica, rumination disorder and avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder. You will learn what each one looks like, how DSM-5-TR (the text revision of the fifth edition of the American Psychiatric Association's Diagnostic and Statistical Manual, 2022) defines it and why each rule is there, who develops it, and how a clinician tells it apart from the many conditions that resemble it.

Feeding disorders across the life span

What do the three have in common? Section 8.1.2 gave DSM-5-TR's definition of the whole family: a persistent disturbance of eating or eating-related behaviour that changes how food is consumed or absorbed and that significantly damages physical health or psychosocial functioning. Within that family, the feeding disorders are distinguished by what they lack. In each of them the disturbance concerns what a person eats (pica: things that are not food), what happens to food after it is swallowed (rumination disorder: it comes back up), or how much and how widely a person eats (avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder: too little, or too narrow a range). What is missing is the disturbance in the experience of body weight or shape that defines anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa. A child with pica is not eating chalk to stay slim; a teenager with avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder may be painfully thin and wish she were not. Keep that negative definition in mind, because it is the single most useful fact in this section: when restriction happens without any fear of fatness, think of the feeding disorders before you think of anorexia.

The three also share a history. In DSM-IV (1994) they lived in the chapter on disorders usually first diagnosed in infancy, childhood or adolescence, under the heading "Feeding and Eating Disorders of Infancy or Early Childhood", which held pica, rumination disorder and a third diagnosis called feeding disorder of infancy or early childhood (a persistent failure to eat enough, with failure to gain weight or loss of weight, beginning before the age of 6). The adult eating disorders were in a separate chapter. That arrangement carried an unspoken message, that these were problems of babies and toddlers that children grew out of. Clinicians knew better. Adults with intellectual disability eat cloth and stones; adolescents and adults ruminate in secret; and many adults who have avoided whole classes of food since childhood never fit the old infant-centred criteria. DSM-5 (2013) therefore merged both groups into one lifespan chapter, "Feeding and Eating Disorders", rewrote the criteria so that they apply at any age, and replaced the infant feeding disorder with a much broader diagnosis, avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder, which can be given to a toddler or to a 60-year-old (Section 8.1.6 tells this history edition by edition). The World Health Organization (WHO) made the same move later. Its ICD-10 (1992) kept the childhood problems among the disorders of childhood, as F98.2 (feeding disorder of infancy and childhood, which explicitly included rumination disorder of infancy) and F98.3 (pica of infancy and childhood), and filed pica in adults under F50.8, "other eating disorders". ICD-11 (in force from 2022) puts all six disorders in one block of the mental disorders chapter: 6B83 avoidant-restrictive food intake disorder, 6B84 pica and 6B85 rumination-regurgitation disorder sit next to anorexia nervosa (6B80), bulimia nervosa (6B81) and binge eating disorder (6B82).

The third thing the feeding disorders share is a rule about other conditions, and it is worth understanding because examiners like to test it. All three are common in people who already have another diagnosis, above all autism spectrum disorder and intellectual developmental disorder (intellectual disability), which Chapter 3 teaches. A child with autism may eat only a handful of foods of one colour and texture; a man with severe intellectual disability may put everything he finds into his mouth. Should each of them receive a second diagnosis for the eating behaviour? If the answer were always yes, every feature of autism or intellectual disability would be counted twice, and the second label would add nothing that the first did not already say. If the answer were always no, a behaviour that is dangerous in its own right (a man who swallows batteries, a girl with autism whose diet has become so narrow that she is losing weight) would disappear inside the larger diagnosis and might never get its own treatment plan. DSM-5-TR solves the problem with one shared criterion, usually Criterion D: when the eating behaviour occurs in the context of another mental disorder or a medical condition, a separate diagnosis is given only if the behaviour is severe enough to warrant additional clinical attention, that is, more severe than the other condition usually produces and needing treatment of its own. The rule protects against both errors: it stops every symptom of a developmental disorder from becoming a second diagnosis, and it lets the clinician name the eating problem when it has become a target for treatment in its own right.

Beyond that shared rule, the three differ in how they relate to the other eating disorders. Rumination disorder cannot be diagnosed if the regurgitation happens only during anorexia nervosa, bulimia nervosa, binge-eating disorder or avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder. Avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder cannot be diagnosed if the restriction happens only during anorexia nervosa or bulimia nervosa, and it requires that there be no disturbance in the way body weight or shape is experienced. Pica's criteria contain no such exclusion, so pica can be diagnosed alongside another feeding or eating disorder when it needs attention in its own right. DSM-5 (2013) said so explicitly in its introduction to the chapter, stating that pica may be diagnosed in the presence of any other feeding or eating disorder; DSM-5-TR dropped that sentence but kept the criteria unchanged, and its text adds one exception: when non-food items such as tissue paper are eaten mainly to control weight, anorexia nervosa is the diagnosis (Section 8.2.2). None of the three has subtypes or severity ratings; each has a single specifier, in remission, for a person who once met the full criteria but has not done so for a sustained period. Table 8.4 sets the three side by side; the rest of the section takes them one at a time.

Table 8.4 The three feeding disorders at a glance (DSM-5-TR unless stated). Codes are those DSM-5-TR prints (ICD-10-CM) and the ICD-11 codes.
FeaturePicaRumination disorderAvoidant/restrictive food intake disorder
Core behaviourEating non-nutritive, non-food substancesRepeated regurgitation; food re-chewed, re-swallowed or spat outAvoiding or restricting food for reasons other than weight or shape
Minimum duration1 month1 monthNone stated; the consequences define it
AgeSuggested minimum of 2 years; onset usually in childhoodAny age; in infants usually 3 to 12 monthsAny age; most often begins in infancy or childhood
Associated conditionsIntellectual disability, autism, pregnancy, iron or zinc deficiencyIntellectual disability, neglect, understimulationAnxiety disorders, autism, OCD, ADHD
PrevalenceAbout 5% of school-age children; about one-third of pregnant womenAbout 1% to 2% of grade-school children (limited data)0.3% of people aged 15 or older (one Australian study)
Main dangerObstruction, bezoar, perforation, infection, lead poisoningMalnutrition and growth failure, especially in infantsMalnutrition as severe as in anorexia; growth failure
Key differentialCulturally normative earth eating; anorexia (tissue paper); factitious disorderReflux, pyloric stenosis and other gut disease; purging in AN or BNPicky eating; anorexia nervosa; phobia of choking
CodesF98.3 (children), F50.89 (adults); ICD-11 6B84F98.21; ICD-11 6B85F50.82; ICD-11 6B83
Exam trap

The feeding disorders are no longer "disorders of infancy". Since DSM-5 all three can be diagnosed at any age, and avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder replaced DSM-IV's feeding disorder of infancy or early childhood (it did not replace pica or rumination disorder, which kept their names). None of the three has a severity rating; the BMI bands you will meet in Section 8.3 belong to anorexia nervosa alone.

Pica

Poonam is 24 and five months into her second pregnancy in a village in Madhya Pradesh. At the primary health centre the auxiliary nurse midwife notices that her haemoglobin is low and that she keeps rubbing her stomach. Gently questioned, Poonam admits that since her third month she has been breaking off pieces of the dried mud plaster on the courtyard wall, and pieces of an old earthen water pot, and chewing them several times a day. The smell of wet earth after the rain makes her mouth water. She eats them when her mother-in-law is not looking, because the older woman scolds her for it, and she has had painful constipation for weeks. "I know it is mud," she says, embarrassed. "But I want it more than food."

Is this a mental disorder, a craving of pregnancy, or a custom? The answer depends on the criteria, so begin with the name. Pica is the Latin word for the magpie, a bird reputed to eat almost anything, and the disorder is the persistent eating of things that are not food. DSM-5-TR's criteria, in plain language, are four:

  • A. Persistent eating of non-nutritive, non-food substances for at least 1 month. The month separates a persistent pattern from a single episode, a dare or a passing experiment.
  • B. The eating is inappropriate to the person's developmental level. Infants explore the world with their mouths and swallow some of what they mouth; that is normal. DSM-5-TR therefore suggests a minimum age of 2 years for the diagnosis. ICD-11 gives the reason in so many words: about 2 years is the developmental age at which a child is expected to tell edible from inedible things.
  • C. The eating is not part of a culturally supported or socially normative practice. In some communities eating earth or clay is believed to have spiritual, medicinal or social value; when it does, it is not a disorder.
  • D. If it occurs in the context of another mental disorder (intellectual developmental disorder, autism spectrum disorder, schizophrenia) or a medical condition (including pregnancy), it is severe enough to warrant additional clinical attention. This is the shared rule of Section 8.2.1.

Two words in Criterion A carry weight. "Non-nutritive" means the substance supplies little or no nourishment. "Non-food" was added in DSM-5 because some diet products, such as diet soft drinks and very low-calorie foods, are almost free of nutrients but are still foods, and eating them is not pica. Notice also what pica usually does not involve: there is typically no aversion to ordinary food. Poonam still eats her meals; she eats mud as well. ICD-11 draws the boundary slightly differently. Its pica includes non-food objects and materials (clay, soil, chalk, plaster, plastic, metal, paper) and also raw food ingredients eaten in large quantities, such as salt or corn flour, so a person who eats spoonfuls of raw flour can be given an ICD-11 diagnosis of pica but would not meet DSM-5-TR's "non-food" rule.

What do people with pica eat? DSM-5-TR's list is a tour of the house and the yard: paper, soap, cloth, hair, string, wool, soil, chalk, talcum powder, paint, gum, metal, pebbles, charcoal or coal, ash, clay, starch and ice. The substances vary with age and with what is within reach. Several have their own names, all built on the Greek phagein, to eat: geophagia (from ge, earth) is eating earth, soil or clay, Poonam's form; pagophagia (from pagos, frost) is the compulsive eating of ice, the form most tightly linked with iron deficiency; amylophagia (from amylon, starch) is eating laundry starch or raw starch; trichophagia (from thrix, hair) is eating hair; lithophagia (from lithos, stone) is eating stones or pebbles; and coprophagia (from kopros, dung) is eating faeces. Swallowed hair cannot be digested, and over months it can collect in the stomach into a matted mass called a trichobezoar (a bezoar is any mass of undigested material trapped in the gut). A very large trichobezoar with a tail that extends from the stomach into the small intestine is called Rapunzel syndrome, after the fairy-tale girl with the long hair.

That brings us to why pica matters. Often the first anyone hears of it is a medical emergency. DSM-5-TR lists the complications: mechanical bowel problems; intestinal obstruction, for example from a bezoar; intestinal perforation; infections such as toxoplasmosis and toxocariasis (parasitic infections caught from soil or faeces contaminated by cats and dogs); and poisoning, above all lead poisoning in children who eat flakes of old lead-based paint. Plain abdominal X-rays, ultrasound and other scans can show an obstruction, and blood tests can measure lead or reveal an infection. Deficiencies of iron and zinc have often been reported in pica, but in many patients no specific biological abnormality is found. Depending on what is eaten, the course can be long and the disorder can be fatal.

Clinical link: which came first, the craving or the deficiency?

Pica and iron deficiency travel together so often that every patient with pica should have a blood count and an iron study. The direction of cause is still argued. On one view, iron deficiency produces the craving, perhaps through low iron in the brain; supporting it, ice craving in particular often stops soon after iron treatment begins. On the other view, pica produces the deficiency: clay and starch displace iron-rich food from the diet and may bind iron in the gut so that less is absorbed. Both may be true in different people, and some patients with pica have normal iron. The practical lesson does not depend on who is right: look for the deficiency, treat it, and see whether the craving goes.

How common is pica? Good data are scarce. DSM-5-TR estimates that about 5 percent of school-age children show it. In pregnancy it is far commoner: roughly one-third of pregnant women engage in pica, especially women living with food insecurity (without reliable access to affordable, nutritious food), and a worldwide meta-analysis put the rate at 28 percent during pregnancy or the postpartum period. Pregnancy brings specific cravings, for chalk or ice for example, and here DSM-5-TR adds an important rule: the diagnosis is appropriate in pregnancy only when the cravings lead to eating non-food substances to an extent that poses medical risks. A pregnant woman who sucks an ice cube every evening does not have a mental disorder; Poonam, who is anaemic and constipated from daily clay eating, may. Lack of available food and vitamin deficiency are also associated with pica, which links it with poverty.

Indian context

Eating mud, chalk, slate pencils, ash or pieces of baked clay is familiar to Indian obstetricians and paediatricians, and it is often hidden out of embarrassment, so it is found only if someone asks. The question matters because anaemia is so common: in the fifth National Family Health Survey (NFHS-5, 2019 to 2021), 52.2 percent of pregnant women aged 15 to 49 were anaemic (haemoglobin below 11.0 grams per decilitre). An antenatal history that includes "Do you eat anything that is not food, such as mud, chalk or ice?" costs nothing and can uncover both pica and the deficiency that may lie behind it.

Pica can begin in childhood, adolescence or adulthood, but onset in childhood is most often reported. It occurs in otherwise normally developing children, whereas in adults it is more likely to appear in the context of intellectual developmental disorder or another mental disorder. The environmental risk factors DSM-5-TR names are neglect, lack of supervision and developmental delay: a toddler left alone for hours in a room with peeling paint is at risk in a way that a closely watched toddler is not. Pica occurs in both sexes. It can badly damage physical health, but it is rarely the sole cause of social impairment; when a person with pica is socially impaired, another disorder (autism, intellectual disability, schizophrenia) is usually responsible.

Now return to Criterion C, because Poonam's case turns on it. DSM-5-TR warns in both directions. If earth eating is a culturally supported practice, such as clay taken as a remedy or as part of a religious observance with the approval of the community, it is not pica. But a behaviour that is common in a community is not therefore sanctioned by it, and DSM-5-TR says pica "should not be assumed to be socially normative without further evaluation". Poonam eats in secret, is scolded for it by her own family, and eats far more than anyone around her thinks proper; she has a craving, not a custom. Add the medical risk, and she meets the criteria for pica in pregnancy.

Telling pica apart from its look-alikes depends on asking why the substance is eaten. Non-food eating occurs within other disorders, including autism spectrum disorder, schizophrenia (where an object may be eaten because of a delusion) and Kleine-Levin syndrome, a rare disorder of recurrent excessive sleep with episodes of abnormal eating (Section 8.8.8); in each case pica is added only if the eating needs attention of its own. In anorexia nervosa some patients eat tissue paper to fill the stomach and blunt hunger; when non-food eating is mainly a means of weight control, anorexia nervosa is the diagnosis. In factitious disorder a person may deliberately swallow foreign objects to produce illness and gain the sick role (Book 3 Section 7.9); the element of deception distinguishes it. And some people swallow dangerous items such as pins, needles or knives as a form of non-suicidal self-injury or within a personality disorder (Chapter 11), where the act expresses distress rather than a craving.

The disorders that most often accompany pica are autism spectrum disorder and intellectual developmental disorder, followed at a distance by schizophrenia and obsessive-compulsive disorder. Pica can also accompany trichotillomania (hair-pulling disorder) and excoriation (skin-picking) disorder, in which the pulled hair or picked skin is swallowed (Book 3 Section 6.6), and avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder, especially when that disorder has a strong sensory component. Whenever pica is known, the assessment should consider gastrointestinal complications, poisoning, infection and nutritional deficiency.

Treatment therefore begins with the body. The physician checks haemoglobin, iron stores and zinc and corrects any deficiency; measures blood lead in a child who may have eaten paint, and removes the source; treats parasitic infection; and, if there is pain, vomiting or constipation, looks for an obstruction with an X-ray or ultrasound. A bezoar may need endoscopic or surgical removal. Then comes the behaviour. In children of normal development, supervision, safe surroundings and treatment of any deficiency are often enough. In intellectual disability and autism, where pica can be dangerous and persistent, behavioural programmes have the best support: enriching the environment so there are other things to do with the hands and mouth, blocking and interrupting attempts to eat non-food items, teaching the difference between edible and inedible things, and reinforcing alternative behaviour. Those methods are taught in Book 2, Sections 4.5 and 4.6. Poonam's treatment was simpler: iron tablets, advice on diet, a frank talk with her and her mother-in-law, and a follow-up visit at which she reported that the craving had faded.

Exam trap

Pica needs 1 month, not 3 months (3 months belongs to bulimia nervosa and binge-eating disorder). The suggested minimum age is 2 years, because mouthing objects is normal in infancy. Culturally sanctioned clay or earth eating is not pica, and in pregnancy pica is diagnosed only when the eating poses a medical risk. Pica can be fatal through obstruction or poisoning, but it is not "the most lethal eating disorder": that is anorexia nervosa (Section 8.3.7), so pica is a distractor in any question about mortality.

Rumination disorder

Kriti is 15 and in Class 10 in Pune. For about eight months, a few minutes after almost every meal, food has come back up into her mouth. It is not vomiting: there is no nausea, no heaving, no rush to the bathroom. The food simply rises, easily, and it tastes of what she has just eaten rather than of sour acid. At home she chews it again and swallows it. At school she spits it into a tissue behind her hand and pretends to cough, and she has stopped eating her tiffin there and skips breakfast on examination days so that nothing will come up in the hall. She has lost weight. Her gastroenterologist has found no disease of her stomach or oesophagus. "I do not decide to do it," she says. "It just happens, like a habit."

Kriti has rumination disorder. The name comes from the Latin ruminare, to chew the cud: cows, buffaloes, sheep and goats are ruminants, animals that bring partly digested food back up from the stomach to chew it a second time. An older medical name for the human behaviour is merycism. DSM-5-TR's criteria are four:

  • A. Repeated regurgitation of food over a period of at least 1 month. The regurgitated food may be re-chewed, re-swallowed or spat out.
  • B. The regurgitation is not attributable to a gastrointestinal or other medical condition, such as gastro-oesophageal reflux or pyloric stenosis.
  • C. It does not occur exclusively during anorexia nervosa, bulimia nervosa, binge-eating disorder or avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder.
  • D. If it occurs in the context of another mental disorder, such as intellectual developmental disorder or another neurodevelopmental disorder, it is severe enough to warrant additional clinical attention.

The key word is regurgitation, the effortless return of swallowed food to the mouth, and it must be kept distinct from vomiting. Vomiting is a forceful reflex, usually preceded by nausea and accompanied by retching, that empties the stomach; it is unpleasant, and people try to avoid it. In rumination disorder, DSM-5-TR says, previously swallowed and perhaps partly digested food is brought up "without apparent nausea, involuntary retching, or disgust". Gastroenterologists, who call the adult form rumination syndrome, have shown how it happens: shortly after a meal the muscles of the abdominal wall contract, without the person being aware of it, squeezing the stomach so that its contents are pushed back up the oesophagus. The contraction is learned, not a disease of the gut, which is why the treatment that works best is behavioural. The behaviour must also be frequent: at least several times a week and typically every day. Many people with the disorder can be watched doing it during a consultation; in others the diagnosis rests on self-report or on reports from parents or carers. Adults and adolescents often describe it as habitual or outside their control, as Kriti does.

Criterion B is there because the commonest cause of food coming back up is not psychological at all.
