In gastro-oesophageal reflux stomach acid and food leak back through a weak valve at the lower end of the oesophagus; in pyloric stenosis, a condition of young infants, the muscle at the outlet of the stomach thickens and causes forceful, projectile vomiting; in gastroparesis the stomach empties too slowly; in a hiatal hernia part of the stomach slides up through the diaphragm. In infants there is also Sandifer syndrome, in which reflux is accompanied by odd arching and twisting movements of the neck and back that can look very like the posture of an infant who ruminates. DSM-5-TR says these conditions can usually be ruled out by the history and by observation, though a gastroenterologist may need to run tests. Criterion C exists because people with anorexia nervosa or bulimia nervosa sometimes regurgitate and spit out food as a way of getting rid of calories; when that happens only within those disorders, the motive is weight control and the diagnosis is the eating disorder, not rumination.

The picture in infants is distinctive. DSM-5-TR describes a characteristic position: the baby strains and arches the back with the head held back and makes sucking movements with the tongue, and seems to get satisfaction from the activity. Between episodes the infant may be irritable and hungry. Weight loss and failure to make the expected weight gains are common, and malnutrition can develop even though the infant seems hungry and takes large feeds, particularly when every feed is followed at once by regurgitation and the food is lost. Older children, adolescents and adults become malnourished mainly when they also restrict what they eat, and many do restrict, because the behaviour is socially unacceptable and they know it. They hide it by putting a hand over the mouth or coughing, avoid eating with others, and may skip meals before school or work, as Kriti skips breakfast before examinations.

Rumination disorder was described first in people with intellectual disability, and it is still commonest there, but limited European data suggest that it may affect about 1 to 2 percent of grade-school children. It can begin at any age. In infants onset is usually between 3 and 12 months. In infants it often remits on its own, but its course can be long and it can cause medical emergencies such as severe malnutrition; it is potentially fatal, particularly in infancy. In older people it may be episodic or may continue until treated. DSM-5-TR notes something about its function: in infants, and in older people with intellectual developmental disorder or other neurodevelopmental disorders, regurgitation and rumination seem to be self-soothing or self-stimulating, in the same way as other repetitive behaviours such as head banging. That observation fits the environmental risk factors DSM-5-TR lists for infants and young children: lack of stimulation, neglect, stressful life situations and problems in the parent-child relationship. An infant left alone in a cot for long periods with little to look at or respond to may find in rumination the stimulation that the environment does not provide.

The functional consequences follow from the malnutrition and the secrecy. In children, repeated loss of food can delay growth and harm development and learning. In older children, adolescents and adults, social life suffers most: meals are avoided, invitations refused, friendships narrowed. Rumination can accompany a medical condition or another mental disorder, such as generalised anxiety disorder; then, as in pica, the separate diagnosis is made only when the regurgitation is more severe than the other condition would explain and needs its own attention.

ICD-11 calls the condition rumination-regurgitation disorder (6B85) and describes it a little differently. It speaks of the intentional and repeated bringing up of swallowed food, which may be re-chewed and re-swallowed or deliberately spat out "but not as in vomiting"; it requires the behaviour at least several times a week over at least several weeks; and it allows the diagnosis only in people who have reached a developmental age of at least 2 years. DSM-5-TR sets no minimum age, which is why an infant of 4 months can receive a DSM-5-TR diagnosis but not an ICD-11 one. In ICD-10, rumination was not a separate disorder at all: "rumination disorder of infancy" was included under F98.2, feeding disorder of infancy and childhood.

Treatment starts by excluding gut disease. For older children, adolescents and adults, the treatment with the best support is behavioural. The person is taught diaphragmatic breathing, slow breathing from the diaphragm with the belly rising and the chest still, and practises it during and after meals. Breathing this way relaxes the abdominal wall, so it is physically incompatible with the abdominal contraction that pushes food up: a competing response, the same principle as in habit reversal for tics and nail biting (Book 2, Section 4.9.1). A systematic review of adult rumination syndrome published in 2019, which found 12 studies with 254 patients between them, concluded that diaphragmatic breathing had the strongest evidence and that baclofen, a drug that reduces the relaxations of the valve between oesophagus and stomach, had some, while judging the quality of all the evidence to be weak. In infants, treatment concentrates on nutrition and on the feeding relationship: helping the caregiver feed the baby calmly and responsively, holding and engaging the infant during and after feeds, and providing the stimulation that may have been missing. Kriti learned diaphragmatic breathing with a psychologist, practised it after every meal, and within a few weeks was eating her tiffin at school again.

Exam trap

Rumination disorder is the repeated regurgitation of food, which is re-chewed, re-swallowed or spat out, without nausea. It has nothing to do with rumination in the sense of repetitive brooding thought, the "chewing over" of worries or of one's sadness that maintains depression and anxiety (Book 3 Sections 5.6 and 6.5.8). Like pica, it needs only 1 month. If the regurgitation has a medical cause (reflux, pyloric stenosis) or occurs only within anorexia or bulimia as a way of disposing of calories, it is not rumination disorder. DSM-5-TR sets no minimum age; ICD-11 requires a developmental age of 2 years.

Avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder

Pranav is 10. Two years ago, at a family wedding, a piece of apple stuck in his throat; he could not breathe for a few terrifying seconds until an uncle thumped his back. Since then he has eaten only plain rice, curd and one brand of glucose biscuits. Rotis, dal with whole grains, fruit, anything with lumps or skin, he pushes away; if his mother insists, he gags. He is hungry, and he watches his younger sister eat with something like envy. "What if it gets stuck again?" he asks. His paediatrician's chart shows that he has fallen from the middle of his growth curve to well below it, and he has stopped going to birthday parties, because there will be food there that he cannot eat and children who will ask why. Pranav does not want to be thin. He wants to be big enough to bowl fast for his school team.

Pranav has avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder (ARFID), the newest of the feeding disorders. DSM-5 (2013) created it to replace and extend DSM-IV's feeding disorder of infancy or early childhood, which had required onset before the age of 6, so that the diagnosis now reaches older children, adolescents and adults. The two adjectives in the name describe two patterns that often occur together: avoidant, refusing particular foods or kinds of food, and restrictive, eating too little overall. What makes it a disorder rather than a preference is what the eating does to the person. DSM-5-TR's criteria:

  • A. An eating or feeding disturbance, for example an apparent lack of interest in eating or food, avoidance based on the sensory characteristics of food, or concern about aversive consequences of eating, associated with one or more of four consequences:
    1. significant weight loss, or, in children, failure to achieve the expected weight gain or faltering growth;
    2. significant nutritional deficiency;
    3. dependence on enteral feeding or oral nutritional supplements;
    4. marked interference with psychosocial functioning.
  • B. The disturbance is not better explained by lack of available food or by an associated culturally sanctioned practice.
  • C. It does not occur exclusively during anorexia nervosa or bulimia nervosa, and there is no evidence of a disturbance in the way one's body weight or shape is experienced.
  • D. It is not attributable to a concurrent medical condition and not better explained by another mental disorder; if it occurs alongside one, its severity exceeds what that condition usually produces and warrants additional clinical attention.

Look first at the four consequences in Criterion A, because they are what keep ordinary fussy eaters out of the diagnosis. Almost every child refuses some foods; almost every adult has foods he will not touch. The criteria do not ask whether someone is choosy but whether the choosiness has done harm, and they name four kinds of harm. Weight loss or faltering growth is a clinical judgement; a child who has not finished growing may not lose weight at all but may simply stop climbing along his expected curve, as Pranav has. Nutritional deficiency is established by asking what the person eats, examining him and doing blood tests, and it can be as serious as in anorexia nervosa: DSM-5-TR mentions hypothermia, bradycardia (a slow heart rate) and anaemia, and notes that in infants the malnutrition can threaten life. Dependence on enteral feeding or supplements means that the person cannot sustain an adequate intake without help. Enteral feeding is feeding directly into the gut through a tube, either a nasogastric tube passed through the nose into the stomach or a gastrostomy tube placed through the wall of the abdomen; DSM-5-TR's examples are an infant with failure to thrive who needs a nasogastric tube, a child with a neurodevelopmental disorder who lives on nutritionally complete supplement drinks, and a person who relies on tube feeding or complete oral supplements with no medical condition to explain it. Marked psychosocial interference means being unable to eat with others, attend school or work, or sustain relationships because of the eating problem, and DSM-5-TR adds that substantial disruption of family life can meet this criterion on its own: a home in which only a handful of foods may be cooked, or parents who drive across the city to one shop or one restaurant because their child will eat nothing else. Pranav meets two of the four: faltering growth and marked interference with social life.

Criterion B removes two things that look like restriction but are not disorders. One is not having enough to eat: food insecurity and poverty explain a thin child far more often than any psychiatric diagnosis. The other is a culturally sanctioned practice. Religious fasting, such as a Navratri fast or the Ramadan fast from dawn to sunset, a family's lifelong vegetarianism, the Jain avoidance of root vegetables, and normal dieting are all excluded. Criterion C is the heart of the diagnosis. It says that ARFID is never diagnosed during anorexia nervosa or bulimia nervosa, and that there must be no evidence of a disturbance in how body weight or shape is experienced. A person who restricts because she feels fat, or because gaining weight would be a failure, does not have ARFID however strong her sensory dislikes. Criterion D, finally, applies the shared rule of Section 8.2.1 to medical and mental conditions, and it is discussed below with the differential diagnosis.

DSM-5-TR describes three presentations, which are examples rather than formal subtypes, and many patients show more than one. The first is avoidance based on the sensory characteristics of food: extreme sensitivity to its appearance, colour, smell, texture, temperature or taste. Before DSM-5 this was described as restrictive eating, selective eating, choosy eating, perseverant eating, chronic food refusal and food neophobia (fear of new foods). Such a person may refuse every brand of a food but one, or be unable to bear the smell of food that others are eating; people with autism and its heightened sensory sensitivities often show the same behaviour. The second is concern about aversive consequences of eating: a conditioned negative response to food after, or in anticipation of, an aversive experience such as choking, a traumatic procedure involving the gut (DSM-5-TR's example is oesophagoscopy, a tube passed down the oesophagus) or repeated vomiting. This is classical conditioning at the dinner table (Book 1, Section 6.2): a frightening event becomes linked with the food or the act of swallowing, and eating itself comes to provoke fear. Older terms for this form are functional dysphagia (difficulty swallowing with no structural cause) and globus hystericus (the sensation of a lump in the throat). Pranav's is this form. The third is an apparent lack of interest in eating or food: the child who is never hungry, forgets to eat, or finds meals a boring interruption of play. Figure 8.4 shows how the three routes and the four consequences fit together.

The associated features change with age. Very young infants may refuse food, gag or vomit; infants and toddlers may not engage with the caregiver during feeding, or may not signal hunger, preferring other activities. In older children and adolescents food avoidance may come with more general emotional difficulties that do not meet the criteria for an anxiety, depressive or bipolar disorder, a pattern sometimes called food avoidance emotional disorder. In infants the feeding relationship matters a great deal. An infant with ARFID may be irritable and hard to console during feeds, or apathetic and withdrawn; a caregiver may present food in ways the infant cannot manage, or read the infant's refusal as aggression or rejection, and poor nutrition then makes the infant more irritable and the feeding harder still. If feeding and weight improve when someone else feeds the child, DSM-5-TR says, parental psychopathology, abuse or neglect should be suspected.

How common is ARFID? Little is known. One Australian study found a frequency of 0.3 percent among people aged 15 or older. It is commoner in children and adolescents than in adults, and there may be a long delay between onset and the first consultation. Lack of interest in food usually begins in infancy or early childhood, and sensory avoidance in the first decade of life; both may persist into adulthood. Avoidance based on aversive consequences can begin at any age, often abruptly after a frightening event, as Pranav's did. The little long-term evidence suggests that sensory-based restriction is relatively stable, but that adults who still have it may function fairly normally. There is no good evidence that ARFID leads on to a later eating disorder, although in some people it precedes anorexia nervosa. Unlike anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa, ARFID seems to be about equally common in boys and girls; when it occurs with autism spectrum disorder, boys predominate. Pregnancy can bring altered sensory sensitivities and food aversions, but these are not usually extreme enough to meet the criteria.

DSM-5-TR groups the risk factors in its usual three headings. Temperamental: anxiety disorders, autism spectrum disorder, obsessive-compulsive disorder and attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder all raise the risk. Environmental: anxiety in the family, and higher rates of feeding problems in the children of mothers with eating disorders. Genetic and physiological: a history of gastrointestinal conditions, gastro-oesophageal reflux disease, vomiting and other medical problems. Similar presentations have been described in the United States, Canada, Australia, Europe, Japan and China. The disorders that most often accompany it are anxiety disorders, obsessive-compulsive disorder and the neurodevelopmental disorders, specifically autism spectrum disorder, attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder and intellectual developmental disorder.

Restriction of food is a symptom of many conditions, so the differential diagnosis is long. DSM-5-TR's rule throughout is that ARFID can be diagnosed alongside another condition if all its criteria are met and the eating problem needs specific attention. Take the conditions in turn.

  • Developmentally normal picky eating. Many toddlers and young children narrow the range of foods they will eat for a while; it usually resolves without treatment and causes none of the four consequences. It is not ARFID unless it becomes severe enough to cause nutritional failure or significant impairment.
  • Anorexia nervosa. Both involve restriction and both can produce low weight. Anorexia nervosa adds the fear of gaining weight (or persistent behaviour that prevents gain) and a disturbed experience of weight and shape, which ARFID lacks, and the two are never diagnosed together. The line can be hard to draw in late childhood and early adolescence, and especially in a patient who denies any fear of fatness yet keeps doing things that prevent weight gain and does not see how dangerous her low weight is, a presentation sometimes called non-fat-phobic anorexia nervosa. DSM-5-TR advises looking at symptoms, course and family history and making the diagnosis over time.
  • Specific phobia and other anxiety disorders. Specific phobia, other type, includes "situations that may lead to choking or vomiting" (Book 3 Section 6.3). When a fear of choking or vomiting has led to food avoidance and the eating problem becomes the main focus of clinical attention, ARFID is the better diagnosis. In social anxiety disorder a person may fear being watched while eating, which can also occur in ARFID.
  • Autism spectrum disorder. Rigid eating and sensory sensitivity are common in autism but often do not cause the impairment ARFID requires; ARFID is added only when it does and needs treatment of its own.
  • Obsessive-compulsive disorder. Restriction may follow preoccupations with food or ritualised eating; ARFID is added only when the eating is a major focus of treatment.
  • Major depressive disorder. Appetite can fall so far that intake drops and weight is lost, but the restriction is of overall intake and it usually lifts as the mood recovers.
  • Schizophrenia spectrum disorders. Odd eating, or avoidance of foods believed to be poisoned or contaminated, may follow from delusions.
  • Paediatric acute-onset neuropsychiatric syndrome (PANS). An abrupt, dramatic onset of obsessive-compulsive symptoms or severely restricted eating, with other neuropsychiatric symptoms, should raise this possibility (Book 3 Section 6.7). Acute onset, late onset or atypical symptoms are the warning signs.
  • Reactive attachment disorder. A child's withdrawal from caregivers can disturb feeding (Book 3 Section 7.4); ARFID is added only if all its criteria are met and feeding is a primary focus.
  • Factitious disorder, and factitious disorder imposed on another. Some people describe diets far more restrictive than the ones they actually eat in order to occupy the sick role, and some caregivers describe ARFID symptoms in a child, or induce failure to gain weight, in which case the caregiver receives the diagnosis (Book 3 Section 7.9).
  • Medical conditions. Gastrointestinal disease, food allergies and intolerances, occult (hidden) cancers and many other illnesses reduce intake through vomiting, nausea, pain, diarrhoea or loss of appetite. ARFID requires that the disturbance go beyond what the physical symptoms explain; it may persist after a medical illness that triggered it has resolved. Because older people, patients after surgery and patients on chemotherapy often lose their appetite, ARFID is added for them only when the eating disturbance is itself a primary focus. Congenital and neurological conditions that impair chewing and swallowing can coexist with ARFID if all criteria are met.

Table 8.5 condenses the four comparisons that matter most in practice.

Table 8.5 Avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder and its three closest look-alikes.
FeatureARFIDAnorexia nervosaPicky eatingPhobia of choking
Why food is avoidedSensory dislike, no interest, or fear of a bad consequenceTo lose weight or avoid gaining itPreference, a passing phaseFear of choking or vomiting
Fear of fatness, body-image disturbanceAbsent (Criterion C)Present, or behaviour that prevents gainAbsentAbsent
Weight and nutritionMay be very low; deficiency; supplementsSignificantly low weightNormal growthUsually normal unless avoidance widens
ImpairmentOne of four consequences requiredMarked, medical and socialNone of noteDistress in the feared situation
Usual onsetInfancy or childhood; any ageAdolescence or young adulthoodToddlers and young childrenAny age, often after an episode
DiagnosisARFIDAnorexia nervosa; never also ARFIDNo disorderSpecific phobia; ARFID if eating is the main focus

Treatment begins, as with every disorder of this chapter that starves the body, with nutrition. The physician assesses weight and growth, checks for deficiencies, and restores intake, with nutritional supplements when the person can drink but not eat enough, and with tube feeding when oral intake cannot sustain life or growth. The psychological treatment designed specifically for the disorder is cognitive-behavioural therapy for ARFID (CBT-AR), developed by the American psychologists Jennifer J. Thomas and Kamryn T. Eddy and published as a manual in 2019. It is a modular, exposure-based treatment in four stages, built around the three routes of Figure 8.4: graded exposure to new tastes and textures for the sensory form, work on hunger cues and regular eating for the lack-of-interest form, and graded exposure to the feared food or the feared act of swallowing for the aversive form. It is intended for people aged 10 and older. The first evidence came from open trials, without a control group, which found it acceptable and promising; randomised controlled trials that include it have only recently begun to report, so its evidence base is still best described as preliminary. With younger children, therapists work through the parents, using family-based approaches adapted from the treatment of adolescent anorexia (Book 2, Section 10.4.7). No drug is approved for ARFID, and none has been shown in good trials to treat it; anxiety that accompanies it is treated in its own right. Pranav's treatment combined a dietitian's plan and supplement drinks to restart his growth with weekly sessions in which, mouthful by graded mouthful, he went from mashed banana to soft roti; a year later he was back on his growth curve and back at birthday parties.

Exam trap

ARFID is restriction without body-image concern or fear of fatness. A child who eats only a few foods and is falling off his growth curve has ARFID, not anorexia nervosa; a girl who restricts because she feels fat has anorexia nervosa, however strong her dislike of certain textures. ARFID replaced DSM-IV's feeding disorder of infancy or early childhood and can be diagnosed at any age. Selective eating without weight loss or any other of the four consequences is ordinary picky eating, not a disorder. And because ARFID involves too little eating, it is never the answer to a question about a patient who binges: in a stem describing recurrent binges with distress and no compensatory behaviour, the ARFID option is a distractor (the answer is binge-eating disorder, Section 8.4.5).

8.3 Anorexia Nervosa: Criteria, the Starving Body and the CourseHigh yield · 7 Qs

Of all the disorders in this chapter, anorexia nervosa is the one most likely to end a young life. It is also one of the most puzzling disorders in psychiatry. A person who is starving is normally consumed by hunger and will eat almost anything; the men of Minnesota in this chapter's opening talked and daydreamed about food. A person with anorexia nervosa is starving too, often far more severely, and yet she turns food away, counts every mouthful, and feels triumph rather than alarm as the scale goes down. This section describes the disorder as the clinician meets it: the three DSM-5-TR criteria and the reasoning behind each one, how the criteria have changed since DSM-IV and how ICD-10 and ICD-11 differ, the subtypes and the BMI severity ratings, what starvation does to every organ of the body and to the mind, who develops the disorder, how it runs and why it kills, and how it is told apart from the many other causes of weight loss. Why anorexia nervosa develops, and how it is treated, are the subject of Section 8.5.

What anorexia nervosa is

Anorexia nervosa is a disorder in which a person restricts the energy she takes in, relative to what her body needs, until her weight is significantly low for her age, sex, stage of development and physical health; she has an intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, or persistently behaves in ways that prevent weight gain, even though she is already underweight; and the way she experiences her body weight or shape is disturbed. The disorder was named twice in 1873, by Sir William Gull in London as anorexia nervosa and by Ernest-Charles Lasègue in Paris as anorexie hystérique (Section 8.1.4 tells the story). The name is a poor one. Anorexia, from the Greek an- (without) and orexis (appetite), means loss of appetite, and Barlow and Durand point out that this is an incorrect description because appetite often remains healthy. Most patients are hungry, at least some of the time; they control their hunger. The engine of the disorder is not a missing appetite but a fear of fatness and a relentless pursuit of thinness. Throughout this section the patient is called "she", because most patients are female, but the disorder occurs in boys and men too (Section 8.3.7).

Malini is 16, in Class 11 at a school in Jaipur, and preparing for the medical entrance examination. She has always been top of her class. After a relative remarked on her weight, she began "eating healthy". Now she eats half a roti and some salad at dinner and little else, and she runs on the terrace at dawn before anyone else is awake. She wears loose kurtas, even in the heat. Several times a day she stands sideways in front of the mirror and checks her stomach. She weighs 39 kg and is 1.58 m tall, and she has not had a menstrual period for five months. Her parents have brought her to the clinic; she has come only because they insisted. "I am fine," she tells the psychologist calmly. "I just need to lose a little from my thighs."

Every element of the definition is visible in those few lines. The restriction is there (half a roti and salad), and so are its methods (dieting and dawn exercise). The weight is significantly low, as Section 8.3.2 will show by calculation. The fear of fatness is not stated, but it is implied by everything she does, including the loose clothes and the mirror checks. And the disturbance in her experience of her body is in her own words: an emaciated girl who sees thighs that need to be smaller. Notice also two things that are typical. She did not come on her own, and she does not think she is ill. Remember Malini; she will be the worked example through the rest of this section.

Exam trap

Anorexia nervosa is primarily fear of gaining weight together with restriction of food intake, leading to a significantly low weight. The distractors each belong elsewhere. "Obsession with cleanliness" is the contamination theme of obsessive-compulsive disorder (Book 3 Section 6.5). "Repeated vomiting after bingeing" describes bulimia nervosa (Section 8.4), although vomiting can also occur in the binge-eating/purging type of anorexia. "Excessive exercise only" is one method of losing weight that many patients use; it is not the disorder.

The three criteria, one by one

DSM-5-TR says that anorexia nervosa has three essential features: persistent restriction of energy intake; intense fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or persistent behaviour that interferes with weight gain; and a disturbance in self-perceived weight or shape. Each is a criterion, and each is worded with care.

Criterion A: restriction leading to a significantly low body weight. The criterion requires "restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to a significantly low body weight in the context of age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health". Significantly low means "less than minimally normal" in adults and, in children and adolescents, "less than that minimally expected". Two features of the wording matter. First, the weight is low because of restriction; weight loss from cancer or tuberculosis does not count. Second, in a young person who is still growing, there may be no weight loss at all. A girl who stays at the same weight for two years while growing taller is getting thinner relative to what she should weigh, so failure to make the expected weight gain, or to keep to her developmental trajectory, meets the criterion.

How does a clinician decide that a weight is significantly low? DSM-5-TR deliberately gives no single number, because normal weight differs between people, but it explains how to use the body mass index (BMI), which is weight in kilograms divided by the square of height in metres. The index was devised by the Belgian mathematician and statistician Adolphe Quetelet (1796 to 1874), which is why ICD-10 calls it "Quetelet's body-mass index"; the name "body mass index" was given to it in 1972 by Ancel Keys, the same physiologist who ran the Minnesota experiment. For adults, DSM-5-TR offers a set of guideposts:

  • A BMI of 18.5 is the lower limit of normal weight used by the United States Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) and the WHO, so most adults at or above 18.5 would not be considered significantly underweight.
  • A BMI below 17.0 indicates moderate or severe thinness by WHO's categories, so an adult below 17.0 is likely to be significantly underweight.
  • An adult with a BMI between 17.0 and 18.5, or even slightly above 18.5, may be judged significantly underweight if the clinical history or other physiological information supports it (a woman who has fallen from a BMI of 24 to 18.7 in four months, with a slow pulse and no periods, is an example).
  • Adults with a BMI of 19.0 or more are not underweight by population standards and should not be diagnosed with anorexia nervosa; if everything else fits, the diagnosis to consider is other specified feeding or eating disorder, atypical anorexia nervosa (Section 8.4.7).

For children and adolescents the clinician uses BMI-for-age percentiles, which compare a young person's BMI with those of others of the same age and sex. The CDC has used a BMI-for-age below the 5th percentile as suggesting underweight. A young person above that line may still be significantly underweight if she has failed to follow her expected growth trajectory, but one whose BMI is above the median (the 50th percentile) for her age should not be diagnosed with anorexia nervosa. Now apply this to Malini. Her BMI is 39 divided by 1.58 squared: 1.58 times 1.58 is 2.4964, and 39 divided by 2.4964 is about 15.6. At 16 she is still an adolescent, so DSM-5-TR judges her by BMI-for-age percentiles, not by the adult signposts, and a BMI of 15.6 is well below the 5th percentile for a girl of her age. (ICD-10's guidelines allow the adult BMI from the age of 16, and on the adult scale 15.6 is also very low.) Criterion A is clearly met.

Criterion B: fear of gaining weight, or behaviour that prevents gain. The criterion reads "intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, or persistent behavior that interferes with weight gain, even though at a significantly low weight". The fear has a strange property: it is not relieved by losing weight. DSM-5-TR notes that the concern about gaining weight may actually increase as weight falls. Barlow and Durand describe the same thing from the patient's side: people with anorexia are never satisfied with their weight loss, staying the same weight from one day to the next causes panic, and only continued loss is acceptable. The second half of the criterion, "or persistent behavior that interferes with weight gain", was added in DSM-5, and the reason is practical. Younger patients and some adults do not recognise or will not admit any fear of weight gain. Rather than leave them undiagnosed, DSM-5-TR allows the clinician to infer Criterion B from collateral history (what the family reports), from observation (food hidden in a napkin, exercise in secret), from physical and laboratory findings, or from the course of the illness (every gain followed by renewed restriction), as long as there is no other explanation for the low weight. The clause also helps with patients who explain their restriction by something other than fear of fatness, such as a feeling of fullness or stomach discomfort, a pattern DSM-5-TR describes as relatively more common in Asian populations (Section 8.5.1). Malini denies being afraid of anything; but her dawn running, her shrinking portions and her distress whenever her mother adds ghee to her dal tell the clinician what she does not say.

Criterion C: a disturbed experience of weight and shape. This criterion has three alternatives, and any one is enough: a disturbance in the way one's body weight or shape is experienced; undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation; or persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. The first alternative takes two forms. Some patients feel fat all over. Others accept that they are thin but are still convinced that certain parts, particularly the abdomen, buttocks and thighs, are "too fat", which is Malini's form. To keep watch on those parts, patients weigh themselves often, measure their thighs or waist obsessively, and check the "fat" areas in the mirror, behaviours known as body checking. The second alternative concerns self-esteem: for these patients, weight and shape are not one part of self-worth but nearly all of it, so weight loss is experienced as an impressive achievement and a sign of extraordinary self-discipline, and weight gain as an unacceptable failure of self-control. The third alternative is about the danger: some patients can acknowledge that they are thin but do not grasp that a slow pulse, a falling blood pressure and missing periods mean their life is at risk.

Three questions about Criterion C are taught in Chapter 2 of this book and need only a pointer here. Whether the body-image disturbance is a misperception of size or a matter of attitude and evaluation (the body-width estimation studies of Peter Slade and Gerald Russell in 1973, and the meta-analysis by Thomas F. Cash and E. A. Deagle in 1997 of 66 studies of perceptual and attitudinal body image) is discussed in Book 3 Section 2.10.3. The conviction of being too fat as an overvalued idea, a belief held with great emotional investment but short of a delusion, is in Section 2.5.6. And poor insight in anorexia nervosa, with the need to rely on informants, is in Section 2.10.4. A related idea from Section 2.1 is useful here: the disorder is largely ego-syntonic, felt by the patient as part of herself and in harmony with her goals. Barlow and Durand put the contrast with bulimia memorably: people with anorexia are proud of their diets and of their extraordinary control, whereas people with bulimia are ashamed of their eating and of their lack of control.

That ego-syntonic quality explains how patients come to clinical attention. DSM-5-TR observes that it is rare for a person with anorexia nervosa to complain of weight loss as such. She is usually brought by her family after marked weight loss or failure to gain. When patients do seek help themselves, it is usually because of distress about the physical and psychological effects of starvation (feeling cold all the time, being unable to concentrate, sleeping badly), not about being thin. Many lack insight into the problem or deny it, so the clinician must gather the history of weight loss and the other features from family members or other sources. Malini meets all three criteria: A by her weight, B by inference from her behaviour, and C in at least two of its forms, her "fat" thighs and her calm failure to see any danger.

Exam trap

DSM-5-TR requires all three: (A) significantly low weight from restriction, (B) intense fear of weight gain or persistent behaviour that interferes with gain, and (C) a disturbed experience of weight or shape, undue influence of weight or shape on self-worth, or failure to recognise the seriousness of the low weight. It does not require a BMI below 17 "in all cases": DSM-5-TR sets no diagnostic BMI cut-off, and 17 is simply the boundary between mild and moderate severity. It does not require amenorrhoea, which DSM-5 dropped. It does not require purging, which defines a subtype, not the disorder.

What changed from DSM-IV, and what ICD-10 and ICD-11 say

Many of the questions you will meet about anorexia nervosa are really questions about which manual says what. The quickest way to master them is to see how the criteria changed and why. DSM-IV (1994) had four criteria. (A) A refusal to maintain body weight at or above a minimally normal weight for age and height, with the example of weight loss leading to a body weight less than 85 percent of that expected (or failure to gain weight during growth, leading to the same result). (B) Intense fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even though underweight. (C) A disturbance in the way body weight or shape is experienced, undue influence of weight or shape on self-evaluation, or denial of the seriousness of the current low body weight. (D) In post-menarcheal females, amenorrhoea, the absence of at least three consecutive menstrual cycles. The two subtypes were defined by what the person had "regularly" done "during the current episode".

The DSM-5 work group, chaired by the American psychiatrist B. Timothy Walsh, made six changes, and each has a reason you can state in an interview.

  1. "Refusal" became "restriction of energy intake". The American Psychiatric Association's reason was that "refusal" implies intention on the part of the patient and is difficult to assess. Restriction describes what the person does without guessing at her motive.
  2. The 85 percent example was dropped. It looked precise but was not: "expected weight" can be calculated in several ways, and clinicians used different methods, so the same patient could be above 85 percent by one method and below it by another. In 2009 Jennifer J. Thomas, Christina A. Roberto and Kelly D. Brownell found 10 different methods in 99 published studies, and the share of a national sample that met the weight rule rose from 0.23 percent under the strictest method to 10.10 percent under the most lenient. DSM-5 replaced the example with clinical judgement, guided by the BMI signposts of Section 8.3.2, and moved BMI into the severity rating.
  3. "Or persistent behaviour that interferes with weight gain" was added to Criterion B, for the patients who will not or cannot report a fear.
  4. "Denial" became "persistent lack of recognition" of the seriousness of the low weight, a more neutral description of the same deficit.
  5. Amenorrhoea was removed. The reasons are taken up below.
  6. The subtypes now describe the last 3 months rather than the whole "current episode", and they speak of "recurrent" rather than "regular" behaviour, because patients so often move between subtypes (Section 8.3.4). DSM-5 also added the BMI-based severity ratings.

The removal of amenorrhoea answers a question that examiners set often, so take it slowly. Amenorrhoea means the absence of menstrual periods. The word is Greek: a- (without), men (month) and rhoia (flow), so it is literally "no monthly flow". Primary amenorrhoea means that menstruation has never begun: the girl has not reached her menarche (her first period) at the expected age. Secondary amenorrhoea means that periods which had begun have stopped. Both occur in anorexia nervosa. In a girl whose illness begins before puberty, DSM-5-TR notes that menarche may be delayed, and ICD-10 spells out the consequence: if the onset is pre-pubertal, the sequence of pubertal events is delayed or even arrested, growth ceases, the breasts do not develop and there is a primary amenorrhoea. In an older girl like Malini, whose periods had been regular, the periods stop: secondary amenorrhoea.

Why do they stop? Menstruation depends on a chain of hormones called the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis. The hypothalamus releases gonadotropin-releasing hormone; this signals the pituitary gland to release luteinising hormone and follicle-stimulating hormone; and these drive the ovaries (or, in males, the testes) to produce sex hormones and mature eggs or sperm. In starvation the hypothalamus turns the first link down. Less gonadotropin-releasing hormone means less luteinising and follicle-stimulating hormone, which means little oestrogen and no ovulation, so periods stop. In males the same shutdown lowers testosterone, and sexual interest and potency fall. DSM-5-TR records the result in its laboratory findings: females have low serum oestrogen, males low serum testosterone. From an evolutionary point of view the shutdown makes sense: a famine is a bad time to begin a pregnancy, and the body suspends reproduction until food returns. Section 8.5 and Book 1 Section 10.9 say more about the hypothalamus in hunger.

If amenorrhoea is so common and so meaningful, why was it dropped? There were two kinds of reason. The first was logical: a menstrual criterion cannot be applied to males, to girls who have not reached menarche or to women after the menopause, and it is hard to judge in women taking the contraceptive pill, whose withdrawal bleeds can look like periods (ICD-10 itself lists this as an "apparent exception"). The second was empirical, and it is the more important: patients who meet every other criterion but still menstruate turned out to be just as ill as those who do not. As Barlow and Durand put it, amenorrhoea can be an objective index of how severely food is restricted, but it is inconsistent, because it does not occur in all cases. The study in the box was one of those that settled the question.

Key study: does losing your periods make you more ill? (Roberto and colleagues, 2008)

Under DSM-IV, a woman who met every criterion for anorexia nervosa except amenorrhoea could not be diagnosed; she was placed in the residual category, eating disorder not otherwise specified. Was she really less ill? Christina A. Roberto, with Joanna Steinglass, Laurel Mayer, Evelyn Attia and B. Timothy Walsh, reviewed the charts of 240 consecutive patients admitted for inpatient treatment at the New York State Psychiatric Institute between 1993 and 2006, and compared those who had stopped menstruating with those who still menstruated. The two groups differed on only two measures, their lowest lifetime BMI and their BMI on admission, both lower in the amenorrhoeic group. They did not differ in age, duration of illness, number of previous admissions, BMI at discharge, scores for depression and anxiety, or the severity of their eating-disorder attitudes. The authors concluded that amenorrhoea mainly reflects weight and nutritional status and adds little diagnostic information. In a review the next year, Attia and Roberto (2009) suggested, as one option, that DSM-5 describe amenorrhoea as a frequent feature that may say something about clinical severity, but no longer treat it as a core criterion. That is exactly what DSM-5 did.

So amenorrhoea is no longer a criterion, but it is still a finding. DSM-5-TR says that amenorrhoea is commonly present and "appears to be an indicator of physiological dysfunction"; it is usually a consequence of the weight loss, but in a minority of patients it actually comes first, before much weight has been lost. Whenever a question asks what amenorrhoea means, or whether it is found in anorexia nervosa, the answer is "absence of menstrual periods" and "yes, commonly". Only when a question asks what DSM-5 or DSM-5-TR requires is amenorrhoea the wrong answer.

The two WHO systems take different approaches again. ICD-10's diagnostic guidelines for F50.0, anorexia nervosa, published in 1992, are the strictest of the four systems, because all five of its requirements must be met:

  1. Body weight maintained at least 15 percent below that expected (either lost or never achieved), or a BMI of 17.5 or less, the BMI being used from the age of 16; pre-pubertal patients may instead fail to make the expected weight gain.
  2. The weight loss is self-induced by avoiding "fattening foods"; one or more of self-induced vomiting, self-induced purging, excessive exercise, and appetite suppressants or diuretics may also be present (they are not required).
  3. Body-image distortion, in which a dread of fatness persists as an intrusive, overvalued idea and the patient imposes a low weight threshold on herself.
  4. A widespread endocrine disorder of the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis, shown in women as amenorrhoea and in men as loss of sexual interest and potency; there may also be raised growth hormone and cortisol, changes in the peripheral metabolism of thyroid hormone and abnormal insulin secretion.
  5. If onset is pre-pubertal, delayed or arrested puberty; with recovery puberty is often completed normally, but menarche is late.

ICD-10 has no subtypes of anorexia nervosa. A patient who lacks one or more of the key features, such as amenorrhoea or significant weight loss, but otherwise presents a fairly typical picture, or who has all the features only to a mild degree, is coded F50.1, atypical anorexia nervosa.

ICD-11 (6B80), in force from 2022, is closer to DSM-5-TR in spirit but keeps numbers. Its anorexia nervosa is characterised by significantly low body weight for height, age and developmental stage that is not due to another health condition or to lack of food, and it names the usual guide: a BMI below 18.5 in adults, or a BMI-for-age below the 5th percentile in children and adolescents. It adds a rule found in neither DSM nor ICD-10: rapid weight loss, for example of more than 20 percent of total body weight within 6 months, may replace the low-weight guideline. The low weight must be accompanied by a persistent pattern of behaviour that prevents the restoration of normal weight (restricting, purging, or raising energy expenditure by exercise), "typically associated with a fear of weight gain", so the fear is expected but not strictly required; and low weight or shape is central to the person's self-evaluation or is inaccurately perceived as normal or even excessive. Amenorrhoea is not required. ICD-11 then classifies by weight and by pattern: 6B80.0, anorexia nervosa with significantly low body weight (adult BMI between 18.5 and 14.0, or between the 5th and the 0.3rd percentile in the young); 6B80.1, with dangerously low body weight (adult BMI under 14.0, or under the 0.3rd percentile), a band ICD-11 links with a high risk of physical complications and greatly increased mortality; and 6B80.2, in recovery with normal body weight, for patients whose BMI has risen above 18.5 but who have not yet achieved a full and lasting recovery, the diagnosis being kept until healthy weight is maintained and weight-losing behaviour has stopped without treatment (for example for at least a year after intensive treatment ends). The first two each have a restricting pattern and a binge-purge pattern (6B80.00 and 6B80.01; 6B80.10 and 6B80.11). Table 8.6 puts the four systems side by side.

Table 8.6 Anorexia nervosa in four classifications.
FeatureDSM-IV (1994)DSM-5-TR (2022)ICD-10 F50.0 (1992)ICD-11 6B80 (2022)
Weight ruleRefusal to keep minimally normal weight; example below 85% of expectedRestriction leading to significantly low weight; no fixed cut-off (BMI signposts 17.0, 18.5, 19.0)At least 15% below expected, or BMI 17.5 or less (from age 16)Usually BMI below 18.5 (5th percentile in the young); rapid loss over 20% in 6 months may substitute
Fear of fatnessRequiredFear, or persistent behaviour that interferes with gainDread of fatness as an intrusive overvalued ideaBehaviour that prevents weight restoration, typically with fear
Body imageDisturbance, undue influence, or denial of seriousnessDisturbance, undue influence, or lack of recognition of seriousnessBody-image distortion; self-imposed low weight thresholdWeight or shape central to self-worth, or low weight seen as normal
AmenorrhoeaRequired (3 consecutive cycles missed)Not required; a common findingRequired as part of endocrine disorder (men: loss of libido, potency)Not required
Subtypes or patternsRestricting; binge-eating/purging (current episode)Restricting; binge-eating/purging (last 3 months)None (F50.1 atypical)Restricting pattern; binge-purge pattern
SeverityNoneMild to extreme by BMINoneSignificantly low (18.5 to 14.0); dangerously low (under 14.0); in recovery
Exam trap

DSM-5 dropped amenorrhoea as a criterion, but amenorrhoea still means absence of menstrual periods and is still a common finding in anorexia, so "absence of menstrual cycle" is the right answer when a question asks what the term means; "intense fear of weight gain", "excessive exercise" and "body-image distortion" are other features of the disorder, not definitions of amenorrhoea. Keep the numbers apart: ICD-10's threshold is BMI 17.5 or less (or 15 percent below expected weight); ICD-11's guide is below 18.5, with "dangerously low" below 14.0; DSM-IV used 85 percent of expected weight; DSM-5-TR sets no diagnostic BMI cut-off at all, and its 17 is the line between mild and moderate, not between ill and well.

Subtypes and severity

Once the three criteria are met, DSM-5-TR asks the clinician to specify a subtype, and the rule for choosing it is narrow and precise. In the restricting type, during the last 3 months the person has not engaged in recurrent episodes of binge eating or purging behaviour (self-induced vomiting, or misuse of laxatives, diuretics or enemas); weight loss is achieved primarily through dieting, fasting and/or excessive exercise. In the binge-eating/purging type, during the last 3 months the person has engaged in recurrent episodes of binge eating or purging behaviour. Malini, who diets and runs but has never vomited or binged, has the restricting type.

Read the binge-eating/purging definition again and notice the "or". Most people with this subtype who binge also purge, but some do not binge at all; they purge regularly after eating quite small amounts of food. Barlow and Durand add that, unlike people with bulimia nervosa, those with the binge-eating/purging type of anorexia tend to binge on relatively small amounts and to purge more consistently, in some cases every time they eat, and that roughly half of all people who meet the criteria for anorexia nervosa binge and purge. (What exactly counts as a binge is defined in Section 8.4.1.) Nalini, 22, shows the purging-only form. She weighs 45 kg at 1.65 m, a BMI of about 16.5. She eats a small breakfast and a small dinner and makes herself sick after each; she has never eaten a large amount in her life and is frightened of the idea. She has anorexia nervosa, binge-eating/purging type, even though she never binges.

Why limit the subtype to the last 3 months? Because the subtypes are not stable. DSM-5-TR says that crossover between them over the course of the disorder is not uncommon, so the subtype should describe current symptoms rather than the longitudinal course. The evidence came from follow-up studies. Kamryn T. Eddy and her colleagues (2002) followed 136 women with anorexia nervosa for 8 to 12 years and found that by the 8-year point 62 percent of those who began with the restricting type had crossed over to bingeing or purging, while the two subtypes differed little in severity of symptoms or in personality. Subtyping, they concluded, may describe a phase of the illness rather than two different illnesses. The subtypes are still worth recording, because they carry different risks. Compared with the restricting type, people with the binge-eating/purging type show higher rates of impulsivity and are more likely to misuse alcohol and other drugs; purging adds its own medical dangers, above all low potassium (Section 8.3.5); and obsessive-compulsive disorder is described especially in the restricting type.

DSM-5-TR also provides two remission specifiers. In partial remission applies when, after the full criteria have been met, Criterion A (the low weight) has not been met for a sustained period but either Criterion B (the fear of weight gain or weight-preventing behaviour) or Criterion C (the disturbed experience of weight and shape) is still present. In full remission applies when none of the criteria has been met for a sustained period. The order matters clinically. Weight is usually the first thing to recover, because it can be restored by feeding; the fear of fatness and the overvaluation of shape recover much later, if at all. A young woman discharged from hospital at a healthy weight who still weighs herself ten times a day and panics at a second chapati is in partial remission, not recovered, and is at high risk of relapse.

Finally comes severity. DSM-5-TR bases the minimum level of severity, for adults, on the current BMI, using ranges derived from the WHO's categories of thinness in adults; for children and adolescents the corresponding BMI percentiles are used. The four levels are:

  • Mild: BMI 17 or more.
  • Moderate: BMI 16 to 16.99.
  • Severe: BMI 15 to 15.99.
  • Extreme: BMI below 15.

Two qualifications make the rating clinical rather than mechanical. The word "current" means that severity changes as weight changes: a patient admitted at a BMI of 14.5 (extreme) may be rated moderate a month later. And the level of severity may be increased to reflect clinical symptoms, the degree of functional disability and the need for supervision. A woman with a BMI of 17.3, which is formally mild, who faints in the bathroom, has a pulse in the thirties and cannot eat a meal without someone sitting beside her, can and should be rated as more severe. The BMI sets the floor, not the ceiling. Now the worked example, with one caution. Because Malini is 16, DSM-5-TR asks for her severity to be judged by her BMI-for-age percentile, not by the adult bands, which apply to adults only. The adult scale is used for her here simply to show how the bands work: her BMI of 15.6 would fall in the severe band (15 to 15.99), and on the percentile measure that DSM-5-TR actually applies to her she is well below the 5th percentile for her age, so her clinician rates her illness as severe. Table 8.7 sets out the bands, and Figure 8.5 places them on a single ruler alongside the numbers used by the other systems.

Table 8.7 DSM-5-TR severity ratings for anorexia nervosa in adults. The weights are for a woman 1.60 m tall and are given only to make the bands concrete.
SeverityCurrent BMI (kg/m2)Weight at 1.60 mICD-11 bandWhat the clinician thinks
Mild17 or more43.5 kg or moreSignificantly low (below 18.5)Underweight; medical review and close outpatient follow-up
Moderate16 to 16.9941.0 to 43.4 kgSignificantly lowPhysical effects of starvation usually present; monitor heart and blood
Severe15 to 15.9938.4 to 40.9 kgSignificantly lowHigh medical risk; admission often needed
ExtremeBelow 15Below 38.4 kgDangerously low only below 14.0Life-threatening; usually hospital refeeding (Section 8.5.6)

The top end of the ruler raises a question that clinicians meet more and more often. Mitali is 28. Over five months she has gone from 75 kg to 59 kg at a height of 1.62 m, a loss of more than a fifth of her body weight; her pulse is slow, her periods have stopped, she is terrified of regaining a gram and she weighs herself every hour. But her BMI is now about 22.5, well inside the normal range. Under DSM-5-TR she cannot be diagnosed with anorexia nervosa, because Criterion A requires a significantly low weight and adults at a BMI of 19 or more should not receive the diagnosis; she has atypical anorexia nervosa, one of the other specified feeding or eating disorders (Section 8.4.7), and she may be just as medically at risk. Under ICD-11 the answer could be different, because its rule that rapid loss of more than 20 percent of body weight within 6 months may replace the low-weight guideline could bring her within 6B80. The same patient, two manuals, two diagnoses: this is why examiners like to ask which system a number comes from.

Exam trap

The restricting type means no recurrent binge eating or purging during the last 3 months, with weight lost by dieting, fasting or excessive exercise. "Required compensatory vomiting", "binge-purge cycles within 3 months" and "required laxative use weekly" describe the binge-eating/purging type or nothing at all (DSM-5-TR sets no weekly frequency for purging in anorexia). "BMI below 17.5 only" is ICD-10's weight threshold, not a DSM subtype, and no DSM subtype is defined by BMI. "Selective eating without weight loss" is picky eating or ARFID (Section 8.2.4). The binge-eating/purging type does not require binges: purging after small meals is enough. And severity is rated by current BMI: mild 17 or more, moderate 16 to 16.99, severe 15 to 15.99, extreme below 15.

The starving body

When Malini's doctor examines her, he finds a resting pulse of 48 beats a minute, a low blood pressure, cold blue hands and a fine down of hair on her forearms and back. None of these findings is random. Almost every physical sign of anorexia nervosa follows from one idea: the body is defending itself against starvation. Faced with too little fuel, it cuts its spending. It slows the heart, lowers the temperature, turns down the thyroid hormone that sets the metabolic rate, switches off reproduction, and lets the bones and the blood-forming marrow run down. If you remember the phrase "slow and low", you can work out most of the findings for yourself, and, just as usefully, you can see at once which answer options point the wrong way. DSM-5-TR sums up the picture: the semistarvation of anorexia nervosa, and the purging that sometimes goes with it, can cause significant and potentially life-threatening medical conditions; the nutritional damage affects most major organ systems; and while most of the disturbances reverse with nutritional rehabilitation, some, including loss of bone mineral density, are often not completely reversible.

What the clinician sees. The most remarkable finding on physical examination, DSM-5-TR says, is emaciation. Commonly there is also significant hypotension (low blood pressure), hypothermia (low body temperature) and bradycardia (a slow heart rate). Some patients develop lanugo, a fine, soft, downy body hair (the word is Latin, from lana, wool), which Barlow and Durand note on the limbs and cheeks; it is the same kind of hair that covers a foetus before birth. The skin is often dry and the hair and nails brittle, and the patient feels the cold when others do not. Some develop a yellowing of the skin associated with hypercarotenaemia, a raised level of carotene in the blood. Some develop peripheral oedema, swelling of the ankles and feet with fluid, especially during weight restoration or after stopping laxatives or diuretics, which frightens patients because it looks and feels like getting fat. Rarely, small pinpoint haemorrhages (petechiae) or bruises (ecchymoses), usually on the arms and legs, show a bleeding tendency. The patient's own complaints, when she makes any, are of amenorrhoea, constipation, abdominal pain, cold intolerance and lethargy, and also, paradoxically, of excess energy: many patients feel restless and driven rather than tired.

The heart. Sinus bradycardia, a slow but otherwise normal rhythm, is common; it is the heart doing less work because there is less fuel to do it with. Rarely, there are arrhythmias, and in some patients there is significant prolongation of the QTc interval, the stretch of the electrocardiogram from the start of the ventricles' electrical activation to the end of their electrical recovery (repolarisation) after each beat, corrected for heart rate. A long QTc makes the heart vulnerable to a sudden, chaotic rhythm that can kill within minutes, and it is one reason deaths in anorexia nervosa can come without warning.

The blood. The bone marrow, starved like the rest of the body, makes fewer cells. Leucopenia (a low white cell count) is common, with all types of white cell reduced but usually an apparent lymphocytosis (lymphocytes making up a larger share of a smaller total). Mild anaemia can occur, as can thrombocytopenia (low platelets) and, rarely, bleeding problems. On the blood chemistry, dehydration shows as a raised blood urea nitrogen. Hypercholesterolaemia, a raised cholesterol level, is common, which surprises students who expect everything to be low; liver enzymes may be raised; and low magnesium, zinc and phosphate, with a raised amylase, are occasionally found.

The hormones. The thyroid tells the clearest story. The gland itself is healthy, and serum thyroxine (T4) is usually in the low-normal range. But T4 is mostly a precursor; the body converts it either into triiodothyronine (T3), the active hormone that sets the metabolic rate, or into reverse T3, an inactive form. In starvation the conversion shifts towards the inactive form, so T3 is decreased and reverse T3 is raised. This pattern, often called the low T3 syndrome or sick euthyroid syndrome, is not thyroid disease but another energy-saving manoeuvre, and giving thyroid hormone to "correct" it would be a mistake. It is the opposite of hyperthyroidism, in which an overactive gland raises T4 and T3 and produces weight loss with a fast pulse, heat intolerance and a high metabolic rate. Among the sex hormones, females have low oestrogen and males low testosterone, the result of the hypothalamic shutdown described in Section 8.3.3, and ICD-10 adds raised growth hormone and cortisol to the endocrine picture. Resting energy expenditure, the calories the body burns at rest, is often significantly reduced.

The bones. Low bone mineral density, with areas of osteopenia (thinning of bone) or osteoporosis (thinning severe enough to make bones fragile), is often seen, and the risk of fracture is significantly raised. Low oestrogen and poor nutrition both drain the skeleton. This is the damage DSM-5-TR singles out as often not completely reversible, and it is especially serious because anorexia nervosa usually strikes in adolescence, the years in which much of the skeleton's lifetime store of bone is laid down. A girl who loses those years may carry fragile bones for life, even after full recovery.

The brain. The electroencephalogram may show diffuse abnormalities reflecting a metabolic encephalopathy, a disturbance of brain function caused by significant fluid and electrolyte disturbances. Brain imaging shows that starvation itself shrinks the brain: as Barlow and Durand note, it induces loss of grey matter and hormonal dysregulation in the brain, changes that are reversible when normal weight is restored. The poor concentration, rigid thinking and preoccupation with food of the starving patient (Section 8.3.6) have a physical basis.

What purging adds. Patients of the binge-eating/purging type carry a second set of findings, the same marks seen in bulimia nervosa. Self-induced vomiting loses stomach acid and potassium, causing metabolic alkalosis (a raised serum bicarbonate), hypochloraemia (low chloride) and hypokalaemia (low potassium); laxative misuse, which loses alkaline fluid from the bowel, can cause a mild metabolic acidosis. Potassium is essential to the electrical stability of the heart, so hypokalaemia in a patient whose heart is already slowed and whose QTc may be long is one of the most dangerous combinations in clinical psychiatry. Vomiting also enlarges the salivary glands, particularly the parotid glands in front of the ears, which gives a puffy, round-cheeked face that contrasts oddly with the thin body; erodes the enamel of the teeth; and can leave scars or calluses on the back of the hand from repeated contact with the teeth while inducing vomiting, the mark known as Russell's sign (Section 8.4.3). Figure 8.6 puts the findings on the body, and Table 8.8 lists them by system.

Table 8.8 The medical findings of anorexia nervosa by system (DSM-5-TR), with the wrong-direction options that examiners use as distractors.
SystemFindingWhy it happensWrong-direction distractor
Energy and temperatureLow resting energy expenditure; hypothermia; cold intoleranceBody lowers its metabolic rate to save fuelHyperthermia; raised metabolic rate
Heart and circulationSinus bradycardia; hypotension; long QTc in some; rarely arrhythmiaLess work for a heart with less fuel; electrolyte lossTachycardia; hypertension
ThyroidT4 low-normal; T3 low; reverse T3 raisedActive hormone turned down (low T3 syndrome)Hyperthyroidism
Sex hormonesLow oestrogen or testosterone; amenorrhoea; delayed menarcheHypothalamic shutdown of the gonadal axis(none commonly set)
Blood cellsLeucopenia with relative lymphocytosis; mild anaemia; low plateletsStarved bone marrow(none commonly set)
Blood chemistryRaised urea; high cholesterol; raised liver enzymes; low magnesium, zinc, phosphateDehydration, liver strain, deficiency(High cholesterol is true, not a distractor)
Electrolytes with purgingHypokalaemia, hypochloraemia, alkalosis (vomiting); mild acidosis (laxatives)Acid and potassium lost in vomit and stoolHyperkalaemia
BoneLow bone density; osteopenia; osteoporosis; fracturesLow oestrogen and undernutrition; often not fully reversible(none commonly set)
Skin and hairLanugo; dry skin; brittle hair and nails; yellow skin; rarely petechiaeMalnutrition; raised carotene(none commonly set)
Eating and gutRestriction despite hunger; constipation; abdominal painRestriction is chosen; the gut slowsPolyphagia (overeating)
BrainGrey matter loss; diffuse EEG changesStarvation; electrolyte disturbance(none commonly set)

One more danger belongs here because it arises from the starving body, although its management belongs to Section 8.5.6. When a severely starved person is fed too quickly, the sudden supply of carbohydrate drives phosphate, potassium and magnesium from the blood into the cells, and fluid shifts with them; the blood levels of these minerals can fall low enough to cause heart failure, arrhythmias, seizures and death. This is the refeeding syndrome. It is not a new discovery. Pinel and Barnes recall that many of the prisoners liberated from the concentration camps at the end of the Second World War were made ill, and some were even killed, by the food their liberators gave them. The lesson for anorexia nervosa is that the first days of feeding are among the most dangerous of the whole illness; Section 8.5.6 explains how refeeding is made safe.

Clinical link: a normal pulse and normal blood tests do not mean she is safe

DSM-5-TR warns that some patients with anorexia nervosa show no laboratory abnormalities at all, even at a dangerously low weight. A normal blood report can reassure a family, a school or a doctor, and a slow pulse in a thin girl who runs every morning can be mistaken for an athlete's heart. Neither reassurance is justified. Death in anorexia nervosa can come suddenly, from an arrhythmia in a heart that has been slowed, thinned of fuel and stripped of potassium, and the weight, the rate of weight loss and the patient's behaviour are better guides to risk than any single test. A clinical psychologist who works with eating disorders should never be the only professional involved: every patient needs a physician who checks the heart, the blood pressure and the electrolytes, and who repeats the checks.

Exam trap

The correct finding in anorexia nervosa is lanugo hair, bradycardia, hypokalaemia (with purging) and amenorrhoea. Every distractor points the wrong way because starvation slows the body down: hyperthyroidism is wrong (T3 is low, with T4 low-normal and reverse T3 raised), polyphagia (overeating) is wrong (the patient restricts), and hyperkalaemia and tachycardia are both wrong (potassium falls with vomiting, and the pulse is slow). Two true findings surprise students: high cholesterol and, in some patients, a complaint of excess energy.

The mind of starvation and the other associated features

Starvation changes the mind as surely as it changes the heart, and this creates a problem for anyone trying to understand anorexia nervosa: which of the patient's psychological features belong to the disorder, and which are simply what happens to any human being who is starved? The Minnesota volunteers of this chapter's opening, healthy young men with no eating disorder, became preoccupied with food, collected recipes, hoarded food-related things and grew depressed, irritable and withdrawn as they lost about a quarter of their weight. Every one of those features appears in anorexia nervosa. DSM-5-TR draws the conclusion carefully, feature by feature.

Mood. When seriously underweight, many people with anorexia nervosa show depressed mood, social withdrawal, irritability, insomnia and diminished interest in sex. Because the same features are seen in people without anorexia nervosa who are significantly undernourished, DSM-5-TR says, many of them may be secondary to the physiological effects of semistarvation, although they may also be severe enough to justify an additional diagnosis of major depressive disorder. The depression is real either way: Barlow and Durand cite a study by Godart and colleagues (2007) in which as many as 71 percent of patients had a depressive disorder at some point in their lives. The practical rule is to assess mood once again after weight has been restored. A low mood that lifts with feeding was largely the starvation speaking; one that persists at a healthy weight needs treatment in its own right.

Obsessions about food. Obsessive-compulsive features, both related and unrelated to food, are often prominent. Most patients are preoccupied with thoughts of food. Some collect recipes or hoard food; Barlow and Durand describe patients who hide food in their rooms and look at it from time to time, and others who take a keen interest in cooking, becoming expert cooks who prepare all the family's meals while eating almost none of them themselves. Pinel and Barnes make the same observation: many patients seem obsessed with food, continually talking about it, thinking about it and preparing it for others. Observations of other forms of starvation suggest that undernutrition intensifies such food-related obsessions and compulsions, which is the Minnesota lesson again. So DSM-5-TR sets a rule for the extra diagnosis: obsessive-compulsive disorder should be added only when the patient has obsessions and compulsions unrelated to food, body shape or weight, such as an excessive fear of contamination or a need to check locks. Malini spends her free periods at school reading recipes on her phone and has started baking cakes for the family that she does not eat; that is the anorexia, not a second disorder.

Control, ineffectiveness and rigidity. DSM-5-TR lists a further set of features that are sometimes associated with the disorder: concerns about eating in public; feelings of ineffectiveness, a sense of being unable to make anything happen in one's life; a strong desire to control one's environment; inflexible thinking; limited social spontaneity; and overly restrained emotional expression. Put these together and a recognisable person emerges. She feels that most of her life is decided by others (parents, teachers, examinations), and she has found one domain, her body, in which her control is absolute; she keeps rules rigidly, finds it hard to be playful or relaxed with friends, and rarely shows anger or sadness openly. These features overlap with the personality that often precedes the illness, perfectionistic, conscientious, anxious and over-controlled, and with Hilde Bruch's idea of a paralysing sense of ineffectiveness. Whether that personality is a cause of the disorder is taken up in Section 8.5.3; here it is enough to recognise it in the consulting room.

The impulsive subgroup. Not every patient fits the over-controlled picture. Compared with the restricting type, people with the binge-eating/purging type have higher rates of impulsivity and are more likely to misuse alcohol and other drugs, a contrast that matters both for risk (Section 8.3.7) and for the comparison with bulimia nervosa (Section 8.4).

Exercise. Some patients show excessive levels of physical activity. Increases in activity often precede the onset of the disorder, and over its course increased activity speeds weight loss. During treatment, excessive activity can be very difficult to control and can jeopardise weight recovery. Patients walk instead of taking the bus, stand rather than sit, do exercises in the bathroom at night, and, like Malini on her terrace at dawn, exercise when no one is watching. Barlow and Durand's patient Julie exercised with a workout video after every meal until, in her mind, she had burned off every calorie she had eaten, and when her parents suggested she was exercising too much, she worked out when no one was around.

Medicines and insulin. Some patients misuse medications, for example by manipulating the dose, in order to lose weight or avoid gaining it. A particularly dangerous form occurs in people who also have type 1 diabetes mellitus: they may omit or reduce their insulin, so that the body cannot use the carbohydrate it eats and the glucose is lost in the urine instead. The weight comes off, at the price of very high blood sugar, risk of diabetic ketoacidosis and accelerated damage to eyes, kidneys and nerves. Every diabetic adolescent whose blood sugar control suddenly worsens without explanation should be asked about weight and shape.

The puzzle of the missing hunger. There is one feature of the starving mind that anorexia nervosa does not share with ordinary starvation, and it is the deepest puzzle of the disorder. In famine, and in the Minnesota experiment, starvation makes food overwhelmingly attractive; the starving person can think of little else and will eat almost anything. Patients with anorexia nervosa are also preoccupied with food, and Barlow and Durand note that most crave food at least some of the time but control their cravings. Yet they can sit in front of a meal and not eat it, week after week. John Pinel and Steven Barnes point out that the normal starvation-driven rise in the reward value of eating is somehow not doing its job. Their tentative suggestion is that eating a meal is physically unpleasant for a severely undernourished body, and that repeated unpleasant meals may produce conditioned taste aversions that reduce the motivation to eat. It is a hypothesis, not an established fact, but it shows how much is still unexplained. The main explanations of the disorder, sociocultural, familial, psychological and biological, are the subject of Section 8.5.

Exam trap

Depressive and obsessional features in an underweight patient are often secondary to starvation: diagnose major depressive disorder only if the mood disturbance is severe enough in its own right (and recheck after weight restoration), and add OCD only for obsessions and compulsions unrelated to food, shape or weight. Preoccupation with food, recipe collecting, hoarding food and cooking for others are features of anorexia nervosa itself. The typical traits are perfectionism, inflexibility and over-control (Section 8.5.3); impulsivity belongs to bulimia nervosa and to the binge-eating/purging type of anorexia, which also has more alcohol and drug misuse.

Who develops it, what happens, and why it kills

How common is it? Less common than most people think, and far more common in women. DSM-5-TR reports two epidemiological studies of community samples in the United States. In them, the 12-month prevalence of anorexia nervosa ranged from 0.0 to 0.05 percent (0 to 0.08 percent in women, 0 to 0.01 percent in men), and the lifetime prevalence from 0.60 to 0.80 percent (0.9 to 1.42 percent in women, 0.12 to 0.3 percent in men). The difference between the two figures is worth a moment's thought. Lifetime prevalence counts everyone who has ever had the disorder; 12-month prevalence counts only those who had it in the past year. A lifetime rate of nearly 1 percent alongside a 12-month rate close to zero tells you that most episodes are over within a few years (prevalence and incidence are explained in Book 3 Section 1.2). The sex difference in adults is large, several women for every man, but one study of adolescents found similar rates in both sexes, a reminder that the ratio depends on who is counted and how.

Anorexia nervosa is not evenly spread across the world. DSM-5-TR says it appears to be most prevalent in post-industrialised, high-income countries such as the United States, many European countries, Australia, New Zealand and Japan. Its prevalence in most low- and middle-income countries is uncertain, but it appears to be increasing in many countries of the Global South, including Asia and the Middle East. Within the United States it occurs in every ethnic and racial group, but seems to be less common among Latinx and Black Americans than among non-Latinx White Americans, and people from underserved groups who have an eating disorder use mental health services much less. These patterns, and the old description of anorexia nervosa as a Western culture-bound syndrome (Book 3 Section 1.10.2), are examined in Section 8.5.1. No reliable national figure exists for India, and none should be quoted.

Boys and men deserve a separate sentence because they are so easily missed. For many years the amenorrhoea criterion meant that a male could hardly be fitted into the diagnosis at all, families and doctors do not expect the disorder in boys, and a boy who runs and diets may be praised for discipline. Men's body concerns also take a different shape more often: the wish to be lean and muscular rather than thin. Its extreme form, muscle dysmorphia, the conviction that one's body is too small or insufficiently muscular, was once called "reverse anorexia" by the researchers who described it in male bodybuilders (Harrison G. Pope and colleagues, 1993); today it is classified as a form of body dysmorphic disorder (Book 3 Section 6.6.2), not as an eating disorder.

When does it start, and how does it run? Anorexia nervosa commonly begins in adolescence or young adulthood. It rarely begins before puberty or after the age of 40, though cases of both early and late onset have been described, and DSM-5-TR warns clinicians not to exclude the diagnosis on the grounds of older age alone. The onset is often linked with a stressful life event, such as leaving home for college or hostel. Younger patients may show atypical features, including denial of any "fear of fat"; older patients are more likely to have been ill for a long time and to show the signs of long-standing disease. Many people go through a period of changed eating, the "healthy eating" phase that Malini began after a relative's remark, before they meet the full criteria.
