The course and outcome are highly variable. Some people recover fully after a single episode. Some follow a fluctuating course, regaining weight and then relapsing. Others have a chronic illness that lasts many years. Hospital admission may be needed to restore weight and treat medical complications. Taking all patients together, most people with anorexia nervosa remit within 5 years of presentation, though remission rates may be lower among those who have needed hospital admission, who are the more severely ill. The functional consequences vary just as much: some patients remain active in their studies, work and social lives, as Malini is still top of her class, while others become socially isolated or fail to fulfil their academic or career potential.

Why it kills. DSM-5-TR puts the crude mortality rate of anorexia nervosa at approximately 5 percent per decade. A crude mortality rate is simply the number of deaths in a period divided by the number of people at risk, with no adjustment for age; 5 percent per decade means that of 100 people diagnosed with the disorder, about five will be dead ten years later. Death most commonly results from the medical complications of the disorder itself or from suicide. The medical deaths come mainly from the heart: the slowed, under-fuelled heart, a long QTc interval, the potassium lost by purging and the shifts of refeeding (Section 8.3.5) can combine to produce a fatal arrhythmia, sometimes in a patient who seemed stable the day before. For comparison, DSM-5-TR gives the crude mortality of bulimia nervosa as nearly 2 percent per decade (Section 8.4.4).

Key study: how deadly are the eating disorders? (Arcelus, Mitchell, Wales and Nielsen, 2011)

Individual follow-up studies of eating disorders gave mortality figures that varied widely, because they followed different patients for different lengths of time. Jon Arcelus and his colleagues at Loughborough University in England combined every usable study published between 1966 and 2010 in a meta-analysis. From 143 potentially relevant articles they found 36 with enough data, covering 166,642 person-years of observation of anorexia nervosa (one person followed for one year counts as one person-year, so 1,000 people followed for 5 years give 5,000). The weighted mortality rate was 5.1 deaths per 1,000 person-years for anorexia nervosa, 1.7 for bulimia nervosa and 3.3 for eating disorder not otherwise specified. They also calculated standardised mortality ratios, the number of deaths observed divided by the number expected in people of the same age and sex in the general population: 5.86 for anorexia nervosa, 1.93 for bulimia nervosa and 1.92 for eating disorder not otherwise specified. In other words, a person with anorexia nervosa was nearly six times as likely to die during follow-up as a peer of the same age and sex. One in five of the deaths in anorexia nervosa was a suicide, and age at assessment was a significant predictor of death. Notice that the two ways of expressing the risk agree: 5.1 deaths per 1,000 person-years is about 5 percent per decade, the figure DSM-5-TR gives.

Is anorexia nervosa the deadliest of all mental disorders? Textbooks often say so, and Barlow and Durand write that it has the highest mortality rate of any psychological disorder reviewed in their book. The broader evidence suggests a more careful phrase. A meta-review (a review of systematic reviews) by Chesney, Goodwin and Fazel (2014), which brought together reviews covering 20 mental disorders and more than 1.7 million patients, found that every mental disorder raised the risk of death, and that the highest all-cause mortality ratios were in the substance use disorders and anorexia nervosa. The safe statement, and the one examiners reward, is that anorexia nervosa is the most lethal of the eating disorders and has one of the highest mortality rates of any psychiatric disorder.

Suicide accounts for much of that excess. DSM-5-TR reports that the suicide rate in anorexia nervosa is about 18 times that of an age- and gender-matched comparison group; a systematic review found suicide to be the second leading cause of death in the disorder, after its medical complications; between a quarter and a third of patients have suicidal ideation; and approximately 9 to 25 percent have attempted suicide. The likely contributors, DSM-5-TR suggests, are greater exposure to sexual abuse, impaired decision-making, high rates of non-suicidal self-injury (a known risk factor for suicide attempts) and comorbid mood disorders. Barlow and Durand add that substance misuse in a person with anorexia nervosa is a strong predictor of death, particularly by suicide. Chesney and colleagues placed anorexia nervosa among the four disorders with the highest suicide risk, together with borderline personality disorder, depression and bipolar disorder. The general risk factors for suicide, and how risk is assessed, are taught in Book 3 Section 5.8; the point here is that a quiet, compliant, high-achieving girl with anorexia nervosa must be asked about suicide as directly as any other patient.

Exam trap

The most lethal eating disorder is anorexia nervosa: crude mortality about 5 percent per decade (DSM-5-TR), standardised mortality ratio about 5.9 (Arcelus and colleagues, 2011), with death from medical complications such as arrhythmia and from suicide. Bulimia nervosa (crude mortality nearly 2 percent per decade), binge-eating disorder and pica are the distractors. If an option says "the highest mortality of any psychiatric disorder", prefer the more exact "one of the highest", because substance use disorders rival it.

Telling anorexia nervosa apart

Weight loss is one of the commonest complaints in medicine, and anorexia nervosa accounts for only a small share of it. The diagnosis rests on two things that the other causes of weight loss usually lack: the person wants to be thin, or at least acts persistently to stay thin (Criterion B), and her experience of her own weight or shape is disturbed (Criterion C). DSM-5-TR adds a general warning: it is especially important to consider other causes of low weight or weight loss when the picture is atypical, for example when the illness begins after the age of 40. With that in mind, take the look-alikes in turn.

Medical conditions. Gastrointestinal disease, hyperthyroidism, occult (hidden) malignancies and acquired immunodeficiency syndrome (AIDS) can all cause serious weight loss. ICD-10 adds, for young patients, chronic debilitating diseases, brain tumours and intestinal disorders such as Crohn's disease or a malabsorption syndrome. People with these illnesses usually do not have a disturbed experience of their body, do not fear weight gain, and do not persist in behaviour that prevents it; most would be glad to gain weight. Hyperthyroidism is worth remembering as the mirror image of the starving body: weight falls, but the pulse is fast, the patient feels hot, and appetite is often increased. Two complications prevent the distinction from being simple. Acute weight loss from a medical illness is occasionally followed by the onset or recurrence of anorexia nervosa, which can at first be masked by the medical condition; and, rarely, anorexia nervosa develops after bariatric (weight-loss) surgery.

Major depressive disorder. Severe weight loss can occur in depression, through loss of appetite and interest in food. But most people with major depressive disorder neither desire excessive weight loss nor fear gaining weight; they would eat if they could want to.

Schizophrenia. People with schizophrenia may eat oddly, refuse food they believe to be poisoned, and occasionally lose a great deal of weight, but they rarely show the fear of gaining weight and the body-image disturbance that anorexia nervosa requires.

Substance use disorders. People who misuse substances may become underweight because they eat poorly, but they generally do not fear weight gain and do not have a disturbed body image. One group needs a closer look: those who use appetite-suppressing substances such as cocaine or other stimulants and who also report a fear of gaining weight. For them, DSM-5-TR says, comorbid anorexia nervosa should be considered, because the substance use may itself be the "persistent behaviour that interferes with weight gain" of Criterion B.

Social anxiety disorder, obsessive-compulsive disorder and body dysmorphic disorder. Some features of anorexia nervosa overlap with each of these. The patient may feel humiliated or embarrassed to be seen eating in public, as in social anxiety disorder; may have obsessions and compulsions about food, as in obsessive-compulsive disorder; or may be preoccupied with an imagined defect in appearance, as in body dysmorphic disorder. DSM-5-TR gives a rule for each extra diagnosis, and the three rules share one logic: add the second disorder only when its symptoms reach beyond food, weight and shape. Social anxiety disorder is not diagnosed if the social fears are limited to eating, but may be added if there are social fears unrelated to eating, such as an excessive fear of speaking in public (Book 3 Section 6.3). Obsessive-compulsive disorder is added only for obsessions and compulsions unrelated to food, such as an excessive fear of contamination (Book 3 Section 6.5). Body dysmorphic disorder is added only if the perceived defect is unrelated to body shape and size, such as a conviction that one's nose is too big (Book 3 Section 6.6).

Bulimia nervosa. People with bulimia nervosa binge, use inappropriate compensatory behaviour such as self-induced vomiting, and are overly concerned with shape and weight, so they can look very like patients with the binge-eating/purging type of anorexia nervosa. The difference is weight: unlike them, people with bulimia nervosa maintain a body weight at or above a minimally normal level. Section 8.4.6 compares the two disorders, and binge-eating disorder, in full.

Avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder. Patients with ARFID may lose a great deal of weight or become seriously deficient, but they do not fear gaining weight or becoming fat and do not experience their body weight and shape in a disturbed way (Section 8.2.4). Table 8.9 gathers the look-alikes and the clue that separates each.

Table 8.9 Low weight without anorexia nervosa: the look-alikes and the clue that separates each.
ConditionFear of fatness or disturbed body image?The deciding clue
Medical illness (gut disease, hyperthyroidism, cancer, AIDS)Usually absentWants to regain weight; physical cause found; hyperthyroidism has a fast pulse
Major depressive disorderAbsentAppetite lost with mood; no wish to be thinner; eating returns as mood lifts
SchizophreniaRarely presentFood refused for delusional reasons; odd eating
Substance use disorderUsually absentPoor intake from the drug habit; stimulants plus fear of gain suggest comorbid anorexia
ARFIDAbsent by definitionSensory dislike, lack of interest or fear of choking or vomiting
Bulimia nervosaPresentWeight at or above minimally normal

Comorbidity. Anorexia nervosa seldom travels alone. Bipolar, depressive and anxiety disorders commonly co-occur with it, and many patients report an anxiety disorder, or symptoms of anxiety, that began before the eating disorder, which is one reason childhood anxiety is counted among its risk factors (Section 8.5.3). Obsessive-compulsive disorder is described in some patients, especially those with the restricting type. Alcohol use disorder and other substance use disorders may also be comorbid, especially with the binge-eating/purging type, a pattern that fits the greater impulsivity of that subtype and, as Section 8.3.7 showed, adds to the risk of death.

Malini's assessment can now be completed. She is a 16-year-old girl with anorexia nervosa, restricting type, severe, with secondary amenorrhoea, bradycardia and hypotension; her physician must check her heart rhythm and electrolytes, and her family must be told plainly that her illness is dangerous. How girls like Malini come to be ill, and how they are helped to recover, are the questions of Section 8.5. Before that, Section 8.4 turns to the disorders in which the struggle with food takes the opposite form: the binge.

Exam trap

A low-weight person who binges and purges has anorexia nervosa, binge-eating/purging type, not bulimia nervosa: weight separates the two, not purging. Weight loss with no fear of fatness points to a medical illness, depression, schizophrenia, substance use or ARFID. Extra diagnoses follow one rule, reach beyond food and shape: social anxiety disorder only for social fears unrelated to eating, OCD only for obsessions unrelated to food (contamination), body dysmorphic disorder only for a defect unrelated to body size (the nose). OCD is commonest with the restricting type; alcohol and drug misuse with the binge-eating/purging type.

8.4 Bulimia Nervosa, Binge-Eating Disorder and the Other Specified Eating DisordersHigh yield · 8 Qs

Anorexia nervosa announces itself. A teenager who weighs 34 kilograms cannot hide her body for long, and sooner or later a parent, a teacher or a doctor sees what is happening. The disorders of this section are different. Most of the people who have them look entirely ordinary, many are of normal weight, and the behaviour at the centre of their lives happens behind a bolted door. Their hallmark is the binge, an episode of eating that the person cannot stop. In bulimia nervosa the binge is followed by an attempt to undo it, most often by vomiting; in binge-eating disorder it is not. Around these two full disorders sits a group of presentations that are just as real and just as dangerous but fit neither pattern, the other specified feeding or eating disorders.

This section first pins down what a binge is, because every diagnosis here depends on that definition. It then teaches bulimia nervosa (its criteria, the marks that purging leaves on the body, who develops it and what happens to them), binge-eating disorder and the other specified forms, and it sets the three main eating disorders side by side so that you can tell them apart in a single question. How bulimia and binge eating entered the manuals is told in Section 8.1.6, and anorexia nervosa's own criteria and medical picture are in Section 8.3. Why any of these disorders develops, and how each is treated, is the business of Section 8.5.

What counts as a binge

Jasleen is 21, a second-year commerce student living in a women's hostel in north Delhi. Three or four evenings a week, on the walk back from her coaching class, she stops at the same small shop and buys two packets of cream biscuits, a large packet of chips and a box of milk sweets. If the shopkeeper looks at her, she says they are for a birthday in the hostel. In her room, with the door bolted, she eats all of it in about twenty minutes, standing up, barely tasting anything after the first few biscuits. "It is as if someone else is eating," she told the psychologist at the college counselling centre. "I tell myself, only one packet. Then I am opening the second one, and I cannot stop until everything is finished." When the food is gone her stomach aches and she feels disgusting. She walks to the bathroom at the end of the corridor, turns the tap on full so that nobody will hear, and makes herself vomit. Afterwards she feels calm, almost light, for perhaps an hour. Then the shame arrives, and with it a promise: tomorrow she will eat nothing until dinner. Nobody in the hostel knows. Her weight is normal for her height.

Jasleen's evenings contain both of the elements that define an episode of binge eating in DSM-5-TR, and the definition insists that both be present. The first is about quantity: eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most people would eat in a similar period under similar circumstances. The second is about control: a sense, during the episode, that one cannot stop eating or cannot control what or how much one is eating. Each phrase in that definition has been chosen to rule something out, and it pays to read them one at a time.

"A discrete period of time". DSM-5-TR gives "within any 2-hour period" as its example and explains that a discrete period is a limited one, usually less than 2 hours. A binge has a beginning and an end. That is why grazing, picking at small amounts of food all day long, is not a binge however much it adds up to by bedtime. The episode does not have to stay in one place, though: a person may begin a binge in a restaurant and carry on eating after reaching home, just as Jasleen's binge begins at the shop counter and ends in her room.

"Definitely larger than most people would eat ... under similar circumstances". The comparison is with other people in the same situation, not with what the patient believes she ought to eat. Context therefore matters. Three helpings of biryani, a plate of kebabs and four gulab jamuns would be a remarkable amount for an ordinary weekday lunch, but at a wedding feast or a Diwali dinner, when everyone at the table eats far more than usual, the same meal is unremarkable. DSM-5-TR makes exactly this point with "a celebration or holiday meal". The number of calories actually eaten in a binge varies enormously from person to person, and Barlow and Durand note that binges usually contain more "junk food" than fruit and vegetables. What defines a binge is an abnormality of amount, not a craving for a particular nutrient, although people do tend to binge on the foods they otherwise forbid themselves: sweets, fried snacks, bread, ice cream.

"A sense of lack of control". The usual sign is that the person cannot stop once she has started, or cannot refrain from starting. Many describe an almost dissociated quality during or after the binge, as Jasleen does ("as if someone else is eating"). The loss of control is not absolute, and DSM-5-TR gives a telling example: a person may go on bingeing while the telephone rings but stop at once if a room-mate or spouse walks in. Control is impaired, not abolished, and shame can still reach the brake. Some people who have binged for years no longer describe an acute feeling of losing control but a general pattern of uncontrolled eating; and if a person has abandoned every effort to control her eating, loss of control is counted as present. A binge can even be planned, as Jasleen's are when she buys the food on her way home: planning the episode does not mean being able to stop it.

Several other features complete the picture, although none is part of the definition. Binges are usually secret, or as inconspicuous as possible, because the person is ashamed of them. They often continue until she is uncomfortably or even painfully full. They have triggers, and DSM-5-TR lists them in order of importance: the commonest is negative affect (sadness, anxiety, anger, loneliness), followed by interpersonal stress, dietary restraint (going hungry by design), negative feelings about weight, shape and food, and boredom. And they do something for the person, which is why they recur. In the short term a binge numbs the feeling that set it off; the delayed consequences are self-criticism and low mood, which become the trigger for the next binge. A behaviour that brings immediate relief and only later costs is precisely the kind of behaviour that learning theory predicts will persist (negative reinforcement, taught in the General Psychology book).

Clinicians who specialise in eating disorders draw finer distinctions than the manual does. The Eating Disorder Examination, a standardised interview developed by Christopher Fairburn and Zafra Cooper (described in the Assessment book), separates an objective bulimic episode, a large amount eaten with loss of control, which is what DSM-5-TR calls a binge, from a subjective bulimic episode, in which the person feels she has lost control but the amount is not objectively large (two chapatis and a bowl of dal eaten by a young woman who allows herself nothing after noon, experienced as a catastrophe), and from objective overeating, a large amount eaten without loss of control (the Diwali dinner). Only the objective episode counts towards a DSM-5-TR diagnosis. Subjective episodes are nevertheless common, especially in anorexia nervosa, and they cause real distress. ICD-11 casts a wider net: it defines a binge by the loss of control, with the person eating notably more or differently than usual, so it does not demand an objectively large amount.

Exam trap

A DSM-5-TR binge needs both elements: an amount definitely larger than most people would eat in a discrete period (usually under 2 hours) under similar circumstances, and a sense of loss of control. Overeating at a wedding feast with no loss of control is not a binge; nor is grazing all day; nor is a "subjective binge" over an ordinary amount, however distressing. A binge is defined by the amount eaten, not by a craving for carbohydrate or any particular food. The commonest trigger is negative affect, not hunger.

Bulimia nervosa: the criteria

With the binge defined, the diagnosis of bulimia nervosa (BN; ICD-10-CM code F50.2 as printed in DSM-5-TR) can be built up from five criteria. It is easiest to learn them as four questions a clinician asks: what does the person do, how often, why does it matter to her, and could it be something else?

What the person does (Criteria A and B). Criterion A is recurrent episodes of binge eating, defined as in Section 8.4.1. Criterion B is recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviour in order to prevent weight gain: self-induced vomiting; misuse of laxatives, diuretics or other medications; fasting; or excessive exercise. Both words in the name do work. The behaviour is compensatory because its purpose is to cancel the binge, to pay back the calories; and it is inappropriate because it is harmful, ineffective or both. The methods fall into two families. The term purging means actively expelling what has been eaten: vomiting, and the misuse of laxatives, diuretics or enemas. Fasting and excessive exercise are compensatory but not purging. The distinction matters for medical risk, since purging is what disturbs the body's salts (Section 8.4.3).

How often and for how long (Criterion C). The binge eating and the compensatory behaviour must both occur, on average, at least once a week for 3 months. The rule exists because occasional binges, and even occasional vomiting after a binge, are not rare among students and young adults; a frequency threshold separates a pattern from an incident, and a duration threshold separates a persistent disorder from a bad fortnight. Notice the word "both": a person who binges every day but compensates only once a month does not meet Criterion C for bulimia nervosa (she may well meet the criteria for binge-eating disorder instead, Section 8.4.5).

Why it matters to her (Criterion D). Self-evaluation is unduly influenced by body shape and weight. This is the psychological core of the disorder, and it is what separates bulimia nervosa from other reasons for overeating and vomiting. For people with bulimia, shape and weight are typically the most important things in deciding how they feel about themselves; a good day is a day on which the scale says the right number. The criterion is not a technicality: of 107 women seeking treatment for bulimia nervosa in a report by Garfinkel (1992), only 3 percent did not share this attitude (cited by Barlow and Durand). DSM-5-TR adds that people with bulimia may closely resemble those with anorexia in their fear of gaining weight, their wish to lose weight and their dissatisfaction with their bodies.

Could it be something else (Criterion E)? The disturbance must not occur only during episodes of anorexia nervosa. This is a rule of precedence. A person who binges and purges while at a significantly low body weight has anorexia nervosa, binge-eating/purging type, and is not given a second diagnosis of bulimia. If someone first diagnosed with anorexia regains a normal weight but goes on bingeing and purging, DSM-5-TR asks that bulimia nervosa be diagnosed only once its full criteria have been met for at least 3 months. Anorexia takes precedence because low weight is the most dangerous feature and dictates the treatment.

Two specifiers complete the diagnosis. In partial remission means that after full criteria were met, some but not all have been met for a sustained period; in full remission means none has been met for a sustained period. Severity is rated by the frequency of the compensatory behaviour, not of the binges: mild, an average of 1 to 3 episodes a week; moderate, 4 to 7; severe, 8 to 13; extreme, 14 or more. The clinician may raise the rating to reflect other symptoms and the degree of disability. Jasleen, who vomits after binges three or four evenings a week, sits on the boundary between mild and moderate. Keep the counting rule in mind, because binge-eating disorder uses the same four bands but counts binges instead (Section 8.4.5).

The compensatory methods deserve a closer look, because the questions ask about them and because each carries its own dangers. Self-induced vomiting is by far the commonest. It brings immediate relief, both from the physical discomfort of a stomach stretched past fullness and from the fear of gaining weight, and in some people it becomes a goal in itself: they binge in order to vomit, or vomit after eating even a small amount. Vomiting is induced at first with the fingers or an instrument (Barlow and Durand's patient once tried a toothbrush, without success) to trigger the gag reflex, but most people become so practised that they can vomit at will. Rarely, people swallow syrup of ipecac, an emetic, which with repeated use damages heart and skeletal muscle. Laxatives (drugs that relieve constipation) and diuretics (drugs that increase the output of urine) are the next commonest; enemas are rare; and these are seldom the only method used. Rarer still are taking thyroid hormone to speed the metabolism and, in people with diabetes mellitus, omitting or cutting insulin doses so that the calories of a binge are lost in the urine as unused glucose. Among the non-purging methods, fasting means going without food for a day or more. Exercise counts as excessive when it significantly interferes with important activities, when it occurs at inappropriate times or in inappropriate places, or when the person continues despite injury or other medical complications: running for two hours at midnight with a stress fracture is not fitness. In one study cited by Barlow and Durand (Davis and colleagues, 1997), 57 percent of patients with bulimia exercised excessively, against 81 percent of patients with anorexia.

DSM-5 (2013) made two important changes to the bulimia criteria of DSM-IV (1994), and Table 8.10 sets them out with the parallel changes for binge-eating disorder. First, the frequency threshold fell from twice a week to once a week, while the 3-month duration stayed the same: research had found that people who binged and purged once a week looked very like those who did so twice a week, so the higher threshold was excluding people who needed the diagnosis. Second, DSM-IV's two subtypes, a purging type (vomiting, laxatives, diuretics or enemas) and a non-purging type (fasting or exercise only), were dropped. The non-purging type had turned out to be rare, 6 to 8 percent of patients with bulimia, and the two types did not differ in the severity of their psychopathology, the frequency of their binges, or their rates of major depression and panic disorder (studies reviewed by Barlow and Durand). DSM-5 added the severity ratings in their place.

Table 8.10 Bulimia nervosa and binge-eating disorder from DSM-IV to DSM-5-TR.
FeatureDSM-IV (1994)DSM-5 (2013) and DSM-5-TR (2022)
Bulimia: frequency and durationBinges and compensation at least twice a week for 3 monthsAt least once a week for 3 months
Bulimia: subtypesPurging type and non-purging typeNo subtypes
Bulimia: severityNot ratedBy compensatory episodes a week: 1 to 3, 4 to 7, 8 to 13, 14 or more
Binge-eating disorder: statusResearch criteria in Appendix B, needing further studyA full diagnosis
Binge-eating disorder: frequency and durationBinge eating on at least 2 days a week for 6 monthsAt least once a week for 3 months
Binge-eating disorder: severityNot ratedBy binges a week, same four bands as bulimia

The ICD systems describe the same disorder with different emphases. ICD-10 (F50.2) describes bulimia nervosa as repeated bouts of overeating with an excessive preoccupation with controlling body weight, leading to overeating followed by vomiting or purgatives; it notes that bulimia shares many psychological features with anorexia, that repeated vomiting can disturb the body's electrolytes, and that there is often, but not always, an earlier episode of anorexia nervosa, from a few months to several years before. ICD-11 (6B81) asks for frequent, recurrent binges, for example once a week or more over a period of at least one month, with repeated compensatory behaviour, a self-evaluation that depends heavily on shape and weight, and marked distress or impairment. Its shorter duration and its looser definition of a binge (Section 8.4.1) mean that ICD-11 will diagnose bulimia in some people whom DSM-5-TR would not.

Exam trap

Compensatory behaviour (RML 2021) is anything done to prevent weight gain after a binge: laxatives, self-induced vomiting, excessive exercise, diuretics, other medications and fasting all count, so "all of the above" is right. Purging (vomiting, laxatives, diuretics, enemas) is one family of compensatory behaviour, not a synonym for it.

Cycles of binge eating and purging are bulimia nervosa. Binge-eating disorder has binges without compensatory behaviour. Anorexia nervosa is defined by restriction and significantly low weight, but beware: a person who binges and purges at a significantly low weight has anorexia nervosa, binge-eating/purging type, not bulimia, because Criterion E gives anorexia precedence.

Frequency: binges and compensation both, on average, at least once a week for 3 months. "Twice a week for 6 months" matches no edition (DSM-IV's bulimia rule was twice a week for 3 months; 6 months was DSM-IV's provisional rule for binge-eating disorder). "Once a day for 1 month" and "three times a week for 1 month" are invented. ICD-11's "at least one month" is a different system; do not mix it into a DSM question.

Weight, body and the marks of purging

Here is the fact that most surprises students: people with bulimia nervosa are usually not thin. DSM-5-TR says they are typically within the normal weight or overweight range, which for adults means a body mass index (BMI) of 18.5 or more and under 30, and Barlow and Durand note that the great majority are within about 10 percent of a normal weight for their height. The disorder does occur among people with obesity, but it is uncommon there. This is why you could share a hostel corridor with Jasleen for a year and never suspect anything.

Why does weight stay normal when a person vomits several times a week? Partly because purging is a remarkably inefficient way to get rid of calories. Vomiting soon after a binge recovers only about half of what was eaten, and less if it is delayed at all (Kaye and colleagues, 1993, cited by Barlow and Durand). Laxatives act on the large bowel, long after most of the food has been digested and absorbed higher up the gut, so they remove water and salts but very few calories; diuretics remove water and salts in the urine and no calories at all. The scale may fall the next morning, but what has been lost is fluid, and it returns. Barlow and Durand even report that young women with bulimia carry more body fat than healthy women matched for age and weight (Ludescher and colleagues, 2009), the very effect they are trying to prevent. The other part of the answer is what happens between binges. People with bulimia typically restrict their total intake, skip meals and choose low-calorie "diet" foods, avoiding anything they think is fattening or likely to set off a binge. Restriction and bingeing roughly cancel out, and the weight stays in the ordinary range, which is precisely what distinguishes bulimia from anorexia nervosa. Menstrual irregularity or amenorrhoea (the absence of periods, Section 8.3.3) often occurs in women with bulimia too, although it is uncertain whether it comes from weight swings, nutritional deficiency or emotional distress.

Bulimia may be invisible on the scale, but purging leaves marks, and a clinician who knows where to look can find them (Figure 8.7). They are worth learning in three groups.

The blood. Repeated vomiting and laxative or diuretic misuse disturb the body's fluids and salts, sometimes severely enough to be medically dangerous. The most important finding is hypokalaemia, a low blood potassium, which can provoke cardiac arrhythmias; low chloride (hypochloraemia) and low sodium (hyponatraemia) may accompany it. The acid-base balance shifts in a direction that tells you the method. Vomiting throws away hydrochloric acid from the stomach, so the blood becomes too alkaline, a metabolic alkalosis shown by a raised serum bicarbonate. Laxative-induced diarrhoea throws away alkaline fluid from the bowel, so it tends to produce the opposite, a metabolic acidosis. Some patients have a mildly raised serum amylase, probably reflecting the form of the enzyme made by the salivary glands. None of these is a diagnostic test for bulimia, which does not exist, but each increases the clinician's certainty, and an unexplained low potassium in a young woman should always prompt a gentle question about vomiting.

The mouth and face. Physical examination is often entirely normal, but the mouth may give the secret away. Stomach acid washing repeatedly over the teeth causes a significant and permanent loss of dental enamel, especially from the inner (lingual) surfaces of the front teeth, the surfaces facing the tongue, which the acid reaches first. The teeth may look chipped, ragged and "moth-eaten", and dental caries become commoner. The salivary glands, particularly the parotid glands in front of the ears, may swell noticeably, which gives the face a fuller, rounder look around the jaw; Barlow and Durand's patient Phoebe had this "chubby" appearance. Ironically, the person who vomits to look thinner may end up with a fuller face.

The hand. People who induce vomiting by pushing their fingers down the throat repeatedly scrape the back of the hand against the upper teeth. Over months this produces calluses, abrasions or scars on the knuckles and the back of the hand. The sign is named after Gerald Russell (1928 to 2018), the London psychiatrist who named bulimia nervosa in his 1979 paper (Section 8.1.6) and described these skin lesions in his patients: it is called Russell's sign. It is useful precisely because it is so specific; few other activities scar the knuckles in that particular place. Its absence proves nothing, since many patients learn to vomit without using their hands at all.

Beyond these signs lie rarer complications, some of them fatal: tears of the oesophagus from forceful vomiting, rupture of the stomach after a very large binge, and cardiac arrhythmias from low potassium. Repeated use of syrup of ipecac has caused serious myopathies of heart and skeletal muscle. People who misuse laxatives for a long time may become dependent on them to empty the bowel at all, and severe constipation and rectal prolapse have been reported. Gastrointestinal complaints of every kind are common. Pinel puts the contrast between the two classical eating disorders in a physician's terms: patients with anorexia need treatment for the effects of starvation (a slow metabolism, bradycardia, hypotension, hypothermia, anaemia), whereas patients with bulimia need treatment for irritation and inflammation of the oesophagus, vitamin and mineral deficiencies, electrolyte imbalance, dehydration and acid reflux.

Clinical link

Because bulimia is secret and weight is normal, the first professional to notice it is often not a psychologist but a dentist puzzled by acid erosion in a young patient, or a physician puzzled by a low potassium. Ask directly and without judgement: "Some people who are worried about their weight make themselves sick after eating. Has that ever happened to you?" Most patients are relieved to be asked. Patients who are still vomiting are usually advised by dentists to rinse with water rather than brush straight away, because acid-softened enamel is easily worn off by brushing.

Exam trap

"Unlike people with anorexia, individuals with bulimia ..." (RML 2022): the answer is that they may appear normal in weight. The other options describe features the two disorders share: people with anorexia's binge-eating/purging type also purge to stay thin; people with both disorders are obsessed with being too fat; and both damage health (and both carry raised mortality). Purging is therefore never what separates bulimia from anorexia; weight is. And Russell's sign belongs to Gerald Russell, who also named bulimia nervosa in 1979; it is a mark of self-induced vomiting, not of starvation.

Who develops bulimia nervosa, and its course

Bulimia nervosa was recognised as a distinct disorder only in the late 1970s, so good figures on how common it is are relatively recent. DSM-5-TR draws on two large community studies of American adults. They put the 12-month prevalence at 0.14 to 0.3 percent, much higher in women (0.22 to 0.5 percent) than in men (0.05 to 0.1 percent), and the lifetime prevalence at 0.28 to 1.0 percent (0.46 to 1.5 percent of women and 0.05 to 0.08 percent of men). Among American adolescents aged 13 to 18, one study found a lifetime prevalence of 1.3 percent in girls and 0.5 percent in boys. Prevalence and incidence are defined in Book 3 Section 1.2.6; the important lesson here is that a disorder affecting around one woman in a hundred over a lifetime is not rare. Within the United States the rates are similar across ethnoracial groups, although people from underserved groups use treatment less. Across the world, bulimia is commonest in high-income industrialised countries (the United States, Canada, Australia, New Zealand and much of Europe, with roughly comparable rates), its prevalence in some regions of Latin America and the Middle East is similar, and it appears to be gradually rising in many low- and middle-income countries.

Bulimia is overwhelmingly a disorder of women, at least among those who reach treatment: 90 to 95 percent of patients are female, according to Barlow and Durand, and DSM-5-TR notes that boys and men are especially underrepresented in treatment-seeking samples for reasons nobody has yet studied systematically. The men who do develop it tend to start a little later, and they cluster in two groups. In one study, David Carlat, Carlos Camargo and David Herzog (1997) reviewed 135 male patients with eating disorders seen over 13 years and found that 42 percent were gay or bisexual, a far higher proportion than in the general population, which has been attributed to strong pressures to be slim in some gay male communities. The second is athletes in sports that demand weight control, wrestlers above all, who must "make weight" to compete in their category; during 1998 stories were widely published about the deaths of three wrestlers from complications of eating disorders (Barlow and Durand). Among women, adolescent girls carry the highest risk: Eric Stice and his colleagues (2009) followed 496 adolescent girls for 8 years and found that more than 12 percent had experienced some form of eating disorder by the age of 20.

The disorder usually begins in adolescence or young adulthood; onset before puberty or after 40 is uncommon. The binge eating frequently begins during or after a diet, a sequence that Section 8.5.1 explains, and a run of stressful life events can also precipitate it. Leaving home for a hostel, as Jasleen did, combines both: a new freedom over food, a new set of peers comparing bodies, and the stress of being alone. Once established, the disturbed eating persists for at least several years in a high proportion of clinical cases. The course may be chronic or intermittent, with remissions alternating with relapses into bingeing. Over longer follow-up, however, the symptoms of many people diminish, with or without treatment, though treatment clearly improves the outcome; a remission lasting more than a year predicts a better long-term course, and severe psychiatric comorbidity predicts a worse one.

Bulimia also moves between diagnoses. In a minority of cases, 10 to 15 percent, it crosses over into anorexia nervosa, and people who cross over commonly revert to bulimia or cross back and forth several times. Others stop compensating but go on bingeing, and so come to meet the criteria for binge-eating disorder or another specified eating disorder. This fluidity is one reason the diagnosis is always based on the current presentation, which DSM-5-TR defines as the past 3 months.

Bulimia nervosa is a serious illness. People who have it carry a significantly raised risk of death, from all causes and from suicide; the crude mortality rate (the number of deaths in a year divided by the average population in that year) is nearly 2 percent per decade. That is lower than anorexia nervosa's figure of about 5 percent per decade (Section 8.3.7), but it is far above that of young women in general. Suicide risk is elevated: in one review a quarter to a third of people with bulimia had had suicidal thoughts, and a similar proportion had attempted suicide. The disorder also costs in quality of life, with the social side of life most often affected, although only a minority report severe impairment in their roles.

Most people with bulimia have at least one other mental disorder, and many have several. Depressive symptoms (low self-esteem above all) and depressive disorders are common. In many people the mood disturbance begins at the same time as the bulimia or after it, and they attribute it to the bulimia; in some it clearly comes first. Barlow and Durand conclude that, on most evidence, depression more often follows bulimia than causes it. Anxiety is just as frequent: in the American National Comorbidity Survey Replication (Hudson and colleagues, 2007), 80.6 percent of people with bulimia had had an anxiety disorder at some point in their lives. Both the mood and the anxiety disturbance often remit when the bulimia is effectively treated. The lifetime prevalence of a substance use disorder, especially alcohol or stimulant use disorder, is at least 30 percent; stimulants are often first taken to suppress appetite. And a substantial proportion of people with bulimia have a personality disorder, most often borderline personality disorder (Chapter 11), a link Section 8.5.3 returns to.

Five conditions have to be told apart from bulimia nervosa. Anorexia nervosa, binge-eating/purging type is diagnosed instead whenever the binges and purges occur only during a period of significantly low weight (Criterion E). Binge-eating disorder is considered when a person binges without regular compensatory behaviour. In Kleine-Levin syndrome, a rare neurological disorder of recurrent hypersomnia (Section 8.8.8), episodes of excessive eating occur, but the characteristic concern with shape and weight is absent. In major depressive disorder with atypical features overeating is common, but there is neither compensatory behaviour nor excessive concern with shape and weight; if both sets of criteria are met, both diagnoses are given. Finally, binge eating appears in the impulsivity criterion of borderline personality disorder, and if the criteria for both disorders are met, both are diagnosed. The risk factors for bulimia are taught with those of the other eating disorders in Section 8.5.

Exam trap

Bulimia nervosa's crude mortality is nearly 2 percent per decade; anorexia nervosa's is about 5 percent per decade, which is why anorexia, not bulimia, is the most lethal eating disorder. Bulimia crosses over into anorexia in only 10 to 15 percent of cases. At least 30 percent have a lifetime substance use disorder, and the commonest personality disorder is borderline. Onset before puberty or after 40 is uncommon.

Binge-eating disorder

For decades the diagnostic manuals had no place for a person who binged but did not compensate. Such people were not rare: Albert Stunkard had described binge eating among obese patients in 1959 (Section 8.1.6), and from the 1990s researchers kept finding people who were deeply distressed by their binges but did nothing to undo them, so that they could not be diagnosed with bulimia. DSM-IV (1994) placed "binge-eating disorder" in its Appendix B, among the conditions needing further study, with provisional criteria. DSM-5 (2013) promoted binge-eating disorder (BED; ICD-10-CM F50.81 in DSM-5-TR; ICD-11 6B82) to a full diagnosis. Barlow and Durand summarise the evidence that justified the move: BED shows a somewhat different pattern of heritability from the other eating disorders, it is more likely to occur in men and to begin later in life, and it is more likely to remit and responds better to treatment. A disorder with its own distribution, its own course and its own response to treatment has earned its own name.

The criteria follow the same logic as those for bulimia, with one crucial absence. Criterion A is recurrent episodes of binge eating, defined exactly as in Section 8.4.1: a definitely large amount in a discrete period, with a sense of loss of control. Criterion B requires that the binges be associated with three or more of five features:

  • eating much more rapidly than normal;
  • eating until feeling uncomfortably full;
  • eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry;
  • eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating;
  • feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed or very guilty afterwards.

Read the list again and you will see what it is for. Each feature is a behavioural sign that the eating has slipped free of hunger and fullness (rapid eating, eating past discomfort, eating without hunger) or a sign of the shame that surrounds it (hiding, self-disgust). Requiring three of them makes the binge recognisably pathological, so that a hearty eater who simply enjoys large meals is not caught. Criterion C requires marked distress about the binge eating, for the same reason. Criterion D sets the frequency at an average of at least once a week for 3 months, the same threshold as bulimia nervosa. Criterion E is the defining difference: the binge eating is not associated with the recurrent use of inappropriate compensatory behaviour, as in bulimia nervosa, and it does not occur only during the course of bulimia or anorexia nervosa.

Severity is rated by the number of binges a week (not compensatory episodes, which by definition are absent), using the familiar bands: mild 1 to 3, moderate 4 to 7, severe 8 to 13 and extreme 14 or more, again with room to raise the rating for other symptoms and disability. Partial remission has a specific meaning here: after full criteria were met, binge eating continues but at an average of less than one episode a week for a sustained period. Full remission means no criteria have been met for a sustained period.

Bhaskar is 38 and manages the accounts office of a garment exporter in Bengaluru. On four or five evenings most weeks, during the long drive home through the traffic, he stops at a takeaway and orders a family-size bucket of fried chicken, two large portions of fries and a litre of cola. He parks in a quiet lane and eats all of it alone in the car in about a quarter of an hour, so fast that he hardly tastes it. "I am not even hungry," he says. "I had lunch at three." Once he starts, he cannot stop until the bucket is empty. He throws the boxes into a bin well away from the house and then eats dinner with his wife and children as though nothing has happened. Afterwards, lying awake, he feels "disgusted, like an animal". He has never made himself vomit and has never taken a laxative; his attempts at dieting last two days. In two years he has gained 14 kilograms, and his physician has warned him about his blood sugar. Push Bhaskar through the criteria. His episodes are binges (a definitely large amount, in a short period, with loss of control). He shows four of the five Criterion B features: he eats very rapidly, without hunger, alone because he is embarrassed, and feels disgusted afterwards. He is markedly distressed. His binges have occurred four or five times a week for two years. He never compensates. The diagnosis is binge-eating disorder, moderate.

Bhaskar's weight raises the most common confusion about this disorder. Binge-eating disorder occurs in people of normal weight, in the overweight and in the obese, and among people who seek treatment it is reliably associated with overweight and obesity. But it is not the same thing as obesity, which is not a mental disorder at all (Section 8.1.2). Most obese people do not binge. And compared with obese people of the same weight who do not binge, obese people with binge-eating disorder eat more calories in laboratory studies of eating, have more functional impairment, a lower quality of life, more distress and more psychiatric comorbidity, and overvalue their shape and weight more. DSM-5-TR adds a practical difference: evidence-based psychological treatments for binge-eating disorder succeed more often than treatments for obesity do in people who have both. Barlow and Durand note that individuals with BED share some of the concern about shape and weight seen in anorexia and bulimia, and that this is what separates them from people who are obese without BED, although overvaluation of shape and weight is not a required criterion for BED as it is for bulimia.

Binge-eating disorder is the commonest of the three main eating disorders. In two American community studies cited by DSM-5-TR, its 12-month prevalence was 0.44 to 1.2 percent (0.6 to 1.6 percent in women and 0.26 to 0.8 percent in men) and its lifetime prevalence 0.85 to 2.8 percent (1.25 to 3.5 percent in women and 0.42 to 2.0 percent in men). The rate in women is two to three times the rate in men, a far less lopsided ratio than in anorexia or bulimia, which is why a man such as Bhaskar is a typical case rather than a curiosity. The prevalence appears comparable across American ethnoracial groups and roughly similar across high-income countries (12-month figures from 0.1 to 1.2 percent); data from low- and middle-income countries are fewer, but in some regions of Latin America the rate seems at least as high as in the United States and Europe. One finding hints at the role of environment: Mexican Americans in the United States have a higher prevalence than their counterparts in Mexico. Presentations differ too: in the United States, Black patients may report less distress about their binges and arrive for treatment bingeing more often than White patients.

Little is known about how the disorder develops. Both binge eating and loss-of-control eating without an objectively large amount occur in children, and they are associated with increased body fat, weight gain and psychological symptoms; binge eating is common in adolescent and college samples, and loss-of-control eating may be a prodromal phase of an eating disorder for some. The order of events differs from bulimia. In bulimia nervosa dysfunctional dieting usually comes first and the binges follow; in many people with binge-eating disorder the binges come first and the dieting follows. Barlow and Durand cite a finding that about half of people with BED dieted before they began to binge and half binged first (Abbott and colleagues, 1998), and another study found that those who binged first were more severely affected and more likely to have additional disorders (Spurrell and colleagues, 1997). BED usually begins in adolescence or young adulthood but can begin later, and people who seek treatment for it are usually older than those who seek treatment for anorexia or bulimia.

The course is more hopeful than for the other eating disorders. Remission rates, both without treatment and after it, are higher than in anorexia or bulimia, although some people follow a persistent, relapsing course comparable to bulimia's, and crossover to the other eating disorders is uncommon. Christopher Fairburn and his colleagues (2000) identified 48 people with BED in the community and followed 40 of them for 5 years. Only 18 percent still met the full criteria at the end, a good prognosis; but over the same period the proportion who were obese rose from 21 to 39 percent. Binge eating that remits can still leave its mark on the body. About a third of people with BED binge to relieve "bad moods", and Carlos Grilo and his colleagues (2001) found that this group is more psychologically disturbed than the two-thirds who do not use bingeing to regulate mood. Suicidal ideation has been reported in about 25 percent of people with the disorder. Binge-eating disorder runs in families, which may reflect additive genetic influences. Its psychiatric comorbidity is comparable to that of bulimia and anorexia, the commonest companions being major depressive disorder and alcohol use disorder, and that comorbidity is linked to the severity of the binge eating, not to the degree of obesity. The disorder is also associated with social role problems, poorer health-related quality of life, more medical illness and death, and heavier use of health care than in people of the same BMI without it.

Four conditions must be distinguished. Bulimia nervosa shares the binges, but people with binge-eating disorder have no recurrent compensatory behaviour and do not usually show the marked, sustained dietary restriction between binges that characterises bulimia (though they may report frequent attempts to diet); BED also responds better to treatment. Obesity differs in the ways just described. In bipolar and depressive disorders, increased appetite and weight gain appear in the criteria for a major depressive episode and in the atypical features specifier (Book 3 Section 5.3.8); overeating in depression may or may not involve loss of control, and if the full criteria for both disorders are met, both diagnoses are given. Binge eating also occurs in bipolar disorder, with the same rule. And binge eating is one of the impulsive behaviours in the criteria for borderline personality disorder; again, if both sets of criteria are met, both are diagnosed.

Exam trap

Frequency for binge-eating disorder: binges on average at least once a week for 3 months, exactly the same threshold as bulimia nervosa. "Once weekly for 6 months" mixes in the old DSM-IV provisional period (which was 6 months, with binges on at least 2 days a week); "twice monthly for 1 month" and "once daily for a year" match no edition.

Diagnosis (practice mock): binges at least weekly for 3 months, no compensatory behaviour and marked distress = binge-eating disorder. Bulimia nervosa needs compensatory behaviour; anorexia nervosa, binge-eating/purging type, needs significantly low weight; avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder (ARFID, Section 8.2) is a disorder of eating too little, without binges and without concern about shape or weight. Severity in BED counts binges; in bulimia it counts compensatory episodes. And BED is not obesity: most obese people do not binge.

Anorexia, bulimia and binge-eating disorder compared

You now have all three of the main eating disorders in view, and the papers love to test them against each other. The quickest way to keep them apart is to notice that they are defined by different combinations of the same few building blocks: body weight, binges, compensatory behaviour and restriction. Table 8.11 lines them up feature by feature, using the figures from Sections 8.3.7, 8.4.4 and 8.4.5.

Table 8.11 The three main eating disorders side by side (DSM-5-TR; prevalence from American community studies).
FeatureAnorexia nervosaBulimia nervosaBinge-eating disorder
Body weightSignificantly low (the defining feature)Usually normal or overweight (adult BMI 18.5 to under 30)Normal, overweight or obese
Binge eatingOnly in the binge-eating/purging typeRequired, at least weekly for 3 monthsRequired, at least weekly for 3 months
Compensatory behaviourPurging in the binge-eating/purging type; fasting and exercise commonRequired, at least weekly for 3 monthsAbsent by definition
Between bingesRestriction is the core of the disorderRestriction and "diet" foodsNo sustained restriction, though diets are often tried
Shape and weightFear of fatness; disturbed body imageSelf-worth unduly based on shape and weightOften overvalued, but not required
Typical traits (Section 8.5.3)Perfectionistic, obsessional, over-controlledImpulsive, emotionally unstableAbout a third binge to relieve bad moods
Sex ratioFar commoner in women90 to 95 percent of patients are womenWomen two to three times men
OnsetAdolescence or young adulthoodAdolescence or young adulthoodAdolescence onwards, also later adulthood
12-month prevalence0.0 to 0.05 percent0.14 to 0.3 percent0.44 to 1.2 percent
Lifetime prevalence0.60 to 0.80 percent0.28 to 1.0 percent0.85 to 2.8 percent
Crude mortality and main dangerAbout 5 percent per decade: starvation, arrhythmia, suicideNearly 2 percent per decade: electrolyte loss, arrhythmia, suicideRaised medical risk, weight gain and obesity
OutcomeMost remit within 5 years of presentationOften chronic or intermittent for yearsHighest remission rate of the three

In practice the clinician asks three questions in a fixed order, and Figure 8.8 draws them as a decision tree. The order is not arbitrary. Weight is asked first because significantly low weight is the most dangerous feature, determines the treatment, and by DSM-5-TR's rules takes precedence: a person at a significantly low weight who binges and purges receives the diagnosis anorexia nervosa, binge-eating/purging type, and not bulimia nervosa. Only if weight is not significantly low does the clinician ask about binges, and only if there are binges does the question of compensation arise. Because the three disorders are defined in this nested way, DSM-5-TR assigns only one of them to a single episode of illness, although, as you saw in Section 8.4.4, a person may move from one to another over the years.

So much for the differences. What do the disorders share? A question asked at RML in 2020 lists three candidates, and all three are correct. The first is a distorted body image. People with anorexia and people with bulimia both tend to see themselves as heavier and less attractive than they are, and both experience their bodies through an overvalued conviction that they are, or are about to become, too fat (the overvalued idea is taught in Book 3 Section 2.5.6, the body image disturbance of anorexia in Section 2.10.3). In anorexia the distortion is often dramatic, an emaciated girl who pinches the skin of her thigh and calls it fat; in bulimia it shows as Criterion D, a self-worth that rises and falls with the scale. The second is an intense fear of being fat, which is written into Criterion B of anorexia and which, as DSM-5-TR notes, people with bulimia may share to the same degree.

The third shared feature, eating regulated by psychological rather than physiological processes, is the one students find hardest to put into words, so it is worth unpacking. In a healthy person, eating is governed mostly by the body: you begin when you feel hungry and stop when you feel full (the physiology is taught in Book 1 Section 10.9). In the eating disorders those signals lose their authority. Eating is governed instead by rules ("nothing after six", "never more than 800 kilocalories", "if I have eaten one biscuit the day is ruined anyway"), by fear of weight gain, by mood ("I binge when I am sad, not when I am hungry") and by judgements of self-worth. A woman with anorexia may be ravenously hungry and still refuse food because her rules forbid it; Jasleen eats two packets of biscuits without any hunger at all because she is lonely and tense; both eat by the calorie count, the clock or the emotion, not by the stomach. Hilde Bruch went further and argued that many patients cannot reliably perceive hunger and fullness in the first place (Section 8.5.2), and Christopher Fairburn's cognitive model makes this psychological regulation its centrepiece (Section 8.5.4).

These shared features raise a deeper question: are anorexia and bulimia really two disorders? Pinel and Barnes answer that it depends on who is asking. To a physician they are clearly different, because starvation and repeated bingeing and purging produce different medical problems needing different treatment (Section 8.4.3). To many scientists they look more like variants of a single disorder. Both begin with an obsession with body image and slimness and with strict dieting; the person with bulimia is simply less able to hold her appetite down, and so enters a cycle of starvation, bingeing and purging. Both groups of patients have distorted body images. And in practice many patients straddle the two diagnoses, and many flip between them as their circumstances change, just as the crossover figures in Section 8.4.4 show. Fairburn's transdiagnostic theory (Section 8.5.4) is the most influential statement of the "one disorder" view.

Exam trap

What separates anorexia from bulimia? Significantly low body weight in anorexia. The distractors each claim a difference that does not exist: body image disturbance is present in both (so "absence of body image disturbance in bulimia" is false); compensatory behaviour occurs in both (so "compensatory behaviours only in anorexia" is false); and purging occurs in anorexia's binge-eating/purging type (so "absence of purging in anorexia" is false).

What do anorexia and bulimia share? A distorted body image, an intense fear of being fat, and eating regulated by psychological rather than physiological processes: all of the above.

Some widely circulated exam notes say that people with bulimia "don't have a distorted view of being overweight" and are "not secretive about the problem". Both statements are wrong. Undue influence of shape and weight on self-evaluation is a required criterion of bulimia nervosa (Criterion D), and DSM-5-TR states that binge eating usually occurs in secrecy, by people who are ashamed of it and try to conceal it.

Other specified and unspecified feeding or eating disorder

Many people who are clearly ill with an eating disorder do not fit any of the full patterns. Under DSM-IV they were placed in a residual category, eating disorder not otherwise specified (EDNOS), which grew until it was the commonest eating-disorder diagnosis in clinical practice and told the next clinician almost nothing (Book 3 Section 1.8.4 tells that story). DSM-5 replaced it with two categories. The first, other specified feeding or eating disorder (OSFED; ICD-10-CM F50.89), is used when symptoms characteristic of a feeding or eating disorder cause clinically significant distress or impairment but do not meet the full criteria for any disorder in the class, and the clinician chooses to record the reason, for example "other specified feeding or eating disorder, bulimia nervosa of low frequency". DSM-5-TR offers five examples, summarised in Table 8.12.

The first example is atypical anorexia nervosa: every criterion for anorexia nervosa is met except that, despite significant weight loss, the person's weight is still within or above the normal range. Gayatri, a 16-year-old in Chennai, weighed 72 kilograms when a cousin's wedding photographs started her dieting. Eight months later she weighs 54 kilograms, a BMI of about 21 for her height, squarely normal. But she eats one meal a day, runs every morning, is terrified of regaining a single kilogram, and her periods have stopped. When she fainted at school her resting pulse was 44 beats a minute. Gayatri's case carries the most important lesson of this category: DSM-5-TR warns that people with atypical anorexia may have many of the physiological complications of anorexia nervosa. A normal BMI does not mean a safe body, because what the body reacts to is the starvation and the speed of the loss, not the final number.

The second and third examples are bulimia nervosa of low frequency or limited duration, in which all the criteria for bulimia are met except that the binges and compensatory behaviour occur, on average, less than once a week, or have lasted less than 3 months, and binge-eating disorder of low frequency or limited duration, the same idea applied to binge-eating disorder. These two exist because a frequency threshold is a convenient line, not a law of nature; a woman who binges and vomits every ten days is ill.

The fourth example is purging disorder: recurrent purging to influence weight or shape (self-induced vomiting, or misuse of laxatives, diuretics or other medications) without binge eating. Radhika, 23, a software engineer in Pune, makes herself sick after ordinary meals four or five times a week, even after a lunch of two idlis, because she "cannot bear the feeling of food inside" and fears it will show on her stomach. She never binges, and her weight is normal. The marks of purging (Section 8.4.3) can be just as severe as in bulimia. The American psychologist Pamela K. Keel and her colleagues described the syndrome in a 2005 paper whose title, "Purging disorder: an ominous variant of bulimia nervosa?", deliberately echoed Gerald Russell's 1979 title for bulimia.

The fifth is night eating syndrome: recurrent episodes of night eating, either eating after waking from sleep or eating excessively after the evening meal, with awareness and recall of the eating. The night eating is not better explained by external influences such as a change in the person's sleep-wake schedule (shift work, for example) or by local social norms (a household that always eats dinner at eleven), and it causes significant distress or impairment. It must not be better explained by binge-eating disorder or another mental disorder, by substance use, by a medical condition or by medication. Albert Stunkard first described the syndrome in 1955 (Section 8.1.6). Barlow and Durand describe the typical pattern: the person consumes a third or more of the day's food after the evening meal, gets out of bed at least once in the night for a high-calorie snack, and is not hungry in the morning, so skips breakfast; the night eating is not a binge, and purging is rare. It is strongly linked with obesity, found in 6 to 16 percent of obese people seeking weight-loss treatment and in as many as 55 percent of those with extreme obesity seeking bariatric surgery. The phrase "with awareness and recall" is the key to a classic confusion. In sleep-related eating disorder, a parasomnia taught in Section 8.10.2, the person eats while partly asleep, sometimes odd or even inedible food, with impaired awareness and little or no memory next morning. The night eater is awake and remembers; the sleep-related eater is not and does not.

The second residual category, unspecified feeding or eating disorder (F50.9), is used when the clinician chooses not to give the reason the criteria are not met, or when there is not enough information for a more specific diagnosis, as in an emergency department. It is a holding diagnosis, not a place to stay.

Table 8.12 DSM-5-TR's examples of other specified feeding or eating disorder, and what each lacks.
PresentationWhat is presentWhat is missing from the full diagnosis
Atypical anorexia nervosaAll anorexia criteria; significant weight loss; often its medical complicationsWeight is within or above the normal range
Bulimia nervosa of low frequency or limited durationAll bulimia criteriaLess than once a week, or less than 3 months
Binge-eating disorder of low frequency or limited durationAll binge-eating disorder criteriaLess than once a week, or less than 3 months
Purging disorderRecurrent purging to influence weight or shapeNo binge eating
Night eating syndromeEating after waking, or excess after the evening meal, with awareness and recall; distressNot binges; not explained by the sleep-wake schedule, local custom or another disorder
Unspecified (F50.9)Eating-disorder symptoms with distress or impairmentReason not recorded, or information insufficient

ICD-10 handled the same territory with its own residual codes, and their names can mislead you if you assume they mean what DSM-5-TR means. ICD-10's atypical anorexia nervosa (F50.1) is a disorder that resembles anorexia but in which a key feature, such as amenorrhoea or a marked dread of being fat, is absent in spite of marked weight loss and weight-reducing behaviour; it is defined by a missing symptom, whereas DSM-5-TR's atypical anorexia is defined by a normal weight. ICD-10's atypical bulimia nervosa (F50.3) covers recurrent overeating and purging without significant weight change, or bulimic behaviour without the typical overconcern with shape and weight. Overeating associated with other psychological disturbances (F50.4, "psychogenic overeating") is overeating set off by stressful events such as bereavement, an accident or childbirth, with obesity itself excluded, and vomiting associated with other psychological disturbances (F50.5, "psychogenic vomiting") is repeated vomiting occurring in dissociative disorders or in hypochondriacal disorder. Pica in adults and psychogenic loss of appetite fall under F50.8, other eating disorders.

Two popular labels deserve a sentence each, mainly so that you recognise them as unofficial. The term orthorexia nervosa, from the Greek orthos, right, and orexis, appetite, was coined by the American physician Steven Bratman in a 1997 article in Yoga Journal for an obsession with eating only "pure" or healthy food, which can become so restrictive that it causes malnutrition and social isolation; it is not a diagnosis in DSM-5-TR or ICD-11. "Diabulimia" is a media term for deliberately omitting or reducing insulin in type 1 diabetes in order to lose weight. DSM-5-TR does not use the word, but it does list insulin omission as a compensatory method in bulimia and as a weight-control behaviour in anorexia; it is one of the most dangerous of all, because it combines the risks of an eating disorder with uncontrolled diabetes.

Exam trap

In DSM-5-TR, atypical anorexia nervosa means every anorexia criterion is met except low weight: weight remains within or above the normal range despite significant loss, and the medical complications of starvation may still occur. In ICD-10 (F50.1), "atypical anorexia" means a key symptom is missing, such as amenorrhoea or dread of fatness. Purging disorder = purging without binges; night eating syndrome = night eating with awareness and recall, unlike the parasomnia of sleep-related eating. OSFED replaced DSM-IV's EDNOS. Orthorexia nervosa (Bratman, 1997) is not an official diagnosis.

8.5 Why Eating Disorders Develop and How They Are TreatedLower yield · 1 Q

Two young women share a hostel room. Both go on a diet in the same month, before the same family wedding. One loses three kilograms, enjoys the wedding, and forgets about the diet by the following week. The other is bingeing and vomiting a year later. Why? No single answer exists, and the honest summary of half a century of research is that eating disorders arise where several kinds of risk meet: a culture that prizes thinness, a diet that sets the body's defences in motion, a family in which weight is talked about, a personality that turns rules into commandments, a way of thinking in which self-worth is measured in kilograms, and a set of genes that makes all of this more likely to take hold. This section takes those levels one at a time, from the outside in, and then turns to treatment: first the medical management and the drugs, which are taught here in full, and then the psychological treatments, which are named here with their evidence and taught in Book 2. The biopsychosocial model and its grid of predisposing, precipitating, perpetuating and protective factors, already applied to a case of bulimia, are in Book 3 Section 1.3.5. Keep the chapter's opening question in mind as you read: why did the starving men of Minnesota long for food, while a girl with anorexia refuses the food in front of her?

Culture, the thin ideal and the diet

Barlow and Durand call anorexia and particularly bulimia "the most culturally specific psychological disorders yet identified", and their argument starts with a simple observation. For many young women in competitive settings, self-worth, happiness and success are judged largely by body measurements and the percentage of body fat, factors that have little or no relation to happiness and success in the long run. The cultural imperative for thinness leads directly to dieting, which they describe as "the first dangerous step down the slippery slope" to anorexia and bulimia. The rest of this subsection asks how good the evidence is for each link in that chain: culture, then internalisation, then the diet.

Start with the message. Michael P. Levine and Linda Smolak (1996) wrote of the "glorification of slenderness" in magazines and on television, where most women are thinner than the average woman in the audience; overweight men appear among television characters two to five times as often as overweight women, so the message to be thin is aimed squarely at women. When Shelly Grabe, L. Monique Ward and Janet Shibley Hyde (2008) pooled 77 studies, they found a strong relationship between exposure to media images of the thin-ideal body and body image concerns in women. But exposure is not destiny: almost every young woman sees these images, and only a few develop an eating disorder. J. Kevin Thompson and Eric Stice (2001) found that the risk depends on how far a woman "buys in" to the message, a process called thin-ideal internalisation: adopting the culture's ideal body as one's own standard and judging oneself against it. DSM-5-TR builds this into its account of bulimia: internalisation of a thin body ideal raises the risk of weight concerns, which in turn raise the risk of bulimia nervosa.

How far internalisation can distort a woman's sense of her own body was shown neatly by April Fallon and Paul Rozin (1985). They showed American undergraduates a row of figure drawings ranging from very thin to very heavy and asked each student to mark the figure closest to their current shape, their ideal shape, and the shape they thought the opposite sex would find most attractive. The men's three marks came out roughly the same: they were, on average, content with their bodies, and they rated their ideal as heavier than the figure women actually found most attractive in men. The women's marks were spread out in a revealing order. Their current figure was heavier than the one they thought men found most attractive, and that in turn was heavier than their own ideal. In other words, the women wanted to be thinner even than the figure they believed men preferred. The drive for thinness was not, or not only, about attracting men; it had become a standard in its own right.

Culture reaches a teenager mostly through her friends. In a study of 523 adolescent girls in 79 friendship cliques (Paxton and colleagues, 1999), members of a clique tended to share the same attitudes to body image, dietary restraint and extreme weight-loss behaviour. A later and more rigorous study (Rayner and colleagues, 2012) suggested that the friendships did not cause the attitudes so much as reflect them: girls tend to choose friends who already think as they do. Either way, the social network maintains the attitudes, and treatment has to reckon with it.

If thinness pressure matters, eating disorders should be commonest where the pressure is most intense, and they are. David M. Garner and his colleagues (1987) followed girls aged 11 to 14 at a ballet school and estimated, conservatively, that at least 25 percent of them developed an eating disorder during the 2 years of the study. Similar results have been found among gymnasts and other female athletes, and DSM-5-TR lists modelling and elite athletics among the occupations and pastimes that raise the risk of anorexia. Men face a different ideal. When Harrison G. Pope and his colleagues (2000) measured college-age men in Austria, France and the United States, the men in all three countries chose an ideal body about 28 pounds (some 13 kilograms) more muscular than their own and believed that women would prefer a body about 30 pounds more muscular, whereas in a pilot study most women preferred an ordinary male body. In its extreme form this drive becomes muscle dysmorphia, the conviction that one is too small, which Pope and his colleagues once called "reverse anorexia nervosa" (Olivardia, Pope and Hudson, 2000, describe it in weight lifters) and which is now a form of body dysmorphic disorder (Book 3 Section 6.6.2).

Key study: television comes to Fiji (Becker and colleagues, 2002)

Question. Does exposure to Western television change how girls eat? Most media studies are correlational: girls who watch more television also differ in other ways. A natural experiment was needed. Method. The American psychiatrist and anthropologist Anne E. Becker had studied Fiji, where traditional culture valued a robust body and hearty eating. Television arrived in the province of Nadroga in 1995, and Becker's team surveyed ethnic Fijian schoolgirls with the 26-item Eating Attitudes Test (EAT-26, a self-report questionnaire described in the Assessment book) and interviews, once in 1995, shortly after the broadcasts began (63 girls), and again in 1998 (a comparable sample of 65 girls). Result. The proportion scoring above the test's cut-off for disordered eating attitudes rose from 12.7 percent to 29.2 percent, and self-induced vomiting to control weight went from none of the girls in 1995 to 11.3 percent in 1998. In interviews the girls described wanting to lose weight to look like the characters they saw on television. Conclusion. The authors concluded that television had a negative impact on eating attitudes and behaviour in a population with no previous exposure to it. Criticism. The two samples were different girls, so the study is cross-sectional rather than truly longitudinal; television arrived along with many other economic and social changes; the EAT-26 measures attitudes, not diagnoses; and self-reported binge eating did not change. Still, few studies show a culture's ideal moving into a population so quickly.

If eating disorders were simply the product of Western media, they should look the same wherever that media has spread, and in their classical form they should be rare elsewhere. For a long time they did seem rare outside the West, and anorexia nervosa was often called a Western culture-bound syndrome (the concept and its critics are discussed in Book 3 Section 1.10.2). The evidence from Asia has complicated that picture in an instructive way. Sing Lee and his colleagues (1993) studied 70 Chinese patients with anorexia nervosa in Hong Kong. In most respects they resembled Western patients, but 41 of them (58.6 percent) never showed any fear of fatness at any stage of their illness.
