They explained their refusal to eat in other ways: a bloated feeling in the upper abdomen (31.4 percent), having no appetite or hunger (15.7 percent), or simply saying they ate less (12.9 percent). These non-fat-phobic patients had been slimmer before they fell ill and were less likely to binge. Lee argued that because non-fat-phobic anorexia shows no culturally peculiar features, the disorder is not strictly a Western culture-bound syndrome; the self-starvation is the constant, and the explanation the patient gives for it is what culture shapes. DSM-5-TR now says the same thing in general terms: the absence of an expressed intense fear of weight gain, sometimes called "fat phobia", appears to be relatively more common in Asian populations, where the reason given for restricting food is usually a more culturally sanctioned complaint such as stomach discomfort. Because Criterion B of anorexia can be met by persistent behaviour that interferes with weight gain, and DSM-5-TR allows the clinician to infer it from the history and the course when a patient denies any fear, such patients can still receive the diagnosis.

Indian context

Indian reports echo Lee's findings. S. K. Khandelwal, Pratap Sharan and Shekhar Saxena (1995), writing from New Delhi in the International Journal of Social Psychiatry, described five young women who presented chiefly with refusal to eat, persistent vomiting, marked weight loss, amenorrhoea and other bodily complaints, but without the overactivity or body image disturbance typical of anorexia nervosa. All were eventually diagnosed and treated as having an eating disorder, but they posed real diagnostic difficulty, and the authors discussed the sociocultural reasons for the apparent rarity and atypical presentation of anorexia in India. The practical lesson for an Indian clinic: a thin young woman who says she does not eat because of "gas" or a heavy stomach, and whose investigations are normal, may still have anorexia nervosa. Systematic Indian prevalence data remain scarce.

DSM-5-TR records that anorexia appears to be increasing in many countries of the Global South, including in Asia and the Middle East, and that bulimia appears to be gradually increasing in many low- and middle-income countries. The usual interpretation, and it is an interpretation rather than an established fact, is that urbanisation, global media, the spread of the thin ideal and the changing roles and pressures of young women are carrying the risks described in this subsection to new places.

Now the diet itself, and the men of Minnesota. Remember what the opening of this chapter described. Thirty-six healthy, psychologically stable young men, with no wish to be thin and no eating disorder, ate about half their usual food for six months and lost about a quarter of their body weight. They became preoccupied with food, talking, reading and daydreaming about it, collecting recipes and hoarding food-related things; they grew depressed, irritable and withdrawn; and when food was given back, many ate enormous meals and kept eating long past fullness. Every one of those features is found in anorexia and bulimia. The lesson is a sobering one: a great deal of what looks like the psychology of an eating disorder is the psychology of starvation, and dieting can cause symptoms, not merely accompany them. Book 1 Section 10.9 explains why. The body regulates its weight loosely around a settling point, Pinel's "leaky barrel", and when intake falls, hunger rises and the body economises, so that a diet is resisted from within. C. Peter Herman and Janet Polivy showed how fragile deliberate restraint is: in Herman and Deborah Mack's milkshake study (1975), chronic dieters who had been made to drink a milkshake went on to eat more ice cream than dieters who had not, as if, once the diet was broken, there was no point in holding back (Book 1 Section 10.9.4).

Put those findings together and you have the engine of the binge-purge cycle. A person who restricts her eating by rigid rules, rather than by hunger, is fighting her own physiology. Sooner or later a rule is broken: a slice of cake at a colleague's farewell. In the all-or-nothing thinking typical of dieters, the broken rule means the day is already ruined ("what the hell, I may as well eat everything"), and a small lapse becomes a full binge. This is the same mechanism that relapse researchers call the abstinence violation effect (Chapter 10). Terrified of the calories, she vomits or fasts, which seems to cancel the damage and frees her to binge again, and she resolves to diet even more strictly tomorrow, which makes the next binge more likely. DSM-5-TR's statement that the binge eating of bulimia frequently begins during or after a diet is exactly what this account predicts. The full cognitive version of the cycle is in Section 8.5.4.

Does dieting actually predict eating disorders, or do people who are going to develop them simply diet first? Only a prospective study can tell, and several have been done. In a survey of 15-year-old London schoolgirls, George C. Patton and his colleagues (1990) found that for most girls dieting was a benign and passing practice, but that dieters were 8 times more likely than non-dieters to have become cases of eating disorder at a 12-month follow-up, and dieters made up most of the new cases. Patton's group later ran a larger cohort study in Victoria, Australia, following students aged 14 to 15 from 44 secondary schools for 3 years with six waves of assessment (1999). Girls who dieted at a severe level were 18 times more likely to develop an eating disorder (in this study a partial syndrome, meeting two of the DSM-IV criteria for anorexia or bulimia) than girls who did not diet, and moderate dieters 5 times more likely; psychiatric morbidity (symptoms such as anxiety and depression) predicted onset independently of dieting, students in the highest morbidity category having an almost sevenfold risk. After these factors were taken into account, body weight, exercise and sex no longer predicted a new eating disorder, and the authors concluded that dieting was the most important predictor; controlling weight by exercise carried less risk than restricting food. Dieting backfires on the scale too. Eric Stice and his colleagues (1999) followed 692 adolescent girls for 4 years and found that girls who dieted faced, in Barlow and Durand's words, a "more than 300% greater risk" of becoming obese than girls who did not. And among 2,992 young women who were dieting, Christopher Fairburn and his colleagues (2005) found that the 104 who went on to develop an eating disorder over the next 2 years had already shown warning signs: binge eating and purging, eating in secret, wishing for an empty stomach, preoccupation with food and fear of losing control over eating.

Keep the conclusion in proportion. Most people who diet never develop an eating disorder, so dieting is not a sufficient cause. But it is the commonest first step, it is the most powerful single predictor yet found, and it is the step that turns cultural pressure into a biological struggle. What decides who keeps falling is the subject of the rest of this section.

Exam trap

In Fallon and Rozin's study (1985) men's current, ideal and "most attractive" figures roughly coincided, while women rated their current figure as heavier than the figure they thought men found most attractive, which was heavier than their own ideal. Risk depends on internalising the thin ideal (Thompson and Stice, 2001), not on mere exposure. Garner and colleagues (1987): at least 25 percent of young ballet students developed eating disorders in 2 years. Patton and colleagues (1999): severe dieters were 18 times likelier to develop an eating disorder. Do not confuse Anne E. Becker (television in Fiji, 2002) with Carolyn Becker (peer-led prevention, Section 8.5.7). And the absence of fat phobia, commoner in Asia, does not rule out anorexia nervosa.

Families

For much of the twentieth century, clinicians who treated anorexia looked first at the family, and the most influential family theory came from Salvador Minuchin (1921 to 2017) and his colleagues Bernice Rosman and Lester Baker at the Philadelphia Child Guidance Clinic. They proposed that severe "psychosomatic" illness in a child, anorexia among them, arose when a physiologically vulnerable child was drawn into her parents' unresolved conflict inside a family with four characteristics: enmeshment (members over-involved in one another's lives, with blurred boundaries between them), overprotectiveness, rigidity, and a failure ever to resolve conflict. The therapy built on this model, including the famous family lunch session in which the therapist asks the parents to get their daughter to eat, is taught in Book 2 Section 7.4.6. So is the evidence: in Psychosomatic Families (1978) Minuchin, Rosman and Baker reported a successful outcome for about 86 percent of 53 patients, but the series had no control group, mixed family therapy with an inpatient behavioural programme, and consisted mostly of young adolescents with short illnesses, the patients most likely to recover anyway. Later research did not find a single "anorexic family" type, and the idea that families cause eating disorders has been abandoned.

Something real lay behind the theory, however, and this chapter's task is to separate the family as a source of risk from the family as a culprit. Barlow and Durand summarise the older clinical picture: the "typical" family of a patient with anorexia was described as successful, hard-driving, concerned with appearances and eager to keep the peace, so that conflicts and negative feelings were denied rather than discussed. When Kathleen Pike and Judith Rodin (1991) compared the families of girls with disordered eating with control families, they did find differences, and later studies filled in the picture. The mothers seemed to act as "society's messengers", wanting their daughters to be thin; they were likely to be dieting themselves, and they were more perfectionistic and less satisfied with their families than comparison mothers. Clinicians also regularly hear patients describe weight talk at the dinner table, teasing about weight by parents and siblings, and critical comments about their bodies; criticism is part of the wider concept of high expressed emotion, taught in Book 2 Section 7.2. Such reports fit the idea that families can transmit the thin ideal, but they are mostly retrospective, collected after the illness began, and so they cannot show cause.

That last point matters because the arrow can point the other way. Once anorexia takes hold, family relationships often deteriorate quickly. Barlow and Durand describe educated, knowledgeable parents, psychologists and psychiatrists among them, who were driven in moments of desperate frustration to slap or hit a daughter who would not put food in her mouth, and who then suffered great guilt. A family observed at that stage will look conflicted, controlling and over-involved, but the starving child may have made it so. The current consensus was stated by the Academy for Eating Disorders in 2010: family factors can play a role in the genesis and maintenance of eating disorders, but current knowledge refutes the idea that they are either the exclusive or even the primary mechanisms that underlie risk.

Two kinds of family influence are better established. The first is familial aggregation, the clustering of a disorder within families. Relatives of patients with eating disorders are 4 to 5 times more likely than the general population to develop one themselves, and the risk is higher for female relatives of patients with anorexia (Michael Strober and colleagues, 2000, cited by Barlow and Durand). But a family shares both genes and environment, and aggregation alone cannot say which is at work; twin studies are needed for that (Section 8.5.5). The second is adversity: DSM-5-TR lists childhood sexual or physical abuse as an environmental risk factor for bulimia nervosa.

The most influential developmental theory of anorexia came from a psychiatrist who knew the patients intimately. Hilde Bruch (1904 to 1984), German-born and working in the United States, treated young women with eating disorders for decades and set out her views in Eating Disorders: Obesity, Anorexia Nervosa, and the Person Within (1973) and in the more popular The Golden Cage: The Enigma of Anorexia Nervosa (1978), whose title captures how her privileged, well-cared-for patients felt trapped. In 1962 she had described three areas of disordered functioning in anorexia: a disturbance of the body image of delusional proportions (Book 3 Section 2.10.3), a disturbance in the accurate perception and interpretation of bodily states such as hunger, and an all-pervasive, paralysing sense of ineffectiveness. Her theory explained the second and third by early experience. Effective parents read an infant's signals accurately: they feed her when she is hungry and comfort her when she is frightened. Ineffective parents impose their own interpretation, feeding the child when she is anxious, comforting her when she is hungry, or deciding for her when she is hungry, cold or tired. Bruch described mothers who proudly recalled that they had always anticipated their child's needs and never allowed her to feel hungry. A child raised in this way, Bruch argued, never learns to recognise and trust her own internal states, so she relies on others to tell her what she feels and needs. She may look like a model child, compliant and high-achieving, but she lacks a sense of being in charge of her own life. When adolescence demands independence she cannot supply it, and she seizes control of the one thing that is unmistakably hers: her body, its size and what goes into it.

Bruch's theory has partial support. People with eating disorders do seem to perceive internal cues, including hunger, fullness and emotions, less accurately than others (this is measured as interoceptive awareness, a scale of the Eating Disorder Inventory, described in the Assessment book), and parents of teenagers with eating disorders do tend to define their children's needs for them. But her evidence was clinical and retrospective, drawn from mothers' recollections after the illness had begun; poor interoceptive awareness could as easily be a result of starvation and bingeing as a cause; and, like the psychosomatic family, the theory lent itself to blaming mothers. What has lasted is her portrait of the patient: the conscientious, compliant girl who feels ineffective and finds in self-starvation a sense of control. That portrait leads straight to the question of personality.

Clinical link

Modern family-based treatment for adolescent anorexia (Section 8.5.7; Book 2 Section 7.7.7) begins by telling the parents, plainly, that they did not cause the illness. The therapist is agnostic about cause and treats the family as the most powerful resource the patient has, putting the parents in charge of refeeding. The shift from "the family as cause" to "the family as treatment" is one of the clearest examples in psychiatry of research overturning a theory that clinicians once took for granted, and of how much harm guilt can do to the people who are best placed to help.

Exam trap

The psychosomatic family (Minuchin, Rosman and Baker, Psychosomatic Families, 1978) = enmeshment, overprotectiveness, rigidity and lack of conflict resolution, with the child drawn into parental conflict; its 86 percent success claim came from an uncontrolled series. Hilde Bruch wrote The Golden Cage (1978) and described a paralysing sense of ineffectiveness and a failure to perceive hunger accurately; do not credit the psychosomatic family to her. Relatives of patients are 4 to 5 times more likely to develop an eating disorder, but familial aggregation does not separate genes from environment, and families are no longer thought to cause eating disorders. Childhood sexual or physical abuse is a DSM-5-TR risk factor for bulimia.

Personality: the over-controlled and the impulsive

Clinicians who have treated both anorexia and bulimia often say that the two kinds of patient feel different in the room, and the research bears them out. The personality most consistently linked with anorexia nervosa is over-controlled. Its central trait is perfectionism, the pursuit of extremely high standards combined with harsh self-criticism when they are not met, and with it come obsessionality, rigidity, anxiety and caution. In studies using Robert Cloninger's temperament measures, people with anorexia typically score high on harm avoidance, a tendency to worry, to fear uncertainty and to avoid risk. DSM-5-TR names the temperamental risks directly: people who develop anxiety disorders or show obsessional traits in childhood are at increased risk of anorexia nervosa. Its description of the associated features of anorexia (Section 8.3.6) reads like the same portrait seen from inside the illness: feelings of ineffectiveness, a strong desire to control one's environment, inflexible thinking, limited social spontaneity and overly restrained emotional expression. Think of the student who rewrites her notes three times, ranks first in her class and still feels she is failing; then imagine those standards turned on her body.

Twin and family studies suggest that some of this is inherited. Tracey Wade and her colleagues (2008), in a study of 1,002 same-sex twins, found anorexia associated with a trait of perfectionism and a need for order that runs in families, and Kelly Klump and her colleagues (2001) pointed to perfectionism together with negative affect as the traits that might carry a genetic vulnerability. But is perfectionism a cause or only a companion? An experiment gave a partial answer.

Key study: making ordinary women perfectionistic for a day (Shafran, Lee, Payne and Fairburn, 2006)

Question. Does perfectionism itself push a person towards restricting food? Method. Roz Shafran and her colleagues asked women without eating disorders to pursue the highest possible standards in everything they did for the next 24 hours; a comparison group was told to do only the minimum. Eating was never mentioned in the instructions. Result. The women whose perfectionism had been raised ate fewer high-calorie foods, restricted their eating more and felt more regret after eating than the comparison women. Conclusion. Raising perfectionistic standards, even temporarily and in general, spills over into dietary restraint. Criticism. One day in healthy volunteers is a long way from a years-long illness, and other work shows that perfectionism alone is only weakly linked with developing an eating disorder: in a study by Kathleen Vohs and her colleagues (1999), perfectionism raised the risk mainly in women who also felt overweight and had low self-esteem. Perfectionism becomes a powerful engine when it is directed at the body.

Christopher Fairburn's group gave this combination a name. What they call clinical perfectionism (Shafran, Cooper and Fairburn, 2002) is the relentless pursuit of personally demanding standards in at least one highly valued area of life, with self-worth judged largely by success in meeting them, despite the costs. When the valued area is eating, shape and weight, clinical perfectionism and the core of an eating disorder become almost the same thing, which is why it appears as one of the four extra maintaining processes in Fairburn's model (Section 8.5.4).

Bulimia nervosa, and the binge-eating/purging type of anorexia, bring a different temperament into the room: impulsivity, mood instability and a tendency to act to relieve distress. DSM-5-TR notes that people with anorexia's binge-eating/purging type have higher rates of impulsivity and are more likely to misuse alcohol and other drugs than those with the restricting type. For bulimia it lists weight concerns, low self-esteem, depressive symptoms, social anxiety disorder and childhood generalised anxiety disorder as temperamental risks. Barlow and Durand summarise the suggestion that what is inherited may be emotional instability and poor impulse control, so that a person reacts strongly to stressful events and eats impulsively to relieve the distress. In a twin study, Wade, Bulik, Prescott and Kendler (2004) found that the shared traits of novelty seeking and emotional instability accounted for much of bulimia's overlap with anxiety and substance use disorders. At least a subgroup of patients cannot tolerate negative emotion, a trait called mood intolerance, and binge, vomit or exercise in order to change how they feel. The personality disorder most often found in bulimia is borderline personality disorder, whose criteria include binge eating as an impulsive behaviour (Chapter 11).

Personality does not map neatly onto diagnosis, however. Drew Westen and J. Harnden-Fischer (2001) asked 103 experienced psychiatrists and psychologists each to describe, with a detailed personality Q-sort, a patient they were treating for anorexia or bulimia. Cluster analysis sorted the patients into three personality types: a high-functioning/perfectionistic group, a constricted/overcontrolled group and an emotionally dysregulated/undercontrolled group. The types cut across the diagnoses: some patients with anorexic symptoms were high-functioning and self-critical while others were highly disturbed, constricted and avoidant; some with bulimic symptoms were high-functioning while others were impulsive and emotionally dysregulated. The personality type predicted eating symptoms, overall functioning, psychiatric hospitalisation and a history of sexual abuse better than the eating-disorder diagnosis alone. The general rule (over-controlled anorexia, under-controlled bulimia) is right as a rule, but individual patients vary.

Exam trap

"A patient with anorexia nervosa has traits of ..." (RML 2024): perfectionism, together with obsessionality, rigidity and anxiety, the over-controlled style. Impulsivity is the trait of bulimia nervosa and of anorexia's binge-eating/purging type, so a question about bulimic traits would key impulsivity. Avoidance is the core of avoidant personality disorder (social inhibition, feelings of inadequacy, hypersensitivity to criticism) and dependence of dependent personality disorder (an excessive need to be taken care of, with submissive, clinging behaviour), both in Chapter 11. Some patients with anorexia do have avoidant or dependent traits, but neither is the characteristic trait; perfectionism is.

Thinking about shape and weight: the cognitive model

Book 2 Section 5.6.7 lists Christopher Fairburn's model of the eating disorders in a single row of a table of cognitive models, as the belief "My worth depends on my shape and weight" with its strict dieting, binge-purge cycles and body checking. Here the same model is taught as what it was designed to be: an explanation of why eating disorders persist. Christopher G. Fairburn, a psychiatrist at Oxford who had developed the first cognitive behavioural treatment for bulimia in the 1980s, published it with Zafra Cooper and Roz Shafran in 2003 in Behaviour Research and Therapy, under the title "Cognitive behaviour therapy for eating disorders: a 'transdiagnostic' theory and treatment". The paper made two arguments. The first was that the existing cognitive behavioural theory of bulimia should be widened to include four additional maintaining mechanisms. The second was that the shared clinical features of bulimia, anorexia and the atypical eating disorders are kept going by the same psychological processes, so that one theory can explain them all. A theory that cuts across diagnoses in this way is called a transdiagnostic theory.

At the centre of the model is what Fairburn calls the core psychopathology: the over-evaluation of eating, shape and weight and their control. Most people judge their worth by how they are doing across many areas of life: their studies or work, their friendships and family, their sport, their music, their faith. If you drew a person's self-evaluation as a pie chart, it would have many slices. In the eating disorders one slice swallows the plate. The person judges herself largely, or even exclusively, by her shape and weight and by her ability to control them. Fairburn's insight was that almost every other feature of the disorders follows from this. It drives strict dieting, which in his account means not simply eating less but obeying many demanding and highly specific dietary rules. It drives body checking, the repeated weighing, pinching, measuring and scrutinising of the body in mirrors and shop windows, comparing it with other women's, and its opposite, body avoidance, refusing ever to be weighed or hiding under loose clothes. And it drives "feeling fat", a sensation that patients experience as a fact about their bodies but which often turns out to be a mislabelling of other states, such as sadness, boredom or bloating.

Now follow the cycle in Figure 8.9. Strict dietary rules are bound to be broken, both because the body fights the diet (Section 8.5.1) and because the rules are so demanding. When a rule is broken, the person reacts in an all-or-nothing way, and the lapse becomes a binge, often set off by an upsetting event or mood. The binge terrifies her, so she compensates by vomiting, laxatives or fasting. Vomiting feels like a safety net, and that is exactly what makes it dangerous: believing that she can undo a binge removes one of the brakes on bingeing. Each binge and purge also confirms her worst fear, that she cannot control her eating, and intensifies her concern about shape and weight, so she diets more strictly still, and the cycle turns again. In patients who restrict successfully and become underweight, a second loop operates. The effects of starvation itself, the preoccupation with food and the rigidity, low mood and social withdrawal that the men of Minnesota developed, strengthen the over-evaluation and the dieting, so that the low weight helps maintain the disorder that produced it.

Push a case through the model. Tanya is 19 and in her first year of an architecture course in Kolkata. Asked what she would need in order to feel good about herself, she says, without hesitating, "to be thin", and then, after a long pause, "I suppose my marks." Her rules are many and precise: no rice, nothing after seven in the evening, never more than 1,000 kilocalories, and if she breaks one the whole day is "spoilt". Every morning she measures her waist with a tailor's tape and pinches her stomach in the mirror. On a Friday after a harsh review of her design project she eats one samosa at the canteen; the day is "spoilt", so she buys and eats a packet of biscuits, a slab of chocolate and three more samosas, then vomits in the hostel toilet. That night she weighs herself, feels fat, and decides to eat nothing on Saturday. Each element of Figure 8.9 is visible: the over-evaluation, the rules, the checking, the lapse, the binge set off by a bad mood, the compensation, and the stricter diet that prepares the next binge. Tanya also shows one of the additional processes: she cannot bear being upset, and bingeing is the quickest way she knows to stop feeling.

The four additional processes were introduced because some patients do not get better with treatment aimed only at the core. In certain patients, Fairburn, Cooper and Shafran proposed, one or more of them interacts with the core and becomes an obstacle to change. Clinical perfectionism is the relentless pursuit of demanding standards with self-worth riding on them (Section 8.5.3); in eating disorders it is often directed both at eating and at other areas such as work. Core low self-esteem is an unconditional, pervasive negative view of oneself, so deep that the person cannot imagine change and pursues control over her shape as the one route to worth that seems open. Mood intolerance is an inability to cope with intense emotions, which the person tries to change by bingeing, vomiting, exercising or, in some, self-harm. Interpersonal difficulties, such as family tension or a social world that keeps the focus on appearance, keep the disorder going from outside. This is what the question about the "psychological regulation" of eating means (Section 8.4.6): in Fairburn's model, what a person eats is decided by rules, fears, moods and judgements of worth, not by hunger and fullness. Note also what the model is not. It is a theory of maintenance, of why the disorder persists once it has begun, rather than a theory of why it begins; that is why it fits so well with the risk factors of the earlier subsections, which explain onset. The treatment derived from it, enhanced cognitive behaviour therapy, is named in Section 8.5.7 and taught in Book 2.

Laboratory studies support the model's claim that small eating events have large cognitive effects in these patients. When McKenzie, Williamson and Cubic (1993) gave women a chocolate bar and a soft drink, women with bulimia judged their bodies to be larger afterwards, whereas the judgements of control women did not change. An older learning account, Rosen and Leitenberg's (1985) anxiety-reduction model, proposed that eating raises anxiety and that vomiting is reinforced because it relieves it; Barlow and Durand note that later evidence suggests that, in treating bulimia, countering the over-restriction of food and the attitudes to shape and weight matters more than reducing the anxiety of eating, which is the emphasis of Fairburn's model.

Fairburn's model explains why people with eating disorders keep dieting; it does not by itself explain the riddle with which this section began, why a starving girl with anorexia does not seem as hungry as a starving man in Minnesota. Pinel and Barnes offer a hypothesis from biopsychology. On the positive-incentive view of eating (Book 1 Section 10.9.1), we eat mainly because of the anticipated pleasure of food, and starvation normally raises the positive-incentive value of eating to enormous heights; for the Minnesota men, all food tasted good. Studies have found the incentive value of tastes to be lower in patients with anorexia than in healthy controls, and Pinel argues that the right comparison, with people starved to the same weight, would make the difference even more striking. Their explanation draws on research by Stephen Woods and his colleagues showing that meals disrupt the body's internal balance and have aversive physiological effects, and that these effects are much greater in people who have been eating little. The meals a person with anorexia does eat may therefore produce conditioned taste aversions (the learned avoidance of a food that has been followed by illness, taught in the General Psychology book), which progressively lower the incentive value of food and make eating less and less attractive. Pinel notes that many concentration camp survivors were made ill, and some were killed, by the food their liberators gave them, and that one clinical complication of feeding meals to famine victims is anorexia. If the hypothesis is right, it has a practical implication: severely undernourished patients should be fed small amounts at intervals through the day, not given meals. It is a hypothesis, untested as a treatment, but it is a good example of how a theory of normal eating can generate a new idea about a disorder.

Exam trap

Fairburn, Cooper and Shafran's transdiagnostic theory (2003) places the same core in anorexia, bulimia and the atypical disorders: the over-evaluation of eating, shape and weight and their control. The four additional maintaining processes, present only in some patients, are clinical perfectionism, core low self-esteem, mood intolerance and interpersonal difficulties; "body image distortion" and "family enmeshment" are not on the list. The model explains maintenance, not onset. The conditioned taste aversion account of anorexia is Pinel's hypothesis, not Fairburn's.

Genes, brain and hormones

Book 3 Section 1.4 used the eating disorders to teach the twin method, and its figures can simply be recalled here. In a large Swedish twin study, Cynthia Bulik and her colleagues (2006) estimated the heritability of anorexia nervosa at about 0.56. For bulimia, Kenneth Kendler and his colleagues (1991) found both twins affected in 23 percent of identical pairs but only 9 percent of fraternal pairs. The consensus, in Barlow and Durand's words, is that genetic make-up is "about half of the equation" among the causes of anorexia and bulimia, and binge-eating disorder also runs in families in a way that suggests additive genetic influence. Two cautions apply. No adoption studies of eating disorders had been reported at the time of Barlow and Durand's review, so strong sociocultural influences shared within families cannot be ruled out. And there is no agreement on what exactly is inherited. One suggestion is a temperament of emotional instability and poor impulse control, which makes a person react strongly to stress and eat impulsively to relieve it (the route to bulimia); another is perfectionism together with negative affect (the route to anorexia, Section 8.5.3).

Molecular genetics has begun to locate some of the genes. DSM-5-TR reports that genome-wide association studies have identified specific risk loci for anorexia nervosa, including loci shared with other psychiatric disorders and with metabolic traits such as insulin resistance and the blood lipid profile. The largest such study, by Hunna Watson and a large international consortium (2019, Nature Genetics), identified eight risk loci and concluded that anorexia has "metabo-psychiatric" origins: part of the risk lies in genes that shape the mind, and part in genes that shape the body's metabolism. That would help to explain one of anorexia's strangest features, the ability of some patients to hold their weight at a level that most people's bodies would fight with every hunger signal they possess.

Within the brain, research has concentrated on the hypothalamus, the regulator of hunger and satiety (its lateral and ventromedial regions and the classic "feeding" and "satiety" centres are taught in Book 1 Section 10.9.2), and on the neurotransmitters that pass through it: noradrenaline, dopamine and, above all, serotonin. Low serotonergic activity is associated with impulsivity in general and with binge eating in particular, which is why most of the drugs studied for eating disorders act on the serotonin system (Section 8.5.6). Anorexia may show the opposite pattern. Walter H. Kaye and his colleagues (1991) studied 17 patients who had recovered from anorexia and had kept a normal weight for a long time, so that starvation could not confuse the results, and found raised levels of the main serotonin metabolite (5-hydroxyindoleacetic acid) in their cerebrospinal fluid compared with 15 controls. They suggested that increased serotonin activity might be linked with the anxious, inhibited and obsessional traits that precede and outlast the illness. Functional imaging (functional magnetic resonance imaging and positron emission tomography) has described a range of abnormalities in anorexia, many suggesting abnormal processing of reward, which would fit Pinel's idea that food has lost its incentive value.

Every one of these brain findings runs into the same problem: cause or consequence? Starvation and repeated bingeing and purging change the brain. Starvation causes a loss of grey matter and hormonal disturbance that reverse when weight is restored (Section 8.3.5), and DSM-5-TR states plainly that it is unclear how far the imaging findings reflect malnutrition rather than a primary abnormality. The current consensus, as Barlow and Durand put it, is that neurobiological abnormalities do exist in people with eating disorders, but that they may be a result of semistarvation or the binge-purge cycle rather than a cause, although they may well help to maintain the disorder once it is established. Studies of recovered patients, like Kaye's, are the best available way around the problem. Two biological risks for bulimia are better established, because they come before the illness: DSM-5-TR lists childhood obesity and early pubertal maturation, both of which plunge a girl into a heavier, more adult body earlier than her peers, at an age when the thin ideal bites hardest.

How do all these levels fit together? Barlow and Durand's integrative model (their Figure 8.4) offers one answer. A person begins with a biological vulnerability, such as an unstable or excessive response to stress associated with impulsive eating, and perhaps a genetic disposition to a particular body build. Social influences, the cultural pressure to be thin and family pressures about appearance, combine with psychological influences, anxiety about appearance and how one is seen by others and a distorted body image, to produce a strong drive to restrict eating, usually by severe dieting. From there the paths divide. A small minority succeed in restricting, lose weight alarmingly and develop anorexia; the majority fail, binge, purge to reduce their anxiety, and so retain their weight in the binge-purge cycle of bulimia. What decides which path a person takes may be physiology at first (a disposition to be thinner) or personality (a tendency to over-control), and Barlow and Durand add that most people with anorexia do go on to binge and purge at some point. Table 8.13 gathers the risk factors of this section by level.

Table 8.13 Risk factors for the eating disorders, by level.

Exam trap

Heritability of anorexia nervosa is about 0.56 (Bulik and colleagues, 2006); for bulimia both twins are affected in 23 percent of identical and 9 percent of fraternal pairs (Kendler and colleagues, 1991); genes are "about half of the equation". Low serotonergic activity goes with impulsivity and binge eating (hence serotonergic drugs for bulimia); raised serotonin activity has been found in people recovered from anorexia (Kaye, 1991). Genome-wide studies link anorexia to metabolic as well as psychiatric loci. Most brain findings in eating disorders may be consequences of starvation or bingeing rather than causes. Childhood obesity and early puberty are risks for bulimia, not anorexia.

Medical management and physical treatments

Treatment of an eating disorder begins with a decision about where it should happen, and that decision is medical before it is psychological. Most people with bulimia nervosa, binge-eating disorder and the milder forms of anorexia are treated as outpatients. Day programmes, in which the patient spends the day in a unit, eats supervised meals there and goes home at night, are a middle course. Admission to hospital, to a specialist eating disorder unit or a medical ward, is reserved for those whose bodies or lives are in immediate danger. For anorexia nervosa, Barlow and Durand summarise the American Psychiatric Association's practice guidance: inpatient treatment is recommended if body weight is below 85 percent of the healthy weight for that individual, or if weight has been lost rapidly and the person continues to refuse food, because severe medical complications, particularly acute cardiac failure, can occur if weight is not restored. Clinicians also admit when the body shows signs of instability: a very slow heart rate, low or falling blood pressure, low body temperature, electrolyte disturbances such as a low potassium or phosphate, fainting, dehydration or changes on the electrocardiogram. A serious suicide risk and the failure of good outpatient treatment are reasons in their own right. People with bulimia rarely need admission, and then usually for dangerous electrolyte disturbance from purging, for suicide risk, or because purging is out of control.

In anorexia nervosa the first goal is to restore weight to at least the low-normal range. This is done mainly with ordinary food in supervised meals and snacks, supplemented if necessary with liquid nutrition; when a patient cannot or will not eat enough by mouth, food is given through a fine tube passed through the nose into the stomach (nasogastric feeding), and feeding into a vein is a last resort. The 2004 eating-disorders guideline of the UK's National Institute for Clinical Excellence (now the National Institute for Health and Care Excellence, NICE) set an aim of an average gain of 0.5 to 1 kilogram a week in hospital and about 0.5 kilogram a week as an outpatient, a figure based on clinical experience rather than trials; its 2017 guideline gives no weekly figure. Restoring weight is, surprisingly, the easier part of treatment: in a wide range of settings at least 85 percent of patients regain weight. It is also essential, because starvation itself changes the brain, and those changes reverse with weight restoration (Section 8.5.5). But it is not a cure. Initial weight gain is a poor predictor of long-term outcome (Hsu, 1988), and without attention to the patient's attitudes to shape and weight and to her life outside hospital, relapse is the rule.

The first two weeks of refeeding are the most dangerous time in the whole treatment, because of refeeding syndrome (defined in Section 8.3.5). When a starved body suddenly receives carbohydrate, insulin rises and drives phosphate, potassium and magnesium from the blood into the cells, where they are needed to process the new fuel. Blood levels, already low after months of starvation, can fall precipitously, with heart failure, arrhythmias, fluid retention, confusion, seizures and death as possible results; the same demand can exhaust the body's small store of thiamine (vitamin B1). Refeeding is therefore always monitored. Electrolytes are measured before feeding begins and frequently during the first week or two, phosphate, potassium and magnesium are supplemented as needed, thiamine is given before and during early feeding, and the pulse, blood pressure and heart rhythm are watched. Traditional teaching was "start low, go slow", beginning with very small amounts and increasing gradually. More recent studies from specialist units suggest that higher starting intakes are safe for many patients provided electrolytes are supplemented and monitored closely, and guidance has been moving in that direction, but the principle of careful monitoring is unchanged. Bone deserves a word too. The loss of bone density in anorexia (Section 8.3.5) is treated by restoring weight and normal hormonal function; the oral contraceptive pill may bring back monthly bleeding without restoring bone, and is not a treatment for it.

Refeeding also raises the most difficult ethical question in the treatment of eating disorders. A woman with severe anorexia may refuse food that is keeping her alive, and her refusal is not the confused refusal of a delirious patient: she may be intelligent, articulate and perfectly oriented, and her disorder is often ego-syntonic, experienced as part of herself rather than as an illness (Book 3 Sections 2.1 and 2.10.4). Does she have the capacity to refuse treatment? Many clinicians argue that the fear of fatness distorts precisely the decision she is being asked to make, so that capacity about food and weight can be impaired even when capacity about everything else is intact. When admission or feeding against the patient's wishes is considered, Indian clinicians act under the Mental Healthcare Act, 2017, whose provisions on capacity and on supported admission are explained in Book 2 Sections 1.10.3 and 1.10.7. Wherever possible, though, the aim is to win the patient's cooperation, because a treatment imposed by force is hard to sustain after discharge.

What can drugs do? For anorexia nervosa, the answer is disappointingly little. No drug has been shown to treat the core disorder. The story of fluoxetine is instructive. In an open trial, Walter Kaye's group (1991) gave fluoxetine to 31 patients after weight restoration and most maintained their weight, but there was no placebo group. When B. Timothy Walsh and his colleagues (2006) ran what Barlow and Durand call a definitive placebo-controlled trial, fluoxetine had no benefit in preventing relapse in weight-restored patients. The antipsychotic olanzapine has a little more support. In a trial led by Evelyn Attia (2019), 152 adult outpatients were randomly assigned to olanzapine or placebo for 16 weeks; BMI rose faster on olanzapine (by about 0.26 versus 0.10 units a month), a modest but real effect in a disorder in which weight is notoriously hard to change, but olanzapine brought no significant benefit for obsessionality or the other psychological symptoms. Drugs in anorexia are also unusually risky. A starved heart, often with low potassium, is vulnerable to the arrhythmias that many psychotropic drugs can provoke by prolonging the QT interval of the electrocardiogram, so an electrocardiogram and electrolyte check come before prescribing. And because the depressive and obsessional features of anorexia are often secondary to starvation (Section 8.3.6), they frequently lift with weight restoration alone; antidepressants are usually kept for depression or obsessive-compulsive disorder that persists once weight has improved.

For bulimia nervosa the drug picture is brighter. The most effective drugs are the antidepressants that also work in mood and anxiety disorders (their classes are taught in Book 3 Section 5.7.2). In two studies by Walsh and his colleagues (1991), tricyclic antidepressants reduced binge eating and purging by an average of 47 percent, and fluoxetine by 65 percent. The US Food and Drug Administration approved fluoxetine (Prozac) for bulimia nervosa in 1996, and it remains the only drug so approved; the recommended dose for bulimia is 60 milligrams a day, higher than the usual starting dose for depression. But the limits are important. Antidepressants work better than placebo in the short term, and adding them to cognitive behaviour therapy increases its benefit modestly (in the largest such study, Walsh and colleagues, 1997, adding two antidepressants, one of them an SSRI, to CBT gave a modest extra gain), yet on their own they do not have substantial long-lasting effects on bulimia, and CBT alone is superior to medication alone. Antidepressants are therefore used alongside psychological treatment, or for patients who cannot get it or have not responded to it. One antidepressant is specifically avoided: bupropion lowers the seizure threshold, and in an early controlled trial in bulimia (Horne and colleagues, 1988) four of the patients given it had grand mal (generalised tonic-clonic) seizures, far more than in earlier studies of the drug, so its US labelling contraindicates it in people with a current or past diagnosis of bulimia or anorexia nervosa.

For binge-eating disorder, the FDA in January 2015 approved lisdexamfetamine, a long-acting stimulant (a "prodrug" that the body converts into dexamfetamine) already used for attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, for adults with moderate to severe binge-eating disorder, making it the first drug approved for the condition. The approval rested on two trials in a total of 724 adults in which it reduced the number of binge days a week compared with placebo. It is not approved for weight loss, and as a stimulant it carries a risk of misuse and dependence (Chapter 10), a real concern in a population in which stimulant misuse is already common. The anticonvulsant topiramate has reduced binge frequency and weight in placebo-controlled trials, but side effects such as tingling and slowed thinking limit its use. The SSRIs give mixed results: in one controlled trial (Grilo, Masheb and Wilson, 2005) fluoxetine did no better than placebo and added nothing to CBT. Table 8.14 brings the treatments together.

Table 8.14 Treatments for the eating disorders: what the evidence supports.
DisorderMedical and drug treatmentPsychological treatment (Book 2)
Anorexia nervosa, adolescentsMedical monitoring; weight restoration; refeeding with electrolyte and thiamine cover; no drug treats the core disorderFamily-based (Maudsley) treatment first (Sections 7.7.7, 10.4.7)
Anorexia nervosa, adultsAs above; admission if weight very low or body unstable; fluoxetine does not prevent relapse; olanzapine gives modest weight gainCBT-E, MANTRA or specialist supportive clinical management; focal psychodynamic therapy; no one clearly superior
Bulimia nervosaFluoxetine 60 mg a day (FDA 1996); other antidepressants; correct electrolytes; avoid bupropionCBT-BN or CBT-E first (Section 5.11.1); IPT catches up more slowly (Section 3.7.8); guided self-help

Psychological treatments and prevention: names, evidence and where to read them

The psychological treatments for eating disorders are taught, with their methods, in Book 2. What this subsection does is name each one, say what the evidence shows for each disorder, and point you to the place where the method is explained. Until the 1980s, Barlow and Durand note, psychological treatments for eating disorders were aimed at low self-esteem, problems of identity and disordered family communication, and they were not as effective as clinicians had hoped. The change came with short-term cognitive behavioural treatments that targeted the eating behaviour itself and the attitudes to shape and weight behind it.

Bulimia nervosa. Cognitive behaviour therapy is the treatment of choice. Christopher Fairburn's original manual for bulimia (CBT-BN) was later broadened into enhanced cognitive behaviour therapy (CBT-E, set out in Fairburn's 2008 book), which is transdiagnostic: it is built on the model of Section 8.5.4 and can be used, with minor variations, for any eating disorder. Its first stage teaches the patient the physical consequences of bingeing and purging and how ineffective vomiting and laxatives are for weight control (Section 8.4.3), and introduces regular eating: small, manageable meals and snacks five or six times a day, with no more than about 3 hours between them, which removes the alternation of restriction and overeating on which the binges feed. Later stages tackle the over-evaluation of shape and weight, body checking, and the triggers for binges. The evidence is strong; Book 2 Section 5.11.1 rates CBT for bulimia and binge eating among its best-supported uses. In a widely cited multicentre trial, W. Stewart Agras and his colleagues (2000) randomly assigned 220 patients with bulimia to 19 sessions of either CBT or interpersonal psychotherapy (IPT). Among those who completed treatment, 45 percent of the CBT group had recovered by the end, against 8 percent of the IPT group; a year later the difference was no longer significant, because the IPT patients had caught up. CBT is preferred because it works faster. IPT, which never discusses eating directly but works on the patient's relationships, is the main alternative (Book 2 Section 3.7.8, which describes Fairburn's Oxford trials). In a later trial comparing 20 sessions of CBT-E over 5 months with 2 years of weekly psychoanalytic psychotherapy in 70 patients (Poulsen and colleagues, 2014), 42 percent of the CBT-E group had stopped binge eating and purging at 5 months against 6 percent of the psychoanalytic group, and 44 against 15 percent at 2 years. Because trained therapists are scarce, guided self-help, in which the patient works through a CBT-based book or programme with brief support from a clinician, has been tested and appears effective for less severe cases.

Binge-eating disorder. CBT adapted for binge eating works well here too. In one study (Agras and colleagues, 1997), 41 percent of 93 obese patients stopped bingeing by the end of CBT, and a year later binge eating had fallen by 64 percent overall; those who had stopped bingeing kept off about 4 kilograms, while those who kept bingeing gained about the same amount. Unlike in bulimia, IPT is every bit as effective as CBT for binge-eating disorder: when Denise Wilfley and her colleagues (2002) treated 162 overweight or obese patients with either, the results were comparable, and 60 percent were not bingeing a year later. CBT delivered as guided self-help has done better than a standard behavioural weight-loss programme (Wilson, Wilfley, Agras and Bryson, 2010), so self-help is a sensible first step, with therapist-led treatment for more severe or complicated cases. Behavioural weight-loss programmes have some effect on binge eating, but much less than CBT, and an important clinical rule follows: if an obese person is bingeing, ordinary weight-loss treatment will fail unless the binge eating is treated (Book 2 Section 9.4.6 covers weight management).

Anorexia nervosa in adolescents. The first-line treatment is family-based treatment (FBT), the Maudsley approach. Its history and method are taught in Book 2 Sections 7.7.7 and 10.4.7: the first controlled trial by Gerald Russell, George Szmukler, Christopher Dare and Ivan Eisler (1987), which found family therapy more effective than individual therapy for patients whose illness had begun early and had not become chronic; the manual by James Lock, Daniel le Grange and their colleagues (2001); and the trial by Lock and colleagues (2010) with 121 adolescents, in which, by the one-year follow-up, 49 percent of those given FBT were in full remission against 23 percent of those given individual adolescent-focused therapy. In FBT the parents, told that they did not cause the illness, take charge of refeeding at home. Britain's National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) guideline of 2017 advises anorexia-nervosa-focused family therapy (FT-AN) as the first psychological treatment to consider for children and young people.

Anorexia nervosa in adults. Here no psychological treatment has been shown to be clearly superior, and the outcomes are humbler. CBT after weight restoration helps prevent relapse: in one study (Pike and colleagues, 2003) only 22 percent of patients given a year of CBT relapsed or dropped out, against 73 percent of those given continued nutritional counselling, which on its own is ineffective. CBT-E has given encouraging results in an uncontrolled series of 99 adults (Fairburn and colleagues, 2013): the 64 percent who completed 40 sessions gained weight substantially and kept their improvement at 60 weeks. The largest comparative trial, the German ANTOP study led by Stephan Zipfel (2014), randomly assigned 242 adult outpatients to 10 months of focal psychodynamic therapy, CBT-E or an optimised version of usual care (outpatient psychotherapy plus structured care from a family doctor). BMI rose in all three groups with no significant difference between them; focal psychodynamic therapy had the advantage in recovery at the 12-month follow-up, and CBT-E produced faster weight gain and more improvement in eating-disorder thinking. NICE (2017) accordingly offers adults a choice of individual eating-disorder-focused CBT, the Maudsley Anorexia Nervosa Treatment for Adults (MANTRA), or specialist supportive clinical management (SSCM), with focal psychodynamic therapy as a further option. Because anorexia is so often ego-syntonic, many patients are ambivalent about recovering, and motivational interviewing (Book 2 Section 6.9) is often used to help them decide to change.

Prevention. Because many cases resist treatment and untreated cases can last for years, preventing eating disorders would be worth a great deal. Eric Stice, Heather Shaw and Nathan Marti (2007) reviewed the prevention programmes and concluded that the best chance of success lay in selective programmes, aimed at girls aged 15 or over who are already concerned about their bodies, that work to reduce the exaggerated focus on shape and weight and to encourage acceptance of one's body, rather than universal programmes offered to everyone. The best known is the dissonance-based prevention programme called the Body Project. In a few group sessions, young women who have internalised the thin ideal voluntarily argue against it, in speech, in writing and in role-plays, for example writing a letter to a younger girl about the costs of chasing thinness. Saying publicly what contradicts one's own belief creates cognitive dissonance (taught in the Social Psychology book), which is reduced by changing the belief, so internalisation of the thin ideal falls and with it body dissatisfaction and bulimic symptoms. The programme has been adapted to run over the Internet without a clinician (the eBody Project, Stice and colleagues, 2012), with similar results. Carolyn Becker and her colleagues have shown that trained peer leaders can deliver it, for example to members of university sororities, the women's student societies of American campuses, who are at higher risk than other students; in one of their trials with 106 sorority members, both the dissonance programme and a healthy-weight programme reduced thin-ideal internalisation, body dissatisfaction, dietary restraint and bulimic symptoms at 14 months. An Internet programme called Student Bodies (Winzelberg and colleagues, 2000) also improved body image and reduced the drive for thinness among university women concerned about their weight.

Exam trap

No drug is an established treatment for anorexia nervosa: fluoxetine did not prevent relapse after weight restoration (Walsh and colleagues, 2006), and olanzapine gives only modest weight gain without improving the psychological symptoms; weight restoration and psychological treatment are the treatments. Fluoxetine (60 mg a day) is the FDA-approved drug for bulimia nervosa (1996), lisdexamfetamine for binge-eating disorder (2015); bupropion is avoided because of seizures. First-line psychological treatments: family-based (Maudsley) treatment for adolescent anorexia; CBT (CBT-BN or CBT-E) for bulimia and binge-eating disorder. IPT catches up with CBT for bulimia only at follow-up, but is as effective as CBT for binge-eating disorder. The Body Project works by cognitive dissonance.
