8.6 Enuresis and Encopresis: The Elimination DisordersLower yield · 1 Q

At what age should a child stop wetting the bed? Ask a room full of parents and you will hear every answer from "two" to "whenever he is ready". Ask a grandmother in a joint family and you may hear that the boy has weak kidneys, that he drinks too much water after dinner, or that he is being lazy. Ask the child, if anyone does, and you will usually hear nothing at all, because wetting and soiling are among the most shameful secrets a child can carry. The two disorders in this section are about exactly these problems: urine and faeces passed in the wrong place by a child old enough to be expected to know better. They are not glamorous, and they rarely appear in films, but they are common, they wreck a child's confidence and a family's temper, and they are among the most treatable conditions in this book. They also teach a lesson that runs through the whole chapter: a problem that looks like naughtiness or a hidden emotional conflict often turns out to have a plain bodily mechanism, and the psychology lies as much in how the family reacts as in what caused it.

Learning to be dry and clean

A newborn empties bladder and bowel by reflex. When the bladder or the rectum is stretched past a certain point, nerve signals trigger a contraction and the contents leave, day or night, wherever the baby happens to be. Over the next few years the child has to learn three things that the reflex does not do: to notice the signal that the bladder or rectum is full, to hold on against it by tightening the sphincter muscles and the pelvic floor, and to let go on purpose, in the right place, at the right time. Last of all, the child has to manage the same feat while asleep, either by waking to the signal or by sleeping through it without letting go. Most children gain control of the bowel before the bladder, and most are dry by day well before they are dry at night. Night-time bladder control is the last skill to arrive, and it is the one that varies most from child to child (normal toilet training belongs to the Developmental book).

The elimination disorders are the disorders of this learning. DSM-5-TR defines the group as the inappropriate elimination of urine or faeces, usually first diagnosed in childhood or adolescence. There are two named disorders. The first, enuresis, is the repeated voiding of urine into inappropriate places; the second, encopresis, is the repeated passage of faeces into inappropriate places. Four general rules apply to both. First, each has a minimum age, and that age is a developmental one, not only a number of birthdays: a child with an intellectual developmental disorder (Chapter 3) is judged against his mental age, so a 9-year-old with a mental age of 4 is not diagnosed with enuresis, because a 4-year-old is not yet expected to be dry. Second, the passage may be involuntary or intentional; most is involuntary, but the diagnosis does not depend on proving which. Third, each disorder has subtypes, nocturnal versus diurnal wetting for enuresis and the presence or absence of constipation for encopresis. Fourth, the two usually occur separately but may occur together, and when they do the link is often constipation, as you will see.

The minimum ages follow the developmental order. Because bowel control normally comes first, the rule for faeces is set a year younger: encopresis may be diagnosed from a developmental age of 4, enuresis only from 5. The names come from Greek. Enuresis is from enourein, to urinate in (from ouron, urine). Encopresis is built on kopros, dung or faeces; the word was introduced in 1926 by S. Weissenberg, in a short German paper, "Über Enkopresis" ("On encopresis"), in the Zeitschrift für Kinderheilkunde, as the faecal counterpart of enuresis. The same root gives coprolalia, the involuntary utterance of obscene words in Tourette's disorder (Chapter 3), and coprophagia, the eating of faeces.

The manuals have moved these disorders around. DSM-III (1980) and DSM-III-R (1987) called them functional enuresis and functional encopresis and placed them among the disorders first evident in infancy, childhood or adolescence; DSM-IV (1994) dropped the word "functional" but kept them in that childhood chapter. When DSM-5 (2013) broke up the childhood chapter (Book 3 Section 1.8.2), the elimination disorders received a chapter of their own, which sits between the feeding and eating disorders and the sleep-wake disorders. ICD-10 filed them in its childhood block as F98.0 nonorganic enuresis and F98.1 nonorganic encopresis, the word "nonorganic" carrying the same message as DSM-III's "functional": no structural disease explains the problem. ICD-11 gives them their own small grouping, the elimination disorders, within Chapter 6, with enuresis as 6C00 and encopresis as 6C01, and it uses the same minimum ages of 5 and 4 years.

The way clinicians think about these children has changed even more than the labels. For the first half of the twentieth century, psychoanalytic writers read bedwetting as the symptom of a hidden emotional conflict, so treatment meant long psychotherapy for the child and, often, blame for the parents. Behaviour therapy challenged that reading by curing the wetting directly with a simple alarm, without any new symptom appearing in its place; that story, and the learning theory behind it, are told in Book 2 Section 4.4.4. Today the evidence points the other way from the old view. DSM-5-TR states that most children with enuresis do not have a comorbid mental disorder, and many clinicians now regard the emotional problems that do occur as more often the result of years of wet beds, teasing and punishment than their cause.

Exam trap

"Lack of bowel control in children" is encopresis: repeated passage of faeces in inappropriate places at a developmental age of at least 4. Lack of bladder control is enuresis (urine, age 5). Anorexia nervosa is an eating disorder (Section 8.3), not a problem of elimination. "Analism" is not a recognised disorder in any manual; it is bait built from Freud's anal stage and the "anal character" (taught in the Personality book). Mnemonics: copro means faeces (as in coprolalia); enuresis has the ur of urine; urine at 5, faeces at 4.

Enuresis: the criteria and types

Lokesh is seven and lives with his parents and grandmother in Lucknow. He has never been dry for more than three or four nights in a row. He sleeps so deeply that when his mother tries to take him to the toilet at eleven o'clock he stumbles, half asleep, and cannot be made to pass urine; at three in the morning he is soaked and still fast asleep. By day he is dry, passes urine four or five times and has no pain. Last Diwali he refused to stay overnight at his cousins' house in Kanpur, and when a cousin asked why, he cried. His grandmother says he has weak kidneys and will grow out of it, as his father did at about ten. His father, who remembers his own childhood shame, has nevertheless started shouting at him in the mornings, and Lokesh now hides his wet shorts under the bed. Does Lokesh have a mental disorder? DSM-5-TR answers with four criteria, which in plain words are these:

  1. The child repeatedly voids urine into the bed or into clothes, whether involuntarily or on purpose (Criterion A).
  2. The wetting is clinically significant, which can be shown in either of two ways: it happens at least twice a week for at least 3 consecutive months, or it causes clinically significant distress or impairment in social, school or other important areas of life (Criterion B).
  3. The child's chronological age is at least 5 years, or, for a child with developmental delay, the developmental level is equivalent to at least 5 years (Criterion C).
  4. The wetting is not caused by the physiological effects of a substance, such as a diuretic or an antipsychotic drug, or by another medical condition, such as diabetes, spina bifida or a seizure disorder (Criterion D).

Each rule has a job. Criterion A is deliberately broad: the occasional child who wets on purpose, out of anger or as a protest, still counts, although intentional wetting should make the clinician look harder at the family. Criterion B sets the threshold, and the word "or" in it matters. A child who wets every night plainly qualifies on frequency alone. But a child who wets only once a week, and therefore fails the frequency rule, still qualifies if the wetting stops her going on a school trip, makes her the butt of jokes in the hostel or poisons her relationship with her parents. The rule recognises that what makes wetting a disorder is partly what it does to the child's life. Criterion C stops clinicians from labelling normal development: plenty of 4-year-olds wet the bed, and that is no disorder at all. Criterion D sends the clinician to rule out a medical cause before calling the problem enuresis. DSM-5-TR's text adds several structural causes to the list in the criterion: an ectopic ureter in a girl (a ureter that opens below the bladder's sphincter, so that she dribbles constantly even though she voids normally as well), posterior urethral valves in a boy (a flap of tissue that obstructs the outflow of urine) and a tethered spinal cord.

Lokesh meets every criterion. He wets most nights (B by frequency, and the Diwali episode shows impairment as well), he is seven (C), and his urine test, his normal daytime pattern and the absence of thirst or constipation make a medical cause unlikely (D).

DSM-5-TR then asks the clinician to specify one of three subtypes, according to when the wetting happens:

  • Nocturnal only. Urine is passed only during night-time sleep. This is the commonest subtype and is also called monosymptomatic enuresis, because wetting is the child's only symptom: there is no daytime urgency, frequency or leaking. The wetting usually happens in the first third of the night. Occasionally it happens in REM sleep, and the child may then recall a dream that involved passing urine. Lokesh has this subtype.
  • Diurnal only. Urine is passed during waking hours, with no bedwetting. DSM-5-TR notes that this subtype may simply be called urinary incontinence, and it divides the children into two groups. Some have urge incontinence: sudden, strong urges to void caused by an overactive bladder muscle (detrusor instability), so that they rush to the toilet and do not always make it. Others show voiding postponement: they consciously put off going until they leak, sometimes because of social anxiety about school toilets and sometimes because they are absorbed in play. The wetting comes most often in the early afternoon on school days or just after school.
  • Nocturnal and diurnal. A combination of the two, also called nonmonosymptomatic enuresis. These children are more likely to have urinary infections and other problems, and their wetting more often persists after an infection has been treated.

Ankita, eight, shows the diurnal picture. Her school's toilets are dirty and the doors do not lock, so she holds on all morning, crossing her legs or squatting on one heel when the urge comes, and leaks on the bus home two or three afternoons a week. She has voiding postponement, and the remedy begins with the school timetable, not with a psychiatrist.

A second, separate distinction describes the course rather than the timing. In primary enuresis the child has never established urinary continence; by definition it begins at age 5, when the diagnosis first becomes possible. In secondary enuresis the wetting starts after a period of established continence (clinicians commonly take a dry spell of about six months as proof of continence). Secondary enuresis most often begins between the ages of 5 and 8, but it may begin at any age, and it is the form most often tied to a stressful event: a new baby, a move, a parent's illness, or starting school. DSM-5-TR reports a finding that surprises many students: the two courses do not differ in the prevalence of comorbid mental disorders. Lokesh, who has never been dry, has primary enuresis.

DSM-5-TR prints the ICD-10-CM code F98.0 for enuresis. In the WHO's ICD-10 it is F98.0, nonorganic enuresis. In ICD-11 it is 6C00, with three subcodes that match the DSM subtypes: 6C00.0 nocturnal enuresis, 6C00.1 diurnal enuresis and 6C00.2 nocturnal and diurnal enuresis.

Exam trap

Enuresis needs twice a week for 3 consecutive months or significant distress or impairment, at an age of at least 5. Do not mix up the two axes: nocturnal, diurnal or both says when the child wets; primary or secondary says whether the child was ever dry. Daytime-only wetting is still enuresis in DSM-5-TR. Bedwetting belongs to the elimination disorders, not to REM sleep behaviour disorder (an exam option that describes REM sleep behaviour disorder as "bedwetting during N3" is a distractor): wetting is commonest in the first third of the night, but it is not tied to N3 or to any single stage (Section 8.10.1 places it on the night's map). The sleep specialists' ICSD-3 lists "sleep enuresis" among its "other parasomnias", but DSM-5-TR does not.

Who wets, and why

Bedwetting is far commoner than most parents believe, which is itself useful news for a family like Lokesh's. Studies from settings as different as the United States, the Netherlands and Hong Kong agree that nocturnal enuresis affects about 5 to 10 percent of 5-year-olds, 3 to 5 percent of 10-year-olds and about 1 percent of people aged 15 or older. In a classroom of forty 7-year-olds, two or three children are probably wetting the bed. Daytime incontinence is a little less common and also falls with age: 3.2 to 9.0 percent at 7, 1.1 to 4.2 percent at 11 to 13 and 1.2 to 3.0 percent at 15 to 17. Rates are higher in boys, in children with learning disabilities or attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) (Chapter 3), and in socially oppressed groups, such as African American children in the United States and Turkish or Moroccan children in the Netherlands. Rates are very high in orphanages and other residential institutions, and DSM-5-TR notes that the way or the age at which these children were toilet trained does not explain it.

The natural course is strongly towards recovery. After the age of 5, about 5 to 10 percent of enuretic children become dry each year without any treatment, so most are dry by adolescence; in about 1 percent the disorder continues into adulthood. Diurnal enuresis is uncommon after the age of 9. When enuresis lasts into late childhood or adolescence, urinary frequency often persists after the wetting stops, and incontinence can return later in life in women. The sexes differ in two ways: nocturnal enuresis is almost twice as common in boys (especially among younger, milder, night-only cases), whereas daytime wetting is commoner in girls, and the female excess grows with age; urinary infections are frequently tied to daytime wetting, especially in girls.

Why do some children go on wetting when their classmates have stopped? DSM-5-TR's answer for nocturnal enuresis is a mismatch between three things: how much urine the kidneys make at night, how much the bladder can hold at night, and whether the child can wake from sleep when the bladder is full. It helps to treat these as three systems, any one of which can fail (Figure 8.10).

The first system is nocturnal polyuria, the production of too much urine at night. Normally the kidneys make much less urine at night than by day, partly because the pituitary releases more vasopressin (antidiuretic hormone) during sleep, and vasopressin tells the kidneys to hold water back. In 1989 the Danish researcher Søren Rittig and his colleagues measured vasopressin across the day and night in 15 enuretic patients and 11 matched controls. The controls showed the expected night-time rise; the enuretic patients showed a much smaller rise, with significantly lower night-time levels, and they produced a larger volume of more dilute urine at night. Rittig's group concluded that an abnormal daily rhythm of vasopressin is an important factor in enuresis, and later work from the same group found the defect in a subset of children, not all. A child with this problem simply fills his bladder before morning. The second system is night-time bladder capacity: some children have a small functional capacity, or a bladder muscle that contracts before the bladder is full (an overactive detrusor), so even a normal amount of urine is too much. The third system is arousal: the child does not wake when the full bladder signals. Parents of enuretic children describe them, like Lokesh, as impossibly deep sleepers, and laboratory studies confirm a high arousal threshold. Yet DSM-5-TR adds a twist: a high arousal threshold does not mean these children sleep well; their sleep quality is often poor. A sleep laboratory study by Edward J. Mikkelsen, Judith L. Rapoport and their colleagues (1980) found that enuretic events were not tied to any particular sleep stage. Wetting is not a "deep sleep disorder" in the sense of happening only in N3, even though it clusters early in the night.

Genes load the dice. DSM-5-TR describes nocturnal enuresis as genetically heterogeneous (different genes in different families), with heritability shown in family, twin and segregation studies. A child's risk is about 3.6 times higher if the mother was enuretic and about 10.1 times higher if the father had urinary incontinence; the relative risk is greater for previously enuretic fathers than mothers, which fits Lokesh's family. In 1995 the Danish geneticist Hans Eiberg and his colleagues traced a dominant form of nocturnal enuresis in several Danish families to a region on chromosome 13 (13q), the first enuresis locus to be mapped, but other families showed no link to that region, and further loci have been reported since, exactly what "heterogeneous" means. Development matters too: delays in speech, language, learning and motor skills, and neuropsychiatric problems, are among the predisposing factors DSM-5-TR lists. The environment adds delayed toileting and psychosocial stress, which are especially important in secondary enuresis.

One more cause is easy to miss. Nocturnal enuresis is linked with heavy snoring and sleep apnoea (Section 8.9), probably because repeated struggles to breathe disturb sleep and change the hormones that regulate urine. DSM-5-TR reports that about 50 percent of enuretic children with proven sleep-disordered breathing become dry after their tonsils and adenoids are removed. A clinician who asks about snoring may cure a bedwetter with an operation no psychologist could have offered.

Culture shapes how wetting is seen more than how often it happens. National prevalence figures are remarkably similar across countries, and the developmental curve is much the same everywhere. Local school surveys in Africa, South Asia, Europe and the Caribbean have reported anything from 4 to 50 percent, but much of that spread comes from differences in method. Explanations differ widely: traditional Chinese medicine attributes enuresis to a long-standing deficiency of kidney yang (a "masculine" energy), and Lokesh's grandmother has her own version. The burden on parents is heavier where care is costly or hard to reach, and DSM-5-TR notes higher risk of emotional disorder in such parents.

The differential diagnosis follows directly from Criterion D. Enuresis is not diagnosed in the presence of a neurogenic bladder (a bladder whose nerve supply is damaged, as in spina bifida), a structural problem such as posterior urethral valves or an ectopic ureter, a medical condition that causes polyuria or urgency (untreated diabetes mellitus, with its sugar in the urine and thirst, or diabetes insipidus, in which vasopressin itself fails), or during an acute urinary tract infection. Two exceptions keep the rule sensible: the diagnosis may still be made if the wetting was regularly present before the medical condition began, or if it persists after the condition has been properly treated. The same logic applies to drugs. Antipsychotics, diuretics and medicines that cause constipation, polyuria or changes in executive functioning can all cause incontinence; then the wetting is noted as a side effect, not diagnosed as enuresis, unless it was already present before the drug was started.

What goes with enuresis? Most enuretic children have no other mental disorder, but behavioural and developmental problems are commoner in children who wet both by day and by night. Encopresis and constipation occur with both day and night wetting; restless legs syndrome and the NREM sleep arousal disorders (sleepwalking and sleep terrors, Section 8.10) are associated with bedwetting; snoring and apnoea you have met; and urinary infections are commoner in children with daytime wetting and nonmonosymptomatic enuresis. Finally, DSM-5-TR measures the harm by its effects on the child's social life (no sleepovers, no camp), self-esteem and standing among peers, and by the anger, punishment and rejection of caregivers. That last item is a reminder that a father who shouts every morning is part of the clinical picture.

Treating enuresis

Treatment starts with an assessment that is mostly medical common sense. The clinician takes a history of the wetting (how often, what time of night, ever dry, any daytime urgency, frequency or leaking), asks about thirst and the volume of urine passed (diabetes), about constipation and soiling, and about snoring and breathing pauses at night. A urine test looks for infection and sugar, and a simple chart kept by the family for a week or two records drinks, daytime voids and wet or dry nights. A child who wets only at night, has a normal urine test and has no daytime symptoms has monosymptomatic enuresis and needs no further investigation; daytime symptoms, infections, a weak urinary stream or neurological signs call for a paediatric or urological work-up. Equally important is what the clinician tells the family: bedwetting is common, involuntary and nobody's fault; most children grow out of it; and punishment makes it worse. Because so many children become dry on their own, there is no hurry to treat a young child who is not bothered; active treatment is driven by the distress of the child and family, and the alarm in particular needs a child old enough to cooperate.

The psychological treatments are taught in Book 2 (Sections 4.4.4 and 10.4.1, with Table 10.9), and only their conclusion is needed here. The urine alarm, introduced as the bell and pad by O. Hobart Mowrer and Willie Mae Mowrer in 1938, wakes the child at the first drops of urine. It was explained first by classical conditioning and later by S. H. Lovibond's avoidance account (1964), and it was strengthened by overlearning (G. C. Young and R. T. T. Morgan, 1972), by Nathan H. Azrin and colleagues' dry-bed training (1974), by retention control training (Kimmel and Kimmel, 1970) and by Arthur C. Houts and colleagues' full-spectrum home training (1983). The bottom line from Houts, Berman and Abramson's 1994 meta-analysis and later work is consistent: the alarm is the first-line treatment for a motivated school-age child whose family can cope with broken nights; roughly two in three children become dry with it over several weeks to a few months; relapse after it is withdrawn is common but usually responds to a second course; and treatments that include an alarm keep their gains better than drug treatment does.

The drugs work on the other systems in Figure 8.10. The most important is desmopressin, a synthetic analogue of vasopressin. Taken at bedtime, it does what the child's own hormone should have done: it makes the kidneys hold water back, so less urine is produced overnight. It is therefore most useful in the child with nocturnal polyuria. Its great advantage is speed: it works within days, which makes it ideal for a short-term need such as a school camp, a wedding in the family or a night at the cousins' house. Its great weakness is relapse: when it is stopped, most children start wetting again, because it suppresses the problem rather than teaching anything. Its risk follows from how it works. A child whose kidneys are holding water back must not drink much in the evening, or the body becomes overloaded with water and the blood sodium falls (hyponatraemia), which can cause headache, vomiting, seizures and, rarely, death from water intoxication. Fluid is therefore restricted from about an hour before the dose until the next morning, and the drug is stopped during vomiting or diarrhoea. In 2007, after reports of severe hyponatraemia and seizures, especially in children using the nasal spray, the UK regulator and then the US Food and Drug Administration (in December 2007) removed bedwetting from the approved uses of the nasal forms of desmopressin; the tablets and melts that dissolve under the tongue remain in use with stronger warnings.

The oldest drug is imipramine, a tricyclic antidepressant (Book 3 Section 5.7.2) that has been used for bedwetting for decades. How it helps has never been settled; its anticholinergic action, which relaxes the bladder muscle, is one proposed explanation, and effects on arousal and on urine output have also been suggested. The anticholinergic idea has a problem: in a controlled trial with 40 severely enuretic boys, Judith Rapoport, Edward Mikkelsen and colleagues (1980) found that imipramine and its relative desipramine both did better than placebo and better than methscopolamine, a purely anticholinergic drug. Most children relapse when it is stopped, and it carries two serious risks: it can affect heart rhythm, and it is dangerous in overdose, which matters in a house with small children and a bottle of tablets. Current guidance, such as that of the UK's National Institute for Health and Care Excellence, keeps it for children in whom the other treatments have failed, after assessment by a specialist, and it is never a first choice. A third group, the anticholinergic drugs such as oxybutynin, calm an overactive bladder muscle. They are used mainly for daytime wetting with urgency and are sometimes added to desmopressin when bedwetting resists desmopressin alone; their side effects include dry mouth and constipation, which can itself worsen wetting.

For daytime wetting the first step is not a drug at all but urotherapy, a programme of bladder training: the child drinks adequately and regularly through the day, voids by the clock (every two to three hours, including at school) rather than waiting for an urgent signal, sits in a relaxed position on the toilet, and has any constipation treated. Ankita's treatment began with a letter to her school asking that she be allowed to use the clean staff toilet at fixed times, and with a water bottle on her desk. Table 8.15 sets the options side by side.

Table 8.15 Treatments for enuresis.
TreatmentHow it worksSpeed and relapseMain risks or limitsWhere taught
Urine alarm (bell and pad)Wakes the child at the first drops; teaches waking or holding onWeeks to months; about two in three dry; relapse common but treatableNeeds a motivated child and a family willing to wakeBook 2, 4.4.4 and 10.4.1
DesmopressinVasopressin analogue; less urine made at nightWorks within days; most relapse when stoppedHyponatraemia if fluids are not restricted; nasal spray no longer approved for bedwetting (2007)This section

Exam trap

Desmopressin works fast but relapse follows withdrawal; the alarm is slower but more durable. If a question asks for the treatment with the best long-term results, the answer is the alarm (or a package that contains it); if it asks for quick dryness before a school trip, it is desmopressin. The alarm is a treatment for enuresis, not encopresis. Desmopressin is a vasopressin (antidiuretic hormone) analogue, not an antidepressant; imipramine is the antidepressant.

Encopresis: the criteria and types

Rajiv is six. For a year his mother has found soiled underwear stuffed behind the washing machine, and at school the other children have started to say that he smells. He denies every accident. He sits on the toilet for a minute and jumps off, saying he does not need to go, and once or twice a week he passes an enormous hard stool that blocks the toilet. His mother remembers that it all began when he was four, after a bout of fever, when a hard motion made him scream and left a streak of blood. His father thinks he is lazy and has started smacking him. To decide whether Rajiv has encopresis, the clinician works through DSM-5-TR's four criteria, which in plain words are these:

  1. The child repeatedly passes faeces into inappropriate places, such as clothing or the floor, whether involuntarily or on purpose (Criterion A).
  2. At least one such event happens each month for at least 3 months (Criterion B).
  3. The child's chronological age is at least 4 years, or the developmental level is equivalent to at least 4 years (Criterion C).
  4. The behaviour is not caused by the physiological effects of a substance, such as laxatives, or by another medical condition, except through a mechanism involving constipation (Criterion D).

Notice how much lower the frequency bar is than for enuresis: once a month, not twice a week. Soiling is so socially disabling that even a monthly accident matters, and, perhaps because the frequency rule is already so easy to meet, there is no "distress" alternative. The odd clause at the end of Criterion D is the one students puzzle over. Why should a medical condition exclude the diagnosis, "except through a mechanism involving constipation"? Because constipation is the usual road to encopresis. If every constipated child were excluded as having "another medical condition", the diagnosis would vanish for most of the children who have it. So the rule says, in effect: if soiling is caused by a disease that acts directly on the bowel (chronic diarrhoea, a damaged spinal cord, a narrowed anus), it is not encopresis; but if the pathway runs through constipation, even constipation that a medical condition helped to start, the diagnosis stands. Laxatives are named because a child taking them can leak for that reason alone.

The clinician then specifies one of two subtypes:

  • With constipation and overflow incontinence. There is evidence of constipation on physical examination or from the history. The faeces are characteristically, though not always, poorly formed, and leakage may be infrequent or continuous, through the day and sometimes during sleep. Only part of the faeces is passed when the child sits on the toilet. The incontinence resolves once the constipation is treated. This is by far the commoner subtype, and it is Rajiv's.
  • Without constipation and overflow incontinence. There is no evidence of constipation. The faeces are usually of normal form and consistency and the soiling is intermittent; the faeces may be deposited in a prominent place, a bed, a corner of the sitting room. This subtype is usually associated with oppositional defiant disorder or conduct disorder (Chapter 11), or it may be a consequence of anal masturbation. It is less common.

Like enuresis, encopresis has two courses: primary, in which the child has never established faecal continence, and secondary, in which soiling begins after a period of continence. Rajiv was clean from three to four, so his course is secondary.

The associated features are mostly about shame. The child often feels deeply ashamed and avoids situations, such as camp or school, that might expose him. Smearing of faeces may be deliberate, but it may also be accidental, the result of a child's attempt to clean up or hide faeces that he passed without meaning to; a parent who sees smears on the bathroom wall should not leap to the conclusion that the child is defiant or disturbed. When soiling is clearly deliberate, features of oppositional defiant or conduct disorder may also be present. Many children with encopresis and chronic constipation also have enuresis, partly because the overloaded rectum presses on the bladder, and some have reflux of urine and chronic urinary infections that settle when the constipation is treated. DSM-5-TR prints the ICD-10-CM code F98.1; in the WHO's ICD-10 the disorder is F98.1, nonorganic encopresis; in ICD-11 it is 6C01, with the subcodes 6C01.0 encopresis with constipation or overflow incontinence and 6C01.1 encopresis without constipation or overflow incontinence.

Who soils, and why

Encopresis is less common than enuresis but far from rare. It affects 1 to 4 percent of children in high-income countries, and prevalences of 2 to 8 percent have been reported from some Asian countries (Iran, South Korea and Sri Lanka). It is commoner in younger children (more than 4 percent at ages 4 to 6, less than 2 percent at 10 to 12), in children who have suffered early abuse or neglect, and in children from low-income families. Under the age of 5 boys and girls are affected about equally, but among older children boys predominate, by a ratio that varies from about 2:1 in the United States to 6:1 elsewhere. Male sex and an age before adolescence are therefore listed as risk factors. Inadequate or inconsistent toilet training, and stresses such as starting school or the birth of a sibling, may predispose to it. Encopresis can persist for years, with intermittent flare-ups.

The key to understanding most encopresis is that the soiling child is usually not failing to hold on; he is holding on too much. Follow the retentive cycle in Figure 8.11. It often begins with a single painful bowel movement: a hard stool after an illness, a change of diet or a period of low fluid intake, perhaps tearing the lining of the anus (an anal fissure). The child learns that passing a motion hurts, so the next time he feels the urge he withholds, tightening his buttocks, standing on tiptoe or hiding behind the sofa until the urge passes. The stool that stays in the rectum loses water and grows harder and larger, which makes the next motion more painful still. As the faecal mass builds up (impaction), the rectum is stretched; a chronically stretched rectum loses its sensitivity, so the child genuinely no longer feels the urge, and the stretched muscles lose the strength to push. Liquid stool from higher in the bowel then seeps around the hard mass and leaks into the underwear without the child noticing: overflow incontinence. That is why the faeces in this subtype are loose and poorly formed, and why a child can soil and be constipated at the same time, a fact that baffles many parents.

Around this bodily loop runs a second loop made by the people around the child. The adults see soiled pants and conclude that the child is lazy, dirty or defiant; they scold, punish or shame him. The child, who often cannot feel the leak and does not know why it happens, denies it and hides the evidence, and the fear of the toilet and of the adults makes him hold on harder, which feeds the bodily loop. Rajiv's hidden underwear and his father's smacks belong to this outer loop. Breaking it is as important as breaking the inner one.

Some children are physically predisposed to constipation. DSM-5-TR describes ineffective straining and "paradoxical" defecation, in which the child contracts the external sphincter and pelvic floor when he should relax them, so that pushing closes the exit. Low fluid intake and diet, coeliac disease (an intolerance of gluten), an underactive thyroid (hypothyroidism) and the side effects of some medicines can also cause constipation. Psychological factors can start the withholding as well: anxiety about defecating in a particular place (the school toilet again), or a broader pattern of anxious or oppositional behaviour. DSM-5-TR's list of factors that contribute to faecal incontinence adds anxiety, depression, behaviour disorders, bullying, poor school performance and lower socio-economic status. Culture enters through diet and drink, hot tropical climates and psychosocial adversity, all of which influence constipation. It also affects whether parents seek help: DSM-5-TR reports that Turkish and Moroccan parents in the Netherlands may not report encopresis because of religious concerns about the impurity of urine and faeces; shame can keep families silent anywhere.

The diagnosis is clinical, made from the history and a physical examination, and usually needs no tests. A faecal mass felt on digital rectal examination supports the subtype with constipation and overflow incontinence, as does an abdominal X-ray showing impaction (although an X-ray is not needed for the diagnosis). Colonic transit studies can separate the two subtypes: the child swallows small markers that show up on X-ray, and a later film shows whether they are held up in the bowel (constipation) or passed promptly (no constipation). Anorectal manometry, which measures the pressures and sensation of the rectum and anus, helps in some children. When the child fails to respond to treatment or there are signs of another illness, further evaluation is needed to exclude medical causes.

That is the differential diagnosis. Faecal incontinence caused directly by another medical condition, such as chronic diarrhoea, spina bifida (where the nerves to the bowel are damaged) or anal stenosis (a narrowed anus), does not warrant a diagnosis of encopresis. Paediatricians also think of Hirschsprung disease (aganglionic megacolon), an inborn absence of nerve cells in the wall of the lower bowel, when constipation dates from the first days of life, often with delay in passing the first stool (meconium) after birth; soiling is unusual in that condition, because the nerve-free segment, which includes the rectum, stays narrow and usually empty while stool backs up above it. ICD-11 names aganglionic megacolon among the conditions that rule out a diagnosis of encopresis. Encopresis, by contrast, typically begins after a period of normal bowel habit or during toilet training. Enuresis is often present as well, particularly in children with the subtype without constipation. Table 8.16 brings the two elimination disorders together.

Table 8.16 Enuresis and encopresis compared.
FeatureEnuresisEncopresis
What is passedUrine, into bed or clothesFaeces, into clothes, the floor or other inappropriate places
Minimum age5 years (developmental)4 years (developmental)
Frequency ruleTwice a week for 3 consecutive months, or significant distress or impairmentOnce a month for 3 months
SubtypesNocturnal only; diurnal only; nocturnal and diurnalWith constipation and overflow incontinence; without
CoursesPrimary or secondary (secondary usually begins at 5 to 8)Primary or secondary

CodesF98.0 (DSM-5-TR, ICD-10); ICD-11 6C00F98.1 (DSM-5-TR, ICD-10); ICD-11 6C01
Exam trap

In the commonest form of encopresis the child is constipated and soiling at the same time: liquid stool leaks around an impacted mass (overflow incontinence). The subtype without constipation is the one linked with oppositional defiant and conduct disorder. Encopresis needs only once a month for 3 months from age 4; enuresis needs twice a week for 3 months from age 5. Faecal incontinence caused directly by diarrhoea, spina bifida or anal stenosis is not encopresis; constipation, even when a medical condition helped to cause it, does not exclude the diagnosis.

Treating encopresis

Because most encopresis is retentive, treatment has to begin with the bowel, and it is a joint task for the paediatrician and the psychologist. The physical treatment has three phases. The first is disimpaction: clearing the hard mass. Current paediatric guidelines, such as the joint European and North American paediatric gastroenterology guideline of 2014, prefer a high-dose oral osmotic laxative, polyethylene glycol, given over several days, and keep enemas for when it cannot be used. Until the mass is gone, nothing else will work, because a stretched rectum full of hard stool cannot feel or push. The second phase is maintenance: the child takes a daily laxative (again usually polyethylene glycol, with lactulose as an alternative) to keep the stools soft enough to pass without pain. The third is a slow taper. Expert opinion recommends continuing maintenance for at least two months, and reducing it only after the child has been free of constipation for at least a month; in practice many children need many months. Fluids and fibre are kept at normal, healthy levels.

Why so long? Because the stretched rectum takes months to shrink back and recover its sensation and strength, and because the child has to unlearn the fear of the toilet. Families who stop the laxative as soon as the soiling stops are the commonest cause of relapse: the hard stool returns, the first painful motion revives the fear, and the cycle restarts. The clinician therefore explains the retentive cycle to the parents and to the child, often with a drawing like Figure 8.11, so that everyone understands that the leaks are not deliberate. Once parents understand overflow incontinence, smacking usually stops on the same day.

The behavioural programme, taught in Book 2 Section 10.4.1, then teaches the bowel and the child a new routine. The child sits on the toilet for about five to ten minutes after each main meal, when the gastrocolic reflex (the bowel's natural wave of activity after food reaches the stomach) makes a motion most likely, with a footstool under his feet so that he can push effectively; sitting and success are rewarded on a chart, and accidents are handled calmly, with no punishment. Michael White's narrative technique of externalising the problem as "Sneaky Poo", which unites parents and child against the problem instead of against each other, is described in Book 2 Section 6.8.3. Children with the non-retentive subtype need less laxative and more attention to the family, to the meaning of the soiling and to any oppositional or conduct problems (Chapter 11). The prognosis is good with persistence, but DSM-5-TR's warning stands: encopresis can persist for years, with intermittent flare-ups, and relapses after illness, travel or stress are common enough that families should expect them and restart the routine rather than despair.

Rajiv's treatment followed this plan. A week of high-dose laxative cleared the mass, a daily dose kept him soft, and he sat on the toilet after breakfast and dinner with his feet on a small stool, earning a sticker for each sitting. The leaks stopped within a month. His father, once he understood that Rajiv could not feel them, stopped smacking him and took charge of the sticker chart. The laxative was tapered after six months.

Finally, DSM-5-TR provides two residual categories for clinically significant elimination problems that do not meet full criteria. Other specified elimination disorder is used when the clinician records the reason, the example given being "low-frequency enuresis" (the manual does not define the phrase further; remember that wetting less often than twice a week that causes significant distress or impairment already meets Criterion B of enuresis); it is coded N39.498 with urinary symptoms and R15.9 with faecal symptoms. Unspecified elimination disorder is used when the clinician does not say why criteria are unmet or lacks information, as in an emergency room; it is coded R32 with urinary symptoms and R15.9 with faecal symptoms.

Clinical link

Elimination disorders are where a psychologist must work hand in hand with a paediatrician. The psychologist who starts a reward chart for a child with an impacted bowel is rewarding something the child cannot do; the paediatrician who prescribes laxatives without addressing a father's punishment and a child's fear of the toilet will see the child back in three months. Shame and punishment make both disorders worse. The single most useful sentence a clinician can say to the family is that the child is not doing it on purpose, and the second is that it is very common and very treatable.

8.7 Normal Sleep, the Sleep-Wake Disorders and Insomnia DisorderLower yield · not asked yet

How do you know whether you slept well last night? Most of us answer from a feeling: we woke refreshed, or we did not; the night seemed long, or it passed in a blink. That feeling is what brings people to a clinic, and for one sleep disorder, insomnia, it is the very thing the diagnosis is built on. Yet the feeling can mislead. People who complain bitterly of sleeplessness are sometimes found to sleep for six hours in the laboratory, and people who say they sleep perfectly are sometimes found to stop breathing a hundred times a night. To diagnose sleep disorders, then, a clinician needs two things this section supplies: a precise vocabulary for what happens during a night, drawn from the sleep laboratory, and a map of the disorders DSM-5-TR recognises. With those in hand, the section turns to the commonest sleep disorder of all, insomnia disorder, and to the drugs used, and misused, to treat it. The other sleep-wake disorders have their own sections (8.8 to 8.10).

A night of sleep, in brief

Book 1 Section 5.7 teaches the stages of sleep and draws the hypnogram of a normal night (Figure 5.5); here you need only the numbers DSM-5-TR uses. Sleep has four stages that can be measured in the laboratory: three stages of non-rapid eye movement (NREM) sleep and rapid eye movement (REM) sleep. N1, the drowsy transition from waking, takes up about 5 percent of sleep in healthy adults. N2, marked by sleep spindles and K-complexes in the EEG, takes up about 50 percent. N3, slow-wave sleep, the deepest stage, takes up about 20 percent in healthy young adults. REM sleep, in which most story-like dreams occur, takes up about 20 to 25 percent. The stages are arranged in time, and that arrangement is the key to many diagnoses: N3 is concentrated in the first third to half of the night (and grows after sleep deprivation), while REM sleep returns about every 80 to 100 minutes and its periods lengthen towards morning. Sleep also changes with age. After childhood and early adolescence, with their large amounts of slow-wave sleep, sleep becomes lighter and more broken across adult life, with more waking and more N1 and less N3. DSM-5-TR draws the practical conclusion: age must be taken into account in every sleep diagnosis, because an amount of night waking that is normal at 70 may be abnormal at 25.

The rest of the normal physiology is in Book 1 and is assumed here. Section 5.7.6 explains the "flip-flop" switch in the hypothalamus that turns sleep on and off and the orexin (hypocretin) neurons that hold it in the waking position; Section 5.6 covers the body clock in the suprachiasmatic nucleus, melatonin, zeitgebers and Borbély's two-process model of sleep pressure and circadian timing; and Section 5.8 covers the theories of why we sleep, what deprivation does and the rebound of REM sleep after it is lost.

The language of the sleep laboratory

The basic tool of the sleep laboratory is polysomnography, the recording of many physiological signals during sleep; the record it produces is a polysomnogram. Three channels are needed to stage sleep at all: the electroencephalogram (EEG), which records the brain's electrical activity from the scalp; the electrooculogram (EOG), which records eye movements; and the electromyogram (EMG), which records muscle tone, usually from the chin. A full clinical study adds more: airflow at the nose and mouth, belts around the chest and abdomen that record breathing effort, a finger probe that measures oxygen saturation of the blood, the electrocardiogram (ECG), EMG from the legs to pick up limb movements, a body-position sensor, a snoring microphone and, in many laboratories, video. Most studies are done at night, during the person's usual sleeping hours, but daytime studies are used to measure sleepiness.

Clinicians describe a recorded night in two families of measures. The first is sleep continuity, the overall balance of sleep and wakefulness: is sleep consolidated, or broken up by waking? Its measures are sleep latency, the time it takes to fall asleep, in minutes; wake after sleep onset, the time spent awake between first falling asleep and the final awakening; the number of awakenings; and sleep efficiency, the percentage of the time in bed that is actually spent asleep. A worked example makes the last one concrete. Kishore, a 58-year-old clerk in Bhopal, goes to bed at 10.30 p.m. and gets up at 6.30 a.m., so he spends 480 minutes in bed. His sleep diary (and, later, his polysomnogram) shows that he takes 45 minutes to fall asleep, lies awake for a total of 75 minutes in the middle of the night and wakes for good at 6.00 a.m., lying in bed for the last 30 minutes. His total sleep time is 480 minus 45, 75 and 30, which is 330 minutes, or five and a half hours. His sleep efficiency is 330 divided by 480, times 100, about 69 percent. Good sleepers usually spend more than about 85 percent of their time in bed asleep, so Kishore's night is badly fragmented, and you will see in Book 2 Section 9.4.3 how sleep restriction therapy uses exactly this number.

The second family is sleep architecture, the amount and distribution of the sleep stages: the minutes of each NREM stage and of REM sleep, the percentage of total sleep each takes up, and the REM latency, the time from sleep onset to the first REM period. In a healthy young adult the first REM period comes after a full NREM cycle, so REM latency is long. When REM sleep begins less than 15 minutes after sleep onset, DSM-5-TR calls it a sleep-onset REM period (SOREMP). A SOREMP is a hallmark of narcolepsy (Section 8.8), and a shortened REM latency is also one of the classic sleep findings in depression (Book 3 Section 5.5.5).

Two daytime tests measure sleepiness and its opposite. In the multiple sleep latency test (MSLT), done the day after an overnight polysomnogram, the person lies down in a dark, quiet room and is told not to resist falling asleep. This is repeated five times across the day, with each nap opportunity starting two hours after the previous one began; a nap trial ends 15 minutes after sleep begins, or after 20 minutes if no sleep occurs. The time taken to fall asleep is measured on each trial, and the mean sleep latency is used as an index of physiological sleepiness: the sleepier the person, the faster he falls asleep. The test also counts SOREMPs, and a mean sleep latency of 8 minutes or less with two or more SOREMPs is one of DSM-5-TR's routes to a diagnosis of narcolepsy. The maintenance of wakefulness test (MWT) turns the instruction round. The person sits upright in a dimly lit room and is asked to stay awake for as long as possible, in four 40-minute trials two hours apart. It measures the ability to resist sleep, and it is used to judge whether treatment has made someone safe in jobs where falling asleep would be dangerous, such as driving.

Laboratories are expensive and unfamiliar, and a single night in one may not be typical. Two cheaper tools fill the gap. In actigraphy a wristwatch-sized device records arm movement, from which the times of sleep and waking can be estimated over one or two weeks at home. The sleep diary, kept each morning for one to two weeks, records bedtime, estimated sleep latency, awakenings, rising time, naps, caffeine and alcohol, and how rested the person feels; it is the basic tool for diagnosing and treating insomnia, because insomnia is defined by the person's own experience of sleep. Questionnaires add a quick summary. The Epworth Sleepiness Scale (Murray W. Johns, 1991) asks how likely the person is to doze in eight everyday situations, such as sitting and reading or sitting in a car stopped in traffic; the Pittsburgh Sleep Quality Index (Daniel J. Buysse and colleagues, 1989) rates sleep quality over the past month. Both are described in the Assessment book. Table 8.17 collects the vocabulary, with the disorder in which each measure matters most.

Table 8.17 The vocabulary of the sleep laboratory.
TermWhat it meansWhere it matters most
Sleep latencyMinutes from lights out to sleep onsetLong in sleep-onset insomnia; very short in narcolepsy and sleep deprivation
Wake after sleep onsetMinutes awake between first sleep and final wakingSleep-maintenance insomnia
Sleep efficiencyTime asleep divided by time in bed, as a percentageLow in insomnia; used to set sleep restriction
Sleep architectureAmounts and percentages of each stageLess N3 with age and in insomnia; altered by hypnotics

How sleep disorders are classified

For most of the twentieth century there was no agreed list of sleep disorders. The first serious attempt came from the sleep specialists themselves. In 1979 the Association of Sleep Disorders Centers and the Association for the Psychophysiological Study of Sleep published the Diagnostic Classification of Sleep and Arousal Disorders in the journal Sleep. It sorted disorders into four groups by the complaint the patient brings: disorders of initiating and maintaining sleep (the insomnias), disorders of excessive somnolence, disorders of the sleep-wake schedule, and the parasomnias. That complaint-led logic still shapes the field.

The psychiatric manuals followed. DSM-III (1980) had no sleep chapter; sleepwalking and sleep terrors appeared among the childhood disorders with physical manifestations. DSM-III-R (1987) was the first edition with a real sleep disorders section, and it introduced the division that dominated the next quarter-century. The dyssomnias were disturbances of the amount, quality or timing of sleep: the insomnia disorders, the hypersomnia disorders and sleep-wake schedule disorder. The parasomnias were abnormal events occurring during sleep: dream anxiety disorder (nightmare disorder), sleep terror disorder and sleepwalking disorder. DSM-IV (1994) kept the division within its "primary sleep disorders". Its dyssomnias were primary insomnia, primary hypersomnia, narcolepsy, breathing-related sleep disorder, circadian rhythm sleep disorder (with delayed sleep phase, jet lag, shift work and unspecified types) and dyssomnia not otherwise specified; its parasomnias were nightmare disorder, sleep terror disorder, sleepwalking disorder and parasomnia not otherwise specified. Alongside these it placed sleep disorders related to another mental disorder (insomnia or hypersomnia type), sleep disorder due to a general medical condition and substance-induced sleep disorder.

Meanwhile the sleep specialists built their own, far more detailed system. The first International Classification of Sleep Disorders (ICSD) appeared in 1990, produced by the American sleep specialists' association (now the American Academy of Sleep Medicine) with sleep societies in other countries. It kept the words dyssomnia and parasomnia but divided the dyssomnias by presumed cause into intrinsic (arising within the body, such as narcolepsy or obstructive sleep apnoea), extrinsic (caused by something outside, such as inadequate sleep hygiene or dependence on hypnotics) and circadian rhythm disorders. A revision followed in 1997, then ICSD-2 in 2005, ICSD-3 in 2014 and a text revision, ICSD-3-TR, in 2023. ICSD-2 abandoned the old two-way split for eight groupings, and ICSD-3 now has seven major categories: insomnia disorders, sleep-related breathing disorders, central disorders of hypersomnolence, circadian rhythm sleep-wake disorders, parasomnias, sleep-related movement disorders and other sleep disorders.

DSM-5 (2013) also dropped the dyssomnia heading, and it explained its approach as a matter of "lumping versus splitting". Insomnia was lumped: DSM-IV's three separate insomnias (primary, related to another mental disorder, and due to a medical condition) became one insomnia disorder, with the coexisting condition recorded as a specifier. Narcolepsy was split: DSM-5 recognises separately coded subtypes, such as type 1, with cataplexy or hypocretin deficiency, and type 2, without them. The reason for the difference is evidence. Where biological validators exist, DSM-5 uses them: low hypocretin in the cerebrospinal fluid for narcolepsy, polysomnography for the breathing-related disorders, and periodic limb movements on polysomnography alongside restless legs syndrome. Where they do not, as in insomnia, finer distinctions only added unreliability. DSM-5 was written for mental health and general medical clinicians who are not sleep experts, so it deliberately uses broader labels than ICSD-3, which elaborates many subtypes "by and for sleep specialists"; DSM-5-TR reports that the simpler approach has better interrater reliability and convergent, discriminant and face validity, and its text links each diagnosis to the matching ICSD-3 disorder.

The WHO made a bigger change still. ICD-10 split sleep disorders by presumed cause: "nonorganic" sleep disorders (insomnia, hypersomnia, sleep-wake schedule disorder, sleepwalking, sleep terrors and nightmares) sat in the mental disorders chapter as F51, while "organic" ones such as narcolepsy and sleep apnoea sat in the nervous system chapter under G47 (Table 8.2 lists the codes). ICD-11 abolished the split, which a recent comparison of the two systems judged rarely possible to make validly in routine practice, and gave the sleep-wake disorders a chapter of their own, Chapter 7, outside the mental disorders (Book 3 Section 1.9.6). Its groupings are close to ICSD-3's: insomnia disorders (7A00 chronic insomnia, 7A01 short-term insomnia), hypersomnolence disorders (beginning with 7A20 narcolepsy), sleep-related breathing disorders (7A40 central sleep apnoeas, 7A41 obstructive sleep apnoea), circadian rhythm sleep-wake disorders (7A60 to 7A65), sleep-related movement disorders (beginning with 7A80 restless legs syndrome) and parasomnia disorders (7B00 disorders of arousal from NREM sleep and 7B01 parasomnias related to REM sleep).

DSM-5-TR's sleep-wake chapter contains ten disorders or groups of disorders, set out in Table 8.18. What unites them is a complaint of dissatisfaction with the quality, timing or amount of sleep, with daytime distress and impairment as a core feature of every one. Four of them share what you can call the rule of threes: symptoms at least three times a week for at least 3 months. Insomnia disorder needs three nights a week for 3 months; hypersomnolence disorder needs excessive sleepiness three times a week for 3 months; narcolepsy needs its sleep attacks at least three times a week over the past 3 months; and restless legs syndrome needs its symptoms three times a week for 3 months. The others have no such rule. The breathing disorders are defined by counts on the polysomnogram, the circadian disorders by a persistent or recurrent mismatch (with "episodic" for 1 to under 3 months and "persistent" for 3 months or more as specifiers), and the parasomnias by "recurrent" or "repeated" episodes.

Table 8.18 The ten sleep-wake disorder groups of DSM-5-TR.
Group (section)Core complaintTime ruleDSM-IV ancestorICSD-3 and ICD-11 home
Insomnia disorder (8.7)Cannot start or keep sleep, or wakes too early3 nights a week, 3 monthsPrimary insomnia; insomnia related to another mental disorderInsomnia disorders; 7A00, 7A01
Hypersomnolence disorder (8.8)Sleepy despite at least 7 hours' sleep3 times a week, 3 monthsPrimary hypersomniaCentral disorders of hypersomnolence; hypersomnolence disorders
Narcolepsy (8.8)Irrepressible sleep, plus cataplexy, low hypocretin or short REM latency3 times a week, 3 monthsNarcolepsyCentral disorders of hypersomnolence; 7A20
