Faced with a patient, though, a clinician does not start from a table; she starts from the complaint. Almost every sleep problem comes through the door as one of three sentences: "I cannot sleep", "I cannot stay awake" or "something happens in my sleep". Figure 8.12 sorts the disorders under those three complaints, which is the way the 1979 classification began and the way this chapter is organised. Notice that sleep apnoea appears twice: it can break up the night and ruin the day.

Why do sleep disorders belong in a manual of mental disorders at all, when ICD-11 has moved them out? The introduction to DSM-5-TR's sleep-wake chapter supplies four good reasons. First, sleep disorders are often accompanied by depression, anxiety and cognitive change, which must be treated too. Second, persistent insomnia and persistent sleepiness are established risk factors for later mental illness, including substance use disorders. Third, a sleep disturbance may be the first sign of an episode of mental illness, which gives a chance to intervene early; and REM sleep behaviour disorder is often an early sign of the neurodegenerative disorders known as alpha-synucleinopathies, Parkinson's disease among them. Fourth, sleep is tied to suicide. A review of many studies found that insomnia may increase the risk of suicidal thoughts, suicidal behaviour and death even after depression is taken into account, and that nightmares increase the risk of suicidal thoughts and behaviour. In one study of college students, 31.3 percent of those with sleep problems had suicidal thoughts, and 82.7 percent of those with suicidal thoughts had sleep problems; and a consensus statement of the American Academy of Sleep Medicine concluded that in teenagers less than 8 hours of sleep is associated with more self-harm, suicidal thoughts and suicidal behaviour. Asking about sleep is therefore part of every suicide risk assessment (Book 3 Section 5.8.7).

Exam trap

ICD-11 moved the sleep-wake disorders into their own Chapter 7; they are not in Chapter 6 with the mental disorders, although the elimination disorders (6C00, 6C01) are. DSM-5-TR still includes them, as ten disorders or groups. "Dyssomnias versus parasomnias" is the DSM-III-R and DSM-IV division, not DSM-5's. The rule of threes (three times a week for 3 months) belongs to four disorders: insomnia disorder, hypersomnolence disorder, narcolepsy and restless legs syndrome. DSM-5 lumped the insomnias into one disorder and split narcolepsy into subtypes.

Insomnia disorder: the criteria

Sonal is 34 and a bank officer in Pune. Five months ago she was promoted to manage a branch with tough targets. Since then, four nights a week, she lies awake until two in the morning, going over the day's figures, turning the pillow, checking the clock at 12.40, 1.15 and 1.50. Some nights she falls asleep at last and wakes at five, unable to drift off again. By day she is irritable with her staff, makes small errors in reports and drinks four cups of strong tea to keep going. On Sundays, when she stays at her mother's flat, she sometimes sleeps well, which puzzles her. She has begun to dread her own bedroom: by nine o'clock she is already anxious about whether she will sleep. She is not depressed, she does not snore, and she takes no medicines. Does she have insomnia disorder? DSM-5-TR's criteria, in plain words and grouped by what each part does, run as follows.

The complaint (Criterion A). The person is predominantly dissatisfied with the quantity or quality of sleep, with at least one of three symptoms: difficulty initiating sleep; difficulty maintaining sleep, with frequent awakenings or trouble getting back to sleep after waking; or waking early in the morning and being unable to return to sleep. In children, the same difficulties may show as being unable to fall asleep, or to return to sleep, without a caregiver's help. Notice that the criterion is built on the person's own dissatisfaction: the diagnosis rests on the subjective experience of sleep (or a caretaker's report), not on a polysomnogram.

The threshold (Criteria B, C and D). The sleep problem causes clinically significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, educational, academic, behavioural or other important areas of functioning; it happens at least 3 nights a week; and it has been present for at least 3 months. Criterion B matters because many people, especially older adults, have broken sleep and still call themselves good sleepers; DSM-5-TR reserves the diagnosis for those with significant daytime distress or impairment, such as fatigue, poor concentration and memory, irritability or low mood.

The context (Criterion E). The sleep difficulty occurs despite adequate opportunity for sleep. This rule separates insomnia from sleep deprivation. A junior doctor on a 30-hour shift, a mother with a newborn or a student cramming for examinations who goes to bed at three and rises at six is sleeping too little, but not because she cannot sleep; she has not been given the chance.

The exclusions (Criteria F, G and H). The insomnia is not better explained by, and does not occur only during, another sleep-wake disorder such as narcolepsy, a breathing-related sleep disorder, a circadian rhythm disorder or a parasomnia; it is not caused by a drug of abuse or a medication; and coexisting mental disorders and medical conditions do not adequately explain the predominant complaint.

Sonal meets every criterion: initial and some late insomnia (A), with irritability and errors at work (B), four nights a week (C), for five months (D), with plenty of time in bed (E), and nothing else to explain it (F to H). Her tea is worth watching, but insomnia that began with a stressful promotion and persists with four cups a day is not "caused" by caffeine in the sense of Criterion G; if her sleeplessness occurred only with heavy caffeine use, the diagnosis would instead be caffeine-induced sleep disorder (Section 8.10.7).

DSM-5-TR asks for two kinds of specifier. The first records any coexisting condition: with mental disorder (including substance use disorders), with medical condition or with another sleep disorder. The second describes the time course: episodic (symptoms lasting at least 1 month but less than 3 months), persistent (3 months or longer) and recurrent (two or more episodes within a year). Read those carefully and you will notice a tension: Criterion D demands 3 months, yet "episodic" describes symptoms lasting less than 3 months. The manual's own note settles the practical question: acute or short-term insomnia, lasting less than 3 months but otherwise meeting the criteria for frequency, severity and distress or impairment, is coded as other specified insomnia disorder. The inconsistency is the manual's own, and its wording is unchanged from DSM-5. The same three course specifiers appear, word for word, for the circadian rhythm sleep-wake disorders, which have no minimum duration, so there they fit without strain; in insomnia disorder, a presentation that lasts less than 3 months falls short of Criterion D, and the note sends it to other specified insomnia disorder. For an examination, trust the criterion: insomnia disorder needs 3 months. DSM-5-TR notes that situational or acute insomnia lasting a few days to a few weeks is very common after life events or a change of schedule, and usually resolves when the precipitant passes.

The most important conceptual change came with DSM-5 and is kept in DSM-5-TR. In DSM-IV the clinician had to decide whether insomnia was primary insomnia (with no other cause) or "insomnia related to another mental disorder", or due to a medical condition; that is, to make a judgement about cause. In practice this was nearly impossible: insomnia can precede depression, accompany it, outlast it or change its relationship with it over time. DSM-5-TR therefore says that when insomnia and another disorder coexist, no causal attribution between them is needed. Insomnia disorder is diagnosed whenever the insomnia is severe enough to warrant independent clinical attention, and the coexisting condition is recorded beside it. The term preferred for this situation is comorbid insomnia. The practical point is that insomnia is treated in its own right, not just left to clear up when the "real" disorder is treated, because often it does not.

Insomnia shows itself in three forms according to the time of night: sleep-onset insomnia (initial insomnia), difficulty falling asleep at bedtime; sleep-maintenance insomnia (middle insomnia), frequent or prolonged waking through the night; and late insomnia, early-morning awakening with inability to get back to sleep. Difficulty maintaining sleep is the commonest single symptom, affecting about 60 percent of people with insomnia in a large US sample, followed by early waking and difficulty falling asleep, but a combination is the commonest presentation of all, and the form often changes over time. Difficulty falling asleep is commoner in young adults, difficulty staying asleep in the middle-aged and old. DSM-5-TR offers illustrative, admittedly arbitrary, numbers used in research: difficulty initiating sleep means a subjective sleep latency of more than 20 to 30 minutes; difficulty maintaining sleep means more than 20 to 30 minutes awake after sleep onset; and early waking means waking at least an hour before the scheduled time and before total sleep reaches six and a half hours. Bedtime must be considered: a woman who wakes at 4 a.m. after going to bed at 9 p.m. has had seven hours of sleep, which is not the same as waking at 4 a.m. after an 11 p.m. bedtime.

The pattern of insomnia in depression, with its middle and terminal waking, is taught in Book 3 Section 5.2.2. Two cautions apply here. The "decreased need for sleep" of mania (Book 3 Section 5.4) is not insomnia: the manic person sleeps three hours and feels rested and full of energy, whereas the person with insomnia lies awake wanting to sleep and suffers for it the next day. And a complaint of nonrestorative sleep alone (sleep of adequate length that leaves the person unrefreshed), without difficulty falling or staying asleep, is not insomnia disorder; it occurs in about 5 percent of people, more often in the young, and when isolated it is diagnosed as other specified sleep-wake disorder. DSM-IV had allowed nonrestorative sleep as a symptom of primary insomnia; DSM-5 removed it from the criteria.

There is one more puzzle, which every clinician meets. People with insomnia usually report longer sleep latencies, more waking and less total sleep than the polysomnogram shows. Pinel describes a study in which people with insomnia said that they took an average of an hour to fall asleep and slept only 4.5 hours a night; in the sleep laboratory they took 15 minutes to fall asleep and slept 6.5 hours. Doctors once concluded that such patients were neurotic and called the problem "pseudoinsomnia", but the practice stopped when some of them turned out to have sleep apnoea, nocturnal myoclonus (periodic limb movements) or other sleep-disturbing problems. DSM-5-TR admits that the reasons for the gap between subjective and objective sleep are not well understood, but it suggests that the hyperarousal you will meet in the next subsection plays a part: the brain of a person with insomnia probably stays partly alert in light sleep, so the night is experienced as wakeful. The lesson is that underestimating one's sleep is part of the disorder, not evidence that the person is exaggerating. As Pinel puts it, insomnia is often a problem not of too little sleep but of too little undisturbed sleep.

DSM-5-TR prints the ICD-10-CM code F51.01 for insomnia disorder, whatever the specifier. In the WHO's ICD-10 the nearest diagnosis is F51.0, nonorganic insomnia, which required the sleep problem at least three times a week for at least one month, a lower bar in duration than DSM-5-TR's (DSM-IV's primary insomnia, too, required one month). ICD-11 has chronic insomnia (7A00), frequent and persistent difficulty initiating or maintaining sleep, despite adequate opportunity, several times a week for at least 3 months, with daytime impairment, and short-term insomnia (7A01) for less than 3 months. ICSD-3 likewise distinguishes chronic insomnia disorder, short-term insomnia disorder and other insomnia disorder; its chronic insomnia disorder closely parallels DSM-5-TR's insomnia disorder.

Exam trap

Insomnia disorder needs symptoms on 3 nights a week for 3 months despite adequate opportunity for sleep, with daytime distress or impairment. Insomnia for less than 3 months is other specified insomnia disorder (ICD-11: short-term insomnia); ICD-10's nonorganic insomnia needed only one month. Lack of opportunity is sleep deprivation, not insomnia. The manic "decreased need for sleep" is not insomnia. DSM-5-TR no longer asks whether insomnia is "primary" or "secondary": it is diagnosed alongside any coexisting disorder with a specifier.

Who develops insomnia, and why

Insomnia is the commonest of all sleep disorders. Across many countries about one adult in three reports symptoms of insomnia, 10 to 15 percent have daytime impairment as well, and 4 to 22 percent, on average about 10 percent, meet the criteria for insomnia disorder. In primary care, 20 to 40 percent of patients complain of significant insomnia, and rates are higher still among people with medical and psychiatric illness, especially mood, anxiety and substance use disorders: 40 to 50 percent of people with insomnia disorder have a comorbid mental disorder. Women are affected more often than men, by about 1.3 to 1 in multinational samples, rising to 1.7 to 1 after the age of 45; in Norway the prevalence in girls aged 16 to 18 is nearly double that in boys. First onset in women is often tied to the birth of a child or to the menopause, and new insomnia at menopause may persist after the hot flushes have gone.

The first episode usually comes in young adulthood, less often in childhood or adolescence; insomnia that begins late in life is often tied to the onset of physical illness. Insomnia may be situational (lasting days or weeks after a life event), persistent or recurrent, coming back with each new stress. Once established it tends to last: chronicity rates are 45 to 75 percent over follow-ups of one to seven years, although even chronic insomnia varies from night to night, with an occasional good night among several bad ones. Many people with insomnia say they were always "light" sleepers. One finding deserves emphasis because it shows how insomnia takes on a life of its own: insomnia that arises during an episode of major depression persists after the depression has lifted in at least 40 to 50 percent of people. Persistent insomnia, in turn, at least doubles the risk of new-onset major depressive disorder, of anxiety disorders and of substance use disorders, may herald a relapse of depression, and, especially when objectively measured sleep is short (under six hours), raises the risk of hypertension, heart disease and stroke.

Why does a few weeks of stress-induced sleeplessness, which everyone has had, turn into years of insomnia for some people? The best-known answer is the 3P model of Arthur J. Spielman and his colleagues (1987), taught with its figure in Book 2 Section 4.9.7. Predisposing factors (an anxious, worry-prone temperament, a family history of poor sleep) make a person vulnerable; precipitating factors (Sonal's promotion, an illness, a bereavement) push sleep below the threshold; and perpetuating factors, the habits and beliefs the person develops to cope, keep the insomnia going after the precipitant has gone. DSM-5-TR uses the same three words, and the model's practical message is that treatment of chronic insomnia should aim at the perpetuating factors, because they are the ones still operating.

What are the perpetuating factors made of? The central idea is hyperarousal: people with chronic insomnia are physiologically and mentally over-activated, by night and by day. DSM-5-TR describes three strands. The first is cognitive arousal: a preoccupation with sleep and distress about not sleeping set up a vicious circle in which the harder the person tries to sleep, the more frustrated and alert she becomes; effort, which helps with most tasks, defeats sleep, because sleep cannot be willed. The second is conditioned arousal: after many nights of lying awake in frustration, the bed, the bedroom and the bedtime routine become cues for wakefulness instead of sleep, by the same classical conditioning that Book 1 Section 6.2 describes. That is why so many people with insomnia, like Sonal at her mother's flat, sleep better away from their own bedroom, and why they nod off in front of the television and wake up the moment they get into bed. The third is maladaptive habit: spending too long in bed "to catch up", keeping an erratic schedule, napping, and watching the clock. The physiological evidence for hyperarousal, though not perfectly consistent, points the same way: more high-frequency (fast) EEG activity around sleep onset and in NREM sleep, signs of activation of the stress hormone system such as raised cortisol, changes in heart rate variability, greater reactivity to stress and a higher metabolic rate. People with insomnia disorder also do not show increased sleepiness on daytime laboratory tests; they are tired but wired.

Key study: Bonnet and Arand's 24-hour metabolic rate (1995)

Question. Are people with insomnia simply short of sleep, or is their whole body running "hot"? Method. Michael H. Bonnet and Donna L. Arand brought 10 people with objectively confirmed insomnia and 10 normal sleepers, matched for age, sex and weight, into the sleep laboratory for 36 hours and measured whole-body oxygen consumption, an index of metabolic rate, repeatedly across the day and night. Result. The people with insomnia used more oxygen at every measurement point, by day and by night, and the night-time excess remained even when periods containing waking or arousals were removed from the analysis. Conclusion. Chronic insomnia may reflect a general, round-the-clock disorder of hyperarousal, not just a bad night. Criticism. The sample was tiny, and a 2018 systematic review found only four studies of the question, two showing raised 24-hour metabolic rate, with small differences of uncertain clinical importance. The hyperarousal idea is well supported overall, but this particular marker is not established.

A purely cognitive account comes from the clinical psychologist Allison G. Harvey, whose cognitive model of insomnia (2002) borrowed from the cognitive models of the anxiety disorders. Harvey proposed that five processes keep insomnia going, whatever started it. The person worries in bed about not sleeping and about the next day (cognitive arousal); she selectively attends to and monitors threats related to sleep, such as the clock, a racing heart or signs of tiredness the next day; she therefore misperceives her sleep and her daytime functioning as worse than they are; she holds unhelpful beliefs about sleep ("if I do not get eight hours, I will collapse at work"); and she uses counterproductive safety behaviours, actions intended to prevent a feared outcome that actually keep the problem going, such as cancelling plans, lying in bed longer or drinking more tea. Sonal's clock-checking and her four cups are textbook examples. The model's cognitive therapy is one of the components of the treatment described in Section 8.7.6.

DSM-5-TR lists the risk factors. Temperamental: an anxious or worry-prone personality or cognitive style, a predisposition to arousal, high reactivity to stress and a tendency to repress emotions. Environmental: noise, light, temperatures that are uncomfortably high or low, and high altitude, which causes periodic breathing in sleep. Genetic and physiological: female sex and advancing age; 35 to 70 percent of people with insomnia disorder have one or more first-degree relatives with insomnia, most often the mother; and insomnia is more frequent in identical than fraternal twins, although how much of the family link is genetic, how much is learned by watching a parent and how much comes through another disorder is unknown, with "sleep reactivity" to stress apparently playing a part. Course modifiers include poor sleep hygiene, such as excessive caffeine and irregular sleep schedules. Culture shapes the meaning more than the frequency: insomnia is a universal human experience, but it may be seen as a normal part of ageing or stress, managed with family support and prayer rather than medical help, and explained by humidity, poor circulation or "internal heat".

Some insomnia is made by doctors. Pinel retells a case reported by the sleep researcher William Charles Dement (1928 to 2020) in 1978. Mr B. was studying for an examination that would shape his career. Under stress and unable to sleep, he saw his physician, who prescribed a moderate dose of a barbiturate, sodium amytal, at bedtime. It worked for a few nights; after a week he slept badly again, so he took two pills. The cycle repeated twice more, and on the night before the examination he was taking four times the original dose. The next night, the pressure off, he took no pills and could not sleep at all, so he decided that he had a serious case of insomnia and went back to the pills. When he finally came to a sleep clinic some years later he was taking about 1,000 mg of sodium amytal every night, and his sleep was more disturbed than ever. This is iatrogenic insomnia, insomnia created by treatment: tolerance demands larger doses, withdrawal produces sleeplessness, and the sleeplessness is read as proof that the pills are needed. Pinel notes that patients may go on for years with ever-increasing doses of one drug after another without realising that the pills are the problem.

Before diagnosing insomnia disorder, the clinician works through the list in the first panel of Figure 8.12, and a few more. Short sleepers need little sleep, have no difficulty falling or staying asleep and no daytime symptoms; some, worried that they "should" sleep eight hours, lie in bed longer and create an insomnia-like pattern, a trap Pinel also describes. Normal age-related change, sleep deprivation from lack of opportunity, and situational insomnia come next. The delayed sleep phase type of circadian rhythm disorder produces sleep-onset insomnia only when the person tries to sleep at conventional times, and good sleep when allowed to sleep late (commonest in adolescents and young adults); the shift work type has a history of shift work (Section 8.9.5). Restless legs syndrome causes difficulty falling and staying asleep, but with an urge to move the legs and unpleasant leg sensations (Section 8.10.6). The breathing-related sleep disorders usually announce themselves with loud snoring, breathing pauses and daytime sleepiness, but as many as half of people with sleep apnoea also report insomnia, especially women and older adults, and about one in seven people with insomnia disorder has moderate to severe obstructive sleep apnoea (Section 8.9.2). Narcolepsy can cause insomnia complaints but is dominated by sleepiness, cataplexy, sleep paralysis and hallucinations (Section 8.8); in the parasomnias the abnormal events, not the insomnia, dominate (Section 8.10); and in substance/medication-induced sleep disorder, insomnia type, a substance is judged to cause the insomnia, as with insomnia occurring only with heavy coffee drinking (Section 8.10.7). One name sounds as if it belongs here and does not: fatal familial insomnia is an inherited prion disease that destroys the thalamus, not a form of insomnia disorder (Book 1 Section 5.8.2).

Insomnia keeps company with physical illness: cancer, diabetes, coronary heart disease, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, arthritis, fibromyalgia and other chronic pain, degenerative brain disease and brain injury. The relationship runs both ways, insomnia raising the risk of many of these conditions and the conditions raising the risk of insomnia. It is also strongly comorbid with bipolar, depressive and anxiety disorders, for which persistent insomnia is a risk factor and an early sign. And people with insomnia often treat themselves, with alcohol or sleeping pills at night and caffeine or stimulants by day, a pattern that worsens the insomnia and can grow into a substance use disorder (Chapter 10).

Exam trap

Insomnia disorder is the commonest sleep disorder (about 10 percent of adults meet criteria; about a third report symptoms), and it is commoner in women. Its central mechanism is hyperarousal, physiological and cognitive, with conditioned arousal to the bedroom; the 3P model is Spielman's (predisposing, precipitating, perpetuating), and treatment targets the perpetuating factors. Underestimating one's own sleep is a feature of insomnia, not malingering. Fatal familial insomnia is a prion disease, not insomnia disorder.

Treating insomnia

Pinel sorts the drugs that affect sleep into three classes. The hypnotic drugs increase sleep; antihypnotic drugs reduce it (the stimulants and some antidepressants, which belong to Section 8.8.7); and chronobiotic drugs adjust the timing of the body's internal rhythms rather than producing sleep directly, the chief example being melatonin. For insomnia the hypnotics are the main concern. Table 8.19 summarises them.

The benzodiazepines, such as diazepam and clonazepam, were developed for anxiety but became some of the most commonly prescribed sleeping pills; those marketed mainly as hypnotics include temazepam, triazolam, flurazepam, estazolam and quazepam. They enhance the action of GABA at the GABA-A receptor, as Book 3 Section 6.9.2 explains, and in the short term they make people drowsy, shorten sleep latency, reduce awakenings and lengthen total sleep, so they can help occasional sleeplessness. Their half-lives differ enormously, and the difference matters: triazolam has a half-life of only about 2 to 4 hours, so it helps sleep onset with little hangover, while flurazepam and quazepam have half-lives of 48 to 120 hours, so the drug from tonight's dose is still acting tomorrow afternoon. Pinel lists five complications of using benzodiazepines as hypnotics for long periods. Tolerance develops, so larger doses are needed for the same effect. Stopping them after chronic use causes rebound insomnia, which worsens the very problem they were meant to treat and persuades the patient to restart. They distort the architecture of sleep, increasing N2 and decreasing both slow-wave sleep and REM sleep. They cause next-day drowsiness and an increased risk of traffic accidents. And, most troubling, chronic use has been associated with a substantially reduced life expectancy. Add the risk of dependence and misuse, and you have the reason Barlow and Durand give for the standard advice: these drugs are for short-term problems, such as sleeplessness during a hospital stay, and should not be used for more than about 4 weeks.

The search for a safer pill produced the non-benzodiazepine benzodiazepine-receptor agonists, nicknamed the Z-drugs: zolpidem, zaleplon, zopiclone and its active half, eszopiclone. Zolpidem, an imidazopyridine, was marketed in the early 1990s with the claim that it had fewer side effects and less addictive potential. It acts at the same benzodiazepine binding site on the GABA-A receptor, and Pinel concludes that it has been found no safer or more effective than the benzodiazepines. The Z-drugs have short half-lives (zaleplon about 1 hour, zolpidem 1.5 to 2.4 hours, eszopiclone 5 to 7 hours). They also carry a peculiar risk. In 2002 Timothy I. Morgenthaler and Michael H. Silber described sleep-related eating, with amnesia, in people taking zolpidem, and reports followed of sleepwalking, sleep-driving and other complex sleep behaviours carried out without awareness. In April 2019 the US Food and Drug Administration added its strongest warning, a boxed warning, to eszopiclone, zaleplon and zolpidem after reports of serious injuries and deaths from such behaviours, and advised that anyone who has had such an episode should not take them again (Section 8.10.2 discusses sleep-related eating).

Melatonin is a hormone made from serotonin in the pineal gland, released at night under the control of the suprachiasmatic nucleus. Because its level is highest in darkness and sleep, it has long been assumed to promote sleep, and it is widely sold for that purpose. Pinel is sceptical: removing the pineal gland in adult mammals has little effect, and a meta-analysis of 19 studies by Eduardo Ferracioli-Oda, Ahmad Qawasmi and Michael Bloch (2013) found only a slight, though statistically significant, sleep-promoting effect. There is better evidence that melatonin shifts the timing of the body clock, so many researchers think it is better classified as a chronobiotic than as a hypnotic; its use for jet lag and the circadian disorders is in Section 8.9.6. Ramelteon, a drug that stimulates melatonin receptors, was approved in the United States in 2005 for difficulty falling asleep; it has a short half-life (about 1 to 2.6 hours) and, unlike the benzodiazepines and Z-drugs, it is not a controlled substance.

The newest class works on a different system altogether. You saw in Section 8.7.1 that orexin (hypocretin) neurons in the hypothalamus hold the brain in the waking position, and Section 8.8.5 explains that losing them causes narcolepsy. The orexin receptor antagonists block that wake-stabilising signal, so sleep can take over: suvorexant (approved in the United States in 2014), lemborexant (2019) and daridorexant (2022). They act by lowering wakefulness rather than by sedating the whole brain through GABA, and next-day sleepiness is their main side effect; suvorexant's label excludes people with narcolepsy, for the obvious reason that they already lack orexin.

Several drugs developed for other purposes are used for their sedative side effects. Sedating antidepressants in low doses, chiefly trazodone, mirtazapine and doxepin, make people drowsy mainly by blocking histamine receptors (trazodone also blocks a serotonin receptor, 5-HT2A); a very low dose of doxepin was approved in the United States in 2010 for difficulty staying asleep. Their classes and other effects are in Book 3 Section 5.7.2. The sedating antihistamines, such as diphenhydramine, are the active ingredients of many sleep aids sold without prescription. Tolerance to their sedative effect develops quickly, and their anticholinergic effects (dry mouth, constipation, retention of urine and confusion) make them risky for older people. The barbiturates, such as Mr B.'s sodium amytal, were the sleeping pills of the first half of the twentieth century; tolerance, dependence and a narrow margin between a sleeping dose and a fatal one have retired them from the treatment of insomnia.

Table 8.19 Drugs used for insomnia.
ClassExamples (half-life in hours)How it actsMain problems
BenzodiazepinesTriazolam (2 to 4), temazepam (8 to 20), estazolam (8 to 24), flurazepam and quazepam (48 to 120)Enhance GABA at the GABA-A receptorTolerance, rebound insomnia, less N3 and REM, next-day drowsiness, accidents, dependence; under 4 weeks
Z-drugsZaleplon (1), zolpidem (1.5 to 2.4), eszopiclone (5 to 7), zopicloneBenzodiazepine site of the GABA-A receptorNo safer than benzodiazepines (Pinel); complex sleep behaviours (US boxed warning, 2019)
Melatonin and ramelteonMelatonin; ramelteon (1 to 2.6)Melatonin receptors; shift the body clockWeak hypnotic effect; melatonin is mainly a chronobiotic
Orexin receptor antagonistsSuvorexant, lemborexant, daridorexantBlock the wake-promoting orexin (hypocretin) systemNext-day sleepiness; not for people with narcolepsy

Sedating antihistaminesDiphenhydramineBlock histamine receptorsRapid tolerance; anticholinergic confusion in older people
Barbiturates (historical)Sodium amytalEnhance GABA-ATolerance, dependence, lethal in overdose; no longer used for insomnia

If the drugs are so limited, what is the first-line treatment for chronic insomnia? It is psychological: cognitive behavioural therapy for insomnia (CBT-I), which major guidelines, including the American College of Physicians in 2016, recommend for adults with chronic insomnia ahead of sleeping pills. It is a package aimed squarely at the perpetuating factors of Spielman's model and Harvey's processes. Its components are named here and taught in Book 2: stimulus control (Richard R. Bootzin, 1972), which re-establishes the bed as a cue for sleep and is the direct answer to conditioned arousal; sleep restriction (Spielman, Saskin and Thorpy, 1987), which limits time in bed to the time actually slept and then widens it as sleep efficiency rises; cognitive therapy for beliefs and worries about sleep; relaxation training against physiological arousal; and sleep hygiene education, which is part of the package but weak on its own (Book 2 Sections 4.9.7 and 9.4.3). Paradoxical intention, from the logotherapy of Viktor Emil Frankl (1905 to 1997), asks the person to lie in bed in the dark and try to stay awake, which removes the performance anxiety of trying to sleep; it is used mainly for sleep-onset insomnia (Book 2 Sections 4.10.1 and 6.5.5). The evidence for CBT-I is rated strong (Book 2 Section 5.11.1), and its great advantage over hypnotics is durability: its gains last after treatment ends, whereas the benefit of a sleeping pill fades when the pill is stopped, and often before. Sonal's psychologist would begin with a two-week sleep diary, a fixed rising time and a rule to leave the bedroom if she has not fallen asleep in about twenty minutes; her tea would move to the morning only.

Clinical link

Mr B. shows the hypnotic trap in a single life: a sensible short-term prescription during a crisis, tolerance within a week, a dose escalated without advice, rebound insomnia on the first night without pills read as proof of "real" insomnia, and years of worse sleep on enormous doses. The lessons for practice are simple. Prescribe hypnotics, if at all, for short periods and the smallest effective dose; warn the patient in advance that the first nights off the drug may be bad and that this is rebound, not relapse; withdraw long-term users slowly; and offer CBT-I, which treats the habits and beliefs that keep insomnia going rather than the single night.

Exam trap

CBT-I, not a sleeping pill, is first-line for chronic insomnia in adults. Benzodiazepines and Z-drugs are for short-term use (under about 4 weeks); chronic use brings tolerance, rebound insomnia, distorted sleep (less slow-wave and REM sleep) and next-day impairment. Melatonin is mainly a chronobiotic, a clock-shifter, with only a slight hypnotic effect. A man told to lie in the dark and try to stay awake is receiving paradoxical intention (Frankl); "go to bed only when sleepy and get up if you cannot sleep" is stimulus control (Bootzin); limiting time in bed to time asleep is sleep restriction (Spielman). Sleep hygiene alone is not CBT-I.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. The minimum developmental age is years for enuresis and years for encopresis.
  2. Encopresis requires at least one event a month for at least months.
  3. Bedwetting only at night, with no daytime symptoms, is called enuresis.
  4. Desmopressin is a synthetic analogue of the antidiuretic hormone .
  5. In the commonest form of encopresis, liquid stool leaks around an impacted mass, which is called incontinence.
  6. Sleep efficiency is total sleep time divided by time in , expressed as a percentage.
  7. REM sleep beginning less than 15 minutes after sleep onset is a sleep-onset REM .
  8. The test in which a person lies in a dark room and does not resist sleep, five times across the day, is the test.
  9. Insomnia disorder requires symptoms on at least 3 nights a week for at least months despite adequate opportunity for sleep.
  10. Because it mainly shifts the timing of the body clock, melatonin is better classified as a than as a hypnotic.

Answers: (1) 5; 4, (2) 3, (3) monosymptomatic, (4) vasopressin, (5) overflow, (6) bed, (7) period, (8) multiple sleep latency, (9) 3, (10) chronobiotic.

8.8 Hypersomnolence Disorder, Narcolepsy and Kleine-Levin SyndromeLower yield · 1 Q

Insomnia, the subject of Section 8.7, is the complaint of a person who cannot sleep. This section turns the problem upside down. Its patients do sleep, sometimes for ten or twelve hours at a stretch, and they are still sleepy. They nod off in lectures, at the wheel, over a plate of food and, in the most dramatic disorder of the section, in the middle of a laugh. Sleep medicine calls the complaint excessive daytime sleepiness, and it is far more dangerous than it sounds, because a person who falls asleep at the wrong moment can kill himself or somebody else on the road. DSM-5-TR gives the disorders of sleepiness two full entries, hypersomnolence disorder and narcolepsy, and mentions a rare third, Kleine-Levin syndrome, only in passing. Before any of them can be diagnosed, though, the clinician has to answer a humbler question. Is this person sleepy because something has gone wrong with the brain's control of sleep, or simply because he is not sleeping enough?

Too much sleep, or too little? Sleepiness and its causes

Start with two words that patients use interchangeably and clinicians must not. What sleep specialists call excessive sleepiness (hypersomnolence) is an increased tendency to fall asleep: the eyelids droop, the head nods, and when the person sits still in a warm room he drifts off, sometimes without meaning to. By contrast, fatigue is a feeling of tiredness, weariness or lack of energy that does not necessarily end in sleep. DSM-5-TR draws the line sharply: the tiredness of fatigue, which is a symptom of many mental disorders (generalised anxiety disorder and depression among them) and of medical conditions such as chronic fatigue syndrome, is not relieved by more sleep and has nothing to do with how long or how well the person sleeps. A simple bedside question separates the two. Ask, "If you sat down in a quiet room after lunch, would you fall asleep?" The sleepy patient says yes, often with embarrassment. The fatigued patient says, "I feel completely drained, but I could not sleep if I tried." The Epworth Sleepiness Scale, which asks how likely a person is to doze in everyday situations such as reading or sitting as a passenger in a car, puts the same question in questionnaire form (the scale itself belongs to the Assessment book).

Once sleepiness is established, the commonest cause by far is the most ordinary one: not enough sleep. DSM-5-TR points out that an average of fewer than 7 hours a night strongly suggests inadequate sleep, and yet the average adult in the United States gets only about 6.75 hours on a typical weeknight. People who are short of sleep give themselves away by their weekends and holidays, when they "catch up" with much longer sleeps. Sleep specialists call this pattern behaviourally induced insufficient sleep syndrome: sleepiness produced by habitually cutting sleep short, through work, study, a long commute, a screen or a social life, rather than by any disorder of the brain. The International Classification of Sleep Disorders counts it among the central disorders of hypersomnolence, and ICD-11 lists it as insufficient sleep syndrome (7A26). DSM-5-TR is firm that hypersomnolence disorder should not be diagnosed if there is any doubt about whether the person sleeps enough at night, and it recommends a simple experiment to settle the doubt: a diagnostic and therapeutic trial of sleep extension for 10 to 14 days. If the sleepiness disappears when the person is given enough time in bed, the diagnosis was insufficient sleep all along.

Karthik, a 19-year-old engineering student in Chennai, shows how the reasoning runs. He was brought to the college counsellor after falling asleep in every lecture of the semester and, more frighteningly, twice on his scooter while waiting at traffic lights; the second time the driver behind him had to sound his horn for a long time before he woke. His parents feared epilepsy, and a relative had suggested that he was "weak" and needed a tonic. A two-week sleep diary told a different story. Karthik went to bed between 1 and 2 a.m. after finishing assignments and playing online games with friends, and rose at 6.15 a.m. to catch the college bus for a 7.30 class: about five hours a night from Monday to Saturday. On Sundays he slept until noon. He had no snoring, no attacks of weakness and no strange experiences on falling asleep. The counsellor negotiated a two-week trial in which the games stopped at 10.30 p.m., his phone stayed in the kitchen and he was in bed by 11. By the second week he was awake through his lectures. Karthik did not have a sleep disorder; he had a sleep debt, and the cure was to pay it.

At the other end of the normal range are long sleepers, people who need more sleep than average, nine or ten hours a night, but who wake refreshed when they get it. DSM-5-TR notes that they have no excessive sleepiness, no difficulty waking and no automatic behaviour as long as they obtain their required amount; they become sleepy only when work or study forces them to sleep less. Being a long sleeper is a variation, not a disorder, in the same way that being tall is not a disease.

When enough sleep has been ruled in and sleepiness persists, the clinician works through a list of conditions that cause it. Obstructive sleep apnoea, the commonest medical cause of daytime sleepiness, fragments sleep with hundreds of brief awakenings that the sleeper does not remember (Section 8.9). A body clock that runs late makes the morning a misery while the evening is alert (Section 8.9.4). Depression, especially major depressive disorder with atypical features, can bring long sleep and heavy limbs (Chapter 5, Section 5.3.8). Many medicines sedate, among them antipsychotics, sedating antidepressants, antihistamines and the benzodiazepines, and drugs of abuse cause sleepiness both in intoxication and in withdrawal (Section 8.10.7). Neurological diseases such as multiple sclerosis and Parkinson's disease, some cancers and the months after a traumatic brain injury all bring sleepiness too. ICD-11 gathers these into its block of hypersomnolence disorders, with separate codes for hypersomnia due to a medical condition, due to a medication or substance and associated with a mental disorder. Only when all of these have been considered does the clinician arrive at the small group that the International Classification of Sleep Disorders calls the central disorders of hypersomnolence: disorders in which the brain's own machinery for staying awake is at fault. The two that matter most are hypersomnolence disorder and narcolepsy.

Exam trap

Sleepiness is a tendency to fall asleep; fatigue is tiredness that sleep does not relieve (generalised anxiety disorder, depression, chronic fatigue syndrome). The commonest cause of daytime sleepiness is simply insufficient sleep, so the first question is always "How many hours do you actually sleep?", and fewer than 7 hours a night points to sleep debt. A long sleeper who needs 9 or 10 hours and feels fine after them has no disorder.

Hypersomnolence disorder

Chitra was 27 and taught Class 4 at a school in Pune. She slept from 10 p.m. to 8 a.m. on school nights, longer at weekends, and still could not stay awake. She set four alarms and slept through all of them; her husband had learned to shake her, open the curtains and come back five minutes later, because on waking she was confused, slow and once, memorably, irritable enough to throw a pillow at him without any memory of it afterwards. She had put the milk away in the bookshelf more than once. After school she lay down "for twenty minutes" and woke two hours later feeling worse than before. In the staff room she dozed over her corrections, and she had stopped driving to her mother's house in Satara after finding herself on the expressway with no memory of the previous few kilometres. The sleepiness had crept up on her at about 19, in her first year of college. She had never collapsed with laughter, never been paralysed on waking and never seen anything strange as she fell asleep. A night in the sleep laboratory showed nearly ten hours of normal, efficient sleep with no breathing pauses; the next day her average time to fall asleep across five naps was 7 minutes, with no REM sleep in any of them.

Chitra has hypersomnolence disorder (DSM-5-TR code F51.11), the DSM name for what sleep specialists, the International Classification of Sleep Disorders and ICD-11 (7A21) call idiopathic hypersomnia, sleepiness of unknown cause. DSM-IV had called it primary hypersomnia, and ICD-10 placed its nonorganic form at F51.1 (Table 8.2). The DSM-5-TR criteria can be grouped into four parts.

  • The core (criterion A). The person reports excessive sleepiness despite a main sleep period of at least 7 hours, and shows at least one of three forms of it: recurrent periods of sleep or lapses into sleep within the same day; a prolonged main sleep of more than 9 hours a day that is nonrestorative (the person does not wake refreshed); or difficulty becoming fully awake after being woken abruptly. The 7-hour floor is the most important phrase in the whole criteria set, because it rules out the sleep debt you met in Karthik. The three forms describe three faces of the disorder: sleep attacks by day, too much sleep by night, and a brain that cannot get out of sleep.
  • Persistence (criterion B). The sleepiness occurs at least three times a week for at least 3 months, the "rule of threes" that hypersomnolence shares with insomnia disorder, narcolepsy and restless legs syndrome (Section 8.7). A bad week before an examination does not count.
  • Harm (criterion C). The sleepiness causes significant distress or impairment in cognitive, social, occupational or other functioning.
  • Exclusions (criteria D, E and F). The sleepiness is not better explained by, and does not occur only during, another sleep disorder such as narcolepsy, a breathing-related sleep disorder, a circadian rhythm sleep-wake disorder or a parasomnia; it is not caused by a drug of abuse or a medication; and any coexisting mental or medical disorder does not adequately explain it.

Three sets of specifiers follow. The clinician may record that the hypersomnolence occurs with a mental disorder (including a substance use disorder), with a medical condition or with another sleep disorder, coding the companion condition alongside, so that a person whose sleepiness far exceeds what his depression could explain can carry both diagnoses. Duration is specified as acute (less than 1 month), subacute (1 to 3 months) or persistent (more than 3 months). Severity is graded by how many days a week the person has difficulty keeping alert, with repeated attacks of irresistible sleepiness while sitting, driving, visiting friends or working: mild on 1 to 2 days a week, moderate on 3 to 4 and severe on 5 to 7.

What does the disorder look like from the inside? DSM-5-TR's description reads almost like Chitra's history. People with hypersomnolence disorder fall asleep quickly and sleep well, with a sleep efficiency (the proportion of time in bed actually spent asleep) above 90 percent. Their sleepiness builds up gradually over a period of time rather than striking as a sudden "attack". Unintended sleep comes first in sedentary situations (lectures, reading, television, long drives) and, as the disorder worsens, in situations that demand attention, such as meetings and social gatherings. The constant pressure of sleep can produce automatic behaviour: routine, low-complexity actions carried out with little or no later memory, so that a driver may find herself several kilometres down the road with no recollection of the drive.

About 40 percent of patients have sleep inertia, also called "sleep drunkenness": a period after waking, from the main sleep or from a nap, in which the person appears awake but is confused, poorly coordinated, sometimes combative or ataxic, disoriented in time and place and unable to remember what happened. It may last from minutes to hours, and DSM-5-TR notes that it helps to separate hypersomnolence disorder from other causes of sleepiness. Patients set several alarms or rely on someone else to get them out of bed. In some the main sleep lasts 9 hours or more, and in the most extreme cases sleep episodes can run up to 20 hours, yet the sleep is unrefreshing and is followed by great difficulty waking. Others sleep a normal 7 to 9 hours but take long daytime naps, more than an hour, that do not restore alertness. Most nap almost every day. Many manage to cut their sleep on working days but sleep up to 3 hours longer at weekends and on holidays. About 80 percent describe their sleep as nonrestorative, although that complaint is not specific, since sleep apnoea produces it too. A subset have a family history of hypersomnolence together with signs of autonomic nervous system dysfunction: recurrent vascular-type headaches, Raynaud's phenomenon (fingers that blanch and turn blue in the cold) and fainting.

Hypersomnolence disorder is diagnosed in about 5 to 10 percent of people who attend sleep clinics in the United States complaining of daytime sleepiness, and about 1 percent of the general population of Europe and the United States have episodes of sleep inertia. Men and women are affected about equally. The disorder usually begins in late adolescence or early adulthood, with a mean age of onset of 17 to 24 years, and develops gradually over weeks to months; it is rare in children. Little is known of its natural history, but for most people the symptoms are persistent and stable unless treated, and spontaneous remission occurs in about 11 to 25 percent after 5 to 7 years. Because sleepiness is so easily mistaken for laziness, most people are diagnosed on average 10 to 15 years after their first symptoms. Stress and alcohol can make the sleepiness temporarily worse, though neither has been shown to cause the disorder; a viral infection preceded or accompanied the onset in about 10 percent of cases; hypersomnolence is common in the months after a traumatic brain injury; and in some families the disorder seems to pass down in an autosomal dominant pattern.

The sleep laboratory confirms the picture (the methods, polysomnography and the multiple sleep latency test, are explained in Section 8.7.2). Overnight polysomnography shows a normal or long sleep, a short time to fall asleep, good continuity and a normal distribution of REM sleep, with sleep efficiency typically above 90 percent. The multiple sleep latency test (MSLT) usually gives a mean sleep latency below 10 minutes, often 8 minutes or less, but sleep-onset REM periods (REM sleep within minutes of falling asleep in a nap) are infrequent. The MSLT has an important weakness: its test-retest reliability is poor, and it does not distinguish well between hypersomnolence disorder and the milder form of narcolepsy, narcolepsy type 2. A two-week sleep diary and actigraphy (a wrist-worn movement recorder) document how much the person habitually sleeps, and in one research protocol in which people were allowed to stay in bed for 32 hours and sleep as much as they liked, those with hypersomnolence disorder slept more than 4 hours longer than healthy controls.

The differential diagnosis follows directly from Section 8.8.1. Long sleepers are refreshed by their long sleep; people with insufficient sleep improve when given time to sleep; narcolepsy brings refreshing short naps, cataplexy and the other REM phenomena described below; fatigue is not relieved by sleep; sleep apnoea shows its breathing pauses on polysomnography; the circadian disorders have a characteristic timing (the person with a delayed clock is sleepy in the morning and alert at night); and parasomnias such as sleepwalking rarely produce long undisturbed sleep or daytime sleepiness. When sleepiness is part of a major depressive episode, especially one with atypical features, or of a medical condition, hypersomnolence disorder is added only if the sleepiness clearly exceeds what that condition would explain. The comorbidities are revealing. Many patients have depressive symptoms, perhaps a consequence of living with an endless need for sleep; more than half have symptoms of attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder; some misuse stimulants to medicate themselves; and neurodegenerative diseases such as Alzheimer's disease, Parkinson's disease and multiple system atrophy can be accompanied by hypersomnolence.

Treatment borrows from narcolepsy. Physicians usually prescribe a wake-promoting drug, a stimulant such as methylphenidate or the newer agent modafinil, and the American Academy of Sleep Medicine's practice parameters of 2007 (Morgenthaler and colleagues) judged modafinil effective for the sleepiness of idiopathic hypersomnia. The response is often only partial, and because naps do not refresh these patients, the scheduled naps that help in narcolepsy are of little use. A regular sleep schedule, honest attention to sleep quantity, and practical adjustments at work and on the road complete the plan. The drugs are described in Section 8.8.7. Chitra was started on modafinil and stopped driving long distances alone; she now teaches through the morning without dozing, though she still needs her four alarms.

Exam trap

Hypersomnolence disorder: sleepy despite at least 7 hours of main sleep, with daytime lapses, more than 9 hours of unrefreshing sleep, or difficulty waking (sleep inertia); at least 3 times a week for 3 months. Its other name is idiopathic hypersomnia (DSM-IV: primary hypersomnia). It has no cataplexy, ever, and its naps are long and unrefreshing, whereas the brief naps of narcolepsy refresh. An option that reads "excessive daytime sleepiness without nocturnal symptoms" describes a disorder of hypersomnolence, not REM sleep behaviour disorder, whose defining feature is dream enactment at night (Section 8.10).

Narcolepsy: from Gélineau to hypocretin

Narcolepsy is one of the few psychiatric and neurological disorders whose story runs all the way from a bedside description to a missing molecule, and the story is worth telling because each step explains a symptom. It begins in Berlin. In 1877 Carl Westphal (1833 to 1890), the neurologist and psychiatrist whom you may remember for naming agoraphobia, published a report in German of a 36-year-old bookbinder who suffered both recurrent attacks of sleep and attacks in which a strong emotion made his body give way beneath him. His mother, Westphal noted, had similar attacks (although she had also once been struck on the head by a falling brick), so the report also gives an early hint that the disorder can run in families.

Three years later, in 1880, the French physician Jean-Baptiste-Édouard Gélineau (1828 to 1906) described a 38-year-old barrel merchant who fell asleep as many as two hundred times a day. Sleep came on him whatever he was doing, and so did a second kind of attack: when he laughed out loud, or even when he was looking forward to a good business deal, his legs gave way and he sank to the ground. Gélineau called these falls "astasia", an inability to stand, and he gave the whole condition a name built from two Greek words, narkē, numbness or stupor, and lēpsis, an attack or seizure: narcolepsy. Both case reports were translated into English for the first time only in 2007, by Carlos Schenck, Claudio Bassetti, Isabelle Arnulf and Emmanuel Mignot, who concluded from the details that both of these nineteenth-century patients very probably had the disorder we now call narcolepsy type 1.

The attacks of weakness acquired their modern name in 1916, when the German neurologist Henneberg called them cataplexy, from the Greek for being struck down. In 1957 two neurologists at the Mayo Clinic in the United States, R. E. Yoss and D. D. Daly, published criteria for diagnosing the narcoleptic syndrome and drew attention to four symptoms that tend to occur together: sleep attacks, cataplexy, sleep paralysis and hypnagogic hallucinations. This combination is still taught as the narcoleptic tetrad. The next clue came from the new science of sleep recording. In 1960 Gerald Vogel, studying the dreams of a narcoleptic patient, found that his patient fell straight from waking into REM sleep, skipping the hour or more of NREM sleep through which healthy sleepers pass before their first REM period (Book 1, Section 5.7.4). That observation turned narcolepsy from a curiosity into a disorder of REM sleep, and every later discovery has built on it.

The final chapter of the story was written by dogs. Narcolepsy occurs naturally in several breeds of dog, and at Stanford University, where William Charles Dement had built one of the world's first sleep disorders centres, a colony of narcoleptic dogs was kept and bred for research over many years. In Doberman pinschers and Labrador retrievers the disorder passes from parent to pup as a single recessive gene, which made it possible, in principle, to find that gene. The Key study box below tells how it was found, and how the finding was carried from dogs to people within a single year.

Key study: from narcoleptic dogs to a missing peptide (1998 to 2000)

In early 1998 two laboratories independently discovered a new signalling peptide made only by a small group of neurons in the hypothalamus. Luis de Lecea and his colleagues in California called the new peptide hypocretin, because it came from the hypothalamus and resembled the gut hormone secretin; Takeshi Sakurai, Masashi Yanagisawa and their colleagues in Dallas, Texas, called it orexin, from the Greek orexis, appetite, because injecting it into rats made the animals eat. The two names describe the same molecule, which exists in two forms, orexin-A (hypocretin-1) and orexin-B (hypocretin-2). Nobody yet suspected that they had anything to do with sleep.

Meanwhile, at Stanford, a team led by Emmanuel Mignot had spent about ten years hunting the gene that causes narcolepsy in the colony's Dobermans and Labradors. In August 1999 Lin, Mignot and their colleagues reported in the journal Cell that canine narcolepsy is caused by a mutation in the gene for the hypocretin (orexin) receptor 2: the dogs made the peptide, but their brains could not receive its message. Two weeks later the Dallas group (Chemelli and colleagues, 1999) reported that mice engineered to lack orexin altogether showed, by both behavioural and EEG criteria, a picture strikingly similar to human narcolepsy; they also found that modafinil, a drug long used for narcolepsy without anyone knowing quite how it worked, activates the orexin neurons. Two independent routes, a broken receiver in dogs and a missing signal in mice, led to the same system.

The decisive test was in people. In January 2000 Seiji Nishino, Mignot and their colleagues reported that hypocretin could not be detected in the cerebrospinal fluid of seven out of nine people with narcolepsy. Later that year Peyron and colleagues found a general absence of hypocretin peptides in the brains of people who had died with narcolepsy, and Thannickal and colleagues, working in the laboratory of Jerome Siegel in Los Angeles, counted the hypocretin neurons themselves: narcoleptic brains had lost 85 to 95 percent of them, while a neighbouring population of neurons was untouched, and the scarring around the area suggested that the cells had been destroyed rather than never formed.

What it means. Human narcolepsy, unlike the dogs', is rarely caused by a faulty gene. It is caused by the loss, usually in adolescence or early adulthood, of a small cluster of hypothalamic cells that make a single wake-promoting peptide. That discovery explained the symptoms, gave the disorder a laboratory test (the measurement of hypocretin-1 in the cerebrospinal fluid, now written into the DSM-5-TR criteria) and opened the way to new drugs, including the orexin-blocking hypnotics described in Section 8.7.6.

Book 1, Section 5.8.4, gave the bare outline of this history in a paragraph. The rest of this section uses it to explain, symptom by symptom, why people with narcolepsy experience what they do.

Exam trap

Gélineau (1880) named narcolepsy; Westphal (1877) described it first; Henneberg (1916) named cataplexy; Yoss and Daly (1957) assembled the tetrad. Hypocretin and orexin are two names for the same peptide, coined in 1998 by two different groups (de Lecea; Sakurai). The dogs (Lin and colleagues, 1999) had a mutation in the receptor gene; human patients have lost the neurons that make the peptide (2000). Do not confuse orexin with leptin, ghrelin or melatonin.

Narcolepsy: criteria, types and the clinical picture

Feroz was 24 and worked the day shift at a call centre in Gurugram. His team leader thought he was lazy, and his colleagues thought he was secretly drinking. Between calls, and sometimes during them, his head dropped onto the desk; he slept for ten or fifteen minutes and woke feeling clear and alert, only to be fighting sleep again an hour later. He fell asleep on the Metro and woke at the end of the line, and once fell asleep with a spoonful of rice halfway to his mouth. Stranger still were the attacks when he laughed. If a colleague told a good joke, Feroz felt his face go slack, his jaw dropped open and his knees buckled; twice he slid to the floor of the canteen, fully aware of everything and able to hear the laughter turn to alarm, but unable to move or speak for half a minute. Some mornings he woke unable to move a finger, with a dark figure standing beside the bed that seemed to press down on his chest; his mother had taken him to a healer, convinced that something had possessed him. His sleepiness had begun at 17, in the year of his board examinations; the attacks with laughter came about a year later.

Feroz has all four symptoms of the tetrad, and his story maps directly onto the DSM-5-TR criteria for narcolepsy, which come in two parts. Criterion A is the sleepiness itself: recurrent periods of an irrepressible need to sleep, lapsing into sleep or napping within the same day, occurring at least three times a week over the past 3 months. Criterion B demands at least one of three pieces of evidence that the sleepiness comes from the narcoleptic process rather than from sleep debt or another disorder. Think of them as a clinical sign, a chemical sign and a laboratory sign.

  1. Cataplexy, occurring at least a few times a month. In people who have had the disease for some time, cataplexy means brief episodes, lasting seconds to minutes, of sudden loss of muscle tone on both sides of the body with consciousness preserved, brought on by laughter or joking. In children, or within 6 months of onset, it may look different: spontaneous grimaces or episodes of jaw opening with the tongue thrust out, or a general floppiness (hypotonia) with no obvious emotional trigger.
  2. Hypocretin deficiency, measured as hypocretin-1 in the cerebrospinal fluid (obtained by lumbar puncture): a value at or below one-third of the values found in healthy people tested with the same assay, or at or below 110 pg/mL. A low value does not count if it is measured during an acute brain injury, inflammation or infection, which can lower hypocretin temporarily.
  3. A REM sleep abnormality in the laboratory: either overnight polysomnography showing a REM sleep latency of 15 minutes or less, or an MSLT showing a mean sleep latency of 8 minutes or less together with two or more sleep-onset REM periods (SOREMPs), naps in which REM sleep appears within minutes of falling asleep.

The criteria then divide the disorder into types according to which evidence is present. The first, narcolepsy type 1, narcolepsy with cataplexy or hypocretin deficiency (code G47.411), is diagnosed when B1 or B2 is met. The second, narcolepsy type 2, narcolepsy without cataplexy and either without hypocretin deficiency or with hypocretin unmeasured (G47.419), is diagnosed when only the laboratory criterion, B3, is met. In practice type 1 is usually diagnosed from sleepiness plus cataplexy, because lumbar puncture for hypocretin is rarely done. Cataplexy can appear years after the sleepiness, so a person first diagnosed with type 2 may be reclassified as type 1 when it does, and normal or intermediate hypocretin levels in a person with cataplexy can fall to undetectable levels over time. Two further subtypes, type 1 or type 2 due to a medical condition (G47.421 and G47.429), cover secondary narcolepsy, for which the underlying condition is coded first. Inherited disorders, tumours and head trauma are its commonest causes; hypocretin neurons can also be destroyed by hypothalamic surgery, multiple sclerosis, encephalitis or stroke, and cataplexy with some sleepiness occurs in rare genetic conditions such as Prader-Willi syndrome, Niemann-Pick disease type C, Möbius syndrome and Norrie disease. Severity is rated as mild (naps needed once or twice a day, little disturbance of night sleep, cataplexy less than once a week), moderate (several naps a day, moderately disturbed nights, cataplexy daily or every few days) or severe (nearly constant sleepiness, often highly disturbed nights with excessive movement and vivid dreams, and cataplexy that resists drugs, with several attacks a day).

Now look at the clinical picture symptom by symptom. In about 90 percent of cases the first symptom is sleepiness or an increased need for sleep. Cataplexy follows within a year in half of patients and within 3 years in 85 percent, although Barlow and Durand note that it can lag behind the sleepiness by as much as 30 years. The sleepiness is worst in sedentary situations and, unlike that of hypersomnolence disorder, is typically relieved by brief naps of 10 to 20 minutes. John Pinel captures what makes it unusual. People with narcolepsy, he points out, sleep only about an hour a day more than average; what defines them is the inappropriateness of their sleep. Most of us fall asleep in a stuffy, dimly lit lecture hall, but people with narcolepsy fall asleep in the middle of a conversation, while eating, while scuba diving or even while having sex. When sleepiness is severe, automatic behaviour appears: the person carries on with an activity in a hazy, semi-automatic fashion and has no memory of it afterwards.

Cataplexy is the symptom that makes narcolepsy unmistakable. DSM-5-TR describes typical attacks as brief, from seconds up to 2 minutes, in which muscle tone is suddenly lost on both sides of the body in response to emotion. Positive emotions are the usual triggers, above all laughter, but also anticipation or surprise; less often anger or embarrassment set it off. The muscles of the neck, jaw, arms, legs or the whole body may be affected, so that the head bobs, the jaw drops, the knees buckle or the person falls. Throughout, the person is awake and aware, and if a clinician manages to test the reflexes during a whole-body attack (most last less than 10 seconds) they are found to be abolished, an important sign that separates real cataplexy from the weakness of functional neurological symptom disorder. In its mildest form, Pinel writes, cataplexy may simply force the patient to sit down for a few seconds until it passes; in its extreme form the patient drops to the ground as if shot and lies there for a minute or two, fully conscious. Many people with narcolepsy learn to avoid jokes, suppress laughter and keep away from emotional situations, which takes a quiet toll on their social lives. In children, and in the first months after an abrupt onset, cataplexy may instead appear as a continuous floppiness with an unsteady walk, drooping eyelids, a slack jaw, a protruding tongue and grimacing.

The other two members of the tetrad occur at the borders of sleep. Vivid hypnagogic hallucinations before or on falling asleep, or hypnopompic hallucinations just after waking, occur in about 20 to 60 percent of people with narcolepsy. They are usually visual or auditory, sometimes tactile, and far more vivid than the drifting, dreamlike thoughts that healthy people have as they fall asleep; Barlow and Durand give examples such as the realistic illusion of being caught in a fire or flying through the air. The experience of being awake but unable to move or speak on falling asleep or waking, sleep paralysis, affects a similar 20 to 60 percent. Both experiences also occur in healthy people, especially under stress or after sleep loss: the phenomena themselves, and Maurice Ohayon's survey finding hypnagogic hallucinations in about 37 percent of the general population, are described in Chapter 2, Section 2.3.6. Combined, they can be terrifying. Nicholas Spanos and his colleagues (1993) interviewed people who reported encounters with unidentified flying objects and found that about 60 percent of the most intense accounts, those involving contact with alien beings, were associated with episodes of sleep; many read exactly like sleep paralysis with hallucinations, the narrator lying in bed, unable to move, sensing presences beside the bed. DSM-5-TR notes that in some cultures sleep paralysis is attributed to a frightening spirit sitting on the sleeper's chest, a belief that makes the condition seem more dangerous and shapes whether and where people seek help, as Feroz's mother did.

Night sleep, paradoxically, is poor. People with narcolepsy fall asleep easily but wake briefly and often, dream vividly, and frequently have periodic limb movements (in about 40 percent of those with type 1) and REM sleep behaviour disorder, in which they act out their dreams (Section 8.10.4). Nocturnal eating occurs, obesity is common, and a sudden onset in young children can bring rapid weight gain and early puberty. IQ is usually normal, although problems with working memory and executive function have been reported. Some clinicians therefore add disturbed night sleep as a fifth feature to the four of the tetrad. Table 8.20 sets out the tetrad and the element of REM sleep behind each symptom; Section 8.8.5 explains why REM sleep behaves this way.

Table 8.20 The narcoleptic tetrad: each symptom is a piece of REM sleep, or of the boundary between sleep and waking, appearing where it does not belong.
SymptomWhat happensElement of REM sleep that intrudesHow common in narcolepsy
Excessive daytime sleepiness and sleep attacksIrrepressible need to sleep; brief, refreshing naps at inappropriate momentsSleep onset straight into REM (sleep-onset REM periods)All patients (criterion A); usually the first symptom
CataplexySudden, brief, bilateral loss of muscle tone triggered by emotion, especially laughter; fully consciousREM muscle atonia switched on while awakeDefines most cases of type 1; absent in type 2
Sleep paralysisAwake but unable to move or speak while falling asleep or wakingREM atonia continuing into wakingAbout 20 to 60 percent; also in healthy people
Hypnagogic and hypnopompic hallucinationsVivid visual, auditory or tactile experiences at sleep onset or on wakingREM dream imagery breaking into wakingAbout 20 to 60 percent; milder forms in healthy people
Disturbed night sleep (often added as a fifth feature)Frequent brief awakenings, vivid dreams, dream enactmentUnstable switching between waking, NREM and REMCommon

Only a minority of patients have all four symptoms of the tetrad at once, so the absence of sleep paralysis or hallucinations never rules the diagnosis out; it is sleepiness plus cataplexy, or the laboratory evidence, that DSM-5-TR requires.

Exam trap

The tetrad is excessive daytime sleepiness (sleep attacks), cataplexy, sleep paralysis and hypnagogic hallucinations. Distractors slip in "catalepsy", "insomnia", "sleepwalking" or "night terrors"; none belongs. Type 1 needs cataplexy or low cerebrospinal fluid hypocretin-1 (at or below 110 pg/mL); type 2 has neither and is diagnosed on the laboratory criterion alone: REM latency at or below 15 minutes at night, or an MSLT mean sleep latency at or below 8 minutes with at least two sleep-onset REM periods. Cataplexy is triggered mainly by positive emotion (laughter, joking), and the person stays conscious.

Why narcolepsy happens

Why should a man who laughs at a joke fall to the floor? The answer begins with Vogel's observation of 1960. Pinel sets out the three lines of evidence that persuaded early researchers that narcolepsy is a disorder of the mechanisms that trigger REM sleep. First, unlike other people, those with narcolepsy often go directly into REM sleep when they fall asleep. Second and third, they experience two characteristic features of REM sleep, dreamlike imagery and loss of muscle tone, while they are awake. Recall from Book 1 (Sections 5.7.3 and 5.7.6) that REM sleep is a package: an active, dreaming brain, rapid eye movements, and a near-total paralysis of the skeletal muscles that stops us acting out our dreams. In narcolepsy the package comes apart. Its pieces can appear separately and at the wrong time: the atonia without the dream in cataplexy, the atonia with the person awake in sleep paralysis, the dream imagery with the person awake in hypnagogic hallucinations, and the whole of REM sleep at the very start of sleep in the sleep-onset REM period. This is the REM intrusion account of narcolepsy, and Table 8.20 is simply that account applied symptom by symptom.

Hypocretin explains why the package comes apart. Book 1, Section 5.7.6, described the brain's "flip-flop switch": sleep-promoting neurons in the ventrolateral preoptic area and the arousal systems of the brainstem and hypothalamus inhibit each other, so the brain snaps between waking and sleep instead of drifting. The hypocretin neurons hold that switch firmly in the waking position. They lie in the hypothalamus. Book 1 and most sources place them in the lateral hypothalamus, while Pinel describes them as lying in the posterior hypothalamus, the region linked to the promotion of wakefulness; Sakurai's original paper located them within and around the lateral and posterior hypothalamus, so the two descriptions refer to the same population of cells. From there they project throughout the brain, with especially rich connections to the wake-promoting reticular formation, and they also restrain the circuits that switch REM sleep on. Remove them, and every boundary becomes unstable. The person cannot stay awake for long, but nor can he stay asleep for long, which is why narcolepsy produces sleep attacks by day and broken sleep by night without a large increase in total sleep across 24 hours. And because REM's switches are no longer held in check, the atonia of REM can be thrown by a burst of emotion while the person is awake. Cataplexy, in short, is REM atonia in the wrong place. It is the mirror image of REM sleep behaviour disorder, in which the atonia that should accompany REM sleep fails and the sleeper acts out his dreams (Section 8.10.4). Figure 8.13 shows the contrast between a normal day and night and the day and night of a person with narcolepsy type 1.

Why are the hypocretin neurons lost? The leading explanation is that the body's immune system destroys them. The evidence comes from several directions. About 85 to 95 percent of people with narcolepsy type 1 carry a particular variant of a gene in the human leucocyte antigen (HLA) system, HLA DQB1*06:02, against 12 to 38 percent of the general population. The HLA genes govern how the immune system presents fragments of proteins to its own cells, so a strong HLA association is the fingerprint of an autoimmune disease. Outbreaks of new cases have followed infections and vaccinations: group A streptococcal throat infections, influenza (notably the pandemic H1N1 influenza of 2009) and other winter infections, and Pandemrix, an H1N1 vaccine used in Europe during that pandemic, have all been followed some months later by narcolepsy in susceptible people. The idea is that an immune response aimed at the infection strikes, by mistake, at the hypocretin neurons. Head trauma and abrupt changes in sleep-wake patterns, such as a change of job or a period of stress, may be additional triggers. Type 2 is different: only about 40 to 50 percent of people with type 2 carry DQB1*06:02, most have normal or intermediate hypocretin levels, and there is no biomarker at all. Its cause is unknown.

Genes matter, but less than the dog story might suggest. Identical twins are only 25 to 32 percent concordant for narcolepsy (Pinel gives "about 25 percent"), which means that in most identical pairs one twin has the disorder and the other does not; the environment, presumably the immune trigger, decides. Narcolepsy occurs in 1 to 2 percent of first-degree relatives of a patient, which is still a 10- to 40-fold increase over the general population. People with narcolepsy commonly say that they have always needed more sleep than the rest of their family.

How common is it? Narcolepsy type 1 affects 0.02 to 0.05 percent of the adult population worldwide, with an incidence in the United States of 0.74 new cases per 100,000 people per year; rates appear lower in Israel and higher in Japan than in Europe and the United States. Pinel's round figure is about 1 in 2,000 people. The true prevalence of type 2 is unknown, partly because its diagnosis is unreliable. Men and women are affected about equally. Onset is most often in childhood, adolescence or young adulthood, with a peak at about 15 to 25 years, and rarely in old age. It can be abrupt or gradual. Children with an abrupt onset have the most severe disease, although it tends to improve partly over the first few years. About half of the adults diagnosed with narcolepsy recall that their symptoms began in childhood or adolescence, a measure of how long the diagnosis is delayed. Once established, the disorder is lifelong. Symptoms stay fairly stable but fluctuate with life events such as pregnancy and stress, and a sudden worsening should make the clinician suspect that the patient has stopped his medication or has developed a second sleep disorder, notably sleep apnoea, which is found in about a quarter of people with narcolepsy. Workload and social pressure through school and college cut night sleep further, and after retirement, with more opportunity to nap, many people need fewer stimulants.

Exam trap

Narcolepsy type 1 is caused by the loss of hypocretin (orexin) neurons in the hypothalamus (lateral in Book 1 and most sources, posterior in Pinel; DSM-5-TR says only "hypothalamic"), probably by an autoimmune attack in people carrying HLA DQB1*06:02. It is not caused by excess hypocretin, by a pineal or melatonin fault, or by damage to the suprachiasmatic nucleus. The identical-twin concordance is only about 25 to 32 percent, so it is not a purely genetic disorder in humans, even though canine narcolepsy is a single recessive gene. Onset peaks at 15 to 25 years; the sexes are about equally affected.

Cataplexy is not catalepsy: the look-alikes

Few pairs of words cause more lost marks than cataplexy and catalepsy. They differ by a few letters, both begin with the Greek prefix kata, "down", and both describe something strange happening to the muscles of a conscious person. Yet they mean nearly opposite things and belong to different disorders.
