Cataplexy, as you have seen, is the hallmark of narcolepsy type 1: a sudden, brief, bilateral loss of muscle tone, set off by strong emotion and above all by laughter, during which the person stays conscious and the reflexes disappear. The head drops, the jaw sags, the knees buckle, or the whole body sinks to the floor, and a minute or two later it is over. Its name comes from the Greek kataplēxis, a striking down, and that is exactly what it looks like: the person is struck down by an emotion.

By contrast, catalepsy is one of the motor signs of catatonia. The person holds a posture for a long time, often an awkward one against gravity, and if the examiner moves a limb into a new position it stays there, sometimes with a slight, even resistance like bending a stick of soft wax, which is why the sign is called waxy flexibility (flexibilitas cerea). Muscle tone is not lost at all; the body is held. Catalepsy is seen in catatonia of any cause, whether it accompanies schizophrenia, a mood disorder or a medical condition, and it can be produced by suggestion in hypnosis. The catatonic signs themselves, and catatonia across the disorders, are taught in Chapter 2, Sections 2.9.3 and 2.9.4, and Chapter 4, Section 4.5.7. The word comes from the Greek katalēpsis, a seizing or grasping: the body is seized and held in place.

Cataplexy has other look-alikes as well, and DSM-5-TR walks through them in its differential diagnosis of narcolepsy. In functional neurological symptom disorder (Chapter 7, Section 7.8) a person may have episodes of weakness that raise the question of cataplexy, which is why they are sometimes called pseudocataplexy. The clues are that the weakness lasts a long time, has unusual triggers and leads to frequent falls; it is usually generalised, without the partial attacks (a nodding head, a sagging jaw) of true cataplexy; and because a full-blown attack may last long enough to happen in the consulting room, the examiner can test the reflexes and find them intact. Such people may report sleeping and dreaming during the naps of the multiple sleep latency test, yet the test shows no sleep-onset REM periods, and home videos or video recordings during sleep studies help to settle the question.

An atonic seizure, the "drop attack" of some epilepsies, also causes a sudden loss of muscle strength, but it is not usually triggered by emotion, it produces an abrupt fall rather than the slower "melting" of cataplexy, it usually occurs in people who have other kinds of seizure too, and it has a distinctive signature on the electroencephalogram. Syncope, an ordinary faint, develops over several seconds as cataplexy does, but it is preceded by dizziness, tunnel vision and muffled hearing, and consciousness is lost. Sleep paralysis involves the same REM atonia as cataplexy, but it happens while lying down at the moment of falling asleep or waking, not in the middle of the day in response to a joke. DSM-5-TR also warns against calling two everyday experiences cataplexy: the "weakness" that athletes feel during exertion, which is physiological, and weakness that occurs only after unusual emotional triggers such as stress or anxiety, which suggests a psychological problem instead. In young children, finally, the grimacing and floppiness of early cataplexy can be mistaken for chorea or for the paediatric autoimmune neuropsychiatric disorders associated with streptococcal infection (PANDAS, Chapter 6, Section 6.7), especially after a sore throat. Table 8.21 puts the look-alikes side by side.

Table 8.21 Cataplexy and its look-alikes.
PhenomenonTrigger and settingConsciousness and muscle toneDuration and reflexesBelongs to
CataplexyStrong emotion, especially laughter or joking; while awakeConscious; sudden bilateral loss of tone (head drop, jaw sag, knees buckle, fall)Seconds to 2 minutes; reflexes abolishedNarcolepsy type 1
CatalepsyNo emotional trigger; posture held or placed by examinerConscious; tone kept, posture held against gravity, waxy flexibilityMinutes to hoursCatatonia of any cause; hypnosis
PseudocataplexyUnusual triggers, often in front of othersWeakness usually generalised, no partial attacksLong-lasting; reflexes intact; no sleep-onset REM on MSLTFunctional neurological symptom disorder
Atonic seizureRarely emotionAbrupt fall, not a slow "melting"Brief; EEG changes; other seizure typesEpilepsy
SyncopeStanding, heat, pain, fearConsciousness lost; dizziness and tunnel vision firstSeconds to a minuteFainting (cardiovascular)
Sleep paralysisFalling asleep or waking, lying downConscious; cannot move or speak; may hallucinateSeconds to minutesNarcolepsy, or isolated in healthy people (Section 8.10.5)
Exam trap

Asked what is most characteristic of narcolepsy, choose cataplexy: a sudden loss of muscle tone triggered by emotion, in full consciousness. Catalepsy, the maintenance of an imposed posture with waxy flexibility, belongs to catatonia, not to narcolepsy, so "catalepsy", "both equally" and "neither" are all wrong. A way to remember: in cataPLEXy the person is perPLEXed by a joke and collapses; in cataLepsy the Limb is Left where it was placed. And do not confuse either with catatonia (the whole syndrome) or cachexia (wasting).

Diagnosing and treating narcolepsy

Because narcolepsy is lifelong and its drugs are powerful, the diagnosis should be made carefully, and DSM-5-TR spells out how. The person first stops all psychotropic medicines, for a period based on each drug's elimination half-life, because antidepressants, stimulants and some other drugs alter REM sleep and so distort the test, and because stopping them abruptly can do the same. He then keeps an adequate, regular sleep schedule, documented by a sleep diary or, preferably, actigraphy, ideally for 2 weeks; chronic insufficient sleep is common and must be ruled out first. Then comes a night of polysomnography followed the next day by an MSLT, in which he is given four or five opportunities to nap at intervals through the day. The test is positive for narcolepsy if the mean sleep latency is 8 minutes or less and REM sleep appears in at least two of the naps.

How good are these tests? For narcolepsy type 1 they perform well. The MSLT is positive in 90 to 95 percent of people with type 1 and in only 2 to 4 percent of healthy controls or people with other sleep disorders. A sleep-onset REM period during the night's polysomnography (REM within 15 minutes of falling asleep) is highly specific, appearing in only about 1 percent of control subjects (DSM-5-TR elsewhere puts its specificity for type 1 at 95 to 97 percent), but it is only moderately sensitive, at 54 to 57 percent: about half of people with type 1 do not show one on a given night. False sleep-onset REM periods occur with shift work, circadian rhythm disorders, severe obstructive sleep apnoea, medication effects and insufficient sleep. For type 2 the tests are much weaker; the reliability of the diagnosis is far poorer, and test-retest agreement on the MSLT may be below 50 percent, partly because type 2 physiology varies from day to day and partly because prior sleep and medication are not always controlled. Polysomnography in narcolepsy also commonly shows frequent arousals, low sleep efficiency, extra stage N1 sleep, periodic limb movements and sometimes sleep apnoea.

Two other tests help in particular situations. Measuring hypocretin-1 in the cerebrospinal fluid is the most direct test for type 1 and is especially useful when cataplexy is atypical or absent, when pseudocataplexy is suspected, and in cases that resist treatment. Unlike the MSLT, its result is not affected by medication, sleep loss or the time of day, though it cannot be interpreted during a severe infection, head injury or coma. About 85 to 90 percent of people with cataplexy have low levels, and in people with typical cataplexy the hypocretin is often already very low or undetectable. Typing for HLA DQB1*06:02 is a useful screen for type 1, since a person without the allele is unlikely to have it, but it is little help for type 2, because 12 to 38 percent of the general population carry the allele anyway.

The differential diagnosis is long because sleepiness is common and narcolepsy is rare. Table 8.22 compares narcolepsy with the three conditions most often confused with it.

Table 8.22 Telling the sleepy apart: narcolepsy type 1 and its commonest mimics.
FeatureNarcolepsy type 1Hypersomnolence disorderInsufficient sleepObstructive sleep apnoea
Night sleep7 to 8 hours, broken by brief awakeningsLong (often 9 hours or more), undisturbedShort on workdays, long at weekendsSnoring, gasping, observed pauses
Waking in the morningUsually refreshedVery difficult; sleep inertiaDifficult on workdaysUnrefreshed, morning headache
Daytime napsShort (15 to 20 minutes), refreshing, often with dreamsLong (over an hour), unrefreshingRefreshingUnrefreshing
Cataplexy, sleep paralysis, hallucinationsCataplexy defines it; others commonNever cataplexy; others uncommonAbsent (sleep paralysis possible)Absent
Key testMSLT: short latency, two or more SOREMPs; low CSF hypocretin-1MSLT: short latency, few SOREMPsResolves with 10 to 14 days of sleep extensionPolysomnography: apnoeas and oxygen drops

Several other conditions need a sentence each. Obstructive sleep apnoea is so much commoner than narcolepsy, and so often accompanies the obesity of narcolepsy, that cataplexy may be overlooked, and narcolepsy should be considered in anyone who remains sleepy after successful treatment of apnoea; the two can coexist. People with narcolepsy often complain of broken nights and may be wrongly treated for insomnia, but unlike people with insomnia disorder they fall asleep, and fall back to sleep, without difficulty. Depression brings complaints of sleepiness too, but there is no cataplexy, the MSLT is usually normal, and the patient's sense of sleepiness is out of proportion to what the laboratory measures; in one meta-analysis of psychiatric patients assessed for sleepiness, a quarter had a mean sleep latency under 8 minutes but hardly any had two or more sleep-onset REM periods. In children, sleepiness may show itself as aggression, inattention and poor schoolwork and be misdiagnosed as attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder. Finally, vivid hypnagogic hallucinations can be mistaken for schizophrenia, especially when patients are unsure whether their experiences were real. The hallucinations of narcolepsy, however, are tied to sleep and are multisensory and "holistic" (a whole scene that is seen, heard and felt) rather than the predominantly verbal voices of schizophrenia, and DSM-5-TR advises that when cataplexy accompanies hallucinations or delusions the first assumption should be narcolepsy, not a second psychotic disorder. High doses of stimulants given for narcolepsy can themselves cause persecutory delusions.

Clinical link

Many people with narcolepsy spend years being called lazy, depressed, drunk, attention-seeking or possessed before anyone makes the diagnosis. Psychologists meet them in schools, colleges and outpatient clinics, often under another label. A short sleep history (How long do you sleep? Do you fall asleep at odd moments? Do your knees go weak when you laugh? Do you ever wake unable to move, or see things as you fall asleep?) takes two minutes and can change a life. After diagnosis the psychologist's work is practical: helping the person plan naps, negotiate reasonable adjustments at school or work, think through driving and career choices, and cope with the loneliness of suppressing laughter to avoid attacks.

The disorder's consequences explain why treatment matters. School performance, driving, work and every activity that demands sustained attention suffer. DSM-5-TR advises that people with narcolepsy avoid jobs that endanger themselves, such as work with heavy machinery, or others, such as driving a bus or flying an aircraft. Once the disorder is controlled most can drive, although rarely long distances alone, and untreated people risk accidents and social isolation. Comorbid conditions are common: obesity, bruxism (teeth grinding), enuresis, early puberty in children whose narcolepsy began young, mood disorders, attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, parasomnias, restless legs and sleep apnoea.

Treatment has two strands. The first is behavioural and costs nothing: a regular sleep schedule, which DSM-5-TR says benefits people with narcolepsy at all ages, and two or three short scheduled naps through the day, which the American Academy of Sleep Medicine's practice parameters (Morgenthaler and colleagues, 2007) found beneficial against sleepiness. The second is pharmacological, and the drugs divide according to the symptom they target.

For sleepiness, narcolepsy has traditionally been treated with stimulants such as amphetamine and methylphenidate, which increase the release or block the reuptake of dopamine and noradrenaline. As Pinel notes, they work but carry a potential for addiction and many undesirable side effects. The usual first choice today is modafinil, which Pinel calls an antihypnotic stimulant and which was approved in the United States for narcolepsy in 1998. It acts mainly as an atypical dopamine reuptake inhibitor, it activates the orexin neurons in animals, and its liability for addiction and dependence is considered low; armodafinil, its R-enantiomer (the mirror-image form of the molecule that does most of the work), followed in 2007. Two newer wake-promoting drugs work in different ways. The first, pitolisant, approved in Europe in 2016 and in the United States in 2019, is an inverse agonist at the histamine H3 receptor: by blocking the brake that histamine neurons place on themselves, it increases the release of histamine, one of the brain's arousal transmitters (which is why old antihistamines make people drowsy). The second, solriamfetol, approved in the United States in 2019, is a noradrenaline and dopamine reuptake inhibitor used for the sleepiness of narcolepsy and of obstructive sleep apnoea.

For cataplexy, the traditional drugs are antidepressants: the tricyclics such as clomipramine (Christian Guilleminault and his colleagues reported on its short- and long-term use in narcolepsy in 1976), the selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors such as fluoxetine, and the serotonin-noradrenaline reuptake inhibitors. Barlow and Durand make the point that every student should remember: they are given not because people with narcolepsy are depressed, but because antidepressants suppress REM sleep, and with it the REM atonia that breaks into waking as cataplexy. The 2007 practice parameters found tricyclics and fluoxetine effective against cataplexy, sleep paralysis and hypnagogic hallucinations alike. Because they hold REM sleep down, stopping them suddenly can let it rebound, and cataplexy may return with a vengeance. The classes and side effects of the antidepressants are in Chapter 5, Section 5.7.2.

A third option, sodium oxybate, the sodium salt of gamma-hydroxybutyrate (GHB), was approved in the United States in 2002 for cataplexy and is recommended for it (Morgenthaler and colleagues, 2007). It is a powerful sedative taken at night, in two doses, at bedtime and again a few hours later, and it consolidates broken night sleep, reduces cataplexy and lessens daytime sleepiness. GHB is also a drug of abuse, so sodium oxybate is tightly controlled, and because it depresses breathing it must never be combined with alcohol or other sedatives. Table 8.23 summarises the drugs.

Table 8.23 Drugs for narcolepsy and hypersomnolence.
DrugClass or actionMain targetMain problem or note
Amphetamine, methylphenidateTraditional stimulants (dopamine and noradrenaline)SleepinessAddiction potential; insomnia, poor appetite, raised blood pressure; psychosis at high doses
Modafinil, armodafinilWake-promoting; atypical dopamine reuptake inhibitorSleepiness (narcolepsy, hypersomnolence, shift work, residual sleepiness in apnoea)Low addiction liability; rare severe skin reactions
PitolisantHistamine H3 receptor inverse agonistSleepinessNewer (Europe 2016, United States 2019)
SolriamfetolNoradrenaline and dopamine reuptake inhibitorSleepiness in narcolepsy and sleep apnoeaNewer (United States 2019)
Antidepressants (tricyclics such as clomipramine; SSRIs such as fluoxetine; SNRIs)Suppress REM sleepCataplexy, sleep paralysis, hypnagogic hallucinationsRebound cataplexy if stopped suddenly; usual antidepressant side effects
Sodium oxybateSodium salt of GHB; taken twice each nightCataplexy, broken night sleep, sleepinessMisuse potential, tightly controlled; dangerous with alcohol or sedatives

Feroz, from Section 8.8.4, was diagnosed with narcolepsy type 1 after a sleep study showed a mean sleep latency of 3 minutes and REM sleep in four of five naps. Modafinil each morning, a fifteen-minute nap in his lunch break and another at 4 p.m., and a low dose of an antidepressant for his cataplexy let him keep his job, and a letter from his doctor explaining the diagnosis did more for his standing with his team leader than any drug.

Exam trap

Stimulants and modafinil treat the sleepiness of narcolepsy; antidepressants treat cataplexy, because they suppress REM sleep, not because the patient is depressed; sodium oxybate (GHB) is recommended for cataplexy and is taken at night. Narcolepsy type 1 is defined by cataplexy or low cerebrospinal fluid hypocretin-1; type 2 by the sleep laboratory alone. Hypnotics such as benzodiazepines are not a treatment for narcolepsy.

Kleine-Levin syndrome

The rarest disorder of sleepiness is also the strangest. A 15-year-old boy in Kochi develops a viral fever. As the fever settles he begins to sleep, eighteen or twenty hours a day, waking only to eat and to use the toilet. When awake he is a different child: slow, apathetic, irritable if disturbed, speaking in a flat, childish way, and saying that everything around him seems unreal, as if in a dream. He eats enormously, raiding the kitchen at night for sweets and biscuits, and makes crude sexual remarks to his cousin that shock the family. Ten days later he wakes one morning his ordinary self, embarrassed and with only a hazy memory of what happened. Three months later it happens again. Between episodes he is entirely well: he sleeps normally, studies normally and behaves normally.

This is Kleine-Levin syndrome, recurrent hypersomnia with disturbances of cognition, mood, eating and sexual behaviour, sometimes popularly called "Sleeping Beauty syndrome". An early case was described by the French physician Brierre de Boismont in 1862. The German neurologist Willi Kleine of Frankfurt described five patients with periodic sleepiness in 1925, and the American psychiatrist Max Levin published cases linking periodic sleepiness with morbid hunger from the late 1920s into the 1930s (the eponym table in Chapter 2, Section 2.6.8, lists them). The British neurologist Macdonald Critchley (1900 to 1997) gave it the name Kleine-Levin syndrome in a paper written with Hoffman in 1942, and in 1962 reviewed the condition in a classic account of periodic hypersomnia and overeating in adolescent males.

Most of what is known about the syndrome comes from a systematic review of 186 cases from the world literature published in 2005 by Isabelle Arnulf and her colleagues at Stanford. Among the primary cases, 68 percent were male, and the median age at onset was 15 years, with 81 percent beginning in the second decade of life. Hypersomnia occurred in every case, cognitive changes (including a characteristic feeling of unreality, or derealisation) in 96 percent, disturbed eating, usually voracious overeating (hyperphagia), in 80 percent, depressed mood in 48 percent, hypersexuality in 43 percent and compulsions in 29 percent. The first episode was most often set off by an infection (38 percent), less often by head trauma (9 percent) or alcohol (5 percent). Typically the person had about seven episodes, each lasting about 10 days and recurring every three and a half months, and the syndrome burned itself out after about 8 years (all median values), lasting longer in women and in those whose episodes were less frequent in the first year. During episodes people may sleep as long as 16 to 20 hours a day, waking only to eat and to use the toilet, as the ICD-11 description puts it. Outside the episodes, sleep, mood, cognition and behaviour are normal, which is the single most useful clue. The syndrome is extremely rare, with estimates of about one or two cases per million people.

Where does it sit in the classifications? DSM-5-TR gives it no criteria of its own and mentions it only as the example of "brief-duration hypersomnolence" under other specified hypersomnolence disorder (G47.19), noting that the International Classification of Sleep Disorders counts it among its central disorders of hypersomnolence. ICD-11 lists it by name among the hypersomnolence disorders (7A22), and ICD-10 placed it among the other sleep disorders (G47.8). DSM-5-TR mentions it twice more, in the differential diagnosis of pica and of bulimia nervosa: in Kleine-Levin syndrome eating is disturbed, but without the overconcern with body shape and weight that defines bulimia nervosa, and without the compensatory behaviour. Other look-alikes are a manic episode (in which the need for sleep falls rather than rises, Chapter 5, Section 5.4), depression with atypical features (which brings oversleeping and overeating but persists rather than switching off completely between episodes) and narcolepsy (which is continuous and has cataplexy). Because a first episode can look like encephalitis, epilepsy, a hypothalamic tumour or intoxication, those have to be excluded before the diagnosis is made.

There is no established treatment. In Arnulf's review stimulants, mainly amphetamines, reduced the sleepiness in about 40 percent of trials but did nothing for the cognitive and behavioural symptoms, and antipsychotics and antidepressants were of little benefit. Only lithium had a higher reported response rate for preventing further episodes (41 percent, against 19 percent with no treatment), and a Cochrane review in 2016 concluded that there was no good evidence that any drug treatment is effective and safe. Care during an episode is mainly supportive: keeping the young person safe at home, supervising eating, protecting him from the consequences of disinhibited behaviour, and explaining to the school and family that the episodes will pass and that the disorder usually fades by adulthood.

Exam trap

Recurrent episodes of hypersomnia with hyperphagia and hypersexuality in an adolescent boy, with normal health between episodes, is Kleine-Levin syndrome. It is not bulimia nervosa (no concern with shape and weight, no purging), not mania (mania reduces the need for sleep) and not narcolepsy (no cataplexy; narcolepsy is continuous, not episodic). DSM-5-TR classes it under other specified hypersomnolence disorder ("brief-duration hypersomnolence"); ICSD-3 and ICD-11 (7A22) name it separately.

8.9 Breathing-Related and Circadian Rhythm Sleep-Wake DisordersLower yield · not asked yet

Section 8.8 dealt with sleepiness that comes from the brain's own machinery for waking. This section deals with two very different reasons why a person whose brain is working normally may still sleep badly and feel dreadful by day. In the breathing-related sleep disorders the sleep itself is ruined from outside, because breathing falters every time the person drops off, and every pause ends in a tiny awakening. In the circadian rhythm sleep-wake disorders the sleep may be perfectly good, but it comes at the wrong time: the body clock and the clock on the wall disagree. Both groups are common, both are under-recognised, and both are routinely mistaken for insomnia, depression or laziness. And both are treated, for the most part, not with sleeping pills but with physical methods: a mask that blows air, a lamp, a carefully timed hormone.

When breathing stops in sleep

When you fall asleep, two things happen to your breathing. The muscles that hold your upper airway open (the muscles of the tongue, the soft palate and the throat) relax, so the airway narrows a little, and the brainstem's drive to breathe becomes more dependent on the level of carbon dioxide in your blood. In most people neither change matters. In some, the narrowed airway collapses altogether; in others, the drive to breathe falters; and in a third group breathing becomes too shallow, for long periods, to clear carbon dioxide. These are the three breathing-related sleep disorders of DSM-5-TR: obstructive sleep apnoea hypopnoea, central sleep apnoea and sleep-related hypoventilation.

Their vocabulary is precise. An apnoea is a total absence of airflow, and a hypopnoea is a reduction in airflow. To count, each event must last at least 10 seconds in an adult, or the length of two missed breaths in a child, and it is typically accompanied by a fall in blood oxygen saturation of 3 percent or more, an arousal on the electroencephalogram, or both. The severity of a breathing disorder is summarised as the apnoea-hypopnoea index (AHI), the number of apnoeas plus hypopnoeas per hour of sleep. As Book 1 (Section 5.8.4) pointed out, each event usually ends with a brief arousal that restarts breathing, so the sleeper may wake dozens or hundreds of times a night without remembering a single one. That is why the commonest complaint is not "I stop breathing" but "I am tired all day" or "I cannot sleep properly".

The disorder was hiding in plain sight for more than a century. In The Pickwick Papers (1836 to 1837), Charles Dickens (1812 to 1870) created Joe, "the fat boy", an enormously overweight servant who falls asleep at every opportunity; modern physicians read the description as an accurate picture of severe obstructive sleep apnoea. Doctors adopted the boy's name. In 1956 a group of Boston physicians, Burwell, Robin, Whaley and Bickelmann, described an extremely obese, constantly sleepy patient who breathed too little, under the title "Extreme obesity associated with alveolar hypoventilation: a Pickwickian syndrome". What happened to such patients during sleep was first recorded in 1965, when the French neurologist Henri Gastaut and his colleagues Tassinari and Duron made all-night polygraphic recordings of patients with the "Pickwick syndrome" and documented the repeated pauses in breathing that ended in arousals. At Stanford, Guilleminault, Tilkian and Dement drew the findings together in a 1976 review titled "The sleep apnea syndromes", which helped to establish sleep apnoea as a disorder in its own right. For the worst cases the only reliable treatment was a tracheostomy, a permanent opening cut in the windpipe below the level of the blockage, until 1981, when Colin Sullivan and his colleagues in Sydney introduced a far gentler solution (see the Key study box in Section 8.9.2). In the same year Fujita and colleagues in the United States described uvulopalatopharyngoplasty, an operation to widen the throat.

Figure 8.14 shows what the three main patterns look like in the sleep laboratory, where polysomnography records airflow at the nose and mouth, the breathing movements of the chest and abdomen (respiratory effort) and the oxygen saturation of the blood. The contrast between the first two traces is the key to the whole subject. In an obstructive apnoea the airflow stops but the chest and abdomen go on heaving, harder and harder, against the closed airway. In a central apnoea both stop together, because the brain has not sent the signal to breathe.

Exam trap

Apnoea means no airflow for at least 10 seconds; hypopnoea means reduced airflow; the apnoea-hypopnoea index counts both per hour of sleep. The "Pickwickian" name comes from Dickens's fat boy Joe, and the 1956 "Pickwickian syndrome" was obesity with hypoventilation; do not attach it to Pick's disease. The sleeper with apnoea usually has no memory of his hundreds of awakenings.

Obstructive sleep apnoea hypopnoea

Harpreet was 52 and had driven long-distance lorries out of Ludhiana for thirty years, hauling grain and textiles to Mumbai and Kolkata. He was heavily built, with a shirt collar of 44 centimetres, and he had snored for as long as anyone could remember; his wife had moved to the next room years ago. What frightened her were the silences. "He snores like thunder," she told the doctor, "then nothing, no breath, for so long that I want to shake him, and then a terrible gasp and he starts again." Harpreet himself complained of something else. However long he slept, he woke with a headache and a dry mouth, he got up twice a night to pass urine, and on the highway he fought sleep constantly. Twice in the past year he had drifted off the road, once into a field and once, at dawn near Panipat, onto the median, waking only when the wheels hit the kerb. His blood pressure at the clinic was 160/100. An overnight sleep study showed 48 apnoeas and hypopnoeas an hour, with his oxygen saturation falling repeatedly into the high 70s.

Harpreet has obstructive sleep apnoea hypopnoea (DSM-5-TR code G47.33; ICD-11 7A41), the commonest breathing-related sleep disorder, in which the upper (pharyngeal) airway repeatedly closes or narrows during sleep. DSM-5-TR offers two routes to the diagnosis, and both require objective evidence from polysomnography (or from a limited home study, called out-of-centre sleep testing).

  • Route 1 (criterion A1): at least 5 obstructive apnoeas or hypopnoeas per hour of sleep, plus symptoms, either nocturnal breathing disturbances (snoring, snorting or gasping, or pauses in breathing during sleep) or daytime sleepiness, fatigue or unrefreshing sleep despite enough opportunity to sleep, not better explained by another disorder.
  • Route 2 (criterion A2): 15 or more obstructive apnoeas or hypopnoeas per hour of sleep, regardless of symptoms.

The two routes reflect two facts. A handful of events an hour is common in otherwise healthy people, especially as they age, so at that level it is the symptoms that make the events a disorder. At 15 or more an hour the events carry health risks whether or not the person notices anything, so no symptoms are needed. Severity is graded by the AHI: mild below 15, moderate 15 to 30 and severe above 30. Harpreet, at 48, is severe. DSM-5-TR adds that the disorder should be regarded as more severe, whatever the count, when the events bring marked oxygen desaturation (for example, more than 10 percent of sleep time spent below 90 percent saturation) or badly fragmented sleep (more than 30 arousals an hour, or less than 5 percent of sleep in deep N3). Children are judged differently: one or more obstructive events an hour, or obstructive hypoventilation with snoring, is already abnormal.

The two cardinal symptoms are snoring and daytime sleepiness. Because the person wakes so often, he may also complain of insomnia, and DSM-5-TR lists a set of common but nonspecific companions: heartburn, nocturia (getting up at night to pass urine), morning headaches, dry mouth, erectile dysfunction and reduced libido. Bed partners report loud snoring and frightening pauses, and Barlow and Durand add heavy sweating at night. Hypertension is present in more than 60 percent; during sleep the heart may show pauses, extra beats or atrial fibrillation; and severe nightly desaturation can drive up the haemoglobin. Most cases are never diagnosed, so DSM-5-TR urges clinicians to ask about snoring and breathing pauses in anyone with disturbed sleep, and to look for central obesity, a crowded throat, a thick neck and raised blood pressure.

The disorder is very common. In the United States, 13 percent of men and 6 percent of women have 15 or more events an hour on polysomnography, and 14 percent of men and 5 percent of women have more than 5 events an hour together with daytime sleepiness. Men are affected two to four times as often as women, but the gap narrows with age, because rates rise in women after the menopause: postmenopausal women are 2.6 to 3.5 times as likely as premenopausal women to have the disorder. Because it is strongly tied to obesity, its prevalence has risen with the world's waistlines. It also occurs in 1 to 4 percent of children, with no sex difference before puberty.

Indian context

The first Indian prevalence study was carried out in Mumbai by Zarir Udwadia and his colleagues (2004). Of 658 apparently healthy urban men aged 35 to 65 who attended a hospital for a routine health check and completed a sleep questionnaire, 250 had an overnight home sleep study. The estimated prevalence of sleep-disordered breathing (an AHI of 5 or more) was 19.5 percent, and of the full obstructive sleep apnoea hypopnoea syndrome (sleep-disordered breathing with daytime sleepiness) 7.5 percent. The main predictors were body mass index, neck girth and diabetes, and snoring, choking at night, unrefreshing sleep, repeated awakening, daytime sleepiness and fatigue each helped to identify the men with the syndrome. The authors called the figures "striking" for a developing country. Screening questionnaires such as STOP-BANG, which combines snoring, tiredness, observed apnoeas, blood pressure, body mass index, age, neck circumference and gender, belong to the Assessment book.

The disorder has several peaks across life. The first comes at 3 to 8 years, when the tonsils and adenoids are large relative to a child's small airway; it falls as the airway grows and the lymphoid tissue shrinks, rises again in adolescence with obesity, and climbs once more in midlife as weight increases and women pass the menopause. It is commonest between 40 and 60. Its onset is insidious and its course persistent: the loud snoring has usually been present for years, often since childhood, and it is a worsening of the snoring, or weight gain, that finally brings the person to a doctor. Over 4 to 5 years the average AHI of adults rises by about two events an hour, faster in those who are older, male or gaining weight, and the disorder can resolve with weight loss, especially after bariatric surgery. In young children the signs are subtler: agitated arousals, odd sleeping postures such as kneeling on hands and knees, daytime mouth breathing, difficulty swallowing and poor articulation, and, in a child who was previously dry at night, a return of bedwetting, which should raise the suspicion of apnoea (Section 8.6). Younger children come to attention for what happens at night; children over 5 more often for daytime sleepiness, hyperactivity and impulsivity, learning difficulties and morning headaches, and the disorder may slow growth.

The risk factors follow from the anatomy. Obesity and male sex are the major ones. Others include a small or receding jaw (micrognathia or retrognathia), a family history of sleep apnoea (the disorder is about twice as common in first-degree relatives of a patient, and shared family factors explain about a third of the variation in the AHI), genetic syndromes that narrow the airway, such as Down syndrome and Treacher Collins syndrome, enlarged tonsils and adenoids in young children, the menopause, pregnancy and polycystic ovary syndrome in women, and endocrine disorders such as acromegaly. Medicines that cause drowsiness can make apnoea worse. Women more often describe fatigue, lack of energy or insomnia than sleepiness, and they under-report snoring, so their apnoea is easily missed. Culture matters too: DSM-5-TR notes that in some groups loud snoring is regarded as a sign of normal, healthy sleep and raises no concern, a belief familiar in many households, which delays diagnosis.

Why does it matter? More than half of people with moderate to severe apnoea report daytime sleepiness. Snoring with sleepiness doubles the risk of accidents at work, and drivers with a raised AHI have up to seven times as many road crashes. Quality of life suffers, especially vitality. Over the long term, obstructive sleep apnoea is consistently associated with hypertension, coronary artery disease, heart failure, stroke, diabetes and an increased death rate, and treating the apnoea reduces cardiovascular illness and death. Its psychological consequences bring patients to mental health services: up to a third of people referred for evaluation report depressive symptoms, about 10 percent at a moderate to severe level, and the severity of the apnoea correlates with the severity of the depression. Irritability, poor concentration and forgetfulness are common, and they often improve with effective treatment.

The differential diagnosis needs polysomnography more than clinical wisdom. Snoring without symptoms and without abnormal breathing on a sleep study, called primary snoring, is not a disorder. Narcolepsy, hypersomnolence disorder, insufficient sleep and circadian disorders all cause sleepiness without snoring or breathing pauses (Table 8.22). Insomnia disorder lacks the snoring, gasping and other signs, although insomnia and apnoea often coexist and must then be treated together (Section 8.7.5). Nocturnal panic attacks (Chapter 6, Section 6.2) can wake a person gasping and choking, but they are less frequent, bring intense autonomic arousal and no daytime sleepiness, and occur in people who also have panic attacks by day; the sleep study shows no apnoeas. Nocturnal asthma can produce identical choking awakenings, but there is a history of asthma and no apnoeas on testing. In children, inattention, hyperactivity and poor schoolwork can be the presenting picture, and attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder and apnoea often coexist. Alcohol, barbiturates, benzodiazepines and opiates all worsen obstructive apnoea, and a person whose apnoea is aggravated by a substance still receives the apnoea diagnosis.

Key study: Sullivan's pneumatic splint (1981)

By the late 1970s the cause of obstructive apnoea was clear: during sleep the soft walls of the throat collapse and seal the airway, and only waking reopens it. Colin Sullivan, a physician in Sydney, reasoned that if the airway collapses because the pressure inside it falls below the pressure of the tissues around it, then raising the pressure inside should hold it open. He connected a blower, by tubing, to a mask that sealed over the nose, and in 1981 he and his colleagues reported in The Lancet that five patients with severe obstructive apnoea, treated with continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP) through the nose at low pressures (4.5 to 10 centimetres of water), had no airway occlusions at all and slept through the whole night without interruption. The pressurised air, they wrote, acts as a "pneumatic splint" for the airway. CPAP soon replaced tracheostomy for most patients and is still the standard treatment four decades later. Its one great weakness, as you will see, is that people have to be willing to wear it.

Treatment is aimed, as Barlow and Durand put it, at helping the person breathe better during sleep. CPAP is the gold standard: the patient sleeps with a mask over the nose, or nose and mouth, connected to a bedside machine that delivers slightly pressurised air. When it is used, it works dramatically. The problem is adherence. Many people find the mask uncomfortable, the air dries the nose, the machine is noisy for a bed partner, and some people feel claustrophobic with something strapped to their face. Psychological methods help here: graded desensitisation to the mask for claustrophobia (Book 2, Section 4.2), education of both patient and partner about what the machine does and why it matters, and motivational interviewing to resolve the patient's ambivalence about wearing it (Book 2, Section 6.9).

Other treatments fill the gaps. Weight loss is the logical answer for an overweight patient, and the disorder can disappear after bariatric surgery, but Barlow and Durand note that voluntary weight loss is rarely maintained, so it is not relied on alone. Some people's apnoea occurs mainly when they lie on their back, and positional therapy (sleeping on one side, or with the upper body raised) helps them. A mandibular advancement device, a dental splint worn at night that holds the lower jaw and tongue forward, is often used for mild to moderate apnoea or by people who cannot tolerate CPAP. Surgery removes or reshapes the tissues that block the airway: uvulopalatopharyngoplasty trims the uvula, soft palate and throat, and in children with large tonsils and adenoids their removal (adenotonsillectomy) is the usual first treatment. Since 2014, when the United States Food and Drug Administration approved it for people who cannot use CPAP, an implanted stimulator of the hypoglossal nerve, which pushes the tongue forward during sleep, has offered another option. Barlow and Durand also report a curiosity: a Swiss instructor of the didgeridoo, a long Australian wind instrument made from a termite-hollowed branch, noticed that his pupils were less sleepy, and several months of daily practice, presumably by training the muscles of the upper airway, has been reported to improve the sleep of people with mild apnoea (De Dios and Brass, 2012); the evidence is modest. Everyone with apnoea should avoid alcohol and sedating medicines in the evening, and a wake-promoting drug (Section 8.8.7) can be added for sleepiness that persists despite effective treatment.

Harpreet refused the CPAP mask at first; he tore it off in a panic on the first night, saying he felt as if he were being smothered. Over three weeks a psychologist helped him wear it while watching television in the evening, then while dozing, then all night, and his wife came to the sessions too. A month later he reported that he felt "ten years younger", his morning headaches had gone, and his blood pressure had begun to fall. He no longer drives through the night.

Exam trap

Obstructive sleep apnoea hypopnoea: the airway collapses while breathing effort continues; diagnosed by polysomnography with at least 5 events an hour plus symptoms, or 15 an hour regardless of symptoms; mild under 15, moderate 15 to 30, severe over 30. Cardinal symptoms: snoring and daytime sleepiness. Treatment of choice: CPAP (Sullivan, 1981), not hypnotics. Central apnoea is the opposite mechanism: the brain fails to drive breathing, so effort stops too.

Central sleep apnoea and sleep-related hypoventilation

In central sleep apnoea the airway is open but the breathing effort itself stops or falls, again and again, through the night. DSM-5-TR's criteria are brief: polysomnography shows five or more central apnoeas per hour of sleep, and the disorder is not better explained by another current sleep disorder. When central and obstructive events occur together, as they often do, central sleep apnoea is diagnosed when central events make up more than half of all the events. Severity depends on how often the events occur and on how much oxygen desaturation and broken sleep they cause. People present with sleepiness or insomnia, with frequent awakenings or with waking breathless, and some have no complaints at all.

Most central apnoea arises from instability in the control system that matches breathing to the level of carbon dioxide in the blood. In idiopathic central sleep apnoea and in Cheyne-Stokes breathing that system is over-sensitive, a property physiologists call high loop gain. Think of a thermostat set so sensitive that the heater roars on at the slightest chill and the room then overheats, so the heater shuts off completely and the room grows cold again. In the same way the over-sensitive controller over-breathes, washes out carbon dioxide, and then stops breathing altogether until carbon dioxide builds up enough to restart it, producing what DSM-5-TR calls periodic breathing, hyperventilation alternating with hypoventilation. DSM-5-TR specifies three subtypes.

  • Idiopathic central sleep apnoea (G47.31): repeated apnoeas and hypopnoeas caused by variable breathing effort with no obstruction and no identifiable cause. It is rare.
  • Cheyne-Stokes breathing (R06.3): a periodic crescendo-decrescendo pattern in which each breath is a little deeper than the last, then each a little shallower, until breathing stops, the cycle repeating about once a minute, with at least five central events an hour and frequent arousals. The pattern was described by two Dublin physicians, John Cheyne in 1818 and William Stokes in 1854. It is common in heart failure: 15 to 44 percent of people with a cardiac ejection fraction below 45 percent have it, and about 20 percent of people with an acute stroke. It also occurs in kidney failure and with atrial fibrillation. Most patients are men over 60, and compared with people with obstructive apnoea they are older and thinner. When the pattern appears even during quiet wakefulness, it marks a worse prognosis.
  • Central sleep apnoea comorbid with opioid use (G47.37): opioids act on the respiratory rhythm generators in the medulla and blunt the drive to breathe. It occurs in about 24 percent of people taking opioids long term for pain not caused by cancer, and in a similar proportion of people on methadone maintenance, becoming more severe at higher doses (especially above the equivalent of 200 mg of morphine a day).

Two further forms are worth knowing. Central apnoea can appear when obstructive apnoea is first treated with CPAP (treatment-emergent central sleep apnoea), presumably because the newly opened airway lets an unstable controller show itself. And periodic breathing resembling Cheyne-Stokes breathing, but with a shorter cycle, occurs normally in many people who climb above about 2,500 metres, a familiar experience on the first nights of a trek in Ladakh or Sikkim; it disappears on descent and has nothing to do with heart failure.

The third disorder, sleep-related hypoventilation, is a slower failure. Breathing does not stop; it is simply too shallow, for long stretches of sleep, to clear carbon dioxide. The criterion is polysomnographic evidence of episodes of decreased breathing with raised carbon dioxide levels, or, where carbon dioxide is not measured, persistently low oxygen saturation that is not explained by apnoeas or hypopnoeas. On the sleep study it shows itself as long, sustained periods of low oxygen rather than the repeated dips of the apnoeas. DSM-5-TR names three subtypes.

  • Idiopathic hypoventilation (G47.34), with no identifiable cause, is very uncommon. It reflects a blunted response of the brain's breathing centres to carbon dioxide, a problem of drive that physiologists summarise as "won't breathe".
  • The congenital form (G47.35), congenital central alveolar hypoventilation, is a rare genetic disorder caused by mutations in the gene PHOX2B, which guides the development of the autonomic nervous system. It usually appears at birth, with shallow, erratic or absent breathing, blueness (cyanosis) and apnoea during sleep, and it is often accompanied by other autonomic problems and by Hirschsprung's disease of the bowel. It was once nicknamed "Ondine's curse", after a European legend about a man who forgets to breathe when he falls asleep.
  • Comorbid sleep-related hypoventilation (G47.36), by far the commonest, follows from another condition: lung disease such as chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) or interstitial lung disease; disorders of the nerves, muscles or chest wall such as muscular dystrophy, amyotrophic lateral sclerosis, post-polio syndrome, cervical spinal cord injury and kyphoscoliosis ("can't breathe"); medicines that depress breathing, especially benzodiazepines and opioids; and obesity. In obesity hypoventilation, the modern name for Burwell's Pickwickian syndrome, a heavy chest wall makes breathing hard work; it is defined by a body mass index above 30 and a raised carbon dioxide level even while awake (pCO2 above 45 mmHg), affects an estimated 0.14 to 0.6 percent of the general population and up to 42 percent of sleep-clinic patients with a body mass index over 35, and is usually accompanied by obstructive apnoea.

Chronic hypoventilation starves the body of oxygen and loads it with carbon dioxide, night after night. The consequences, which worsen as the blood gas abnormalities extend into the waking day, include pulmonary hypertension leading to failure of the right side of the heart (cor pulmonale), an excess of red blood cells (polycythaemia), cardiac arrhythmias, morning headaches, sleepiness and impaired thinking. People may present to a psychiatrist with poor concentration, low mood or confusion long before anyone measures their blood gases.

Treatment starts with the cause. For Cheyne-Stokes breathing that means optimising the treatment of heart failure; for opioid-related central apnoea, reducing the opioid dose where possible. Positive airway pressure is used for many forms of central apnoea. A more sophisticated device, adaptive servo-ventilation, varies its pressure support breath by breath to smooth out periodic breathing, and it seemed the natural treatment for Cheyne-Stokes breathing in heart failure. The SERVE-HF trial (Cowie and colleagues, 2015) tested it in 1,325 patients with heart failure, an ejection fraction of 45 percent or less and predominantly central apnoea. Adaptive servo-ventilation controlled the apnoeas, but it did not improve the main outcome, and all-cause and cardiovascular deaths were significantly higher in the treated group; it is now avoided in that group of patients, a reminder that correcting a number on a sleep study is not the same as helping the patient. For hypoventilation the mainstay is non-invasive ventilation at night through a mask, usually bilevel positive airway pressure, which gives more pressure on breathing in than on breathing out, together with treatment of the underlying lung, muscle or weight problem. Children with congenital central alveolar hypoventilation need ventilatory support for life, through a tracheostomy or, in some, by electrical pacing of the diaphragm. In every form, sedatives and alcohol are best avoided. Table 8.24 compares the three disorders.

Table 8.24 The three breathing-related sleep disorders of DSM-5-TR.
FeatureObstructive sleep apnoea hypopnoeaCentral sleep apnoeaSleep-related hypoventilation
MechanismUpper airway collapses; effort continuesDrive to breathe fails; effort stops (unstable control, high loop gain, opioids)Breathing too shallow for long periods; carbon dioxide rises
Polysomnographic rule5 or more obstructive events an hour with symptoms, or 15 or more regardless5 or more central apnoeas an hour; central events over half of all eventsDecreased breathing with raised carbon dioxide, or sustained low oxygen not due to apnoeas
Who gets itObese, middle-aged men; postmenopausal women; children with large tonsilsMen over 60 with heart failure or stroke; long-term opioid usersPeople with COPD, neuromuscular or chest-wall disease, severe obesity; rare congenital form
SubtypesSeverity: mild, moderate, severeIdiopathic; Cheyne-Stokes breathing; comorbid with opioid useIdiopathic; congenital central alveolar (PHOX2B); comorbid
Main treatmentsCPAP; weight loss; positional therapy; oral appliance; surgery; hypoglossal nerve stimulationTreat the cause (heart failure, opioids); positive airway pressure; caution with adaptive servo-ventilation in heart failureNon-invasive (bilevel) ventilation; treat the cause; lifelong ventilation in the congenital form
Clinical link

A middle-aged, overweight man complains that he sleeps badly and wakes unrefreshed, and asks for "a sleeping tablet". If his real problem is undiagnosed sleep apnoea, a benzodiazepine, an opioid or a nightcap of alcohol can make it worse, because all of them relax the airway muscles and blunt the drive to breathe; DSM-5-TR warns that sedating medicines can worsen the course of apnoea. Before treating "insomnia" or "fatigue" in such a person, a psychologist or psychiatrist should ask the bed partner about snoring and breathing pauses, and refer for a sleep study when the answers are yes.

Exam trap

Central sleep apnoea: at least 5 central apnoeas an hour, the brain not driving breathing; its subtypes are idiopathic, Cheyne-Stokes breathing (heart failure, stroke; crescendo-decrescendo) and opioid-related. Hypoventilation means shallow breathing with raised carbon dioxide, not pauses; its congenital form is linked to PHOX2B. "Pickwickian syndrome" is today's obesity hypoventilation.

When the body clock is out of step

The biology of the body clock was taught in Book 1, Section 5.6, and only its essentials are needed here. A master clock in the suprachiasmatic nucleus of the hypothalamus keeps a rhythm of slightly more than 24 hours (about 24.2 hours when people live without time cues), and it is reset every day by zeitgebers, of which light reaching it from the retina is by far the strongest; in darkness it directs the pineal gland to release melatonin. Sleepiness at any moment depends both on this circadian signal and on the sleep pressure that builds up the longer we are awake (Borbély's two-process model). People differ in the timing of their clocks, from larks to owls; adolescents drift towards owlishness and older adults back towards larkishness; jet lag and shift work throw the clock out of step with the world; and rotating shifts are tolerated better when they rotate forwards, as Czeisler and colleagues showed in 1982. With that foundation, the disorders follow almost by logic.

DSM-5-TR groups them as the circadian rhythm sleep-wake disorders and gives them one set of criteria with several types. Criterion A requires a persistent or recurrent pattern of sleep disruption that is mainly due either to an alteration of the circadian system itself or to a misalignment between the person's internal rhythm and the sleep-wake schedule demanded by his physical environment or by his social or working life. The two halves of that sentence matter. In some types the clock itself is abnormal (it runs late, early, without a rhythm, or on a day longer than 24 hours); in the shift work type the clock may be perfectly normal and the problem is that the job demands sleep at the wrong time. Criterion B requires that the disruption produce excessive sleepiness, insomnia or both. Notice that these are exactly the complaints of the other sleep disorders; what marks a circadian disorder is not the symptom but its timing, and the tell-tale sign is that, when allowed to sleep on their own preferred schedule, people with the delayed and advanced types sleep normally. Criterion C requires clinically significant distress or impairment, which is what separates a disorder from a mere preference for late nights.

The clinician then specifies the type: delayed sleep phase type (code G47.21, which may be specified as familial and as overlapping with the non-24-hour type), advanced sleep phase type (G47.22, which may be specified as familial), irregular sleep-wake type (G47.23), non-24-hour sleep-wake type (G47.24), shift work type (G47.26) or unspecified type (G47.20), and the course: episodic (symptoms lasting at least 1 month but less than 3 months), persistent (3 months or longer) or recurrent (two or more episodes within a year). The diagnosis rests mainly on the history and on a sleep diary or actigraphy kept for at least a week, including weekends, when social obligations are looser and the person's natural timing shows through. ICD-11 lists the same disorders as delayed (7A60), advanced (7A61), irregular (7A62), non-24-hour (7A63) and shift work type (7A64), adds jet lag type (7A65), and, like the other sleep-wake disorders, places them in its Chapter 7, outside the mental disorders; ICD-10 had placed the nonorganic disorders of the sleep-wake schedule at F51.2 (Table 8.2).

That brings us to the most frequently tested omission in the group. DSM-IV included a jet lag type of circadian rhythm sleep disorder, and Barlow and Durand still describe it: difficulty falling asleep at the proper time and fatigue by day after rapid travel across several time zones. DSM-5 removed it, and DSM-5-TR mentions jet lag only in the differential diagnosis of the shift work type, to be recognised from the travel history. The International Classification of Sleep Disorders (ICSD-3), whose circadian disorders otherwise closely parallel DSM-5-TR's, keeps jet lag type, and so does ICD-11.

Exam trap

DSM-5-TR has five named circadian types: delayed sleep phase, advanced sleep phase, irregular sleep-wake, non-24-hour sleep-wake and shift work (plus unspecified). Jet lag is not one of them; it was a type in DSM-IV and remains a type in ICSD-3 and ICD-11 (7A65). The symptom is insomnia, excessive sleepiness or both; the clue is the timing, and people with the delayed and advanced types sleep normally when allowed to keep their own hours.

The five circadian types

In the delayed sleep phase type the main sleep period is shifted later, usually by more than 2 hours, relative to the times the person wants or needs to sleep and wake. He cannot fall asleep until 2 or 3 a.m. however early he goes to bed, and cannot wake for a 7 a.m. alarm without great difficulty, often with a prolonged, confused sleep inertia, after which he is sleepy through the morning and comes alive in the evening. Left to his own hours, on holidays, his sleep is of normal length and quality. It is the night owl taken to an extreme, and it is most common in adolescents and young adults: studies in Norway and Sweden have estimated 3.3 to 4.6 percent in that age group, against 0.2 to 1.7 percent of adults in Norway and New Zealand. Symptoms usually begin in adolescence or early adulthood, last months or years before diagnosis, often lessen with age and frequently relapse. An exacerbation is typically triggered by a new school or work schedule with an early start, and people who can arrange their work around their late clock may lose their symptoms altogether. Several mechanisms have been proposed: a circadian period longer than average, hypersensitivity to evening light (which delays the clock) or hyposensitivity to morning light (which should advance it), a weak homeostatic sleep drive, and genes. A family history is common, and one study of families with strong heritability found a mutation in the clock gene CRY1, carried by about 0.6 percent of the population, that increases the inhibition of the activator clock genes CLOCK and BMAL1. Behaviour matters too: when researchers measured the evening rise of melatonin in dim light (the dim light melatonin onset, the most widely available laboratory marker of circadian phase), only 57 percent of carefully diagnosed patients had a physiologically delayed clock, and in the other 43 percent late habits rather than a late clock seemed to be the main problem. Repeated attempts to fall asleep too early can breed a secondary insomnia disorder. The type is associated with depressive disorders, personality disorders, somatic symptom and illness anxiety disorders, obsessive-compulsive disorder, attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder and autism spectrum disorder. It must be told apart from the ordinary late schedule of many adolescents and young adults, which causes no distress and so is not a disorder.

The advanced sleep phase type is the mirror image: sleep and wake times several hours, usually more than 2 hours, earlier than desired. The person is overwhelmingly sleepy by 8 p.m. and awake, unable to return to sleep, at 3 or 4 a.m.; the complaints are early-morning insomnia and sleepiness in the evening. These people are extreme "morning types", whose circadian markers, such as the rhythms of melatonin and core body temperature, run 2 to 4 hours earlier than normal. If social life forces them to stay up later they still wake early, so they become chronically sleep deprived, and the use of hypnotics or alcohol to stay asleep and stimulants to stay awake can lead to substance misuse. The prevalence is about 1 percent in middle-aged adults in the United States, and the type is more common in older people, because ageing advances sleep timing. Onset is usually in late adulthood, though familial forms begin earlier and pass down in an autosomal dominant pattern; mutations in the clock genes PER2 and CKI have been found in such families. Habits perpetuate it: by going to bed early these people miss the evening light that would delay their clocks, and by waking early they catch the morning light that advances them further. The important differential is depression. Early-morning waking is the classic terminal insomnia of a major depressive episode (Chapter 5, Section 5.2.2), and it also occurs in bipolar disorder, insomnia disorder and medical conditions. In the advanced sleep phase type, however, the person who is allowed to go to bed early sleeps a normal amount, and the mood is normal.

In the irregular sleep-wake type there is no discernible circadian rhythm at all. There is no main sleep period; sleep is broken into at least three periods scattered through the 24 hours, the longest usually falling between 2 and 6 a.m. and lasting less than 4 hours, so the person complains of insomnia at night and naps through the day. It is most often seen in people with neurodegenerative disorders such as Alzheimer's disease, Parkinson's disease and Huntington's disease, in children with neurodevelopmental disorders, after traumatic brain injury, and in anyone living in isolation, in a hospital ward or an institution where light is dim and the day has little structure; people with major neurocognitive disorder are exposed to much less bright light than others, even at home. Its prevalence is unknown, it is commoner in older adults, and its course is persistent. The caregiver's sleep, broken by the patient's night-time wandering, often suffers as much as the patient's, and is an important part of the clinical picture. It must be told apart from a voluntarily irregular schedule and poor sleep hygiene.

In the non-24-hour sleep-wake type the internal clock is not synchronised to the 24-hour day at all, so sleep and wake times drift steadily, usually later and later, from one day to the next. You have met this pattern before: it is the free-running rhythm of the volunteers who lived in caves without time cues (Book 1, Section 5.6.1), lived permanently. The person passes through a cycle. When his internal night happens to coincide with the real night he sleeps well and has no symptoms; as his clock drifts later he develops sleep-onset insomnia; and when his internal night falls in the daytime he struggles to stay awake at work and lies awake at night. Then, weeks later, the cycle comes round again. The type is most common in blind or visually impaired people with reduced light perception, because the light signal that resets the clock never reaches it; its prevalence among blind people in the United States is estimated at 50 percent. It is rare in sighted people, in whom there is often a history of delayed sleep phase, of little exposure to light and to structured social and physical activity, or of brain injury, and in whom sleep is often long. Hospitalised people with neurological and psychiatric disorders, and depressed or bipolar people who live in social isolation, are vulnerable. The diagnosis needs a long sleep diary or actigraphy, and repeated measurement of melatonin can track the drifting phase.

The shift work type is the commonest in daily life and the one in which the clock itself is normal. The person works regularly (not merely as occasional overtime) outside the window of about 8 a.m. to 6 p.m., especially at night, and has both excessive sleepiness, including unintended sleep, at work and impaired sleep at home; both sets of symptoms are usually needed for the diagnosis, and they typically resolve when the person returns to day work. Night workers make up 16 to 20 percent of the workforce in the United States, and the disorder is estimated to affect 5 to 10 percent of them; Barlow and Durand cite a finding that almost two-thirds of workers on rotating shifts complain of poor sleep. It becomes more common with age, especially after 50, and tends to worsen the longer the schedule continues, because although older workers shift their clocks about as fast as younger ones, the shift disrupts their sleep more. A morning-type disposition and a need for more than 8 hours of sleep predispose to it; so do strong competing demands at home, as in parents of young children, whereas people who can commit fully to a nocturnal life seem protected. Shift workers are more often obese, so sleep apnoea may add to the problem. The consequences reach far beyond tiredness: poor performance, accidents at work and on the drive home, interpersonal problems, more alcohol and other substance use, depression, and, with prolonged exposure, gastrointestinal disorders, cardiovascular disease, diabetes and cancer. In people with bipolar disorder, the lost nights of shift work can set off episodes of mania. The diagnosis depends on the severity of the symptoms and distress, since some difficulty is a normal part of shift work. If the symptoms persist even when the person has lived on a day routine for several weeks, another sleep disorder (sleep apnoea, insomnia or narcolepsy) should be suspected, and people who fly across many time zones very often may have similar symptoms, distinguished by the travel history.

Devika, 23, works in IT support in Hyderabad for an American client. Her team handles calls during American office hours, which means a shift from about 7 p.m. to 4 a.m. in India, and every fortnight the roster switches her between that shift and a morning shift that starts at 6 a.m. After a night shift she reaches her family's flat in the early morning as the city wakes; she sleeps from 6 a.m. to noon in a bright, warm room, woken by traffic, the pressure cooker, the doorbell and her younger brother's online classes, and she rarely manages more than four or five hours. At work she fights sleep between 3 and 5 a.m., and one morning she dozed on the back of a colleague's two-wheeler and nearly fell off. She has stomach upsets, has missed two cousins' weddings and feels permanently jet-lagged. On her morning-shift weeks she cannot fall asleep at night. Figure 8.15 shows her pattern beside the other four types and normal sleep.

Exam trap

Delayed sleep phase: late to sleep, late to rise, typical of adolescents and young adults. Advanced: early to bed, early to rise, typical of older adults; do not confuse its early waking with the terminal insomnia of depression. Non-24-hour: the free-running clock, in about half of blind people. Irregular: no main sleep period, typical of dementia and institutions. Shift work: the clock is normal, the schedule is not.

Treating circadian disorders

Barlow and Durand note that medication is not usually the first treatment for circadian disorders; instead, the clinician tries to move the clock, or to move the person's schedule to fit the clock. Two tools move the clock, light and melatonin, and the secret of using them is timing. The effect of a pulse of light on the clock depends on when in the circadian day it arrives, a relationship plotted as a phase-response curve. Light in the evening and the early part of the night delays the clock, so the person becomes sleepy later the next day; light in the early morning advances it, so he becomes sleepy earlier. DSM-5-TR builds this into its account of the delayed type, where hypersensitivity to evening light, a delay signal, and hyposensitivity to the phase-advancing effect of morning light are among the suspected causes. Melatonin has a phase-response curve that runs roughly the other way: taken in the late afternoon or evening it advances the clock, and taken in the morning it delays it.

From those two principles the treatments follow. The first, bright light therapy, uses a light box, a bank of lamps giving more than 2,000 lux, against the roughly 250 lux of ordinary indoor light, as Barlow and Durand describe; several hours of exposure have reset the rhythms of many patients. For the delayed type, the person sits in bright light soon after waking, keeps the evening dim and may take a small dose of melatonin in the evening, a few hours before his habitual bedtime, so that both signals pull the clock earlier. For the advanced type the timing is reversed, with bright light in the evening. For the irregular type in dementia or in institutions, the aim is to strengthen weak zeitgebers: bright light and structured activity by day, darkness and quiet by night. For the non-24-hour type in blind people, light cannot help, so a melatonin signal is given at the same time every evening to entrain the clock; tasimelteon, a drug that stimulates the melatonin MT1 and MT2 receptors, was approved in the United States in January 2014 specifically for non-24-hour sleep-wake disorder in totally blind people. Melatonin taken at bedtime at the destination also reduces jet lag, as Pinel notes.

A second strategy moves the schedule rather than the clock, and it rests on a fact you met in Book 1: phase delays are easier than phase advances. It is much easier to stay up later than to fall asleep earlier, just as flying west is easier than flying east. The technique called chronotherapy exploits this for the delayed type. Instead of trying to drag bedtime earlier, the person goes to bed several hours later each night, going right round the clock, until bedtime arrives at the desired hour, where it is then held firmly. Its drawback, as Barlow and Durand point out, is that it means sleeping in the daytime for several days, which is impossible for most people with fixed responsibilities. The same principle governs shift schedules: rotating shifts clockwise, from day to evening to night, helps workers adjust better than rotating them backwards, and companies that move workers to shifts that start later rather than earlier have improved both productivity and job satisfaction, as Pinel reports.

For the shift work type, the remaining measures are practical. A forward-rotating or, where possible, a stable roster; bright light at work during the night shift; a dark, quiet, cool bedroom for daytime sleep, with blackout curtains, earplugs and a family that understands that a daytime sleeper is not available; a safe journey home rather than a drowsy ride at dawn; and, for severe sleepiness at work, a wake-promoting drug. Modafinil was approved in the United States for the sleepiness of shift work disorder in 2004 (Section 8.8.7). Hypnotics have at most a short-term role, and the risk of alcohol and sedative misuse in people fighting their own clocks is real. General sleep hygiene, described in Book 2, Section 9.4.3, applies to every type. Bright light is also a treatment for depression with a seasonal pattern (Chapter 5, Section 5.3.11), and the disturbed rhythms of the mood disorders are discussed in Chapter 5, Section 5.5.5.

Devika's case was managed with exactly these tools. Her employer agreed to move her to a stable night team instead of the fortnightly switch between nights and early mornings, the office cab dropped her home after the shift, her family moved her into the back bedroom with blackout curtains and an agreement about noise until 2 p.m., and she began sleeping from 5.30 a.m. to 1 p.m. with a short nap before work. Within a month she was sleeping six and a half to seven hours by day and no longer dozing at her desk. Table 8.25 summarises the five types and their treatment.

Table 8.25 The five circadian rhythm sleep-wake disorders of DSM-5-TR.
TypePatternTypical patient and causeTell apart fromTreatment

Advanced sleep phaseSleep and wake several hours early; evening sleepiness, early wakingOlder adults; familial dominant forms (PER2, CKI)Early waking of depression; insomnia disorderEvening bright light; regular schedule

Exam trap

Morning light advances the clock and evening light delays it, so the delayed type gets morning light and the advanced type evening light. Melatonin works the opposite way (evening melatonin advances). Delays are easier than advances, which is why chronotherapy moves bedtime later each night and why shifts should rotate clockwise (day, evening, night). Light boxes give more than 2,000 lux. Tasimelteon is for non-24-hour disorder in the totally blind.
