8.10 Parasomnias, Restless Legs Syndrome and Substance-Induced Sleep DisordersMedium yield · 2 Qs

Picture four homes, in four Indian cities, on the same night. At about eleven o'clock, an hour and a half after he was put to bed, an eight-year-old boy gets up, walks down the dark corridor with his eyes wide open and begins to fumble with the bolt of the front door. At a quarter to twelve, in another flat, a four-year-old girl sits bolt upright and screams as if something were tearing at her; her mother runs in and finds her drenched in sweat, staring straight through her and impossible to comfort. At four in the morning a young woman jerks awake with her heart pounding, instantly alert, and could tell you every detail of the chase she has just escaped. And at five, a retired bank officer in his late sixties shouts, swears and swings his fist at an intruder who exists only in his dream. The blow lands on his wife.

None of these four people has a problem with the amount of sleep. The disorders of Sections 8.7 to 8.9 were disorders of how much a person sleeps, how deeply and at what hour. The disorders in this section are disorders of what happens during sleep: strange behaviours, frightening experiences and bodily events that break into the night. Look again at the clock times in the four stories. They are not accidental. By the end of this section you will be able to diagnose each household from the time on the clock and the answer to one question: does the sleeper remember? The section then turns to restless legs syndrome, the commonest of the disorders of movement around sleep, and ends with the sleep disorders that drugs and medicines cause, so that, with Sections 8.7 to 8.9, you can place any sleep complaint you meet.

What a parasomnia is, and why timing tells them apart

The word parasomnia means "beside sleep" (Greek para, beside, and Latin somnus, sleep). DSM-5-TR defines the parasomnias as disorders characterised by abnormal behavioural, experiential or physiological events occurring in association with sleep, with specific sleep stages, or with the transitions between sleeping and waking. Each of the three adjectives does work. A behavioural event is something the sleeper does: walking, eating, shouting, punching. An experiential event is something the sleeper lives through: overwhelming terror, a dream of being hunted, the certainty that someone is sitting on one's chest, the sound of an explosion inside the head. A physiological event is something the body does: the pounding heart and dilated pupils of a sleep terror, or the bladder emptying in bedwetting. The phrase "in association with sleep, specific sleep stages or sleep-wake transitions" is just as important, because it tells you that every parasomnia has a home in the night, and the home is the clue to the diagnosis.

Section 8.7.3 placed the parasomnias in the classification of sleep disorders: they are the second great family beside the dyssomnias, the disorders of the amount, quality and timing of sleep. DSM-5-TR is economical. It recognises only three parasomnia disorders: the non-rapid eye movement (NREM) sleep arousal disorders (sleepwalking and sleep terrors), nightmare disorder, and rapid eye movement (REM) sleep behaviour disorder. It notes that the commonest of all are the NREM sleep arousal disorders and REM sleep behaviour disorder. The International Classification of Sleep Disorders, third edition (ICSD-3, 2014), written by sleep physicians for sleep physicians, lists many more, from sleep paralysis to exploding head syndrome, and Section 8.10.5 meets them. Restless legs syndrome is not a parasomnia in either system: it is a sensorimotor disorder of the waking person lying in bed, and DSM-5-TR gives it a place of its own (Section 8.10.6).

Now recall the architecture of a night from Book 1, Section 5.7.4: four to six cycles of about 90 minutes, with deep N3 (slow-wave) sleep crowded into the first cycles and REM periods growing longer towards morning. From those two facts follows the most useful rule in this section, which Figure 8.16 draws. An event in the first third of the night, from a sleeper who is hard to wake, confused if woken and remembers nothing next morning, almost certainly comes out of N3: it is one of the NREM sleep arousal disorders. An event in the last third of the night, after which the sleeper is at once wide awake and tells a vivid, story-like dream, almost certainly comes out of REM sleep: it is a nightmare, or, if the sleeper moved and struck out, REM sleep behaviour disorder. Events at the edges of sleep, while falling asleep or waking up, belong to the transitions: sleep paralysis and the hallucinations of the sleep-wake border. And restless legs syndrome strikes before sleep has even begun, while the person lies still in the dark.

Why should recall and orientation follow the stage so faithfully? Because the two kinds of sleep are at opposite distances from waking. N3 is the stage furthest from it: the cortex is dominated by large slow waves, the threshold for waking is at its highest, and the systems that lay down new memories are effectively offline. An arousal out of N3 is therefore partial and messy, and whatever happens during it leaves almost no trace. REM sleep is, in its brain activity, the stage closest to waking. Its dreams are long and story-like, waking from it is quick and complete, and the dream is still fresh when the sleeper opens his eyes. The muscles tell the same story from the other side. In REM sleep the large muscles of the body are actively paralysed (REM atonia, Book 1, Section 5.7.3), so a person in normal REM sleep cannot get up and walk; in N3 the muscles keep their tone, so a half-aroused sleeper can rise, walk and even drive, but has no dream to act out. That is why sleepwalkers are not, as folklore holds, acting out their dreams, and why the people who really do act out their dreams have something wrong with the machinery of REM atonia.

Older textbooks described waking, NREM sleep and REM sleep as three separate rooms with doors between them: the brain is in one room or another, and passes quickly through the doors. The American sleep physicians Mark W. Mahowald and Carlos H. Schenck, working together in Minneapolis, spent their careers collecting the patients who do not fit that picture, and in a review in Nature in 2005 they drew the lesson. The three states, they argued, are not mutually exclusive. Pieces of one state can appear while the brain is mainly in another, or the brain can oscillate between states so fast that it seems to be in two at once. They called this state dissociation, and they proposed that most parasomnias are exactly such mixtures (Figure 8.17). In sleepwalking and sleep terrors, elements of waking (walking, seeing, fear) erupt out of NREM sleep while the rest of the brain stays asleep. In cataplexy and sleep paralysis, the atonia of REM sleep appears in a person who is awake, and in hypnagogic hallucinations the dream imagery of REM spills into waking (Section 8.8.4). In REM sleep behaviour disorder the reverse happens: the movement of waking appears inside REM sleep, just as, in a lucid dream, the self-awareness of waking appears inside a dream. A few patients show features of both sleepwalking and REM sleep behaviour disorder, which DSM-5-TR calls the parasomnia overlap syndrome. In healthy sleep the switches that move the brain between states (the "flip-flop" switch of Book 1, Section 5.7.6) snap cleanly from one position to another; a parasomnia is what happens when a switch flips only part of the way, or only in part of the brain.

Exam trap

Sleepwalking (somnambulism) and sleep terrors come from NREM sleep, mainly N3, in the first third of the night; they never come from REM sleep, when the body is paralysed. Nightmares and REM sleep behaviour disorder come from REM sleep, mostly in the last third. "Night terror" and pavor nocturnus are other names for sleep terrors, not for nightmares. Sleep talking (somniloquy) can happen in any stage. Restless legs syndrome is a sleep-wake disorder in its own right (a sleep-related movement disorder in ICSD-3), not a parasomnia, and the dyssomnias are the disorders of the amount and timing of sleep, not of events within it.

NREM sleep arousal disorders: sleepwalking and sleep terrors

Abhinav is eight and lives with his parents and his grandmother in a third-floor flat in Pune. Twice in the past month his grandmother, a light sleeper, has heard the bolt of the front door and found him standing in the open doorway a little before midnight, in his school vest, his eyes wide open and his face quite blank. When she spoke to him he mumbled something about "the bus", let her lead him back to bed without a word of protest, and was asleep again within seconds. On another night he walked into the drawing room and urinated into the shoe rack. In the morning he remembers nothing and laughs at the stories. His father admits, a little sheepishly, that he "used to walk" as a boy. In another part of the city, Yamini, aged four, sits bolt upright about an hour after she has been put to bed and lets out a piercing scream. Her eyes are open and her pupils wide, her heart is racing and her hair is wet with sweat; she thrashes, pushes her mother away and does not seem to know her. After five minutes she sinks back into sleep. Next morning she is cheerful and has no idea that anything happened.

Abhinav and Yamini have the two forms of a single disorder. DSM-5-TR groups them as the NREM sleep arousal disorders, and the criteria are best understood in four groups.

  • The event (Criterion A). There are recurrent episodes of incomplete awakening from sleep, usually during the first third of the main sleep episode, taking one of two forms. In sleepwalking, or somnambulism, the person repeatedly rises from bed during sleep and walks about, with a blank, staring face, relatively unresponsive to other people's efforts to talk to him, and can be woken only with great difficulty. In sleep terrors, also called night terrors or pavor nocturnus, there are recurrent abrupt terror arousals, usually beginning with a panicky scream, with intense fear and signs of autonomic arousal (dilated pupils, a racing heart, rapid breathing and sweating), and the person is relatively unresponsive to efforts to comfort him. The phrase "incomplete awakening" is the heart of the disorder: the person is neither properly awake nor properly asleep.
  • The missing memory (Criteria B and C). No dream imagery, or very little (at most a single visual scene), is recalled, and there is amnesia for the episode. These two criteria separate the NREM arousals from nightmares, where the dream is the whole complaint and is remembered in detail.
  • The threshold (Criterion D). The episodes cause clinically significant distress or impairment. Isolated episodes are so common in childhood that, without this rule, a large share of the world's children would carry a diagnosis. DSM-5-TR adds that whether the events amount to a disorder depends on their frequency, their potential for violence or injury, embarrassment, and the disruption and distress of other people in the household, and that severity is best judged by the nature and consequences of the behaviour rather than by frequency alone. One sleepwalk onto a balcony matters more than ten quiet walks to the kitchen.
  • The exclusions (Criteria E and F). The disturbance is not caused by a drug or medication, and coexisting mental and medical conditions do not explain it. Complex behaviours from sleep that begin after a sleeping pill is started are diagnosed as substance/medication-induced sleep disorder, parasomnia type (Section 8.10.7), and episodes that are really seizures, panic attacks or dissociative states belong to those diagnoses.

The clinician then specifies the type: sleepwalking type (code F51.3), with two possible specifiers, "with sleep-related eating" and "with sleep-related sexual behaviour (sexsomnia)", or sleep terror type (F51.4). The same two codes, F51.3 sleepwalking (somnambulism) and F51.4 sleep terrors (night terrors), were used for these "nonorganic sleep disorders" in ICD-10's Chapter V; ICD-11 moves them to its new sleep-wake chapter as disorders of arousal from non-REM sleep (7B00).

What does an episode actually look like? Most last 1 to 10 minutes, although some go on for up to an hour; the longest an episode can last is not known. The eyes are typically open. A sleepwalking episode often starts in a small way, with the sleeper sitting up, looking about or picking at the sheet, and then grows more complex: he gets out of bed, walks into a cupboard or out of the room, and sometimes out of the building. Sleepwalkers use the bathroom, eat, talk, run, or make frantic attempts to escape a threat that only they perceive. Most of what they do is routine and simple, but DSM-5-TR records cases of unlocking doors and even driving a car, and urinating in a cupboard or a wastebasket is a classic, embarrassing feature. Sleep is a state of relative analgesia, so a sleepwalker may cut his foot or bruise his shin and feel nothing until he wakes. If woken, he is briefly confused and disoriented, and then recovers fully. In a sleep terror the person typically sits up abruptly, screaming or crying, with a terrified face and a sense of overwhelming dread and a compulsion to escape; fragments of an image may be reported (a spider, the wall falling in), but never the story of a nightmare. Most people do not wake fully, return to sleep and have no memory of it in the morning. There is usually only one episode a night, occasionally several, and episodes rarely happen in daytime naps.

Two specialised forms of sleepwalking have their own specifiers. In sleep-related eating the person has unwanted, recurrent episodes of eating during partial arousals, with amnesia that ranges from complete to full awareness without the ability to stop. The food may be things the person would never choose awake, and inedible things such as sweet wrappers, small food boxes and even small toys may be swallowed. The person may find the evidence only next morning: an open refrigerator, crumbs in the bed, a cut finger. The consequences are real: poor control of diabetes, weight gain, cuts and burns from cooking half asleep, and poisoning by inedibles. It is more common in women, and sedative hypnotics, especially the "Z-drugs" such as zolpidem at higher doses or combined with other sedatives, can trigger it (Section 8.7.6). Do not confuse it with night eating syndrome (Section 8.4.7), in which the person is fully awake and aware when she gets up to eat: DSM-5-TR regards night eating syndrome as an abnormality in the circadian timing of meals, not a partial arousal from sleep. In sexsomnia (sleep sex), sexual behaviour, from masturbation and fondling to intercourse, arises from sleep without conscious awareness. It is more common in men and can damage relationships and lead to criminal charges, which is why it carries a specifier of its own.

ICSD-3 adds a third disorder of arousal, the confusional arousal, which DSM-5-TR mentions only in its text. Here the sleeper sits up in bed, looks around in a muddled way, may talk slowly or answer strangely, and then lies down again, without leaving the bed and without the terror of a sleep terror. Confusional arousals are common in young children, and in adults they appear after sleep deprivation and in untreated sleep apnoea, whose repeated arousals can produce confused, amnesic episodes of exactly this kind.

Why should a child walk, eat or scream while asleep? DSM-5-TR points out that many people have both types of episode on different occasions, which argues for a single underlying mechanism: varying degrees of simultaneous wakefulness and NREM sleep, which produce complex behaviour with varying degrees of awareness, movement and autonomic activation. Two rare recordings show what that means inside the brain. In 2000 the Swiss neurologist Claudio Bassetti and his colleagues injected a tracer into a man with a history of sleepwalking during an episode recorded in the laboratory and imaged his brain with single photon emission computed tomography (SPECT). Pathways from the thalamus to the cingulate cortex, a region involved in movement and emotion, were active, while the brain's other thalamocortical arousal systems remained switched off. Sleepwalking, they wrote, is "a dissociation between body sleep and mind sleep". In 2009 Michele Terzaghi and colleagues in Italy captured a confusional arousal in a 20-year-old man who had electrodes implanted deep in his brain for the investigation of epilepsy. During the episode his motor and cingulate cortex showed a waking pattern, while his frontal and parietal association cortex was full of the slow delta waves of deep sleep. In other words, the parts of the brain that move the body and generate fear were awake, and the parts that plan, judge and record memories were asleep. That one finding explains the blank face, the clumsy but purposeful walking, the terror without a story, and the amnesia. The arousals of sleep terrors also begin precipitously, without the gradual rise of heart rate and breathing that precedes waking from a REM nightmare, and the heart rate can double or triple. As a group, sleepwalkers show an instability of deep NREM sleep, with frequent brief arousals out of N3, but the overlap with normal sleepers is too great for this to serve as a diagnostic test.

How common are these disorders? Isolated episodes are very common all over the world. Between 10 and 30 percent of children have sleepwalked at least once, and the 12-month prevalence of sleepwalking in children across countries is about 5 percent; in Canada, 12 to 14.5 percent of children have episodes. Among adults in the United Kingdom 1.0 to 7.0 percent report sleepwalking episodes, but only 0.5 to 0.7 percent have them weekly or monthly. Lifetime estimates of sleepwalking range from about 6.9 to 29.2 percent around the world, and the past-year prevalence in adults is 1.5 to 3.6 percent. The prevalence of sleep terror disorder is unknown, but sleep terror episodes are extremely common in toddlers: in Canadian samples 34.4 to 36.9 percent of children at 18 months and 19.7 percent at 30 months have them, against 2.2 percent of Canadian and British adults. Barlow and Durand, citing Daniel J. Buysse and colleagues (1993), give round figures of about 6 percent of children (more boys than girls) and about 2 percent of adults with sleep terrors. The sex ratios shift with age: sleepwalking is more common in girls in childhood but in men in adulthood; sleep terrors are more common in boys than girls but equally common in men and women; and sleep-related eating is more common in women.

The course is usually kind. These disorders are commonest in childhood and are frequently outgrown: remission rates by adolescence are 50 to 65 percent, and among 10- to 18-year-olds the frequency falls to 1.1 percent for sleepwalking and 0.6 percent for sleep terrors. Older children and adults may remember more of the frightening images of a sleep terror than young children, who usually recall nothing or only a vague fear. In adults, violent or sexual behaviour during episodes is more likely. And a first episode of sleepwalking in an adult who never sleepwalked as a child is a warning sign, because it should prompt a search for a specific cause such as obstructive sleep apnoea, nocturnal seizures or the effect of a medication.

Two kinds of risk factor interact. The first is anything that deepens or fragments N3 sleep: sedative drugs, sleep deprivation, disruption of the sleep-wake schedule, fatigue and physical or emotional stress all make episodes more likely, and fever and sleep loss can raise their frequency sharply. This is why a child who sleepwalks is most likely to do so on the night after a late wedding, a long journey or a bout of fever. The second is heredity, and here the figures are striking. A family history of sleepwalking or sleep terrors is found in up to 80 percent of sleepwalkers; when both parents sleepwalked, as many as 60 percent of their children do; the prevalence of sleep terrors is as much as ten times higher among the first-degree relatives of people with sleep terrors; parental sleepwalking predicts sleep terrors in the children; and sleep terrors are much more concordant in identical than in fraternal twins. The exact mode of inheritance is unknown. Abhinav's father, who "used to walk", is part of his diagnosis. In children these disorders are usually not linked to any significant mental disorder, which is worth telling worried parents. In adults, sleepwalking has been associated with major depressive episodes and obsessive-compulsive disorder, and people with sleep terrors may score higher on measures of depression and anxiety.

The diagnosis is made mainly from the history given by the family. Polysomnography with video recording can document an episode if one happens to be captured (sleep deprivation the night before makes this more likely), and during an episode the electroencephalogram (EEG) may show the slow delta rhythm of N3 continuing into the "awakening", but outside an episode there is no reliable laboratory marker of the tendency to sleepwalk or have sleep terrors.

Key study: the sleepwalking defence (Broughton and colleagues, 1994)

In the early hours of 24 May 1987, Kenneth Parks, a young man from Pickering, Ontario, got up in the night, drove about 20 kilometres to his parents-in-law's house in Scarborough, near Toronto, killed his mother-in-law with a tyre iron and attacked his father-in-law, who survived. He then drove to a police station and told the officers that he thought he had just killed two people. He was charged with murder, and his defence was that he had been asleep throughout. The sleep researchers Roger J. Broughton, Rosalind D. Cartwright and their colleagues examined him and published the case in the journal Sleep in 1994. They weighed the alternatives (a complex partial seizure, a dissociative state, REM sleep behaviour disorder and ordinary waking violence) against the evidence for sleepwalking: a personal and family history of sleepwalking and related disorders, possible precipitating factors, neurological, psychiatric and psychological assessments, and polysomnographic findings. A jury acquitted him, and in 1992 the Supreme Court of Canada upheld the acquittal, holding that the evidence raised a reasonable doubt that he had acted voluntarily. Barlow and Durand note that such cases remain controversial, although there is evidence that some violent acts do coincide with sleepwalking. DSM-5-TR adds a point that matters in court: injuries to others during NREM arousals are confined to people who are close by, often people trying to restrain the sleeper; victims are not sought out.

The differential diagnosis follows from the mechanism. Nightmare disorder differs on every axis: the person wakes easily and completely, reports a vivid story-like dream, and the episodes come later in the night (Section 8.10.3); parents of children with NREM arousals sometimes mistake a reported fragment of an image for a nightmare. Breathing-related sleep disorders can produce confusional arousals with amnesia, but they also bring snoring, breathing pauses and daytime sleepiness, and in some people sleep apnoea actually precipitates sleepwalking. REM sleep behaviour disorder (Section 8.10.4) arises from REM sleep, later in the night, in older people who wake easily and recall a dream that matches their movements. Sleep-related seizures can closely mimic NREM arousals, but they tend to be stereotyped (the same sequence of movements each time), occur several times a night, appear in daytime naps and can arise from wakefulness, which NREM arousals never do. Alcohol-induced blackouts can involve very complex behaviour with no memory afterwards. Dissociative amnesia with fugue (Section 7.5) can be extremely hard to tell from sleepwalking, but it arises from a period of wakefulness during the night rather than suddenly out of sleep, and a history of repeated childhood abuse is usually present; malingering, too, occurs during wakefulness. Panic disorder can cause abrupt awakenings from deep NREM sleep with fear (the nocturnal panic attack of Section 6.2.2), but the person wakes rapidly and completely, without the confusion, amnesia or motor activity of a sleep terror, and remembers the attack. Finally, medication-induced complex behaviours during sleep, with benzodiazepines, Z-drugs, opioids, cocaine, nicotine, antipsychotics, tricyclic antidepressants and other drugs, are diagnosed as a substance/medication-induced sleep disorder.

Treatment starts with reassurance and safety. Most children outgrow these episodes, and parents can be told that, despite the drama, the child is not suffering during a sleep terror and will not remember it. The common belief that waking a sleepwalker is dangerous is, as Barlow and Durand point out, not true, although a half-awake sleeper may be confused or may lash out if grabbed, so the usual advice is to guide him gently back to bed. The home is made safe: outer doors and windows locked, keys put away, a bell or alarm fixed to the bedroom door, sharp objects and car keys out of reach, a ground-floor bedroom or at least no top bunk. Because sleep loss and an irregular schedule trigger episodes, a regular bedtime and enough sleep are part of the treatment, and adults are advised to avoid alcohol and sedatives and to have any sleep apnoea treated.

Key study: scheduled awakenings for sleep terrors (Durand and Mindell, 1999)

Sleep terrors in a given child tend to occur at about the same time each night. V. Mark Durand and Jodi A. Mindell used that regularity. In what Barlow and Durand describe as the first controlled study of its kind, they asked the parents of children who were having sleep terrors almost every night to wake the child briefly about 30 minutes before the usual time of the episode, and then to let the child go back to sleep. Over several weeks the awakenings were faded out. This simple technique of scheduled awakenings (also called anticipatory awakenings) almost eliminated the sleep terrors. The likely reason is that a brief, planned arousal resets the sleep cycle and prevents the partial arousal out of N3 that would have produced the terror. It is now a standard first behavioural step for frequent childhood sleep terrors and sleepwalking.

For adults, hypnosis has a small but encouraging evidence base: in 1991 Thomas D. Hurwitz, Mahowald, Schenck and their colleagues reported 27 adults with sleepwalking or sleep terrors taught self-hypnosis in one to six office visits, of whom 74 percent reported much or very much improvement over long follow-up. Psychological treatment of any stress that is driving the episodes may also help (relaxation methods are taught in Book 2, Section 4.8). Drugs are kept for frequent episodes that are dangerous to the sleeper or to others. Here the benzodiazepine clonazepam, taken at bedtime, is the best known: in 1996 Schenck and Mahowald reported 170 adults treated with nightly benzodiazepines for at least six months for disorders of disrupted sleep, 69 of them for injurious sleepwalking and sleep terrors. Control was complete or substantial in 86 percent of the whole group, most took clonazepam for an average of three and a half years, the dose hardly rose, and misuse was rare. Benzodiazepines reduce the amount of deep N3 sleep, which may be part of how they work (their pharmacology is in Section 6.9.2). But the paradox is worth noticing: in another person a sedative hypnotic can be the very cause of complex behaviour from sleep.

Clinical link

When an adult presents with new sleepwalking, the first question is not "what stress are you under?" but "what has changed in your body or your medicines?" Ask about snoring and breathing pauses, a new sleeping pill (especially a Z-drug, taken late or with alcohol), new antidepressants or antipsychotics, and any history suggestive of seizures, and ask the bed partner. Adult-onset sleepwalking is a reason for a sleep study, not just for reassurance.

Exam trap

Somnambulism is sitting up, walking or performing other complex behaviour while asleep, arising from NREM sleep (mostly N3) in the first third of the night, with a blank stare, difficulty waking and amnesia (RML 2022). Read the distractors as diagnoses: "extreme fear, agitation and screaming while asleep" is sleep terrors (pavor nocturnus), the other NREM arousal disorder; "acting out nightmares" is REM sleep behaviour disorder, from REM sleep late in the night; "uncomfortable sensations in the legs causing movement and loss of sleep" is restless legs syndrome. Two further traps: the eyes of a sleepwalker are typically open, and waking a sleepwalker is not dangerous.

Nightmare disorder

Rehana is 23, a postgraduate student in Lucknow. Since she was in school she has had the same kind of dream three or four nights a week. She is being chased through a crowded railway station by someone she cannot see; her legs will not run; the train she must catch is pulling out; and just as a hand closes on her shoulder she wakes, usually at about four or five in the morning, with her heart thudding. She is instantly awake, knows exactly where she is and can describe the station platform by platform. It takes her an hour or more to get back to sleep, and the dread clings to her through the morning. Lately she has begun to stay up until two o'clock studying, so as to "have less night left", and she is exhausted in class. Nothing especially terrible has ever happened to her.

Everyone has bad dreams. What makes Rehana's a disorder? DSM-5-TR's criteria for nightmare disorder (F51.5) answer in five steps. Criterion A describes the dreams: repeated occurrences of extended, extremely dysphoric and well-remembered dreams that usually involve efforts to avoid threats to survival, security or physical integrity, and that generally occur during the second half of the main sleep period. Each word in that sentence excludes something. "Extended" and "well-remembered" exclude the fragmentary single image of a sleep terror. "Dysphoric" is broader than frightening: the emotion may be fear, but also anger, disgust, shame or grief. "Second half" places the dreams in REM sleep. Criterion B is the decisive clinical sign: on waking from the dream the person rapidly becomes oriented and alert, exactly the opposite of the confused, unreachable child of a sleep terror. Criterion C requires clinically significant distress or impairment. Criterion D excludes nightmares caused by a substance, and Criterion E requires that coexisting mental disorders and medical conditions do not adequately explain the predominant complaint of dysphoric dreams. The nightmares of post-traumatic stress disorder, for example, are part of that disorder (Section 7.2), and a separate diagnosis of nightmare disorder is added only if their frequency or severity needs clinical attention in its own right.

DSM-5-TR then offers more specifiers than for almost any other sleep disorder, because nightmares turn up in so many settings. The clinician may note that they occur during sleep onset; that they occur with a mental disorder (including a substance use disorder), with a medical condition or with another sleep disorder, in which case the associated condition is coded straight after F51.5; and their duration, acute (one month or less), subacute (more than one month but less than six) or persistent (six months or more). Severity is rated simply by frequency: mild is less than one episode a week on average, moderate one or more a week but less than nightly, and severe nightly. Rehana's nightmares, three or four a week for years, are moderate and persistent.

Nightmares are typically lengthy, elaborate, story-like sequences of images that seem real. They arise almost exclusively during REM sleep, which is why they can occur at any time of the night but are most likely in its second half, when REM periods are longest and dreaming most intense. Anything that intensifies REM sleep early in the night, such as sleep loss, fragmented sleep, jet lag or drugs that act on REM, can bring nightmares forward, even to the first minutes of sleep. When a nightmare comes in a sleep-onset REM period, the frightened sleeper often wakes unable to move, a combination of nightmare and sleep paralysis. Because the body is paralysed in REM sleep, movement and speech are not characteristic of a nightmare: at most there is a brief cry or jerk as the dream ends and the dreamer wakes. If a person really does fight, kick or run in a frightening dream, the clinician should think of REM sleep behaviour disorder. Autonomic arousal is mild (some sweating, a faster heart and faster breathing), and it builds up before the awakening, whereas the arousal of a sleep terror erupts out of quiet sleep. Most nightmares are not replays of real events; those that follow trauma may replicate the threatening situation ("replicative nightmares"), and post-traumatic nightmares, unusually, can also arise from light N2 sleep.

Where is the line between a nightmare and an ordinary bad dream? Many researchers draw it at waking: a nightmare is a disturbing dream that wakes the sleeper, and a bad dream is one that does not and is recalled only later. On this definition, Barlow and Durand report, college students in a study by Antonio Zadra and Don C. Donderi (2000) had on average about 30 bad dreams and 10 nightmares a year. Nightmare disorder is not about having nightmares, which almost everyone does, but about having them so often, or so intensely, that they cause real distress or disrupt life, as when a person begins to fear or avoid sleep.

The prevalence of nightmares in childhood is about 1 to 5 percent; 1.3 to 3.9 percent of parents report that their preschool child has nightmares "often" or "always", and the figure rises to 5.2 percent at ages 5 to 15. Among adults, 6 percent have nightmares at least monthly; in several countries weekly nightmares are reported by 2 to 6 percent and frequent nightmares by 1 to 5 percent, although these estimates mix idiopathic and post-traumatic nightmares. Nightmares often begin between the ages of 3 and 6 but reach their peak prevalence and severity in late adolescence or early adulthood. Children exposed to acute or chronic stress are most likely to develop them, and in a minority frequent nightmares persist into adulthood and become almost lifelong, as Rehana's threaten to. Adult women report nightmares more often than adult men, a difference not found in children or the elderly, and the content differs, with women more often dreaming of sexual harassment or of loved ones disappearing or dying, and men of physical aggression, war or terror.

The risk factors are partly physiological and partly psychological. Sleep deprivation or fragmentation and irregular sleep-wake schedules that change the timing, intensity or amount of REM sleep put people at risk, which is one reason why Rehana's late nights may be making her nightmares worse, not better. In community studies of middle-aged adults in Hong Kong and Finland, frequent nightmares were associated with low income, mood disturbance, insomnia or sleep-disordered breathing, the use of antidepressants and frequent heavy drinking. People with nightmares report more past adverse events, though not necessarily trauma. Twin studies show a genetic influence on the tendency to nightmares and on their co-occurrence with other night-time behaviours such as sleep talking. And one protective factor is worth knowing: adaptive parental behaviour at the bedside, such as soothing a child after a nightmare, may protect against chronic nightmares. Culture shapes what a nightmare means. DSM-5-TR notes that in several cultural settings nightmares are taken as signs of a person's spiritual state or of the condition of the dead, and that sensitivity to such beliefs makes disclosure easier; among Cambodian refugees, frequent nightmares are strongly associated with post-traumatic stress disorder.

Clinical link

People with frequent nightmares are at substantially greater risk of suicidal thoughts and behaviour, even when sex and mental illness are taken into account (Section 8.7.3 sets out the links between sleep and suicide). A complaint of nightmares is therefore a reason to ask directly about suicidal ideas, especially in a young person who is also depressed or has been through trauma. The nightmares are also treatable in their own right, which gives the clinician something concrete to offer.

Many drugs can cause nightmares, and a careful medication history belongs in every assessment. DSM-5-TR lists dopaminergic drugs, beta-adrenergic blockers and other antihypertensives, amphetamines, cocaine and other stimulants, antidepressants, smoking-cessation aids and melatonin. Stopping a drug can do it too. Antidepressants and alcohol suppress REM sleep, and when they are withdrawn REM sleep rebounds (Book 1, Section 5.8.3), bringing long, intense and often disturbing dreams. When nightmares are severe enough to need attention in their own right and a drug is to blame, the diagnosis is a substance/medication-induced sleep disorder (Section 8.10.7).

The differential diagnosis is mostly a matter of applying the timing rule. Sleep terrors occur in the first third of the night, from N3, with little or no recall, intense autonomic arousal, and confusion rather than full waking. REM sleep behaviour disorder is suggested by complex vocal and motor activity during frightening dreams, usually in late middle-aged and older men; if nightmares came first and need attention in their own right, both diagnoses can be made. In bereavement, dysphoric dreams are about loss and sadness and are followed by reflection rather than distress. In post-traumatic stress disorder and acute stress disorder the nightmares are tied to the trauma. In narcolepsy nightmares are common, but excessive sleepiness, with or without cataplexy, makes the diagnosis (Section 8.8). Sleep-related seizures produce stereotyped movements and, if dreams are recalled, stereotyped imagery that may echo the person's daytime auras. Breathing-related sleep disorders cause awakenings with autonomic arousal but rarely with a remembered nightmare, and nocturnal panic attacks wake the person in fear but without a dream. Table 8.26 puts the five commonest night-time frights side by side; it is the most useful single table in this section.

Table 8.26 Telling the night-time disturbances apart.
FeatureNREM arousal disorders (sleepwalking, sleep terrors)Nightmare disorderREM sleep behaviour disorderNocturnal panic attack
Sleep stageNREM, mostly N3REMREM without atoniaNREM, as sleep deepens towards slow-wave sleep
Time of nightFirst thirdSecond halfMore than 90 minutes after sleep onset; later in the nightEarly to middle part of the night
Typical personChild; most outgrow it by adolescenceAny age; onset often at 3 to 6, peak in late adolescenceMan over 50, increasingly women and younger peopleAdult with panic disorder
What is seenWalking with eyes open and a blank stare; or a scream, terror and thrashingLittle movement; perhaps a cry on wakingShouting, punching, kicking, falling out of bed; eyes closedSudden waking in fear, without motor behaviour
Autonomic arousalLittle in sleepwalking; intense in sleep terrors (heart rate may double or triple)Mild, building before the awakeningNot a defining featureMarked, as in a daytime panic attack
On wakingHard to wake; confused; returns to sleepWakes fully and quickly; orientedWakes fully and quickly; orientedWakes fully and quickly; oriented
RecallNone or a single image; amnesia next morningVivid, story-like dreamDream recalled, matching the movementsThe panic is remembered; no dream
Physical treatmentSafety, regular sleep, scheduled awakenings; clonazepam if dangerousImagery rehearsal; prazosin (mixed evidence); review drugsBedroom safety; clonazepam or melatoninTreatment of panic disorder (Chapter 6)

The best-studied psychological treatment is imagery rehearsal therapy, a cognitive behavioural technique in which the person, while awake, writes down a recurring nightmare, changes the story in any way she likes (the hand on Rehana's shoulder might become a friend's, the train might wait), and rehearses the new version in imagination for a few minutes every day. The best-known trial is described in the box. Other cognitive behavioural methods, such as exposure, relaxation and the treatment of the insomnia that so often accompanies nightmares, are also used, and post-traumatic nightmares are usually treated within trauma-focused therapy (Book 2, Section 5.6.5). The drug most often used is prazosin, an alpha-1 adrenergic blocker that dampens the brain's noradrenaline response at night; notice the irony that beta-adrenergic blockers, which act on a different adrenaline receptor, are among the drugs that cause nightmares. Barlow and Durand list prazosin, with cognitive behaviour therapy, among the treatments that help, and early trials in military veterans with post-traumatic stress disorder were positive. But the largest trial, by Murray A. Raskind and colleagues in 2018, randomised 304 veterans with chronic post-traumatic stress disorder and frequent nightmares to prazosin or placebo for 26 weeks and found no difference in distressing dreams or sleep quality. The evidence for prazosin is therefore best described as mixed. For a person like Rehana, a regular sleep schedule (ending her late nights), imagery rehearsal and a review of any medicines are the sensible first steps.

Key study: rewriting the nightmare (Krakow and colleagues, 2001)

Barry Krakow and his colleagues in Albuquerque, New Mexico, randomly assigned 168 women with chronic nightmares, almost all of them survivors of sexual assault and 95 percent with moderate to severe post-traumatic stress disorder, either to three sessions of imagery rehearsal therapy (88 women) or to a waiting list (80 women). The treated women were taught to write down a nightmare, change it, and rehearse the changed version while awake. At follow-up three and six months later, the treated group had fewer nights a week with nightmares and fewer nightmares, slept better, and had fewer post-traumatic stress symptoms, with moderate to large effects; the waiting-list group improved only slightly. The study, published in the Journal of the American Medical Association, showed that a brief treatment aimed at the nightmares themselves, rather than at the trauma behind them, could help. Its limits were a waiting-list rather than an active control and a large loss of participants to follow-up.

Exam trap

Nightmares come from REM sleep in the second half of the night, and the dreamer wakes fully oriented with vivid recall. If the description is a scream early in the night, a child who cannot be comforted, and no memory next morning, the answer is sleep terrors (pavor nocturnus), however frightening it sounds. Nightmare disorder's severity is graded by frequency (mild less than weekly, moderate weekly but not nightly, severe nightly), and its code is F51.5, next to F51.3 for sleepwalking and F51.4 for sleep terrors. Prazosin, an alpha-1 blocker, is used to treat nightmares; beta blockers can cause them.

REM sleep behaviour disorder

Ravindra is 67 and retired three years ago from a nationalised bank in Mysuru. His wife brought him to the neurology outpatient department after he punched her in the face at about five one morning. For several years, she said, he had talked and shouted in his sleep, sometimes swearing in a way he never would when awake; lately he had begun to kick and flail, and once he fell out of bed and cut his eyebrow on the bedside table. That morning, woken by her cry, he was instantly alert and appalled, and he could tell the doctor exactly what he had been dreaming: two men had broken into the house, and he was fighting them off to protect her. He sleeps well otherwise and is not sleepy in the day. Asked about other changes, he mentions in passing that for some time he has not been able to smell the jasmine in the garden.

The story of this disorder begins with cats. In 1965 the French neurophysiologist Michel Jouvet (1925 to 2017), who worked in Lyon and had named REM sleep "paradoxical sleep", reported with his colleague Delorme that cats with small lesions in the pontine tegmentum, near the locus coeruleus, no longer lay limp during REM sleep. Instead, while remaining unresponsive to what was actually around them, they raised their heads and seemed to attack, to defend themselves or to explore, as if acting out their dreams. For two decades the observation remained a laboratory curiosity. Then, in 1986, Carlos Schenck, Mark Mahowald and their colleagues in Minneapolis described five patients in the journal Sleep: four men aged 67 to 72 who had injured themselves or their wives with aggressive behaviour during sleep, often while trying to act out a dream, and a 60-year-old woman with disruptive but non-violent dream behaviour. Polysomnography found no seizures but showed REM sleep with a variable loss of the normal chin atonia and greatly increased twitching of the limbs. Serious neurological disease was present in four of the five, and in two of them clonazepam produced an immediate and lasting suppression of the behaviour. The authors called it "a new category of parasomnia" and pointed out that it reproduced, in people, the findings in Jouvet's cats of 21 years before. Pinel and Barnes quote one of their patients, a man who dreamed he was playing football: "as I cut back there is this 280-pound tackle waiting, so I gave him my shoulder and knocked him out of the way. When I awoke I was standing in front of our dresser. I had knocked lamps, mirrors, and everything off the dresser; hit my head against the wall; and banged my knee against the dresser."

REM sleep behaviour disorder (RBD; code G47.52) is defined in DSM-5-TR by seven criteria, which group naturally.

  • The behaviour and its timing (A and B). There are repeated episodes of arousal during sleep associated with vocalisation and/or complex motor behaviour. These behaviours arise during REM sleep and therefore usually occur more than 90 minutes after sleep onset, are more frequent in the later part of the sleep period, and are uncommon in daytime naps. The 90-minute rule is simply the latency of the first REM period.
  • The state on waking (C). On waking from an episode the person is completely awake, alert and not confused or disoriented. This is the sharpest bedside contrast with the NREM arousal disorders.
  • The proof (D). Either polysomnography shows REM sleep without atonia, that is, muscle activity in the electromyogram during REM sleep, when the muscles should be silent; or there is a history suggestive of RBD together with an established synucleinopathy such as Parkinson's disease or multiple system atrophy. A diagnosis made by this second route, without polysomnography, is a provisional one ("probable RBD"). Without polysomnography and without such a disease, RBD cannot be diagnosed at all, and the presentation is recorded as other specified sleep-wake disorder (Section 8.10.7).
  • The threshold and the exclusions (E to G). The behaviours cause clinically significant distress or impairment, which may include injury to the person or the bed partner; they are not caused by a substance, a medication or another medical condition; and coexisting mental and medical conditions do not explain them.

The behaviours are typically motor responses to action-filled or violent dreams of being attacked or of trying to escape from a threat, and DSM-5-TR calls them dream-enacting behaviours. The vocalisations are often loud, emotional and profane. The sleeper may punch, thrust, hit, kick, run, or jump or fall out of bed, and, unlike the sleepwalker, he keeps his eyes closed: he is acting in the dream's world, not navigating the bedroom. On waking he can usually recall the dream, and its content matches what the bed partner saw. Not all cases are dramatic; some patients have only subtle twitches, talking or gestures that come to light during a history or a sleep study. The injuries are not trivial. In surveys of patients and bed partners about 55 percent of people with RBD had injured themselves or someone else during an attack, and 12 percent of the injuries were serious, including fractures of long bones and ribs and subdural haematomas, which require medical attention.

Polysomnography shows increased tonic (sustained) or phasic (brief, twitch-like) muscle activity during REM sleep. Because different muscles are affected to different degrees, DSM-5-TR recommends recording not just the chin but also the arms (arm recording, from the biceps, is more specific for RBD), the forearm flexors and the front of the shins, with continuous video. REM sleep without atonia is present in virtually all cases of RBD, but it can also be found in people with no dream enactment at all; such isolated REM sleep without atonia is of uncertain significance, although one small study found that 7 to 14 percent of people with it later developed RBD.

How common is it? The prevalence was about 1 percent in a Swiss general population sample of middle-aged and older people and about 2 percent in a sample of elderly South Koreans. RBD is more common in men over 50, which is why the classic patient looks like Ravindra, but it is increasingly found in women and younger people, and women are younger than men both at onset and at diagnosis; in younger samples, one study found equal rates in men and women under 50. It is commoner among psychiatric patients, possibly because of their medicines. Culture affects what families notice: Chinese patients diagnosed in Taiwan were more likely than White patients in the United States to wander out of the bedroom, but less likely to be injured, perhaps because their families noticed the problem earlier. RBD occurs in about 30 percent of people with narcolepsy, in whom it appears at a younger age and equally in both sexes.

Why does it happen? Recall from Book 1, Section 5.7.6 that REM sleep is generated in the brainstem: "REM-on" neurons in the sublaterodorsal nucleus of the pons switch on REM sleep and, through circuits in the medulla, actively inhibit the motor neurons of the spinal cord. That inhibition is REM atonia, and its function, as Pinel and Barnes put it, appears to be to prevent the acting out of dreams. When these pontine and medullary circuits are damaged, the motor commands generated in the dream reach the muscles, just as in Jouvet's cats. RBD is thus the mirror image of cataplexy (Section 8.8.6): in cataplexy the atonia of REM sleep switches on in a waking person, whereas in RBD the movement of waking breaks into REM sleep. The commonest reason for the damage, it turns out, is the slow spread of a protein called alpha-synuclein, which clumps into the Lewy bodies of Parkinson's disease. The diseases in which it accumulates, Parkinson's disease, dementia with Lewy bodies (DSM-5-TR's major or mild neurocognitive disorder with Lewy bodies) and multiple system atrophy, are together called the synucleinopathies. According to the influential staging scheme of Heiko Braak and colleagues (2003), the Lewy-body pathology of Parkinson's disease begins low in the brainstem, close to the circuits of REM atonia (and in the smell pathway), and reaches the dopamine neurons of the substantia nigra, whose loss causes tremor and stiffness, only later. That sequence fits the clinical story perfectly: dream enactment and a failing sense of smell first, the movement disorder years afterwards.

That brings us to the most important fact about RBD. In people whose RBD has no obvious cause, so-called idiopathic or isolated RBD, the risk of developing a defined neurodegenerative disease, most often a synucleinopathy, is about 75 percent within 10 to 15 years of diagnosis, an annual risk of about 6 to 7 percent. In sleep clinic samples more than 70 percent of patients with initially "idiopathic" RBD eventually develop such a disease, and RBD often predates any other sign of it by many years, often by more than a decade. DSM-5-TR therefore says that the neurological status of people with RBD should be closely monitored. Some quieter markers of the same process can be looked for along the way: a poor sense of smell (like Ravindra's lost jasmine), loss of colour vision, and a fall in blood pressure on standing up. Figure 8.18 lays out the sequence.

Not every case of RBD is a prelude to Parkinson's disease. In young people, and especially young women, DSM-5-TR advises looking for narcolepsy, a medication effect, a brainstem lesion (a tumour, a stroke or a plaque of multiple sclerosis) or an autoimmune encephalopathy. And many widely prescribed drugs, including tricyclic antidepressants, selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) and serotonin-norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs), can produce REM sleep without atonia on the polysomnogram and even frank RBD. This is diagnosed as substance/medication-induced sleep disorder, parasomnia type, and it is not known whether the drugs cause the problem or unmask a hidden predisposition. A middle-aged patient whose dream enactment began soon after starting an antidepressant still deserves watching.

The differential diagnosis runs in both directions from the NREM arousal disorders. Confusional arousals, sleepwalking and sleep terrors can easily be confused with RBD, but they usually affect people under 50, arise from NREM sleep early in the night, leave the person confused with incomplete recall, and show normal REM atonia in the sleep laboratory. Nocturnal seizures produce stereotyped behaviour; a full EEG seizure montage during polysomnography distinguishes them, and REM sleep without atonia is generally absent. Obstructive sleep apnoea can closely mimic RBD, with talking, shouting, gesturing, punching and unpleasant dreams, but the behaviours occur only during the arousals at the end of apnoeas, there is no REM sleep without atonia, and they disappear once the apnoea is treated with continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP, Section 8.9.2). Sleep-related psychogenic dissociative disorder (an other specified dissociative disorder, Section 7.5) arises from a period of definite wakefulness during the night and is more common in young women. Malingering can mimic RBD, so polysomnographic documentation is mandatory. And asymptomatic REM sleep without atonia, found on a recording without any history of dream enactment, is not RBD at all. DSM-5-TR also offers a home for the patient who seems to act out dreams but has had no sleep study and has no synucleinopathy: other specified sleep-wake disorder, "repeated arousals during REM sleep without polysomnography or history of Parkinson's disease or other synucleinopathy" (Section 8.10.7).

Treatment has three aims: to keep the patient and partner safe, to suppress the behaviour, and to watch the brain. Safety comes first and costs nothing: remove guns, knives and other dangerous objects from the bedroom, pad the corners of furniture, move the bed away from windows, place the mattress on the floor or add bed rails, and, if attacks continue, let the partner sleep in another bed or room. Two drugs have long been the mainstays. The benzodiazepine clonazepam, taken at bedtime, suppressed the behaviour in two of Schenck's first five patients, and in Schenck and Mahowald's 1996 series of 170 adults treated with nightly benzodiazepines (52 of them for RBD), control was complete or substantial in most. Clonazepam can, however, cause morning drowsiness, unsteadiness, falls and confusion in older people and may worsen sleep apnoea. Melatonin, the hormone of darkness (Book 1, Section 5.6.2), is safer, which is why it is often preferred for older patients and those with memory problems or sleep apnoea. The American Academy of Sleep Medicine's 2023 guideline, led by Michael J. Howell, gives conditional recommendations for clonazepam, immediate-release melatonin, the dopamine agonist pramipexole and, in patients with mild cognitive impairment, transdermal rivastigmine for isolated RBD; for drug-induced RBD it suggests stopping the offending drug where possible. No treatment has yet been shown to slow the underlying neurodegeneration, and an international consensus led by Schenck in 2013 laid out how trials of "neuroprotective" treatment in RBD should be designed, precisely because these patients can be identified so early. For the clinician, then, Ravindra's treatment is clonazepam or melatonin, a safer bedroom, and a yearly review of smell, mood, memory and movement.

Clinical link

Telling a patient that his dream enactment carries a high risk of Parkinson's disease or dementia is one of the hardest conversations in sleep medicine. There is not yet a treatment that prevents the conversion, and the timing in any one person is uncertain. Many clinicians therefore ask what the patient wants to know, explain the risk honestly as a probability rather than a certainty, and frame regular follow-up as a positive step (earlier recognition of symptoms, access to research trials, planning). The psychologist's role includes screening for the depression, anxiety and cognitive change that may appear along the way.

Exam trap

REM sleep behaviour disorder is the loss of the normal muscle atonia of REM sleep, so the person acts out dream content (punching, kicking, shouting) and wakes alert with vivid recall. Check the distractors. "Inability to enter any REM sleep" is wrong: the patient has plenty of REM sleep, only without paralysis. (A near-total loss of REM sleep is rare, and seems surprisingly harmless: Pinel and Barnes describe a man studied by Peretz Lavie and colleagues in 1984 who had almost no REM sleep after a brain injury, yet completed high school, college and law school and built a thriving law practice.) "Bedwetting during N3" describes enuresis (Section 8.6), which usually happens in the first third of the night. "Excessive daytime sleepiness without nocturnal symptoms" describes hypersomnolence disorder (Section 8.8). RBD is linked to the synucleinopathies (Parkinson's disease, dementia with Lewy bodies, multiple system atrophy), not to Alzheimer's disease, and it is treated with clonazepam or melatonin. It is the mirror image of cataplexy: REM atonia missing in sleep, not REM atonia intruding into waking.

The other parasomnias and sleep-related movements

DSM-5-TR stops at three parasomnia disorders, but a sleep clinic sees many more night-time events, and the examiner may name any of them. They come from ICSD-3, which sorts the parasomnias into three groups (NREM-related, REM-related and other) and puts the repetitive movements of sleep into a separate family, the sleep-related movement disorders. ICD-11 follows the same plan in its sleep-wake chapter. Most of these conditions are common, brief and harmless, and the clinician's main task is to recognise them, reassure, and tell them apart from the disorders that matter. Table 8.27 summarises them, marking which are DSM-5-TR diagnoses and which are ICSD-3 terms. When one of them causes enough distress to need a diagnosis in DSM-5-TR, it is recorded as other specified sleep-wake disorder (Section 8.10.7).

The most dramatic of them is recurrent isolated sleep paralysis. The person wakes, or is falling asleep, and finds that she cannot move her body or speak, although she is fully conscious and can move her eyes and breathe. The episode lasts from a few seconds to a few minutes and ends on its own or, many sufferers say, when someone touches or speaks to them. It is often accompanied by terror and by vivid hallucinations: a sense of a presence in the room, a dark figure, footsteps, or a weight pressing on the chest. The explanation is the state dissociation of Figure 8.17: the atonia and the dream imagery of REM sleep persist into a mind that has already woken. ICSD-3 classifies it as a REM-related parasomnia, and ICD-11 places it in the same group as REM sleep behaviour disorder and nightmare disorder. It is "isolated" because it occurs without narcolepsy; when sleep paralysis comes with excessive daytime sleepiness and cataplexy, it is part of the narcoleptic tetrad (Section 8.8.4). It is surprisingly common. A systematic review by Brian A. Sharpless and Jacques P. Barber (2011), pooling 35 studies and 36,533 people, found that 7.6 percent of the general population, 28.3 percent of students and 31.9 percent of psychiatric patients had had at least one episode, and 34.6 percent of patients with panic disorder. DSM-5-TR notes that many normal sleepers report occasional sleep paralysis, especially under stress or after sleep deprivation, which is presumably why students, with their irregular hours before examinations, report it so often.

Because the experience is so uncanny, nearly every culture has a name and an explanation for it, usually a supernatural being that sits on the sleeper's chest. In Newfoundland it is the "Old Hag", and a person who has had an episode is said to have been "hag-ridden"; in Japan it is kanashibari, literally "metal binding"; and Barlow and Durand note that many African Americans know it as "the witch is riding you", and that isolated sleep paralysis was reported by 59.6 percent of African Americans with panic disorder in a study by Paradis, Friedman and Hatch (1997). DSM-5-TR observes that such supernatural explanations can make sleep paralysis seem more dangerous and shape whether and where people seek help. Wherever such beliefs are held, a calm explanation of REM atonia, and of the harmlessness of the episodes, is often the most useful treatment, together with regular and sufficient sleep. Hypnagogic and hypnopompic hallucinations as experiences are described in Book 3, Section 2.3.6; when they are frequent and distressing on their own, ICSD-3 diagnoses sleep-related hallucinations, one of its "other parasomnias".

Another of the other parasomnias has a name that sounds alarming and a nature that is harmless. In exploding head syndrome the person hears a sudden, very loud noise, a bang, an explosion, a gunshot or a clash of cymbals, as she is drifting off to sleep or waking up, sometimes with a flash of light or a jolt of fear, but with no pain. The American physician Silas Weir Mitchell (1829 to 1914) described such "sensory discharges" in 1876, and the British neurologist J. M. S. Pearce gave the syndrome its name in a short report in The Lancet in 1988 and in 1989 described 50 patients, emphasising that it is benign and needs neither extensive investigation nor treatment. ICSD-3 lists it among the other parasomnias. Its importance for a clinician is mainly negative: it is not a stroke, a seizure or a psychotic hallucination, and saying so relieves a great deal of anxiety.

The speech of sleep talking, or somniloquy, ranges from mumbling to clear sentences and sometimes to conversations with an imagined partner. As Pinel and Barnes point out, it has no special link with REM sleep: it can occur in any stage, and often happens during the transition to waking. ICSD-3 does not even call it a disorder; it is one of the "isolated symptoms and normal variants". Sleep enuresis (bedwetting) is the clearest example of the two systems disagreeing: ICSD-3 lists it among the other parasomnias, but DSM-5-TR and ICD-11 place enuresis among the elimination disorders (Section 8.6.2). The nocturnal-only type, the commonest, usually occurs in the first third of the night, and occasionally in REM sleep, when the child may remember a dream of passing urine.

The second ICSD-3 family, the sleep-related movement disorders, contains disorders of simple, usually stereotyped movements that disturb sleep, rather than the complex behaviours of the parasomnias. Restless legs syndrome is the chief of them and has Section 8.10.6 to itself. The others are as follows.

  • In sleep bruxism the sleeper grinds or clenches the teeth. The bed partner hears the grinding; the sleeper may wake with an aching jaw, a headache or sensitive teeth, and the dentist sees worn enamel. It occurs mainly in light NREM sleep, is common in children and adults, and is worsened by stress, caffeine, alcohol and tobacco. The usual treatment is a dental guard worn at night to protect the teeth.
  • In sleep-related rhythmic movement disorder there is repetitive, rhythmic movement of large muscle groups at sleep onset or during light sleep: head banging against the pillow or cot, body rocking on hands and knees, or head rolling from side to side. The old Latin name for head banging is jactatio capitis nocturna. It is very common in infants and toddlers, who often hum as they rock, and most outgrow it; it becomes a disorder when it injures the child or seriously disturbs sleep, and it is commoner and more persistent in children with intellectual disability or autism, in whom it must be told apart from stereotypic movement disorder (Chapter 3).
  • The condition called periodic limb movement disorder (PLMD) is characterised, in Pinel and Barnes's words, by periodic, involuntary movements of the limbs, often twitches of the legs during sleep. Typically the big toe extends and the ankle, knee and sometimes hip flex, each movement lasting between half a second and ten seconds and recurring every 5 to 90 seconds in runs of at least four; this is how the periodic limb movements in sleep (PLMS) are scored on polysomnography under the American Academy of Sleep Medicine's rules. Most patients complain of poor sleep and daytime sleepiness but, Pinel and Barnes note, are unaware of the cause; it is the bed partner who is kicked. PLMS are found in up to 90 percent of people with restless legs syndrome recorded over several nights, in about 40 percent of people with narcolepsy type 1 (Section 8.8.4), and in many healthy older people, in whom they are often harmless. The diagnosis of PLMD is therefore made only when frequent PLMS cause clinically significant sleep disturbance or daytime impairment that nothing else explains. ICSD-3 adds that PLMD is not diagnosed in a person with restless legs syndrome (or with narcolepsy, untreated obstructive sleep apnoea or REM sleep behaviour disorder), because the movements are an expected part of those disorders.
  • Sleep-related leg cramps are sudden, painful, involuntary contractions of the calf or foot muscles that wake the sleeper and are relieved by stretching the muscle; and sleep starts, or hypnic jerks, are the single sudden jerk of the whole body, often with a feeling of falling, that almost everyone has felt at the moment of dropping off, a normal variant rather than a disorder.
Table 8.27 The minor parasomnias and sleep-related movements, with their stage and status.
ConditionWhat happensStage or timingWhoWhere it is classified
Confusional arousalSits up confused and muddled; stays in bedN3, first thirdYoung children; adults after sleep lossICSD-3 NREM parasomnia; DSM-5-TR text only
Sleep-related eatingEats during partial arousal, even inediblesNREM arousalMore often women; Z-drug usersDSM-5-TR specifier of sleepwalking; ICSD-3 separate disorder
Recurrent isolated sleep paralysisAwake but cannot move or speak; fear, a presenceFalling asleep or waking (REM)Common; students, stress, sleep lossICSD-3 REM parasomnia; ICD-11 with RBD
