Exam trap

Sleep paralysis on its own (isolated sleep paralysis) is common in healthy people, especially under stress or sleep loss; it suggests narcolepsy only when it comes with irresistible daytime sleepiness and cataplexy. Periodic limb movement disorder is noticed by the bed partner, not the sleeper; restless legs syndrome is felt by the waking person before sleep. Bedwetting is a parasomnia in ICSD-3 but an elimination disorder in DSM-5-TR. Bruxism, head banging and PLMD are ICSD-3 sleep-related movement disorders, not DSM-5-TR diagnoses. Sleep talking occurs in any stage and is a normal variant.

Restless legs syndrome

Sheela is 58 and teaches Malayalam at a government higher secondary school in Kochi. For about a year she has spent part of every night walking up and down the verandah. The moment she lies down, or sits still in the evening to correct notebooks, a "crawling", as if ants were moving inside her calves, begins deep in both legs, with an irresistible urge to move them. If she gets up and walks, the feeling fades almost at once; when she lies down again it returns. It is worst between ten and one at night and is hardly there in the morning. A long bus journey to her daughter's house in Thrissur last month was torture. It takes her two hours to fall asleep, she is exhausted at school, and her husband complains that, once she does sleep, her legs jerk against him every half-minute or so. She has had three children, and a blood test last year showed that she was anaemic. A night-time cough syrup containing an antihistamine, which a pharmacist suggested "for sleep", made the crawling worse.

The condition is old. In 1672, in his Latin treatise De Anima Brutorum (On the Soul of Brutes), the English physician Thomas Willis (1621 to 1675), better known for the circle of arteries at the base of the brain that bears his name, described patients in whom, as soon as they went to bed to sleep, such leapings and restlessness arose in the arms and legs that they could no more sleep than if they were "in a place of the greatest torture", as the English translation of 1685 put it. Nearly three centuries later the Swedish neurologist Karl-Axel Ekbom (1907 to 1977) gave the first systematic clinical account in his doctoral thesis of 1945, which he called Restless Legs, and the term stuck. In his honour, and Willis's, the condition is now also called Willis-Ekbom disease. Ekbom's name is attached to a second, entirely different condition, delusional parasitosis or Ekbom syndrome (Book 3, Section 2.6.6), and an examiner who writes "Ekbom" may mean either.

DSM-5-TR defines restless legs syndrome (RLS; code G25.81 in DSM-5-TR, G25.8 in ICD-10, where it sits among the movement disorders of the nervous system, and 7A80 in ICD-11's sleep-wake chapter) in five criteria.

  • Criterion A is the syndrome itself: an urge to move the legs, usually accompanied by, or in response to, uncomfortable and unpleasant sensations in the legs, with all three of the following features. (1) The urge begins or worsens during periods of rest or inactivity. (2) It is partly or totally relieved by movement, at least for as long as the movement lasts. (3) It is worse in the evening or at night than during the day, or occurs only in the evening or at night. Each feature does diagnostic work. Rest provokes it, so it is not simply leg pain; movement relieves it, so it is not a cramp or arthritis, which movement worsens or does not help; and evening worsening reflects a circadian rhythm in the symptom, which DSM-5-TR stresses occurs independently of how active the person has been.
  • Criterion B sets the threshold of frequency and duration, the familiar "rule of threes": at least three times a week for at least 3 months. (ICSD-3 has similar criteria but no frequency or duration rule.)
  • Criterion C requires significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, educational, academic, behavioural or other important areas of functioning.
  • Criteria D and E exclude mimics: the symptoms are not attributable to another mental disorder or medical condition (arthritis, leg oedema, peripheral ischaemia, leg cramps), are not better explained by a behavioural condition (positional discomfort, habitual foot tapping), and are not caused by a drug of abuse or a medication (DSM-5-TR's example is akathisia).

DSM-5-TR describes RLS as a sensorimotor, neurological sleep disorder. People find the sensations hard to describe; the words they use are creeping, crawling, tingling, burning or itching, usually felt deep inside the legs, and sometimes in the arms too. The diagnosis rests almost entirely on the person's own report. In severe cases the relief from moving may no longer be obvious. RLS delays sleep onset, wakes the person from sleep, fragments sleep and shortens it, and so leads to daytime sleepiness, fatigue and low mood; up to half of patients report a negative effect on mood and a lack of energy. Several supportive features help when the diagnosis is uncertain: periodic limb movements in sleep, found in up to 90 percent of people with RLS recorded over several nights (the jerking that Sheela's husband feels); periodic leg movements while awake and lying still; a family history in first-degree relatives; and a reduction in symptoms, at least at first, with dopaminergic treatment. Children can have RLS too, but because the diagnosis depends on self-report, DSM-5-TR requires the description to come in the child's own words, not the parent's. Children rarely use the word "urge"; they say their legs "have to" or "got to" move, and since they sit still for hours in class, two-thirds report daytime symptoms, so the clinician must compare equal periods of sitting in the day and in the evening.

When symptoms occur at least three times a week with moderate or severe distress, the prevalence in the United States and Europe is about 1.6 percent, and RLS severe enough to impair functioning, or accompanied by depression or anxiety, affects about 2 to 3 percent of the population in Western Europe, the United States and South Korea; reported rates are lower in several Asian populations, such as Japan and South Korea. RLS is about twice as common in women as in men, and its prevalence rises steadily with age until about 60, then stays stable or falls slightly. Onset is typically in the second or third decade: about 40 percent of adults with RLS had symptoms before the age of 20, and 20 percent before 10. Familial cases begin earlier and progress slowly; when onset is after 45, rapid progression and aggravating factors are typical. Pregnancy is the great natural experiment. RLS is two to three times commoner in pregnancy than in the general population, peaks in the third trimester, and in most women improves or disappears soon after delivery. Women who have never given birth have the same risk as men of the same age, so the sex difference is explained at least in part by parity. Sheela's three pregnancies may well have been part of her story.

What causes it? DSM-5-TR separates predisposing factors (female sex, advancing age, genetic risk variants and family history) from precipitating ones, of which iron deficiency is the most important; precipitated RLS is often time-limited, with normal sleep returning when the trigger goes. Genome-wide association studies have found several risk variants, the strongest in the gene MEIS1: among people of European ancestry, the 7 percent of the population who carry this variant have nearly double the risk. The mechanism seems to involve the brain's dopamine and opioid systems and its handling of iron, and the fact that dopaminergic drugs, opioids and iron all relieve RLS is part of the evidence. Iron is a necessary co-factor for making dopamine, and studies suggest that many patients have too little iron in the brain even when their blood count is normal, which helps explain why low iron stores matter so much. RLS is also common in chronic renal failure (uraemia) and in pregnancy, and it can improve dramatically once these conditions resolve. Genetic susceptibility appears to matter even in these "secondary" cases, as if the additional factor tips a predisposed person over the threshold. Serotonergic antidepressants can induce or aggravate RLS in some people, and conversely RLS may predispose to depression, whose symptoms can lift when the RLS is treated.

Indian context

Two of the strongest precipitants of restless legs syndrome, iron deficiency and pregnancy, are extremely common in India. In the fifth National Family Health Survey (NFHS-5, 2019 to 2021), 57.0 percent of Indian women aged 15 to 49 and 52.2 percent of pregnant women were anaemic. Whatever the exact Indian prevalence of RLS itself, any woman, pregnant or not, who describes "ants in the legs" at night should have her iron status checked, and a woman who is being given iron for anaemia may find that her nights improve too.

RLS keeps company with many other conditions. It is associated with higher rates of depression, generalised anxiety disorder, panic disorder and post-traumatic stress disorder; the main medical condition associated with it is cardiovascular disease; and associations have been reported with hypertension, migraine, Parkinson's disease, multiple sclerosis, peripheral neuropathy, diabetes, fibromyalgia, osteoporosis, obesity, thyroid disease and cancer, and with narcolepsy and obstructive sleep apnoea. On polysomnography, people with RLS take longer to fall asleep and have a higher arousal index, and periodic limb movements, the motor sign of RLS, are usually present, both in sleep and during a waking immobilisation test, in which the person sits still in bed in the evening and the symptoms are provoked.

The differential diagnosis is long, because many conditions make legs uncomfortable at night. The most important, DSM-5-TR says, are leg cramps, positional discomfort, arthralgias or arthritis, myalgias, positional ischaemia (numbness), leg oedema, peripheral neuropathy, radiculopathy and habitual foot tapping. Clues that point away from RLS are muscle cramps, relief by a single change of position, pain limited to the joints, muscles that are sore to the touch, and abnormal findings on examination. Nocturnal leg cramps, in particular, do not bring an urge to move or frequent limb movements. Among the less common mimics is akathisia, the inner restlessness and compulsion to move caused by dopamine-blocking drugs such as antipsychotics (Book 3, Section 4.10.4). Akathisia is felt in the whole body rather than mainly in the legs, is tied to the drug rather than to the evening, and lacks the periodic limb movements of RLS; DSM-5-TR notes that worsening at night and PLMS are more typical of RLS than of akathisia or peripheral neuropathy. Of course, a person can have RLS and one of its mimics at once, and each must be assessed in its own right. Table 8.28 sets the four night-time leg complaints side by side.

Table 8.28 Restless legs syndrome and its look-alikes.
FeatureRestless legs syndromeAkathisiaNocturnal leg crampsPeriodic limb movement disorder
What the person reportsUrge to move the legs with creeping, crawling sensationsInner restlessness of the whole body; cannot keep stillSudden, painful tightening of the calf or footNothing directly: poor sleep and daytime tiredness
Who noticesThe person, while awakeThe person and onlookersThe person, woken by painThe bed partner, who is kicked
WhenAt rest, in the evening and at nightAny time of day while on the drugSuddenly, often at nightDuring sleep, mostly NREM
What relieves itWalking or moving the legsMoving helps only a little; reducing the drug helpsStretching the muscleNot applicable
Main cause or linkGenes, iron deficiency, pregnancy, renal failureDopamine-blocking drugs, especially antipsychoticsA muscle event; no urge to moveOften RLS, narcolepsy or older age

Treatment begins with the simple things, and here a recent change in the guidelines is worth knowing. The American Academy of Sleep Medicine's guideline on RLS and periodic limb movement disorder, published by John W. Winkelman and colleagues in 2025, makes three good-practice points. First, every patient with clinically significant RLS should have iron studies, including serum ferritin and transferrin saturation, ideally taken in the morning after avoiding iron supplements for a day. Iron should be given, by mouth or intravenously, if ferritin is 75 nanograms per millilitre or less or transferrin saturation is below 20 percent, and intravenously only if ferritin is between 75 and 100; in children the threshold is a ferritin below 50. These thresholds are higher than those used for anaemia in the general population, because what matters in RLS is the brain's iron supply. Second, the first step in management is to remove aggravating factors: alcohol, caffeine, sedating antihistamines (like Sheela's cough syrup), serotonergic antidepressants, dopamine-blocking drugs, and untreated obstructive sleep apnoea. Third, RLS is common in pregnancy, so each treatment's safety in pregnancy must be weighed. Many patients also find that walking, stretching, massage or an absorbing activity in the evening eases milder symptoms.

For the drugs, the guideline gives strong recommendations to the alpha-2-delta ligands, gabapentin enacarbil, gabapentin and pregabalin, which bind to a subunit of nerve-cell calcium channels and damp down the release of excitatory transmitters, and to intravenous ferric carboxymaltose in patients with suitable iron levels; oral ferrous sulphate gets a conditional recommendation. The surprise is the treatment that used to come first. The dopamine agonists pramipexole, ropinirole and rotigotine (a skin patch), and levodopa (Pinel and Barnes note that L-dopa "can help some patients"), work well in the short term, but the guideline now suggests against their standard use because of augmentation: after months or years of treatment, the drug itself makes the symptoms worse, so that they start earlier in the day, become more intense, come on more quickly at rest and may spread to the arms. They may still be used by patients who value short-term relief more than the long-term risk. Low-dose opioids, such as extended-release oxycodone, receive a conditional recommendation, mainly for severe or refractory cases, with the precautions that any opioid demands. The guideline suggests against clonazepam, bupropion, carbamazepine, valproate and valerian, and strongly against cabergoline. Pinel and Barnes's older statement that there are no effective treatments for RLS and PLMD is therefore out of date for RLS. For Sheela, the plan would be iron studies and iron replacement, stopping the antihistamine, and, if the symptoms persist, gabapentin or pregabalin at night.

Exam trap

An urge to move the legs with uncomfortable sensations, brought on by rest, relieved by movement and worse in the evening, is restless legs syndrome (Willis-Ekbom disease), with the DSM-5-TR threshold of at least three times a week for 3 months. It is not akathisia (whole-body restlessness from antipsychotics, not tied to the evening), not leg cramps (painful, relieved by stretching, no urge to move) and not periodic limb movement disorder (twitches in sleep that the sleeper does not notice). RLS is a sensorimotor disorder of the waking person, not a parasomnia. Its key precipitants are iron deficiency, pregnancy and renal failure; the long-term problem with dopamine agonists is augmentation. "Ekbom syndrome" in a question about delusions means delusional parasitosis.

Substance/medication-induced sleep disorder, and the "other" categories

A 46-year-old bus conductor in Mysuru, a daily drinker for twenty years, stops drinking abruptly after a frightening episode of vomiting blood. For the next week he barely sleeps: he lies awake until three, wakes every hour, and when he does sleep he has dreams so vivid and violent that he is afraid to close his eyes. His wife says that, before he stopped, he used to "fall asleep like a stone" after his evening quarter of whisky and then toss and turn from two o'clock onwards. Both patterns, the broken second half of the night while he drank and the wrecked sleep of withdrawal, are produced by the drug, and if the sleep problem is severe enough to need treatment in its own right, it has its own diagnosis.

DSM-5-TR's criteria for substance/medication-induced sleep disorder follow the same pattern as every other substance-induced disorder in the manual.

  • A. There is a prominent and severe disturbance of sleep.
  • B. The history, examination or laboratory findings show both that (1) the symptoms developed during or soon after intoxication or withdrawal, or after exposure to or withdrawal from a medication, and (2) the substance or medication involved is capable of producing them.
  • C. The disturbance is not better explained by an independent sleep disorder. Evidence of an independent disorder would be that the symptoms began before the substance use, that they persist for a substantial period (about a month) after acute withdrawal or severe intoxication has ended, or that there have been episodes unrelated to substances.
  • D. The disturbance does not occur only during a delirium.
  • E. It causes clinically significant distress or impairment.

A note to the criteria adds the rule that decides between this diagnosis and plain intoxication or withdrawal: a substance/medication-induced sleep disorder is diagnosed instead of intoxication or withdrawal only when the sleep symptoms predominate in the clinical picture and are severe enough to need clinical attention in their own right. Almost everyone in alcohol withdrawal sleeps badly; only some need this diagnosis.

The clinician then specifies the type: insomnia type (difficulty falling or staying asleep, frequent waking, or nonrestorative sleep), daytime sleepiness type (excessive sleepiness or fatigue while awake, or less often a long sleep period), parasomnia type (abnormal behaviour during sleep) or mixed type (several kinds of sleep symptom with none predominating). The insomnia and daytime sleepiness types are the commonest, and the parasomnia type is seen less often. The clinician also specifies the onset: with onset during intoxication, with onset during withdrawal, or with onset after medication use (at the start of a medication, with a change in its use, or during its withdrawal). Because some withdrawal states are protracted, the sleep disturbance can begin as late as four weeks after the substance was stopped. Conversely, when a medication is stopped, the sleep disturbance usually remits within days to several weeks; if it persists beyond four weeks, other causes should be considered.

The coding follows the logic of the other substance-induced disorders (Chapter 10). There is one combined code, and no separate diagnosis of a substance use disorder is given: the name of the use disorder, if there is one, is simply written first. For alcohol, for example, the code is F10.182 with a mild alcohol use disorder, F10.282 with a moderate or severe one, and F10.982 with none. DSM-5-TR's own examples show how a full diagnosis reads: "F13.282 severe lorazepam use disorder with lorazepam-induced sleep disorder, with onset during withdrawal, insomnia type", or, for a prescribed medicine taken as directed, "F19.982 bupropion-induced sleep disorder, with onset during medication use, insomnia type". There are two exceptions. Because caffeine use disorder is not an official DSM-5 diagnosis, caffeine-induced sleep disorder has a single code, F15.982; and because ICD-10-CM assumes that tobacco-induced sleep disorder occurs only with moderate or severe tobacco use disorder, it too has a single code, F17.208. ICSD-3 takes a different approach: it lists sleep disorders "due to a medication or substance" under each type of sleep problem (hypersomnia, movement disorder, parasomnia), and it has no separate diagnosis of insomnia due to a substance, because the evidence is that a single cause of chronic insomnia cannot be identified reliably.

The bus conductor's story is the commonest one, and alcohol shows best how one drug can do opposite things at different times of the night. In acute intoxication, at doses above about 1 gram of alcohol per kilogram of body weight (for a 60-kilogram man, about 60 grams of alcohol, roughly a "quarter", 180 millilitres, of ordinary 42.8 percent Indian whisky), alcohol is immediately sedating: it shortens the time to fall asleep, increases N2 and N3 sleep and reduces REM sleep. As the alcohol is metabolised, the second half of the night turns restless, with more waking, more REM sleep and vivid, anxious dreams. Habitual drinkers keep the short-lived sedation in the first half of the night and the broken sleep in the second. In acute withdrawal, sleep continuity is extremely disrupted and REM sleep rebounds in amount and intensity, with vivid dreaming that, in its extreme form, is part of alcohol withdrawal delirium (Chapter 10). Even after withdrawal is over, people with chronic alcohol use may complain for months or years of light, fragmented sleep, with a long sleep latency and a deficit of slow-wave sleep. Alcohol also worsens obstructive sleep apnoea and sleep-related hypoventilation (Section 8.9). And DSM-5-TR singles out one consequence that is unique to this diagnosis: an increased risk of relapse. The severity of sleep disturbance during alcohol withdrawal, including the REM rebound, predicts the risk of returning to drink, which is why a recovering drinker's sleep deserves attention, not just sedation. Table 8.29 summarises the main substances.

Table 8.29 What the main substances and medicines do to sleep.
SubstanceDuring use or intoxicationDuring withdrawalPoints to remember
AlcoholQuick sleep, more N2 and N3, less REM early; broken sleep and vivid dreams laterSeverely broken sleep; REM rebound with vivid dreamsLight, fragmented sleep for months or years; worsens sleep apnoea; sleep disturbance predicts relapse
CaffeineInsomnia, depending on dose and timing: longer sleep latency, less N3, more wakingDaytime sleepiness in heavy usersBlocks adenosine (Book 1, Section 5.6.3); single code F15.982
Amphetamines, cocaine and other stimulantsLess sleep, longer sleep latency, less REM and N3Long night sleep and excessive daytime sleepinessEcstasy (MDMA): disturbed sleep within 48 hours; lasting problems with heavy use
OpioidsSleepiness and less REM and N3 at first; insomnia as tolerance developsDisturbed sleepWorsen obstructive sleep apnoea; central sleep apnoea with long-acting opioids (Section 8.9.3)
CannabisMay hasten sleep; more N3, less REM; tolerance with chronic usePoor sleep and unpleasant dreams for several weeks; less N3, more REMConsider it in any adolescent with new insomnia
Sedatives, hypnotics, anxiolyticsSleepiness; daytime sleepiness with long-acting drugsRebound insomnia, worst with short-acting drugs; serious withdrawalZ-drugs: sleepwalking and sleep-related eating (Section 8.7.6)
TobaccoInsomnia, less N3, poor sleep efficiency, daytime sleepinessImpaired sleep; heavy smokers wake with cravingSingle code F17.208
AntidepressantsInsomnia with some (fluoxetine, bupropion); REM sleep without atonia and RBD with SSRIs, SNRIs and tricyclics; may worsen RLSREM rebound with nightmaresThe classes are in Section 5.7.2
Other medicinesNightmares with beta blockers, other antihypertensives and dopaminergic drugs; disturbed sleep with corticosteroids and antihistaminesVaries with the drugRecord as other (or unknown) substance, F19 codes

A few points in Table 8.29 deserve a sentence each. Caffeine taken in low to moderate doses in the morning usually has no effect on night-time sleep, even in people with insomnia; it causes insomnia in proportion to how much is taken and how late, by blocking the adenosine that builds up sleep pressure during the day (Book 1, Section 5.6.3). The stimulants show the two faces of a single drug: insomnia during intoxication and sleepiness during withdrawal, when the multiple sleep latency test can show increased daytime sleepiness. Opioids are sedating at first, but tolerance soon brings complaints of insomnia, and because they depress breathing they worsen obstructive sleep apnoea and, especially in chronic use of long-acting opioids, produce central sleep apnoea. The newer hypnotics (ramelteon, low-dose doxepin and the orexin antagonist suvorexant) do not appear to carry significant abuse potential or major withdrawal syndromes, whereas short-acting benzodiazepines are the most likely to cause rebound insomnia (Section 8.7.6 treats the hypnotics). Smokers who smoke heavily may wake regularly at night with craving. Two groups are especially at risk and especially easy to miss. Adolescents and young adults who use cannabis or ecstasy may not seek help, so new insomnia in a teenager should prompt a careful, private question about drugs, and a parent's or teacher's report may help. Older people take more medicines, may put their poor sleep down to age and fail to report it, and people with dementia may be unable to report it at all, so a caregiver's account is essential. Sleep diaries kept for two weeks, actigraphy (a wrist-worn movement monitor) and, where needed, a drug screen help to confirm the diagnosis.

The differential diagnosis turns on chronology. A sleep disturbance that began before any drug, or persists long after stopping it, points to an independent sleep disorder. People with a primary sleep problem often medicate it themselves (alcohol for insomnia is the classic example), and when the substance clearly makes the sleep problem worse, both diagnoses can be made. When the sleep disturbance is due to a medical condition and the medicine for it, the order of events decides: sleep problems that came with the illness, before any treatment, suggest insomnia disorder, hypersomnolence disorder or nightmare disorder "with [the medical condition]", whereas symptoms that appeared only after a particular medicine was started suggest the substance/medication-induced diagnosis. If the evidence cannot settle the matter, the diagnosis is unspecified sleep-wake disorder.

That last diagnosis is one of six residual categories which, with the disorders of Sections 8.7 to 8.10, let a clinician place any sleep presentation. Other specified insomnia disorder (G47.09) is used when insomnia causes distress but does not meet the full criteria and the clinician records why, for example "short-term insomnia disorder" (less than 3 months) or insomnia "restricted to nonrestorative sleep". Unspecified insomnia disorder (G47.00) is used when the clinician does not specify the reason or lacks the information. Other specified hypersomnolence disorder (G47.19) covers presentations such as "brief-duration hypersomnolence", the example DSM-5-TR gives being Kleine-Levin syndrome (Section 8.8.8), and unspecified hypersomnolence disorder (G47.10) is its unspecified partner. Finally, other specified sleep-wake disorder (G47.8) covers presentations that fit none of the specific disorders and neither of the insomnia or hypersomnolence residual categories, with the reason recorded; DSM-5-TR's own example is "repeated arousals during REM sleep without polysomnography or history of Parkinson's disease or other synucleinopathy", the patient who seems to have RBD but cannot yet meet its Criterion D. Recurrent isolated sleep paralysis and exploding head syndrome, which have no DSM-5-TR category of their own, belong here when they are troublesome enough to diagnose. Unspecified sleep-wake disorder (G47.9) is used when the reason is not recorded or there is insufficient information.

Remember

To place a night-time event, ask four questions. When? First third (NREM arousal disorder, enuresis), last third (nightmare, RBD), the edges of sleep (sleep paralysis, sleep-related hallucinations, exploding head syndrome, hypnic jerks), or before sleep (restless legs). Awake afterwards? Confused and unreachable (NREM arousal) or at once alert (REM events, panic). Remembered? Nothing or a single image (NREM arousal), a whole story (nightmare, RBD), the fear but no dream (nocturnal panic). Moving? With eyes open and walking (sleepwalking), with eyes closed and fighting (RBD), only the legs and without knowing it (PLMD). Then check the medicines and drinks, because any of these patterns can be substance-induced.

Exam trap

A substance/medication-induced sleep disorder is diagnosed instead of intoxication or withdrawal only when the sleep symptoms predominate and need attention in their own right, and no separate substance use disorder diagnosis is coded. Its four types are insomnia, daytime sleepiness, parasomnia and mixed; its onset specifiers are during intoxication, during withdrawal and after medication use. Symptoms that persist about a month after withdrawal ends point to an independent sleep disorder. Caffeine (F15.982) and tobacco (F17.208) have single codes. Alcohol suppresses REM sleep early in the night and produces REM rebound later and in withdrawal; stimulants cause insomnia in use and sleepiness in withdrawal.

Scan Your Brain

Answers: (1) NREM (N3, slow-wave), (2) dissociation, (3) scheduled (anticipatory), (4) oriented, (5) nightly, (6) atonia, (7) synucleinopathy, (8) melatonin, (9) Willis-Ekbom; iron, (10) augmentation.

The men of the Minnesota Starvation Experiment, revisited

Go back to the thirty-six conscientious objectors beneath the football stadium in Minneapolis and read their year again with this chapter in your hands. Almost every change in their bodies is on the list of Section 8.3.5. Their heart rate, body temperature and breathing fell, and some of them developed oedema of the limbs: the "slow and low" economy of the starving body, which a physician finds in anorexia nervosa as bradycardia, hypothermia and hypotension, with an oedema that frightens patients during weight restoration. Their minds followed the same script. They thought, talked and read about food, collected recipes, hoarded food-related things and grew depressed, irritable and withdrawn, with rising scores on the depression, hysteria and hypochondriasis scales of the MMPI. That is why DSM-5-TR warns that many of the depressive and obsessional features of anorexia nervosa may be secondary to semistarvation, why mood is assessed again once weight has been restored, and why obsessive-compulsive disorder is added only for obsessions unrelated to food, shape or weight (Section 8.3.6): Malini's recipe reading and the cakes she bakes and does not eat are the Minnesota men's preoccupation, not a second disorder. Even the ruler on which her severity is graded carries Keys's mark, because it was he who gave the body mass index its name in 1972 (Section 8.3.2). And many of the men said afterwards that the hardest part of the experiment was not the starvation but the rehabilitation. The hunger did not switch off when the food returned; some ate enormous meals, a few until they were sick. A body defending its weight resists a diet from within, which is why a rigid diet so often ends in a binge (Section 8.5.1), and feeding a starved person back to health, the problem Keys set out to solve, is still the first and most dangerous step in treating anorexia nervosa, now monitored day by day for the refeeding syndrome (Sections 8.3.5 and 8.5.6).

The opening asked why the men of Minnesota longed for food, while a girl with anorexia nervosa refuses the food in front of her and a young woman with bulimia nervosa eats far more than she wants and then makes herself sick. The chapter's answer has three parts. The men longed for food because starvation raises the positive-incentive value of eating to enormous heights; for them, all food tasted good. A girl with anorexia nervosa is starving too, and often hungry, but her restriction is driven by an intense fear of gaining weight and by a self-worth measured in kilograms, the over-evaluation of eating, shape and weight and their control that Fairburn, Cooper and Shafran (2003) placed at the core of anorexia, bulimia and their atypical forms (Section 8.5.4); Pinel and Barnes add the hypothesis that meals in a starved body are unpleasant and produce conditioned taste aversions that lower the incentive value of food still further. A young woman with bulimia nervosa diets by rigid rules against her own physiology, so a broken rule releases a binge, and because her self-evaluation is unduly influenced by shape and weight (Criterion D, Section 8.4.2) she vomits to undo it, which prepares the next diet and the next binge. What the men never had was that over-evaluation: they had no wish to be thin, and their preoccupation with food was the work of hunger, not of a fear of fatness. Hunger supplies much of the psychology of an eating disorder, and dieting is its commonest first step; what turns starvation into anorexia nervosa or bulimia nervosa is the conviction that one's worth depends on one's shape and weight.

Chapter summary

8.1 Eating, Elimination and Sleep: Three Families of Disorder and Their History. Feeding, voiding and sleeping are bodily functions every child must learn; their disorders fill DSM-5-TR's chapters 10, 11 and 12. A feeding or eating disorder is a persistent disturbance of eating or eating-related behaviour that alters the consumption or absorption of food and impairs health or functioning; anorexia nervosa, bulimia nervosa and binge-eating disorder are mutually exclusive in a single episode, and obesity and food addiction are not diagnoses. Morton described nervous consumption (1689), Lasègue anorexie hystérique (April 1873) and Gull named anorexia nervosa (read 1873, printed 1874); Bruch turned attention to body image (The Golden Cage, 1978), Stunkard described night eating (1955) and binge eating (1959), and Russell named bulimia nervosa (1979). DSM-5 dropped amenorrhoea and set once a week for 3 months, and ICD-11 moved the sleep-wake disorders to their own Chapter 7.

8.2 Pica, Rumination Disorder and Avoidant/Restrictive Food Intake Disorder. The feeding disorders lack the disturbance of weight or shape that defines anorexia and bulimia; since DSM-5 they apply at any age, and beside another disorder each is diagnosed only when severe enough to warrant additional clinical attention. Pica is the persistent eating of non-nutritive, non-food substances for at least 1 month, inappropriate to developmental level (minimum age 2) and not culturally sanctioned; it goes with iron and zinc deficiency, pregnancy, intellectual disability and autism, and risks obstruction, bezoars and lead poisoning. Rumination disorder is repeated, effortless regurgitation for at least 1 month, treated with diaphragmatic breathing. ARFID, which replaced feeding disorder of infancy or early childhood, is sensory avoidance, lack of interest or fear of aversive consequences causing weight loss, deficiency, supplement dependence or psychosocial interference, without body-image disturbance; Thomas and Eddy's CBT-AR (2019) treats it.

8.3 Anorexia Nervosa: Criteria, the Starving Body and the Course. Anorexia nervosa needs restriction leading to a significantly low weight, intense fear of weight gain or persistent behaviour that interferes with gain, and a disturbed experience of weight or shape, undue influence of it on self-worth or failure to see the danger. DSM-5-TR sets no diagnostic BMI cut-off; the work group chaired by Walsh dropped DSM-IV's 85 percent example and amenorrhoea, which Roberto (2008) found added little. ICD-10 uses a BMI of 17.5 or less, ICD-11 below 18.5. The restricting type has no recurrent binge eating or purging in the last 3 months; severity runs from mild (17 or more) to extreme (below 15). The starving body is "slow and low": lanugo, bradycardia, hypotension, hypothermia, low T3 and osteoporosis, with hypokalaemia from purging. Crude mortality, about 5 percent per decade, is the highest of the eating disorders.

8.4 Bulimia Nervosa, Binge-Eating Disorder and the Other Specified Eating Disorders. A binge is a definitely large amount eaten in a discrete period (usually under 2 hours) with loss of control. Bulimia nervosa adds recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviour (vomiting, laxatives, diuretics, fasting, excessive exercise), both on average once a week for 3 months (twice a week in DSM-IV), with self-evaluation unduly influenced by shape and weight, outside episodes of anorexia. Weight is usually normal, so the clues are hypokalaemia, alkalosis, dental erosion, parotid swelling and Russell's sign, and crude mortality is nearly 2 percent per decade. Binge-eating disorder, a full diagnosis since DSM-5, needs three of five features, marked distress and no compensation. Weight, then binges, then compensation tell the three apart; they share body-image distortion, fear of fatness and psychological regulation of eating. Atypical anorexia, purging disorder and night eating syndrome are other specified forms.

8.5 Why Eating Disorders Develop and How They Are Treated. Eating disorders arise where several risks meet. The thin ideal harms when it is internalised (Thompson and Stice, 2001), as ballet students (Garner, 1987) and television in Fiji (Becker, 2002) show, while Sing Lee's non-fat-phobic patients (1993) show culture shaping the explanation. Dieting is the commonest first step: the Minnesota men showed that starvation itself produces many symptoms, and severe dieters were 18 times likelier to fall ill (Patton, 1999). Minuchin's psychosomatic family is no longer thought causal; perfectionism marks anorexia, impulsivity bulimia, and heritability is about half. Fairburn, Cooper and Shafran's transdiagnostic theory (2003) puts the over-evaluation of eating, shape and weight at the core. Weight is restored with monitored refeeding; no drug treats anorexia, fluoxetine (1996) helps bulimia and lisdexamfetamine (2015) binge-eating disorder. Family-based treatment comes first for adolescent anorexia, CBT for bulimia and binge eating.

8.6 Enuresis and Encopresis: The Elimination Disorders. Enuresis is the repeated voiding of urine into bed or clothes, twice a week for 3 consecutive months or with significant distress or impairment, at a developmental age of at least 5; it is nocturnal only (monosymptomatic, the commonest), diurnal only or both, and primary or secondary. Bedwetting follows a mismatch of night urine output (low vasopressin, Rittig, 1989), bladder capacity and arousal, with strong heredity; most children outgrow it. The urine alarm (Mowrer and Mowrer, 1938) is first-line and durable; desmopressin works fast but relapse follows. Encopresis, Weissenberg's word (1926), is the repeated passage of faeces in inappropriate places, once a month for 3 months, from age 4; lack of bowel control is encopresis, not enuresis. Its commoner subtype, with constipation and overflow incontinence, follows the retentive cycle and is treated by disimpaction, maintenance laxatives and scheduled toileting without punishment.

8.7 Normal Sleep, the Sleep-Wake Disorders and Insomnia Disorder. Polysomnography (EEG, EOG and EMG) describes sleep continuity (sleep latency, wake after sleep onset, sleep efficiency) and architecture (REM latency; a sleep-onset REM period begins within 15 minutes), and the multiple sleep latency test measures sleepiness. Classification moved from DSM-III-R's dyssomnias and parasomnias to DSM-5-TR's ten groups, ICSD-3 (2014) and ICD-11's Chapter 7. Insomnia disorder is dissatisfaction with sleep, through difficulty initiating or maintaining it or early waking, on 3 nights a week for 3 months despite adequate opportunity, with daytime distress, and it is diagnosed alongside any coexisting disorder; about 10 percent of adults meet the criteria. Spielman's 3P model (1987), hyperarousal (Bonnet and Arand, 1995) and Harvey's cognitive model (2002) explain its persistence, and Dement's Mr B. shows iatrogenic insomnia. Hypnotics are for short-term use; CBT-I, with stimulus control (Bootzin, 1972), sleep restriction and Frankl's paradoxical intention, comes first.

8.8 Hypersomnolence Disorder, Narcolepsy and Kleine-Levin Syndrome. Sleepiness differs from fatigue; its commonest cause is insufficient sleep. Hypersomnolence disorder (idiopathic hypersomnia) is sleepiness despite at least 7 hours of main sleep, three times a week for 3 months, with long unrefreshing naps and sleep inertia. Narcolepsy, described by Westphal (1877) and named by Gélineau (1880), needs an irrepressible need to sleep three times a week for 3 months plus cataplexy, low hypocretin-1 or a short REM latency; its tetrad of sleep attacks, cataplexy, sleep paralysis and hypnagogic hallucinations is REM sleep out of place. Type 1 follows the loss of hypothalamic hypocretin (orexin) neurons (found 1998 to 2000), probably by autoimmunity in carriers of HLA DQB1*06:02. Cataplexy, an emotion-triggered loss of tone, is not catalepsy, the waxy flexibility of catatonia. Modafinil, antidepressants and sodium oxybate treat narcolepsy. Kleine-Levin syndrome is recurrent hypersomnia with hyperphagia and hypersexuality, mostly in adolescent males.

8.9 Breathing-Related and Circadian Rhythm Sleep-Wake Disorders. In obstructive sleep apnoea hypopnoea the upper airway collapses while breathing effort continues; it needs at least 5 obstructive events an hour with snoring or sleepiness, or 15 regardless of symptoms, and it picks out obese, middle-aged men. Dickens's fat boy gave the "Pickwickian" name, Gastaut recorded the apnoeas (1965), and Sullivan's CPAP (1981) remains the standard treatment, with psychological help for adherence; Udwadia's Mumbai study (2004) found the syndrome in 7.5 percent of men. In central sleep apnoea, including Cheyne-Stokes breathing and its opioid-related form, the drive to breathe fails, and sleep-related hypoventilation is shallow breathing with raised carbon dioxide. The circadian rhythm sleep-wake disorders are the delayed sleep phase, advanced sleep phase, irregular, non-24-hour and shift work types; jet lag was dropped after DSM-IV. Timed bright light, melatonin, chronotherapy and forward-rotating shifts treat them.

8.10 Parasomnias, Restless Legs Syndrome and Substance-Induced Sleep Disorders. Parasomnias, which Mahowald and Schenck explained as state dissociation, are told apart by timing and recall. Sleepwalking (somnambulism) and sleep terrors (pavor nocturnus) are NREM sleep arousal disorders: incomplete awakenings from N3 in the first third of the night with amnesia, common in children and mostly outgrown, helped by scheduled awakenings (Durand and Mindell, 1999). Nightmare disorder arises from REM sleep late in the night, with rapid orientation and vivid recall; imagery rehearsal helps (Krakow, 2001). REM sleep behaviour disorder is dream enactment from loss of REM atonia, mostly in men over 50, and often heralds a synucleinopathy such as Parkinson's disease; clonazepam or melatonin treats it. Restless legs syndrome (Willis-Ekbom disease) is an evening urge to move the legs, relieved by movement and precipitated by iron deficiency. Substance-induced sleep disorders are diagnosed when sleep symptoms predominate in intoxication or withdrawal.

From the past papers

  1. Anorexia nervosa is primarily characterized by : (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Obsession with cleanliness (b) Fear of gaining weight and food restriction (c) Repeated vomiting after bingeing (d) Excessive exercise only

    Answer: (b) Fear of gaining weight and food restriction. DSM-5-TR defines anorexia nervosa by restriction of energy intake leading to a significantly low weight, an intense fear of gaining weight (or behaviour that prevents gain) and a disturbed experience of weight or shape (Sections 8.3.1 and 8.3.2). Obsession with cleanliness is the contamination theme of obsessive-compulsive disorder, repeated vomiting after bingeing describes bulimia nervosa (although vomiting also occurs in anorexia's binge-eating/purging type), and excessive exercise is only one of the methods patients use to lose weight.

  2. Amenorrhea is Present in anorexia nervosa. This term means: (RML 2021)

    (a) An intense fear of weight gain. (b) Excessive exercise. (c) Absence of menstrual cycle. (d) Body-image distortion.

    Answer: (c) Absence of menstrual cycle. Amenorrhoea (a-, without; men, month; rhoia, flow) is the absence of menstrual periods, common in anorexia because starvation shuts down the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis, although DSM-5 dropped it as a criterion (Section 8.3.3). Fear of weight gain, excessive exercise and body-image distortion are other features of the disorder, not the meaning of the word.

  3. Bulimia nervosa is characterised by recurrent binge eating and inappropriate use of compensatory behaviours. An example of compensatory behaviour is: (RML 2021)

    (a) Use of laxatives (b) Self-induced vomiting (c) Excessive exercising (d) All of the above

    Answer: (d) All of the above. Inappropriate compensatory behaviour is anything done to prevent weight gain after a binge: self-induced vomiting, misuse of laxatives, diuretics or other medications, fasting and excessive exercise (Section 8.4.2). Vomiting and laxatives are purging, while exercise and fasting are compensatory without being purging, so every option is an example and none alone is the answer.

  4. Unlike people with anorexia, individuals with bulimia (RML 2022)

    (a) Often purge themselves to stay thin (b) Are obsessed with being too fat (c) Can do damage to their health (d) May appear normal in weight

    Answer: (d) May appear normal in weight. People with bulimia nervosa are typically of normal weight or overweight, which is the key difference from anorexia nervosa, defined by a significantly low weight (Sections 8.4.3 and 8.4.6). Purging to stay thin (in anorexia's binge-eating/purging type), an obsession with being too fat and damage to health occur in both disorders, so none of them is a difference.

  5. Which of the following symptoms do anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa have in common? (RML 2020)

    (a) distorted body image (b) intense fear of being fat (c) eating regulated by psychological and not physical processes (d) all of the above

    Answer: (d) all of the above. Both disorders involve a distorted body image, an intense fear of being fat, and eating governed by rules, fears, moods and judgements of self-worth rather than by hunger and fullness (Section 8.4.6). What separates them is body weight, not any of these three features, so picking a single option would miss the other two.

  6. A patient with anorexia nervosa has traits of: (RML 2024)

    (a) Perfectionism (b) Impulsivity (c) Avoidance (d) Dependence

    Answer: (a) Perfectionism. The personality most consistently linked with anorexia nervosa is over-controlled: perfectionism, with obsessionality, rigidity and anxiety (Section 8.5.3). Impulsivity is the trait of bulimia nervosa and of anorexia's binge-eating/purging type, while avoidance and dependence are the cores of avoidant and dependent personality disorders, which some patients show but which are not the characteristic traits.

  7. Lack of bowel control in children is known as: (RML 2024)

    (a) Encopresis (b) Analism (c) Enuresis (d) Anorexia

    Answer: (a) Encopresis. Encopresis is the repeated passage of faeces into inappropriate places, at least once a month for 3 months, at a developmental age of at least 4 (Sections 8.6.1 and 8.6.5). Enuresis is the same problem with urine (from age 5), anorexia is an eating disorder, and "analism" is not a recognised disorder in any manual, only bait built from Freud's anal stage.

  8. In narcolepsy, which of the following is most characteristically present? (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Both cataplexy and catalepsy equally (b) Cataplexy: sudden loss of muscle tone triggered by emotions (c) Neither cataplexy nor catalepsy (d) Catalepsy: maintenance of imposed posture with waxy flexibility

    Answer: (b) Cataplexy: sudden loss of muscle tone triggered by emotions. Cataplexy, a brief, bilateral loss of muscle tone set off by emotion, above all laughter, with consciousness preserved, is the hallmark of narcolepsy type 1 and one of its diagnostic criteria (Sections 8.8.4 and 8.8.6). Catalepsy, a posture held with waxy flexibility, is a sign of catatonia, so "catalepsy", "both equally" and "neither" are all wrong.

  9. Which one of the following characterises somnambulism? (RML 2022)

    (a) Sitting, walking or performing complex behaviour while asleep (b) Extreme fear, agitation, screaming while asleep (c) Acting out nightmares (d) Uncomfortable sensations in the legs causing movement and loss of sleep

    Answer: (a) Sitting, walking or performing complex behaviour while asleep. Somnambulism (sleepwalking) is an NREM sleep arousal disorder: an incomplete awakening, usually from N3 in the first third of the night, with rising, walking or other complex behaviour, a blank stare and amnesia (Section 8.10.2). Extreme fear and screaming describe sleep terrors, acting out dreams describes REM sleep behaviour disorder, and uncomfortable leg sensations that force movement describe restless legs syndrome.

  10. Anorexia nervosa per DSM-5 requires: (practice mock)

    (a) Significantly low body weight, intense fear of weight gain or behaviours that interfere with weight gain, and disturbance in body image (b) BMI <17 in all cases (c) Amenorrhoea (still required) (d) Episodes of compensatory purging in all cases

    Answer: (a) Significantly low body weight, intense fear of weight gain or behaviours that interfere with weight gain, and disturbance in body image. These are DSM-5-TR's three criteria (Section 8.3.2). There is no diagnostic BMI cut-off (17 is only the line between mild and moderate severity), amenorrhoea was dropped in DSM-5, and purging defines the binge-eating/purging subtype, not the disorder.

  11. Anorexia nervosa, restricting subtype, is defined in DSM-5 by: (practice mock)

    (a) Required compensatory vomiting (b) No binge-eating or purging behaviours during the current 3-month episode; weight loss via dietary restriction, fasting, excessive exercise (c) Binge-purge cycles within 3 months (d) Required laxative use weekly

    Answer: (b) No binge-eating or purging behaviours during the current 3-month episode; weight loss via dietary restriction, fasting, excessive exercise. The restricting type means no recurrent binge eating or purging during the last 3 months, with weight lost by dieting, fasting or excessive exercise (Section 8.3.4). Vomiting, binge-purge cycles and laxatives describe the binge-eating/purging type, and DSM-5-TR sets no weekly frequency for purging in anorexia at all.

  12. Anorexia nervosa is associated with which medical finding? (practice mock)

    (a) Hyperthyroidism (b) Lanugo hair, bradycardia, hypokalaemia, amenorrhoea (c) Polyphagia (d) Hyperkalaemia and tachycardia

    Answer: (b) Lanugo hair, bradycardia, hypokalaemia, amenorrhoea. Starvation makes the body "slow and low": lanugo, bradycardia, hypotension, hypothermia, amenorrhoea and, with purging, hypokalaemia (Section 8.3.5). Hyperthyroidism is wrong (T3 is low, with reverse T3 raised), polyphagia is wrong because the patient restricts, and hyperkalaemia and tachycardia both point the wrong way.

  13. Which is the most lethal eating disorder in terms of mortality rate? (practice mock)

    (a) Bulimia nervosa (b) Binge eating disorder (c) Anorexia nervosa (d) Pica

    Answer: (c) Anorexia nervosa. Anorexia nervosa has a crude mortality of about 5 percent per decade and a standardised mortality ratio of about 5.9 (Arcelus and colleagues, 2011), from medical complications such as arrhythmia and from suicide (Section 8.3.7). Bulimia nervosa's crude mortality is nearly 2 percent per decade, and binge-eating disorder and pica, though harmful, are not the most lethal.

  14. Bulimia nervosa per DSM-5 requires binge-purge episodes occurring on average at least: (practice mock)

    (a) Twice per week for 6 months (b) Once per week for 3 months (c) Once per day for 1 month (d) Three times per week for 1 month

    Answer: (b) Once per week for 3 months. DSM-5 lowered the threshold from twice a week (DSM-III-R and DSM-IV) to binges and compensation both at least once a week for 3 months (Sections 8.1.6 and 8.4.2). "Twice per week for 6 months" matches no edition (the 6 months was DSM-IV's provisional rule for binge-eating disorder), and the other two options are invented.

  15. DSM-5 criteria for Binge Eating Disorder require binge episodes to occur on average at least: (practice mock)

    (a) Once weekly for 6 months (b) Twice monthly for 1 month (c) Once weekly for 3 months (d) Once daily for a year

    Answer: (c) Once weekly for 3 months. Binge-eating disorder uses the same threshold as bulimia nervosa, binges on average at least once a week for 3 months (Section 8.4.5). "Once weekly for 6 months" borrows DSM-IV's provisional period (binges on 2 days a week for 6 months), and the other two options match no edition.

  16. A patient presents with episodes of binge eating ≥1/week for ≥3 months, without compensatory behaviours, with marked distress about the binges. The DSM-5 diagnosis is: (practice mock)

    (a) Binge-eating disorder (b) Bulimia nervosa (c) Anorexia nervosa, binge-purge subtype (d) Avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder

    Answer: (a) Binge-eating disorder. Recurrent binges at least weekly for 3 months with marked distress and no compensatory behaviour is binge-eating disorder, a full diagnosis since DSM-5 (Section 8.4.5). Bulimia nervosa needs compensatory behaviour, the binge-eating/purging type of anorexia needs a significantly low weight, and ARFID is a disorder of eating too little, without binges and without concern about shape or weight.

  17. Anorexia nervosa is differentiated from bulimia nervosa primarily by: (practice mock)

    (a) Absence of body image disturbance in bulimia (b) Significantly low body weight in anorexia (c) Presence of compensatory behaviours only in anorexia (d) Absence of purging in anorexia

    Answer: (b) Significantly low body weight in anorexia. Significantly low body weight defines anorexia nervosa and takes precedence, whereas people with bulimia are usually of normal weight or overweight (Section 8.4.6). Body-image disturbance is present in both, compensatory behaviour occurs in both, and purging occurs in anorexia's binge-eating/purging type, so the other three options claim differences that do not exist.

  18. REM sleep behavior disorder (RBD) is characterised by: (practice mock)

    (a) Inability to enter any REM at all (b) Loss of REM atonia, causing patient to enact dream content (c) Bedwetting during N3 (d) Excessive daytime sleepiness without nocturnal symptoms

    Answer: (b) Loss of REM atonia, causing patient to enact dream content. In REM sleep behaviour disorder the normal paralysis of REM sleep fails, so the sleeper acts out his dreams and wakes alert with vivid recall, and the disorder often precedes a synucleinopathy such as Parkinson's disease (Section 8.10.4). The patient has plenty of REM sleep, bedwetting is enuresis (which is not tied to N3), and excessive daytime sleepiness without nocturnal symptoms describes hypersomnolence disorder.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. Anorexia nervosa (DSM-5-TR) needs all three: restriction of energy intake leading to a significantly low body weight; intense fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or persistent behaviour that interferes with weight gain; and a disturbed experience of weight or shape, undue influence of weight or shape on self-evaluation, or failure to recognise the seriousness of the low weight. No BMI cut-off, amenorrhoea or purging is required. asked 3 times
  2. Low body weight, not purging, separates anorexia nervosa from bulimia nervosa: people with bulimia are usually of normal weight or overweight, and a person at a significantly low weight who binges and purges has anorexia nervosa, binge-eating/purging type. asked 2 times
  3. Inappropriate compensatory behaviour in bulimia nervosa is self-induced vomiting, misuse of laxatives, diuretics or other medications, fasting or excessive exercise; cycles of binge eating and compensation are bulimia nervosa, and binges without compensation are binge-eating disorder. asked 2 times
  4. Amenorrhoea means the absence of menstrual periods; it is common in anorexia nervosa, from the starvation shutdown of the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis, but it entered the criteria in DSM-III-R and was dropped in DSM-5. asked 2 times
  5. Enuresis is the repeated voiding of urine into bed or clothes, at least twice a week for 3 consecutive months or with significant distress or impairment, at a developmental age of at least 5; night wetting clusters in the first third of the night but is not tied to N3 or any single stage. asked 2 times
  6. Bulimia nervosa and binge-eating disorder both need episodes on average at least once a week for 3 months (DSM-5); DSM-III-R and DSM-IV required twice a week for bulimia, and "6 months" comes only from DSM-IV's provisional criteria for binge-eating disorder. asked 2 times
  7. Anorexia nervosa, restricting type: no recurrent binge eating or purging during the last 3 months, with weight lost by dieting, fasting or excessive exercise; the binge-eating/purging type needs recurrent binges or purging, and purging after small meals is enough. asked 2 times
  8. ARFID is avoidance or restriction of food (sensory dislike, lack of interest or fear of aversive consequences) causing weight loss, deficiency, supplement dependence or psychosocial interference, with no disturbance of body weight or shape; selective eating without any of these consequences is ordinary picky eating. asked 2 times
  9. BMI numbers belong to different systems: DSM-5-TR rates adult severity as mild 17 or more, moderate 16 to 16.99, severe 15 to 15.99 and extreme below 15, with no diagnostic cut-off; ICD-10's threshold is a BMI of 17.5 or less; ICD-11's guide is below 18.5, with "dangerously low" below 14.0. asked 2 times
  10. Anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa share a distorted body image, an intense fear of being fat, and eating regulated by psychological rather than physiological processes. asked once
  11. Perfectionism, with obsessionality, rigidity and anxiety, is the characteristic trait of anorexia nervosa; impulsivity belongs to bulimia nervosa and to anorexia's binge-eating/purging type. asked once
  12. Lack of bowel control in a child is encopresis: repeated passage of faeces into inappropriate places, at least once a month for 3 months, at a developmental age of at least 4; the commoner subtype is with constipation and overflow incontinence. asked once
  13. Somnambulism (sleepwalking) is sitting up, walking or other complex behaviour during an incomplete awakening from NREM sleep (mostly N3) in the first third of the night, with a blank stare, difficulty waking and amnesia. asked once
  14. Sleep terrors (night terrors, pavor nocturnus) are abrupt arousals from N3 with a panicky scream, intense fear and autonomic arousal, little or no dream recall and amnesia; nightmares come from REM sleep later in the night, with full waking and vivid recall. asked once
  15. Restless legs syndrome (Willis-Ekbom disease) is an urge to move the legs with unpleasant sensations, brought on by rest, relieved by movement and worse in the evening, at least three times a week for 3 months; iron deficiency is its most important precipitant. asked once
  16. Cataplexy, a sudden, brief, bilateral loss of muscle tone triggered by emotion (especially laughter) with consciousness preserved, is the most characteristic sign of narcolepsy; catalepsy, a posture held with waxy flexibility, belongs to catatonia. asked once
  17. The narcoleptic tetrad is sleep attacks, cataplexy, sleep paralysis and hypnagogic hallucinations (assembled by Yoss and Daly, 1957; narcolepsy named by Gélineau, 1880); narcolepsy type 1 follows the loss of hypothalamic hypocretin (orexin) neurons. asked once
  18. Binge-eating disorder, a full diagnosis only since DSM-5, is recurrent binges with three or more of five features (eating rapidly, until uncomfortably full, when not hungry, alone from embarrassment, with disgust or guilt afterwards), marked distress and no recurrent compensatory behaviour. asked once
  19. The medical findings of anorexia nervosa are "slow and low": lanugo, bradycardia, hypotension, hypothermia, amenorrhoea, low T3 and, with purging, hypokalaemia; hyperthyroidism, polyphagia, hyperkalaemia and tachycardia are wrong-direction distractors. asked once
  20. Anorexia nervosa is the most lethal eating disorder: crude mortality about 5 percent per decade (bulimia nervosa nearly 2 percent) and a standardised mortality ratio of 5.86 (Arcelus and colleagues, 2011), from medical complications and suicide. asked once
  21. REM sleep behaviour disorder is the loss of normal REM atonia, so the sleeper acts out dreams and wakes alert with recall; about 75 percent of people with idiopathic RBD develop a synucleinopathy such as Parkinson's disease within 10 to 15 years. asked once
  22. Excessive sleepiness despite a main sleep of at least 7 hours, three times a week for 3 months, is hypersomnolence disorder (idiopathic hypersomnia), which never has cataplexy and whose long naps do not refresh. asked once
  23. Pica is the persistent eating of non-nutritive, non-food substances for at least 1 month, inappropriate to developmental level (minimum age of 2 suggested) and not culturally sanctioned; it can kill through obstruction or poisoning but is not the most lethal eating disorder. asked once
